# Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ (Chapter 21) — Expanded Verse-by-Verse Commentary

## Introduction to the Sūrah

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Assembly of the Prophets and the Approaching Reckoning**

Al-Anbiyāʾ, "The Prophets," is the twenty-first chapter of the Qur'an, comprising 112 verses of late-to-mid Makkan revelation. It takes its name from its glorious, panoramic assembly of sixteen named prophetic figures—along with Maryam—marching in a single, unbroken spiritual chain across verses 48–91. In classical hadith literature, Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ is grouped alongside the earliest treasures of revelation: ʿAbdullāh ibn Masʿūd famously stated in Ṣaḥīḥ al-Bukhārī (4994) that *“Banī Isrā'īl (al-Isrāʾ), al-Kahf, Maryam, Ṭā-Hā, and al-Anbiyāʾ are among the ancient treasures (*min al-ʿitāq al-uwal*) and they are of my earliest acquisitions.”* Emerging from the intense intellectual clashes of the Makkan period, Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ confronts human heedlessness with volcanic urgency: opening with the terrifying announcement that human reckoning has drawn dangerously near while mankind turns away in heedless play, and culminating in the magnificent declaration that the Prophet Muḥammad ﷺ is sent as nothing less than an all-encompassing mercy to all creation (*Raḥmatan li-l-ʿālamīn*, 21:107).

**The Slumber of Heedlessness and the Mockery of Truth**

The historical atmosphere of al-Anbiyāʾ is characterized by the frivolous cynicism of the Qurayshi elite. Whenever a new reminder from the Most Compassionate was recited, they listened to it while amusing themselves, whispering conspiratorially: *“Is this man anything more than a human being like yourselves? Will you then surrender to sorcery with your eyes wide open?”* (21:2–3). They dismissed the Qur'an as muddled dreams, invented poetry, or fabrications, demanding sensational physical miracles like those granted to the ancient messengers. Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ shatters this frivolous attitude. It reminds the audience that the cosmos was not created as an idle game or sport (*wa-mā khalaqnā al-samā'a wa-l-arḍa wa-mā baynahumā lāʿibīn*, 21:16), but with absolute, terrifying purpose. Truth is an active, crushing reality: *“Nay, We hurl the truth against falsehood, and it smashes its head, and behold, it vanishes!”* (21:18).

**The Grand Prophetic Procession and Thematic Arc**

The architecture of Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ moves from cosmic physics to prophetic history, and from the crucible of suffering to eschatological fulfillment. The opening movement (1–47) establishes the proximity of the Judgment, demonstrates the absurdity of polytheism (arguing that had there been gods other than Allah in heaven and earth, both would have been corrupted and ruined, 21:22), and presents profound cosmic signs: the heavens and earth joined together before being violently split apart, all living matter created from water (21:30), firm mountains stabilizing the crust, and every celestial sphere swimming in its appointed orbit (*kullun fī falakin yasbaḥūn*). The second movement (48–91) unfolds the glorious roll-call of the prophets, illustrating how each responded to intense existential crises: Moses and Aaron receiving the Criterion (*al-Furqān*); Abraham smashing the idols of his people, surviving the raging furnace transformed by divine command into coolness and peace (*kūnī bardan wa-salāmā*, 21:69); Isaac and Jacob gifted as leaders guiding by divine command; Lot delivered from the vile city; Noah saved from the great distress while his mockers drowned; David and Solomon gifted with wisdom, understanding the speech of birds and winds, and the mountains glorifying God with David; Job (*Ayyūb*) calling out in the depths of disease and poverty: *“Adversity has touched me, and You are the Most Merciful of the merciful,”* whereupon his health and family were restored; Ishmael, Idrīs, and Dhū al-Kifl praised for unyielding patience; Jonah (*Dhū al-Nūn*) crying out from the triple darkness of the night, the sea, and the belly of the whale: *“There is no deity except You; exalted are You. Indeed, I have been of the wrongdoers,”* an immortal prayer guaranteed to deliver believers from distress (21:87); Zechariah crying out in loneliness, gifted John; and Maryam who guarded her chastity, into whom the divine spirit was breathed. The third movement (92–112) unites all these diverse messengers into a single spiritual fraternity: *“Indeed, this community of yours is one single community, and I am your Lord, so worship Me”* (21:92). It warns of the breaching of Gog and Magog (*Ya'jūj wa-Ma'jūj*), describes the sky being rolled up on the Last Day like a scribe rolling up a scroll, confirms that the righteous servants shall inherit the earth (21:105), and closes with the universal prophetic charter: *“And We have not sent you, [O Muḥammad], except as a mercy to the worlds”* (21:107).

**The Unity of the Prophetic Mission**

The core theological argument of Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ is the indivisible unity of divine guidance. Across millennia, geographical divides, and varied trials, every prophet walked the identical path: calling humanity to *Tawḥīd*, enduring the mockery of their contemporaries, experiencing extreme human vulnerability, turning to God with desperate prayer, and finding miraculous deliverance through divine grace. Prophethood is not an ancient fairy tale; it is the living proof that God does not abandon humanity to the chaos of heedlessness. By framing Muḥammad ﷺ as the universal culmination of this mercy, al-Anbiyāʾ assures the believer that no furnace is too hot for God's coolness, no whale's belly is too dark for God's light, and no wound is too deep for God's healing.

---

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:1

> ˹The time of˺ people’s judgment has drawn near, yet they are heedlessly turning away

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Reckoning That Comes Closer the More One Strays**

The opening of al-Anbiyāʾ announces a temporal paradox: the reckoning draws near precisely because people turn away. The Arabic verb *iqtaraba* — to draw near, to come close — places the hearer inside a process already in motion. The reckoning is not a future appointment that one awaits; it is a present pressure that one ignores. Every additional hour of heedlessness is an hour closer to the hour that heedlessness can no longer evade. The classical commentators cited in the source for this verse (al-Rāzī, al-Qurṭubī, al-Zamakhsharī) record the observation that *iqtaraba* ordinarily describes someone who has covered more than half the distance and is therefore closer to the arrival than to the start. Used of the Day of Judgment, the verb converts cosmic future into imminent present.

The pairing of the two halves of the verse is a single moral claim, not two. The reckoning is near; people turn away heedlessly. The nearness is fixed; the turning is chosen. The juxtaposition means that the alarming fact is not the closeness of the reckoning but the leisure with which the addressees meet it. The same pairing returns in 70:6–7, where the deniers see the hour as far off while God sees it as near. There is no contradiction: the distance is measured in calendar years, the proximity in moral weight. A thing can be centuries away in time and already pressing on the conscience of anyone who has stopped to think.

**Reckoning as a Qur'anic Architectural Term**

The word *ḥisāb* — reckoning, account — is one of the Book's central architectural terms. It names the operation by which every deed, intention, and word is entered, weighed, and returned to its owner. The same root appears at 38:16, 40:28, and elsewhere to designate the Day itself, suggesting that for the Qur'an, the Day of Judgment and the operation of accounting are not two things but one. The classical commentators add an observation worth keeping: although the reckoning is announced as near, the precise moment is not given. The Qur'an insists on the fact of the reckoning without disclosing the hour, because disclosure would collapse moral seriousness into arithmetic. If people knew the day, every act in its shadow would be a forced move.

This verse therefore resists a temptation common in religious rhetoric — the temptation to treat the future as if it were safely distant. *Ṭabarī* records the reading that *their reckoning* refers to the personal reckoning each soul will face at death, and that this reckoning is even closer than the universal one, because the term of every soul is bounded by the next breath. This is consistent with the broader Qur'anic argument that human beings typically live as if the reckoning concerned someone else, in some other century, in some other country. The verse denies that displacement.

**The Three Forms of Heedlessness**

The Sufi commentator al-ʿAjlūnī, cited in the source, distinguishes three kinds of heedlessness with respect to the Hereafter. The first is the heedlessness of those engrossed in worldly affairs, who never raise their eyes from the ledger of profit and loss. The second is the heedlessness of those who, having renounced the world, become so absorbed in devotional remembrance that they give no thought to either Paradise or Hell. The third is the heedlessness of those who have not yet been reached by the reminder at all. The verse addresses itself most directly to the first group, because they hear the reminder and turn away; but the typology is useful, because it shows that turning away is not always loud. Sometimes it is quiet, polite, and dressed in the language of having better things to do.

The phrase *wa hum fī ghaflatin muʿriḍūn* — while they are in heedlessness turning away — packs both terms into a single motion. Heedlessness is the inward state; turning away is its outward gesture. The verse diagnoses a coordinated posture: people do not simply fail to know; they actively refuse to be told. The *muʿriḍūn* — those who turn aside — are not ignorant; they have turned their backs on what they know. To turn aside from a known truth is worse than never to have heard it, because the truth has already lodged in the conscience and the conscience has chosen to ignore it.

**Why Nearness, Not Distance, Is the Point**

A religion that announced the reckoning as distant would invite procrastination. The Qur'an does the opposite. By placing the reckoning as already drawing near, it converts every ordinary day into a day that matters. *Ibn Kathīr* notes that some of the early generations understood the verse as a warning that the present age is the field of the next, and that the field does not wait. The verse therefore calls for a particular kind of attention: not the dramatic attention of a person expecting catastrophe tomorrow, but the steady attention of a person who knows that the reckoning is already in progress. Each act is being recorded; each breath is being weighed; the close of the book is nearer than the open page.

The verse thus breaks one of the most persistent illusions of religious life: that ethical seriousness can be deferred until one is older, freer, less burdened, more settled. The reckoning is not waiting at the end of a comfortable interval; it is approaching, and the approach itself is part of what is being announced. The hearer is being given a chance to live differently now, in the time that remains, rather than to mourn the loss of that time later.

**A Word for an Audience That Already Knew**

The Qur'an addresses a community that had heard the same warning in many forms. The opening of al-Anbiyāʾ is therefore not a first announcement but a renewed one. The Meccan audience knew the language of reckoning; they had heard it in other sūrahs; they had among them converts and opponents who could recite the warnings themselves. The verse addresses that familiarity directly: you have heard this; the question is what you do with the hearing. The structure is the structure of prophetic speech generally — to call a community back to what it already half-knows, by saying it again with enough force that the half-knowledge becomes whole.

This is also why the verse is so short. The Qur'an is not arguing for the nearness of the reckoning; it is announcing it. The brevity is the form the announcement takes. A long argument would invite the hearer to assess the argument; a short declaration forces the hearer to assess himself. The opening of al-Anbiyāʾ is the opening of a sūrah that will spend many verses arguing with its audience; it begins with a sentence that does not argue at all.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:2

> Whatever new reminder comes to them from their Lord, they only listen to it jokingly

**Expanded Commentary**

**Play as the Chosen Posture of Those Who Refuse**

The verse pairs with 21:1 to form a single moral statement. The reckoning draws near; people turn away heedlessly; and the form that turning takes is play. The Arabic *laʿib* — play, sport, amusement — names a deliberate downgrading of seriousness. Whatever new reminder descends, they receive it not as a summons but as a performance. The verse is not accusing the audience of being uninformed; it accuses them of being misinformed on purpose. They know that the message is a message; they have decided to treat it as entertainment.

The commentators note that *laʿib* in the Qur'an is associated with a settled state of *ghaflah* — heedlessness. The same pairing appears at 6:91, 43:83, 52:12, and 70:42. To treat revelation as play is therefore not a casual reaction; it is the consistent outward gesture of a particular inward condition. The condition cannot be cured by louder announcements, because the louder the announcement, the more elaborate the play. The Qur'an often responds to this dynamic by intensifying the warning rather than softening it — a counter-intuitive move, but one consistent with the verse's own logic. Play is met, in the Qur'an's rhetoric, with a severity that refuses to play along.

**The Word "New" — And What It Reveals**

The verse uses the adjective *ḥadīth* — new, fresh. Whatever new reminder comes to them, they listen jokingly. The adjective is striking. It implies that the previous reminders were already known, that the audience has been addressing this same message for some time, and that even the novelty of a fresh revelation is insufficient to break the posture of play. A new warning should, by its very newness, force a reconsideration; here it is folded into the same reception as the old ones. The hearers are not embarrassed by repetition; they are entertained by it.

The structure is rhetorically devastating. A community that has heard the same message many times might be expected, by the time a fresh reminder comes, to be either converted or hardened. The Qur'an says they are hardened, and it specifies the form of hardness: they listen to the message as one listens to a song heard before. The pleasure of recognition replaces the labour of response. *Rūmī* would later say, in a different idiom, that when the ear has been tickled many times by a familiar melody, the heart stops expecting the melody to be a summons.

**From Jesting to Refusal — the Grammar of Turning Away**

Classical Arabic syntax places *laʿibīn* — jokingly, in play — in the accusative as a *ḥāl*, a state of being. They listen *while* they are in a state of play. The construction is significant: it tells us that the play is not a momentary lapse but the condition under which the listening occurs. They are not people who listen first and then make jokes afterward; they are people whose listening and whose joking are simultaneous. To them, the act of receiving revelation and the act of being amused are one continuous motion.

This is why the verse refuses to characterise them as ignorers. An ignorer at least fails to receive. These hearers receive; they simply refuse to receive as receivers. They convert the reception into a spectacle, and the spectacle into an occasion for collective amusement. The Qur'an names this dynamic in its own vocabulary — *laʿawa* — turning away, treating the message as something that one hears and passes by. The grammatical pairing of *laʿibīn* (joking) with *wa hum fī ghaflah* (in heedlessness) in the verses just before shows that play, in this idiom, is the surface expression of a deeper refusal.

**Why the Qur'an Refuses to Match Their Lightness**

A preacher addressing such an audience might be tempted to lighten his own rhetoric, to meet play with play, to make the message charming enough to slip past the defence. The Qur'an takes the opposite path. It intensifies the warnings, multiplies the signs, and catalogues the fates of earlier communities that took the same posture. The strategy is deliberate: play can only be defeated by refusing to play. The seriousness of the announcement is itself the antidote to the lightness of the reception. The audience can resist an argument; they cannot easily resist the sustained gravity of a voice that will not be amused.

The verse is also a record. It says that whatever new reminder comes, *they only listen to it jokingly*. The word *only* — *illā* — is restrictive. It excludes every other mode of reception. They do not sometimes weep, sometimes ponder, sometimes dispute; they listen *only* in jest. This is the Qur'an's way of saying that the audience is consistent. The consistency is itself a verdict. To be uniformly amused by a uniform warning is to have decided, once and for all, what one thinks of it.

**A Diagnosis That Does Not Flatter Its Readers**

The verse is dangerous because it is not addressed to a past audience alone. It is read every day by people who recognise in themselves some version of the pattern — the habit of hearing the message and turning it into material for conversation, for performance, for the amusement of friends. The Qur'an's diagnosis is unflattering: the joke is not a separate activity from the hearing; the joke *is* the hearing, and the hearing has already chosen. To call it jokingly is to say that the decision was made before the sentence was finished. The verse, like the rest of al-Anbiyāʾ, refuses to leave the reader a comfortable category in which to file the warning. The audience of the seventh-century market and the audience of the twenty-first-century reader are joined in the same diagnosis; the diagnosis does not soften for either.

**A Closing Image of the Receiver Taken Captive**

The closing image the verse leaves is of a person who has been captured by the very posture he chose. To listen jokingly is to train the heart to take every fresh reminder as another item in the same repertoire. Over time, the play hardens; what began as a way of keeping distance becomes the very mechanism by which the heart refuses any approach. The Qur'an elsewhere calls this hardening *qaswa* — a heart sealed off from the kind of attention that would let the reminder through (2:74). The verse at 21:2 is the first step toward that seal. The community that hears a fresh reminder and turns it into amusement has begun the process by which no further reminder will be able to reach it. The Qur'an's warning is, in this sense, both descriptive and predictive.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:3

> with their hearts ˹totally˺ distracted. The evildoers would converse secretly, ˹saying,˺ “Is this ˹one˺ not human like yourselves? Would you fall for ˹this˺ witchcraft, even though you can ˹clearly˺ see?”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Private Counsel of Those Who Have Already Decided**

The verse moves from public reception to private counsel. The audience has been described as listening jokingly; the verse now describes what they say to one another when the listening is over. The shift is from the macro to the micro, from the public performance to the back-room conversation. *Tanājū* — to converse secretly, to mutter in private — is a Qur'anic term of art. It designates the conference of those who share a settled position and want to coordinate the next move without being overheard. The verse imagines the inside of that conversation: *Is this one not human like yourselves?*

The question is rhetorical, but its force is not in the question mark. It is in the framing. *Human like yourselves* — *basharun mithlukum*. The objection is not that Muhammad is human; everyone in Mecca knows he is human. The objection is that he is human *and nothing more*. The secret conference is constructing an ontology: a human being, like them, without apparatus, without visible credentials, without the apparatus that would distinguish him from any other man in the market. The argument is from the visible to the invisible, and the inference is dismissive: because he looks like us, he cannot be what he says he is. The dismissal is the entire content of the conference.

**The Visible — As the Limit of What They Will Admit**

The verse ends the question with *wa antum tubṣirūn* — even while you see. The phrase is decisive. The audience has eyes; they can see him; the seeing ought to be a witness. But they have converted the seeing into evidence against him. To see a man is to know he is a man, and to know he is a man is to know he cannot be the bearer of revelation. The argument is the oldest objection to prophecy in the Qur'an: *Why has no treasure been granted to him, or a garden for him to eat from?* (25:8); *If only an angel were sent down with him!* (6:8). The objection recurs because it is the natural objection of a mind that has decided in advance what a messenger must look like.

The Qur'an's response to this objection across the sūrahs is to insist that messengers are sent in the form of their addressees (21:7). The choice is not a deficiency; it is a condition of legibility. A messenger from outside the human form could not be tested, could not be opposed, could not be refused on the basis of a free decision. The secret counsellors of 21:3 want a messenger who cannot be refused. The form of the question reveals what kind of messenger they would accept: one whose refusal would be impossible because the credentials would be physically coercive. *Tubṣirūn* — you see — exposes the limit of their empiricism. They will only believe what they can see; they have ruled out in advance that what they see might be the form of what they cannot.

**The Hearts That Distract Themselves**

The opening of the verse — *their hearts distracted* — uses the verb *shaghal*, the root from which *shaghl* (occupation, pre-occupation) comes. The hearts are not empty; they are full of something else. Distraction is not absence of mind; it is presence of a different mind. The audience is thinking about something else while the reminder is being delivered. The Qur'an names the something else in 63:9: *Do not let your wealth and your children distract you from the remembrance of Allah* (63:9). The distraction has a content; it is the cares of the world, the business of trade, the management of households. The hearts are not vacant. They are at work on something other than what is being addressed.

The grammar is also pointed. *Qulūbuhum fī shughlin* — their hearts in occupation. The preposition *fī* marks containment: the hearts contain the distraction, the way a vessel contains liquid. The reminder arrives, but the vessel is full. Nothing new can be poured in. The image explains why every new revelation is met with the same response. The vessel is full; the reminder spills. The hearers do not reject what they have not heard; they reject what they have no room to hold.

**Witchcraft As the Decided Diagnosis**

The secret counsel chooses the word *siḥr* — witchcraft, sorcery — to describe what the messenger brings. The choice is significant. The Qur'an elsewhere records that the same accusation was made against Moses (7:109; 26:35) and against earlier messengers. Witchcraft is not a natural category of dismissal; it is a category that mixes marvel with menace. To call a message witchcraft is to concede that something is happening while denying that the something is from God. The conference is not denying that the recitation is striking; it is denying that the striking is real. The verses deliver a verdict that the audience will not yet speak aloud.

*Wa antum tubṣirūn* — even while you see — is therefore the verse's final blade. The accusation of witchcraft is being made by people who have witnessed the recitation as recited and who know, in some part of themselves, that the experience of hearing it is not the experience of hearing an ordinary speech. The seeing is the very thing they must discount to keep the conference coherent. The Qur'an records this double-think without commentary; the commentary is the verse itself, by way of completion.

**What the Qur'an Does Not Argue**

The verse is unusual in the Qur'an for not immediately rebutting the accusation. It records the conference and lets the record itself do the work. The conference is presented as a closed system: an objection, a diagnosis, an agreement among the like-minded. The Qur'an has no stake in arguing within the conference; its stake is in showing what such conferences look like from outside. The verse's power is documentary. It presents a familiar motion — the private rejection of an unwelcome messenger — and asks the reader to recognise it.

The fact that the conference is recorded at all is the answer. A messenger whose message was merely human flattery would not generate the particular vocabulary of dismissal — witchcraft, sorcery, magic — that recurs in the Qur'an. The vocabulary betrays the weight of the phenomenon. The audience would not need such strong words if the message were weak. The diagnosis is its own refutation, recorded as part of the revelation rather than smuggled in afterward.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:4

> The Prophet responded, “My Lord ˹fully˺ knows every word spoken in the heavens and the earth. For He is the All-Hearing, All-Knowing.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Short Reply to a Long Conference**

The previous verse records a secret conference; this verse records a one-sentence reply. The disproportion is the point. The audience has accused; the Prophet answers in a single sentence that does not address the accusation directly. He does not defend himself against the charge of being a human like themselves; he does not argue with the diagnosis of witchcraft; he does not answer the implicit demand for visible credentials. He says: my Lord knows every word spoken in the heavens and the earth, and He is All-Hearing, All-Knowing.

The asymmetry is the whole theology of prophecy. The accusation is from below; the response is from above. The conference of 21:3 argued from the visible to dismiss the invisible; the reply of 21:4 invokes the One who sees what is spoken in both realms and therefore is not dependent on the verdict of the conference. The Prophet's reply is not a defense; it is a transference of jurisdiction. He declines to litigate his own case in a court that has already decided it, and he cites instead the court that has not.

**"Every Word Spoken" — The Comprehensiveness of God's Knowledge**

The verse specifies *every word spoken in the heavens and the earth* — *kulli qawlin yuqālu fī al-samāwāti wa al-arḍ*. The construction is precise. *Qawl* in Arabic covers not only speech but the more general category of uttered intention — promises, threats, propositions, declarations. *Yuqālu* — that is said — extends the range beyond the present moment to every word that is, was, or will be uttered in either realm. The divine Knowledge, the verse says, encompasses the entire speech of both worlds. Nothing said in the heavens escapes; nothing said on the earth escapes.

The address therefore performs two acts at once. First, it reassures the Prophet that his message has been heard by the only One whose hearing matters. The conference of the disbelievers is recorded; the conference is known; the Lord of the heavens and the earth has heard it. Second, it warns the same conference that their whispered counsel is not private. The vocabulary chosen for divine Hearing is total. *Al-Samīʿ* — the All-Hearing — is paired with *al-ʿAlīm* — the All-Knowing — to cover both the audible surface and the intelligible interior of every utterance. The whisper, the innuendo, the half-finished sentence: all are in the register.

**Why the Reply Cites Hearing and Knowing, Not Speaking**

The verse names two divine attributes — Hearing and Knowing — but does not name Speaking. The omission is telling. The audience has accused the Prophet of fabricating speech; the Prophet's response cites the One who hears everything. The shift is from the speaker's claim to the hearer's record. Whatever is said in the heavens and the earth, including this accusation and this recitation, has already been entered into a book (10:61; 27:75). The divine Hearing is not passive reception; it is the taking of testimony. The conference has spoken; the testimony is taken.

This is also why the reply is short. A long reply would compete with the conference; a short reply refuses the competition. The Prophet declines to extend the exchange. The whole Qur'anic response to objections against the Prophet is structured this way: the objections are recorded fully, sometimes at length, and the rebuttals are brief or implicit. The structure tells the hearer that the conversation is not between the messenger and his opponents; it is between God and both. The messenger's role is to convey, not to litigate. He has conveyed; the rebuttal is in the divine attribute invoked in the verse.

**A Reply That Includes the Accusers**

The verse says "every word spoken in the heavens and the earth." The accusers' words are among those spoken in the earth. The verse therefore does not bypass the conference; it absorbs it. The Prophet is not telling his Lord what the audience has said; he is acknowledging that his Lord already knows. The acknowledgment is a verdict. The audience has not yet spoken the verdict, but the verdict is implicit in the very fact that the words have been heard.

This implicit verdict is what gives the verse its quiet force. The conference of 21:3 was self-congratulatory; the conference imagined itself as the private jury that would settle the question of the Prophet. The verse tells the hearer that the conference is itself on trial. *Every word spoken* — including the conference's words — has been heard. The hearer is being asked to imagine his own whispering as it sounds in the divine register. The hearing is not the angelic recording of moralists; it is the testimony of One whose Knowledge is commensurate with His Hearing. The whisper will not be the final word because the whisper has already been heard by One whose hearing is total.

**The Reverse of the Conference's Diagnosis**

The conference of 21:3 used the visible to dismiss the invisible. The Prophet's reply reverses the operation. He uses the invisible (God's Knowledge of every word in both realms) to make the visible (the conference, the accusation, the whisper) suddenly transparent. The reversal does not argue against the conference; it changes the court. The conference had argued that because the messenger is human, he is not the bearer. The reply argues that because God knows every word in both realms, the human messenger's words are also within the divine Knowing, and therefore to argue with the messenger is to argue with the One who sent him.

The verse is therefore not a defence but a re-framing. The Prophet has not argued the question of his credentials; he has argued the question of the courtroom. The conference wanted the case tried by the visible; the Prophet has moved it to the invisible. The move is irreversible, and it is also the move the rest of the Qur'an will repeat: every challenge to the message is reframed as a challenge to the One who sent it. The conference cannot win the argument without addressing the One whose Knowledge is total. The reframing is therefore the answer.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:5

> Yet they say, “This ˹Quran˺ is a set of confused dreams! No, he has fabricated it! No, he must be a poet! So let him bring us a ˹tangible˺ sign like those ˹prophets˺ sent before.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**Three Diagnoses, One Refusal**

The verse records three competing accusations, and the structure of the verse is itself a confession. *Aḍghāthu aḥlām* — confused dreams; *bal iftarāhu* — he fabricated it; *bal huwa shāʿirun* — he is a poet. The repetition of *bal* — no, rather — shows the audience revising its own diagnosis in real time. They have not settled on a single account of what the Qur'an is. The first account is that the recitation is a chaos of dream-images; the second is that it has been manufactured; the third is that it is the work of a poet. The verses will not be reconciled, because the verses are not arguments; they are the successive guesses of an audience that knows it does not know.

The diagnosis matters because of its instability. An audience confident in its objection would give one objection. This audience gives three in a single sentence and abandons each as it utters it. *Bal* marks the overturning. The first diagnosis is rejected by the second; the second is rejected by the third; the third lingers only because it is the last and has nothing to contradict it. The instability is the verdict. The Qur'an has destabilised its accusers without yet speaking; the accusers are contradicting themselves.

**The Demand for a Tangible Sign**

After three diagnoses, the verse records a demand: *fa lītīnā bi-āyatin kama al-arṣalu al-awwalūn* — so let him bring us a sign like those sent before. The demand is the operative one; the three diagnoses are its rhetorical setup. The audience is willing to grant the possibility that the recitation is *something*; they are not willing to grant that it is enough. Whatever the recitation is — dreams, fabrication, poetry — it lacks what earlier prophets had: the visible, coercive, externally verifiable sign.

The pattern repeats. Pharaoh demanded a sign of Moses and was given nine (7:133); the people of Salih demanded a she-camel (7:73); the Quraysh demanded a fountain, a garden, an angel (25:7–8). The common element is the demand that the sign be of the kind that bypasses the freedom of the hearer. A sign that admits no refusal is the only sign the audience will accept. The request is not for evidence; the request is for compulsion.

**Why the Qur'an Records These Objections at Length**

A reader might expect the Qur'an to interrupt the objections with a quick rebuttal. It does not. It records the full range of objections and then, in 21:6, gives a one-sentence response. The disproportion is intentional. The Qur'an wants its audience to see the full catalogue of refusal so that the response can be understood as a response to the whole pattern, not to any particular objection. The objections are not the result of careful investigation; they are the by-products of a refusal to investigate.

The audience's three diagnoses also correspond to three classical responses to any extraordinary text. *Aḥlām* — dreams — was the response to visionary literature in the pre-Islamic world; what cannot be argued is dismissed as oneiric. *Iftirā* — fabrication — is the response of those who suspect forgery; what does not match their expectation must have been made up. *Shiʿr* — poetry — was the response of an audience that had a developed concept of poetry as a mode of eloquence with its own rules; anything that resembled it but exceeded it was classified under the same rubric. The Qur'an names all three so that no future objection will have the air of novelty.

**The "Signs Like Those Sent Before"**

The audience's demand for a sign like the earlier prophets reveals a deep feature of the objection: they are not objecting to the content of the message; they are objecting to the form of its delivery. They want the sign they would recognise, namely the coercive, externally visible miracle. Earlier prophets had been sent to communities that demanded such signs; the Qur'an does not dispute the historical fact. It disputes the inference drawn from it. The demand that a later prophet deliver an earlier prophet's sign is the demand that the same age be repeated; it confuses the conditions of revelation with the content of revelation.

This is also the only objection that reveals the audience's anxiety. They are not, in this verse, accusing the Prophet of being a fraud; they are asking him to produce a coercive proof. The request presupposes that they would accept the message if the sign were given. But the Qur'an, in verses that follow, will not give the requested sign, because to give it would be to confirm the very ontology the audience has constructed — an ontology in which God coerces assent. The Qur'an's reply, in 21:6, is a refusal grounded not in inability but in principle.

**A Reply the Audience Cannot Use**

The verse's structure is decisive. It records three diagnoses, each of which the next cancels; it then records a demand that the demand itself makes absurd. The audience has asked for what earlier prophets brought; the answer is the same answer given to those earlier communities — the answer did not save them, and the sign was not the cause of their belief when it was given. The audience's demand, in other words, has already been answered historically, and the answer was negative. The sign they want has already come to other peoples, and those peoples did not believe. The objection is therefore not about evidence; it is about will. The catalogue of objections is the catalogue of one refusal wearing different masks.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:6

> Not a ˹single˺ society We destroyed before them ever believed ˹after receiving the signs˺. Will these ˹pagans˺ then believe

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Counter-Evidence That Closes the Argument**

The previous verse closed with a demand for a tangible sign; this verse answers the demand with a fact. Not one community that was destroyed believed after the sign was sent. The Qur'an addresses the request by noting that the request has already been granted historically, and the historical record is against the requesters. When the signs came, those who received them did not believe, and they were destroyed. The argument is empirical: you are asking for what has already happened, and what happened did not produce what you say it would produce.

The verse has a chilling structure. The audience wants a sign; the Qur'an says that signs have been given; the sign-givers were destroyed because the signs did not save them; the addressees are invited to consider whether they want the same fate. The argument is not that signs cannot be given; it is that the signs, when given, did not produce belief in those who received them. The audience has imagined that belief follows sign; the Qur'an denies this empirically. The audience is asked whether it will be the exception.

**"Will These Then Believe?" — The Rhetorical Turn**

The closing question is rhetorical but it is also a concession. The Qur'an is not denying that the audience *could* believe. It is denying that the *demand* for a sign, understood as a precondition for belief, is itself a sign of belief. A community that says "we will believe only when we see" is a community that has already refused to believe without seeing. The request reveals the will; the will is the obstacle. The verse addresses the obstacle, not the request.

The verse therefore does not refuse to give signs. It refuses to give them on the audience's terms, because the audience's terms convert signs into tests of power rather than tests of sincerity. A sign given on the audience's terms would not produce the belief the audience is offering; it would produce a coerced submission that the audience would call belief. The Qur'an refuses the counterfeit because the counterfeit is not what the audience actually wants, however much it says it does.

**The History the Verse Assumes**

To grasp the verse, the audience must already know the history of earlier communities: the people of Noah, of 'Ad, of Thamud, of Lot, of the dwellers of the thicket (al-Aykah), of Pharaoh. The Qur'an asks the audience to recall that every one of these communities was sent a sign and rejected it. The she-camel of Salih was a sign; the nine signs of Moses were signs; the angelic visitors of Lot were signs. The audience knows these stories; the stories are part of the cultural memory of the Arabs and of the People of the Book whom the Qur'an also addresses. The verse is therefore not arguing; it is reminding. The reminder is enough to dissolve the demand.

The dissolution is the verse's polemical edge. The audience has asked for something the Qur'an says it has been given. The audience has been answered in a form it does not want to receive, because the form is the verdict. The signs were given; the signs did not save; the destroyers came. The audience is being told, in this single sentence, that its demand has been historically tested and historically falsified. The audience is asked whether it wants to repeat the experiment.

**Why "After Receiving the Signs" Is the Critical Phrase**

The verse specifies *min baʿdi mā jāʾahum* — after what came to them. The order matters. The verse is not saying that no community believed before the sign; it is saying that no community believed *after* it. The audience's argument depends on the unspoken premise that the sign would produce belief. The verse specifies that, in the historical record, the sign did not. Belief, where it existed, preceded the sign; refusal, where it existed, persisted despite it. The chronological reversal exposes the audience's argument as a contradiction. They are asking for what has already been shown not to work.

The verse also protects the principle that signs are not coercive. A sign that produces belief by compulsion is not a sign of the kind the Qur'an endorses. The signs in the historical record were not coercive in that sense; they were indications. The communities saw them and chose against them. The choice is recorded; the destruction is recorded; the destruction is the consequence of the choice, not of the sign.

**The Audience as the Possible Exception**

The closing question — *Will these then believe?* — is asked without specifying the answer. The verse is not saying they will not; it is saying that to claim they will, on the basis of receiving a sign, contradicts the historical pattern. The exception is logically possible but experientially absent. The verse therefore turns the request back on the requester. If you believe the sign would change you, you are claiming to be the exception; if you do not believe that, the request is incoherent; if you do not know which, the request is premature. Each possibility cancels the demand.

The argument is the architecture of the entire sūrah's polemic against the Meccan audience. Throughout al-Anbiyāʾ, the Qur'an returns to the pattern: a demand, a reminder of historical pattern, a refusal to repeat what has not worked. The sūrah's audience is invited, by the cumulative pressure of these reminders, to be the exception on the right side rather than on the wrong side. The closing question of 21:6 is the first move in a long argument that the Qur'an will make in many forms.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:7

> We did not send ˹messengers˺ before you ˹O Prophet˺ except mere men inspired by Us. If you ˹polytheists˺ do not know ˹this already˺, then ask those who have knowledge ˹of the Scriptures˺

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Form of the Messenger As the Form of the Addressees**

The previous verse closed with an empirical observation: signs did not produce belief in those who received them. This verse begins with an explanation: messengers were sent in the form of those to whom they were sent. *Mere men inspired by Us* — *rijālan nūḥī ilayhim* — names the condition without apology. The messengers were not angels; the messengers were not visible composites of celestial and terrestrial matter; the messengers were men who received inspiration. The Arabic *nūḥī* is the same root as *waḥy*, the Qur'anic term for revelation; the verb is in the first person plural, locating the inspiration in God's act and the reception in the human agent.

The verse addresses an objection raised earlier in the sūrah and elsewhere in the Qur'an: if this message is from God, why is the messenger human? The verse answers by saying that this is the historical pattern. The objection is to a pattern that the Qur'an affirms. The affirmation is not a defence; it is a definition. A messenger must be someone the addressees can see, speak with, refuse, and believe. The messenger's visibility is part of the test.

**Why Not Angels — And Why the Verse Says So**

If the audience had wanted a non-human messenger, the consequence would have been the abolition of the test. A messenger who is visibly distinct from the audience — an angel, a thunder-bearing apparition — would coerce assent. The hearer would not choose; the hearer would be overwhelmed. The Qur'an elsewhere makes the same argument: *Had We sent an angel, the matter would have been decided and they would have had no respite* (32:18). The structure of the test requires a messenger the addressees can refuse. The form of the messenger is therefore the form of the test itself.

The verse does not pretend that this form is intuitive. It says that if the audience does not know this, they should ask those who have knowledge of the Scriptures. The reference is to the scholars of the Jews and Christians, who held the earlier revelations and who could confirm that the messengers sent to them were men. The verse therefore turns the audience toward an external verification. The objection that the messenger is human is not original to Mecca; the same objection was raised against the messengers of the Jews and Christians; those communities did not refuse the messengers on this ground; the objection is locally Meccan and historically minor.

**"Those Who Have Knowledge of the Scriptures"**

The verse appeals to the People of the Book as witnesses. This is a recurring Qur'anic strategy. The Qur'an does not ask the Meccans to take its word for what earlier prophets were like; it asks them to consult those who held the same texts. The appeal is courteous in form and devastating in implication. If the messenger's humanity were an objection that had invalidated earlier missions, the People of the Book would have a record of it. They do not. The historical record is consistent: messengers were men; messengers were accepted as men by those who believed; the objection is a later rationalisation by a community that has refused the message on other grounds.

The verse therefore performs a double move. It affirms the historical pattern and it locates verification in the hands of those who already possess the relevant evidence. The Meccan audience is invited to break out of the closed circle of its own objections and to consult the external record. The Qur'an does not guarantee the audience will do so; the verse, however, has refused to argue within the closed circle. The argument has been moved to a forum that has access to the relevant facts.

**The Verb "Nūḥī" — Inspiration Without Mediation**

The verse uses the verb *nūḥī ilayhim* — we inspire them, we cast upon them. The verb is unusual; the more common term for revelation is *awḥaynā* or *nazzalnā*. The form *nūḥī* suggests a throwing or casting, an act that bypasses the ordinary channels of communication. The messenger does not choose the message; the messenger receives it as one receives something thrown. The verb forecloses the objection that the messenger has composed the message in the form of poetry or dreams. The inspiration is not produced by the messenger; it is cast upon him. The act is unilateral.

This is also why the verse insists that the messengers were *mere men* — *rijāl*. The *mere* is significant. The messengers were not superhuman; they were not semi-divine; they were not exempt from the human condition. *Rijāl* here is the word for adult males of the human species. The qualification is exhaustive: the messengers were nothing more than this, and yet the inspiration reached them. The form is not a disqualification; the form is the receiver. A glass does not need to be a fountain to hold water; it only needs to be clean.

**The Qur'an's Defence of Its Own Messenger**

The verse is doing work that the audience had asked the Prophet to do for himself in 21:4 and 21:5. The audience had questioned his credentials; the Qur'an now supplies the credentials. The Prophet is not asked to argue his case; the case is argued for him. The argument is theological, historical, and grammatical. The Prophet is a man because messengers have always been men. The form is consistent with what the People of the Book would confirm. The inspiration is cast upon him from above; he did not generate it. Each of these is an answer to a separate objection raised in the preceding verses. The Prophet's defence is woven into the structure of the sūrah's polemic, not left to him to deliver.

The verse therefore also teaches a lesson about prophetic speech. A messenger's authority is not the authority of his own defence; it is the authority of the message that defends him. The audience has asked the Prophet to defend himself; the Qur'an refuses the format of the question. The defence is given by the One who sent him, and the defence is given in the form of the verse: short, declarative, and based on a record the audience already possesses.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:8

> We did not give those messengers ˹supernatural˺ bodies that did not need food, nor were they immortal

**Expanded Commentary**

**What the Messengers Were Not**

The previous verse established that the messengers were men; this verse establishes what kind of men. They did not have bodies that did not need food; they were not immortal. The verse is in the negative. It does not say what the messengers were like; it says what they were not like, and the negatives are revealing. The audiences of every earlier prophet had imagined that a messenger must be exempt from ordinary human needs. The Qur'an denies the exemption twice in one short verse.

The negation is paired. *Lā yaʾkulūna al-ṭaʿām* — they do not eat food — and *wa mā kānū khālidīn* — they are not immortal. To not eat is to be subject to the recurring necessity of replenishment; to not be immortal is to be subject to the terminal necessity of death. The pairing of the two negations places the messengers inside the human condition from two sides at once. A being that does not eat and a being that does not die are both beyond the test. The messengers were inside it.

**Why the Audience Wanted the Messengers to Be Otherwise**

The Meccan audience had wanted, in 21:5, a tangible sign like the earlier prophets. They had wanted, in 21:3, a non-human messenger whose credentials would be visible. The Qur'an now says that what the audience wants is precisely what the messengers were not. The negation is therefore not an admission; it is a refusal. The audience has asked for the impossible, and the impossibility is now identified as a refusal rather than a failure.

The refusal is grounded in the structure of the test. A messenger who did not need food could not share the hunger of those to whom he was sent; a messenger who did not die could not share the mortality of his audience. The shared condition is the ground of legibility. The messenger's message is intelligible because the messenger experiences what the audience experiences. To exempt the messenger from experience would be to exempt the message from the form in which it is to be received. The test would no longer be a test of willing obedience; it would be a demonstration of involuntary recognition.

**The Qur'an's Rejection of the Apotheosis of Messengers**

The verse protects the doctrine of messengership from two opposite distortions. The first distortion is the audience's distortion: the messenger is so fully human that his message is reduced to fabrication. The second distortion, which the verse also forecloses, is the opposite: the messenger is so transcendent that his humanity is dissolved. Both distortions have their long histories in religious controversy. The Qur'an refuses both. The messengers were men; the messengers were not exempt men. The messengership is a function, not a metaphysical transformation.

This verse is a guardrail for the rest of the sūrah's account of prophets. The sūrah will narrate Abraham, Moses, David, Solomon, Job, Jonah, Zachariah, Mary, and others. Each is treated as a human figure with a specific test, a specific vocation, and a specific outcome. The verse at 21:8 tells the reader in advance that none of these figures is to be deified. They ate food; they died; their significance is in what they were given to convey and in what they chose to do with the conveyance. The biographical material that follows is to be read as the history of obedient humans, not as the history of beings who had crossed out of the human condition.

**The Cost of Being a Messenger Without the Immaterial Body**

The verse also acknowledges, by negation, what messengers bore. To not eat would be to not be hungry; to be immortal would be to not be afraid. By saying the messengers were neither, the verse affirms that they were hungry and afraid. The narratives that follow include a man who plotted against his idols and was thrown into the fire; a man whose son was asked of him as a sacrifice; a man who lost his family and his health and cried out in his affliction; a man who fled his community in rage and was swallowed by the whale. The verse at 21:8 prepares the reader to take these narratives as the stories of men under pressure, not as the stories of heroes exempted from pressure. The messengers paid the price of their calling in the same currency as those to whom they were sent.

This is the source of their credibility. A messenger who had not been hungry could not have spoken with authority about hunger. A messenger who had not died could not have spoken about death. The verse is therefore not just a refusal of audience expectations; it is a description of the moral equipment without which the message could not be received.

**The Verse As a Rule for Reading the Rest of the Sūrah**

The reader who has read 21:8 will not be tempted to read the prophet narratives of al-Anbiyāʾ as mythic biographies of demigods. The prophets are men; the men ate; the men died. The verses that follow will narrate specific historical and human tests. The verse at 21:8 is the antecedent of every story that follows. It places the reader in the correct interpretive frame before the stories begin: these are not the stories of beings who escaped the human condition; these are the stories of beings who bore it with extraordinary fidelity and were given, in return, the mission that ordinary men could not bear.

This framing is part of the sūrah's polemic. The audience had asked for an inhuman messenger. The sūrah's answer, given across multiple verses, is that the inhuman messenger would have been useless. The audience is being given a sūrah full of men, and the men are the answer to their request. The audience will recognise, by the end of the sūrah, that the men were sufficient.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:9

> Then We fulfilled Our promise to them, saving them along with whoever We willed and destroying the transgressors

**Expanded Commentary**

**What the Messengers Were Given — And What Was Done With It**

The previous two verses described the messengers; this verse describes what happened to them. *Thumma ṣadafnā lahum* — then we fulfilled for them — is a single verb carrying enormous weight. *Ṣadafa* means to fulfil, to bring to completion, to make good on a promise that had been made. The promise was made before the test; the fulfilment comes after. The structure is the structure of prophetic history: a promise, a period of testing, a fulfilment that is neither automatic nor late.

The verse says that God saved the messengers and those God willed, and destroyed the transgressors. The couplet — saving and destroying — is the recurring shape of prophetic history in the Qur'an. The messengers are saved; their enemies are destroyed. The middle term — *man nashāʾu maʿahum* — whoever We willed — names the saved community without specifying its size. In Noah's case it is the few on the ark; in Moses' case it is the children of Israel; in every case it is the remnant that the divine will determines.

**The Distribution of Mercy and Judgment**

The verse names two acts together: saving and destroying. The pairing is essential. A Qur'anic theology that spoke only of mercy would lose the sharpness of the test; a Qur'anic theology that spoke only of destruction would lose the meaning of the calling. The messengers are saved because they bore the message; the transgressors are destroyed because they refused it. Between these two groups are those whose salvation is not automatic — *man nashāʾu* — those whom God wills to save. The middle term is not a euphemism; it is a recognition that salvation is not determined by membership in any visible category. It is determined by the divine will working through the conditions of the test.

The verse therefore refuses two simplifications. The first is that salvation is automatic for the messengers and their communities; the verse says *man nashāʾu*, whoever We willed, not whoever was with them. The second is that destruction is automatic for those who oppose; the verse says *al-mujrimīn*, the criminals, the doers of harm. The qualification matters. The destruction that came upon the communities was not punishment for refusal as such; it was punishment for the specific acts by which the refusal was enacted. The Qur'an elsewhere insists that *lā takūnu kal-ladhīna ẓalamū* — do not be like those who did wrong (2:254). The wrong is specified; the destruction is attached to the specified wrong.

**The Grammar of "Then" — Time, Sequence, and Fulfillment**

The verse begins with *thumma* — then. The particle marks sequence and elevation. The *thumma* of 21:9 is the *thumma* that, in classical Arabic rhetoric, signals both temporal succession and a kind of escalation. The fulfilment came; it came at the proper time; it came after a delay sufficient to constitute a real test. The delay is part of the fulfilment; it is not a postponement.

The Qur'an elsewhere contrasts *thumma* — then — with *wa* — and — to mark different kinds of succession. Prophetic history is presented as a series of *thumma* clauses, each marking a new stage of the divine promise. The pattern — promise, test, fulfilment — repeats throughout the sūrah and throughout the Qur'an. The repetition is not redundancy; it is the disclosure of a structure. The structure says: every mission will be tested; the test will be permitted to run; the conclusion will come; the conclusion will involve saving and destroying; the distribution of saving and destroying is determined by the divine will acting through the moral conditions of the test.

**A Verse That Refuses to be a Threat**

A reader might expect this verse to be a threat: we saved them and destroyed the others, and you could be next. The verse does not quite work that way. The audience of al-Anbiyāʾ is not addressed as if it were the next community in the sequence. The verse is descriptive, not hortatory. It says what happened to earlier messengers; it does not say what will happen to the present audience. The sūrah will make that argument elsewhere; 21:9 is doing a different job.

The job is to anchor the meaning of messengership. The messengers were men; the messengers were not immortal; and the messengers were saved in the end. The combination says: the mission is bearable; the mission is not permanent suffering; the mission is followed by deliverance. The verse's address to the present audience is therefore implicit but real. If the messengers were saved, then the present Prophet and his community have grounds to expect that they too will be saved, provided they bear what the messengers bore. The verse is a promise inside the polemic.

**"Saving Along with Whomever We Willed" — The Limits of Collective Salvation**

The verse contains an ambiguity the Qur'an elsewhere resolves. The saved are the messengers *and whoever We willed* — *man nashāʾu*. The phrase is general. In some accounts (Moses, Pharaoh's magicians who believed) the saved are specific; in others (Noah's ark) they are family; in others (Abraham's fire) they are individuals. The Qur'an does not give a uniform rule. Salvation is not guaranteed by belonging to a community; it is determined by the divine will acting in particular cases.

This is one of the most demanding features of Qur'anic soteriology. A theology of automatic collective salvation would exempt the individual from the test; a theology of pure individual judgment would dissolve the community. The verse refuses both extremes. Salvation is real and is given; salvation is selective and is not automatic. The community is the locus of the calling; the community is not the guarantor of the outcome. The outcome is in God's hand. The verse records the fact and refuses to derive from it a rule that would make the divine will predictable.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:10

> We have surely revealed to you a Book, in which there is glory for you. Will you not then understand

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Pivot from History to Address**

The previous verses narrated the history of earlier messengers; this verse pivots to the present. The Qur'an has been recording what happened to Noah, Abraham, Moses, and the rest; it now turns to the audience of Muhammad and says: we have revealed to you a Book. The pivot is deliberate. The history was not for its own sake; it was preparation for the present address. The same God who saved the messengers and destroyed the transgressors has now revealed a Book. The audience is asked to recognise the continuity.

The verse has a striking structure. It contains an affirmation, a description, and a question. The affirmation — *we have surely revealed* — is in the form that marks weight in the Qur'an, the *la-qad* construction that opens a sentence with a strong oath-like confirmation. The description — *in which there is glory for you* — names the content of the Book from the audience's perspective. The question — *will you not then understand?* — is the rhetorical close that turns the verse into an appeal.

**"Glory for You" — The Meaning of the Qur'an for Its Audience**

The Arabic *fīhi majjukum* — in it is your glory, your honour, your dignity — names the function of the Book from the inside of the community. The audience is told that the Book is not merely a warning or an indictment; it is the source of their honour. The word *maji* or *majj* is rare in the Qur'an; its appearance here attaches the entire vocabulary of dignity to the recited text. The audience's prestige, the verse says, is not in their ancestry, their wealth, or their numbers. It is in the Book.

This is one of the most counter-intuitive claims in the Qur'an. The audience of al-Anbiyāʾ was an aristocratic trading community that took its prestige from lineage and commercial reach. The verse relocates the source of prestige: not in the things the audience already had, but in the Book it is receiving. The relocation is not a flattery; it is a redefinition. The Book's function for the audience is to give them a basis for dignity that does not evaporate with their fortunes.

**The Question That Refuses to Argue**

The verse ends with *afalā taʿqilūn* — will you not then understand? The question is one of the Qur'an's recurring turns. It assumes that the material before the audience is sufficient; it asks only whether the audience will use the minds they have. The question is not addressed to those who lack information; it is addressed to those who have the information and have not yet acted on it. The verb *yaʿqil* in the Qur'an names the kind of understanding that issues in action, not the kind that is satisfied with classification.

The question is therefore not neutral. It implies that the audience has understood enough to act; it asks whether they will. The implied accusation is that they have been receiving the message long enough to have formed the appropriate response. The continuing refusal of the response is no longer a question of evidence; it is a question of will. The verse, by closing with this question, forecloses the audience's habitual claim that more information is required. The information has been given; the question is whether the audience will use what it has been given.

**"We Have Surely Revealed" — The Divine Voice in the First Person**

The verse returns to the divine first person plural — *we have revealed*. This is the first time in the sūrah since 21:5 that God has spoken in the first person about the Book itself. The earlier verses had the Prophet speaking (21:4) or had third-person narration (21:6–9). The shift back to the first person plural signals that the Book is being spoken of as the direct object of the divine act. The Book is not a human product; the Book is what God has revealed.

The first-person construction also performs a rhetorical function. The audience is being told that the source of the message has spoken about the message. The information about the Book is not mediated through the Prophet's claim; it is delivered as the Speaker's own description. The audience cannot dismiss the description as the Prophet's opinion; the description is delivered by the One whose self-description the entire Qur'an presupposes.

**A Verse That Returns to the Reader**

The verse is one of the most reflexive in the sūrah. It is about a Book; it is delivered as part of a Book; it asks the reader of the Book to understand the Book. The reflexivity is not a literary accident; it is a feature of the Qur'anic rhetoric. The Qur'an is a Book that asks its readers to understand it; the asking is itself a teaching; the teaching cannot be performed by a text that does not mention itself.

The community that reads the verse today is asked the same question the audience of Muhammad was asked: do you understand? The question is preserved in the text so that every generation of readers encounters the demand. The Book that contains the question is the same Book the question is about. The reader is being asked to confirm, by reading, that the question has been received. The receiving is itself the beginning of the answer.

**Glory As a Technical Term**

The word *majj* — glory, honour — is one of the Qur'an's terms for the kind of dignity that comes from being chosen by God for a particular role. The children of Israel were given *maǧd* at 4:54 — *and He gave them a kingdom and great wisdom*. The same root appears in the prayer of Solomon at 27:15 — *and He gave me a kingdom and a speech of clarity*. The vocabulary is consistent: *majd* is not biological or political; it is the gift of a position before God. The verse tells the audience of the Qur'an that the Book is the source of this kind of dignity. They are being made, by the Book, into a people with a role. The role is the content of the glory. The glory is not the possession of the people; the people are the instruments of the role.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:11

> ˹Imagine˺ how many societies of wrongdoers We have destroyed, raising up other people after them

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Question That Asks the Audience to Count**

The verse opens with an interjection — *wa kam qaṣamnā* — usually translated "and how many" but carrying in Arabic the sense of "how many have We shattered." The verb *qaṣaʿa* is violent. It means to shatter, to break into pieces, to demolish. The verse does not say that the communities were dismissed, or dismissed gently; it says they were shattered. The choice of verb is the choice of what happened to them. Communities built on wrongdoing do not leave the field quietly; they are broken open.

The verse's address to the audience is implicit but emphatic. *Imagine* — the translation's glossing particle — is doing the work of inviting the audience to do the imaginative labour of counting. The audience is asked to consider not one destroyed community but the number of them, and the question is asked with the conviction that the count will not be small. The verse is a generalised historical claim, but it is also a recruitment. The audience is asked to join the long list of communities that heard and did not listen.

**The Raising of Another People After Them**

The second half of the verse names the consequence: after the destruction, another people were brought into being. The Arabic verb *anʿashnā* — to raise, to bring forth, to cause to grow — is the same root used for the generation of new vegetation or new life. The community that replaces the shattered one is not a fixed thing; it is grown. The replacement is part of the divine pedagogy. A community that has exhausted its capacity to receive the message is broken open, and the space is filled by another community that has its own opportunity to hear.

This dynamic — destruction, then replacement — is one of the most sobering patterns in the Qur'an. It is not a single incident; it is a recurring cycle. The verse therefore refuses the audience's hope that they are the exception. The history of previous communities is not a record of unique events; it is a record of a pattern. The pattern will repeat as long as the conditions that produced the first destructions continue to produce their consequences. The audience's task, the verse implies, is to break out of the pattern, not to assume that the pattern will spare them.

**The Commentators' Identification of a Specific People**

The classical commentators, including al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī, mentioned a specific people they connected with the verse: the people of Ḥadūr in Yemen, to whom a prophet was sent and who rejected him. The identification is not certain; the verse itself does not name the community. The general statement is firmer than the particular identification, and the Qur'an elsewhere gives examples without tying them to a single place. The function of the specific identification in the commentary is to remind the reader that the abstract claim of the verse has a concrete historical referent; the destruction is not a theoretical event.

The verse's deeper claim is that destruction is not the end of the story. After the destruction comes the raising of another people. The replacement is a divine act; the people are not raised by themselves. The verse therefore refuses both the despair that comes from seeing wrongdoing flourish and the complacency that comes from imagining that wrongdoing is permanent. The shattering is real, and the replacement is also real. The one is the consequence of moral failure; the other is the consequence of divine patience.

**A Verse That the Reading Community Must Inhabit**

The verse is also a hermeneutical instruction. By asking the audience to imagine the number of destroyed communities, the verse trains them to read their own moment as one entry in a longer list. The audience is not to imagine themselves at the beginning of history, with all the options still open; they are to imagine themselves in the middle of a long sequence, with the past as a witness to what comes of the choices they will make. The imagining is not idle. It is an exercise in moral location. The audience is being asked to find themselves in the sequence.

The verse is one of many in the Qur'an that read the present through the past. *How many a town have We destroyed* — the phrasing recurs at 6:6, 7:4, 17:17, 19:98, 28:58, 38:3, 50:36. The repetition is not redundancy; it is the consistent application of a method. The Qur'an reads history as a single body of evidence, and each verse that mentions the destruction of past communities is a reminder that the evidence is cumulative. The audience is asked to weigh the present against the cumulative evidence. The weighing has a predictable outcome: the present is reminded that it is not the first generation to face the test, and that previous generations have, in the main, failed it.

**The Spiritual Reading That Does Not Excuse the Historical Reading**

The Sufi commentator al-ʿAjlūnī offered a reading in which the destroyed communities are the wicked hearts of individuals, and the raised-up communities are the wholesome hearts that come after spiritual transformation. The reading is not a replacement of the historical reading; it is an extension. The verse can be read as a general law that operates at the level of the soul as well as the level of the city. A heart that hardens into wrongdoing is shattered by trials and deprivations; in its place, after the shattering, a new heart can be raised.

This extension is consistent with the Qur'an's recurring habit of applying historical patterns to the interior life. The communities that refused the messengers are also the habits of the heart that refuse the reminder. The destruction is also the breaking of those habits. The raising of another people is also the formation of a new interior orientation. The verse therefore speaks to individuals as well as to communities, and it does not consider the two readings to be in competition. The pattern operates at both scales; the audience is being addressed at both scales.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:12

> When the wrongdoers sensed ˹the arrival of˺ Our torment, they started to run away from their cities

**Expanded Commentary**

**Sensing Before Suffering**

The Arabic verb *lammā* — when — marks a moment of perception rather than a moment of impact. The wrongdoers do not yet taste the torment; they sense it. The verb *ḥassa* — to feel, to sense, to perceive with the senses — is more delicate than *ḍaraba* or *aṣāba*, which would name the actual striking. The verse therefore distinguishes between the apprehension of the punishment and the punishment itself. The wrongdoers perceive what is coming before it arrives.

This perception is one of the most morally charged moments in the Qur'anic narrative. The wrongdoers, having refused to hear the warning, are now granted a perception that does not depend on their willingness to listen. The senses do not require the heart's consent. The wrongdoers see the destruction coming, and the seeing is given to them by the same God who gave them the warnings they refused. The warnings came as a courtesy; the perception comes as a fact. The verse marks the transition between the two economies. Until now, the audience had been addressed; now the audience perceives.

**The Run That Cannot Save**

The verb *yusrifūna* — they run, they flee — names a specific kind of motion. It is the panicked, undisciplined flight of those who know that the place they are in is about to become unlivable. The wrongdoers do not run toward a destination; they run away from a presence. The verb is the verb of a community whose geography has suddenly become hostile. The cities that were their homes, their markets, their places of wealth, are now the location of the imminent destruction. They flee from what they had built.

The futility of the flight is the verse's silent polemic. The wrongdoers who fled were the ones who had built the cities; the cities had been their instrument of pride. Now the pride is the very thing they must abandon. The verse does not say that the flight failed; it says that the flight happened. The failure is implicit. They ran from the cities; the cities were about to be shattered. There is no outside the punishment, because the punishment is not a local event; it is the closure of the space in which their wrongdoing had been possible.

**A Reading That Combines External and Internal Peril**

The classical commentators, including al-Ṭabarī, read *ḥassaū ba'sanā* as "when they sensed Our might" — that is, when they perceived the imminent destruction *before* it arrived. The reading is important. The verse is not saying that the wrongdoers were hit and then ran; it is saying that they perceived the coming blow and tried to escape it. The wilful had finally been replaced by the involuntary. They had refused the warnings; now they sense the consequence.

This reading allows the verse to operate on two levels at once. On the historical level, the wrongdoers perceive the approach of an invading army or a divine chastisement; they flee. On the interior level, the person who has done wrong senses the approach of the consequences of that wrong — the remorse, the recompense, the breakdown of the life that the wrong had made possible — and tries to run from it. The flight is the same in both cases. The escape is not available in either case. The verse, like many Qur'anic verses, is read both as a record of past events and as a description of the moral structure of wrongdoing itself. The reading is not a choice between two interpretations; it is a recognition that the verse speaks at both levels.

**"From Their Cities" — The Loss of Place**

The verse specifies *min diyārihim* — from their cities, their settled places. The preposition *min* is significant. They flee *from* a place, not *to* a place. The destination of the flight is unnamed. The wrongdoers, having built their cities as the secure base of their wealth, are now leaving them without a destination. The cities had been the visible signs of their prosperity; the cities are now the visible signs of their impending destruction. The places that had defined them are now the things from which they must flee.

This is one of the recurring features of the Qur'an's eschatological language. The promise of a settled life in a built environment is real; the threat of the loss of that life is also real; the two are not contradictory. The same verse that speaks of building cities and enjoying their fruits (28:57–58) speaks of being driven out of them. The cities are not the permanent possession of anyone; they are entrusted, and the trust can be revoked. The verse at 21:12 records the moment of revocation for the wrongdoers. The audience of the Qur'an is invited to recognise that the same revocation is always one bad decision away.

**The Verse That Prepares for the Mockery of 21:13**

The next verse will mock the wrongdoers by inviting them to return to their luxuries. The present verse prepares for that mockery by establishing that the wrongdoers are now in flight. The contrast is the contrast of someone running for his life being invited, with a smile, to go home. The verse at 21:12 makes the contrast possible by showing the audience the flight; without the flight, the mockery at 21:13 would be cruel without being ironic. With the flight, the mockery becomes a poetic image of the futility of the escape. The two verses together perform the Qur'an's habitual move: it shows the seriousness of the situation by one rhetorical figure, and it deepens that seriousness by an ironic counter-figure. The audience is asked to feel both the gravity and the irony; the gravity is the more important, but the irony sharpens it.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:13

> ˹They were told,˺ “Do not run away! Return to your luxuries and your homes, so you may be questioned ˹about your fate˺.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Voice That Speaks from Inside the Punishment**

The previous verse described the flight of the wrongdoers; this verse records what was said to them as they fled. The voice is unnamed. The commentators record that it could be the angels, or it could be God.
