The grammatical alternative matters less than the rhetorical effect: the voice comes from the inside of the destruction, addressing those who are trying to flee it. The voice does not say "you cannot escape"; it says "return to what you had."

The command is a command to undo the flight. The wrongdoers had run; they are now asked to reverse course. The reversal is impossible physically, and the impossibility is the point. The wrongdoers are being invited to do what they cannot do, and the impossibility is the form their punishment takes. The command to return is therefore not a literal command. It is a poetic figure by which the punishment is depicted as a closed space. There is no exit. The flight failed before it began.

**"So You May Be Questioned" — The Closed Circuit of Account**

The phrase *laʾallakum tusʾalūn* — so that you may be questioned — is one of the Qur'an's most carefully chosen expressions. The verb *suʾāl* can mean to be asked, to be questioned, or to be held accountable. The form *tusʾalūn* is in the passive; the questioner is not named. The audience is asked to consider who would be doing the questioning. The implied agent is God; the implied forum is the reckoning that the opening verse of the sūrah announced. The flight was an attempt to escape that reckoning; the command to return is an invitation to enter it.

The phrase *laʾallakum* — perhaps, so that — adds an ironic distance. The Qur'an often uses this particle to mark an outcome that has been determined but is being presented in softened form. The wrongdoers will be questioned; the question is whether they will be questioned now or later. The flight delays nothing. The questioning is already in motion.

**"Luxury" and "Dwellings" — The Two Forms of Attachment**

The verse names two objects of attachment: *matārikum* — the luxuries, the comforts, the things enjoyed — and *masākinikum* — the dwellings, the homes, the places of residence. The pairing is exhaustive. A community's attachment to its life is typically to its pleasures and to its homes. The verse names both. The luxuries are the consumable pleasures; the dwellings are the stable containers of those pleasures. To lose both is to lose the entire structure of a worldly life.

The command to return to these is therefore not a command to recover. It is a command to face the empty form of the life they had built. The luxurious things and the dwellings are still there; what is gone is the community's capacity to inhabit them without the gaze of the questioning that has now been promised. To return to the luxuries is to return to a life whose meaning has been revealed as preliminary. The luxuries will not be destroyed; they will simply no longer shelter what they had sheltered. The verse names this transformation in one short command.

**The Commentators' Identification of Mockery**

Al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī record that the command is understood to be a form of mockery or taunt. The wrongdoers are being invited, with a smile, to do what they cannot do, in order that they may know the futility of their flight. The reading is consistent with the rhetorical shape of the verse. A literal command to return would be pointless if the wrongdoers could simply obey; the command only makes sense if the obedience is impossible, in which case the command is a figure for the impossibility of escape. The mockery is therefore not cruelty for its own sake; it is a way of communicating a moral truth that direct statement could not communicate. The truth is that there is no escape from the reckoning.

A second reading, also preserved in the commentaries, takes the command more literally: go back to your lives, so that the reckoning may be deferred. The reading is recorded, but the first reading is more consistent with the verse's tone. The Qur'an rarely offers the wrongdoer a deferment; the deferment would be a participation in the same delay that had already misled them. The voice that says "return" is therefore best understood as a voice that is showing the wrongdoer the futility of flight, not offering them a route back to comfort.

**"That You May Be Questioned" — A Word for the Living Reader**

The closing phrase *laʾallakum tusʾalūn* does its quietest work in the verse's afterlife. The audience of the Qur'an who reads this verse is not in flight from an invading army; the audience is in the middle of an ordinary life. The verse nevertheless speaks. The luxuries and the dwellings of the contemporary reader are not the same objects as those of the wrongdoers, but the attachment is the same. The verse's question is whether the contemporary reader's luxuries and dwellings are a place to live or a place to be questioned from. The *tusʾalūn* is not addressed only to those who are running. It is addressed to anyone who has set up a life and has not yet faced the question that the life is asking.

The Qur'anic word *suʾāl* does not name a casual interrogation. It names the formal, accountable, summoning kind of question that a ruler addresses to a subject. The verse therefore places the wrongdoer — and, by implication, the reader — in the position of someone who has been summoned by an authority whose authority is total. The luxuries and dwellings are not places of residence in this economy; they are places to be questioned from. The verse names the place for what it is.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:14

> They cried, “Woe to us! We have surely been wrongdoers.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Confession That Arrives Too Late**

The wrongdoers, having been commanded to return, now cry out in confession. *Yā waylanā* — woe to us, alas for us — is one of the Qur'an's standard cries of distress. It is the cry of those who have recognised, finally, the situation they are in. The verse records the cry as the response of the wrongdoers to the command of 21:13; the cry is also, by extension, the response of any human community that has been told, finally, that the reckoning it had refused to consider has arrived.

The interesting feature of this verse is its placement. The wrongdoers confess; the previous verse had told them to return so that they may be questioned. The confession and the questioning are not the same act. The confession is the response of the wrongdoers to the situation; the questioning is the formal accounting that will follow. The Qur'an records both, and the sequence tells the reader something: the confession does not stop the questioning. The wrongdoers have now admitted their wrongdoing; they will still be questioned. The admission is not the end of the matter; it is the beginning of the formal part.

**The Verb That Names the Wrong**

The wrongdoers say *innā kunnā ẓālimīn* — we have been wrongdoers. The verb *ẓalama* in the Qur'an is specifically the verb of putting something in the wrong place, of usurping, of transgression against the right of another. The word is not *khatiʾa* (mistake), not *dhanb* (sin in the sense of omission), but *ẓulm* — the active, deliberate displacement of what was rightly placed. The wrongdoers confess to having actively displaced the order of things; the confession is therefore an acknowledgment of agency, not of mere failure. They did not simply fall short; they rearranged the world to their own advantage.

The confession is also an acknowledgment of a specific moral economy. The verb *ẓalama* implies a counterpart whose right has been violated. The wrongdoers, in confessing, are implicitly acknowledging the counterpart — the wronged parties, the violated order, the God whose right has been usurped. The confession is therefore not a private moment of remorse; it is a public acknowledgment that the wrongdoers' actions had a victim. The Qur'an refuses the privatised remorse that modernity often proposes. The wrongdoer's confession is a confession in front of someone whose right was violated, even if the one confessing has not yet named the one wronged.

**Why the Confession Comes Too Late**

The verse is read by al-Qurṭubī as the confession made when the angels began to carry out the punishment. The timing is the entire point. The wrongdoers had refused to confess when confession would have changed the outcome; they confess when the outcome is no longer in question. The Qur'an elsewhere calls this *istighātha baʿd al-amr* — calling for help after the matter has been decided (40:66). The confession is real; the time for it has passed. The verse therefore does not promise that confession is enough; it records that confession, even at the last moment, still does what confession does. It acknowledges the truth of what happened.

The audience of the Qur'an reads this verse in time, before the punishment, before the reckoning. The verse is therefore doing a different kind of work for them than it did for the wrongdoers. For the wrongdoers, the verse records the confession they could not withhold; for the audience, the verse records the confession they should make earlier. The pedagogical structure is this: the audience is shown the late confession so that they may understand what an earlier confession would have looked like, and so that they may recognise the difference between a confession that comes too late and a confession that comes in time. The late confession does not undo the wrong; the earlier confession might have.

**The Word "Woe" — An Interjection with Weight**

The Arabic *wayl* is one of the Qur'an's heaviest words of distress. It is sometimes translated "woe," sometimes "alas," and sometimes left untranslated because no English word carries the same finality. The form *waylanā* — woe to us — extends the interjection from a single object to the speakers themselves. The wrongdoers are saying "woe to us." They are the objects of the woe they are naming; they are the targets of the distress they are expressing. The interjection collapses the distance between the speaker and the topic of the speech. The wrongdoers are not commenting on their situation; they are entering it.

The verb *qālū* — they said — is in the simple past. The cry has already happened; the verse is recording it as a fact. The audience of the Qur'an is asked to take the cry as already having been uttered in the story; the story is already past tense from the perspective of the recitation. The audience's task is not to await the cry but to recognise that the cry is the inevitable response of those who, having been given the moment to confess, failed to confess in time and now confess only when the confession has been overtaken by the events it could have addressed. The cry is therefore both inevitable and insufficient.

**The Verse's Call to a Different Kind of Confession**

The audience of the Qur'an reads 21:14 as a record of a confession that did not save. The verse is therefore a call to a different kind of confession — one made before the punishment, before the angels begin to carry out the sentence, before the time for confession has run out. The Qur'an makes this kind of confession available. The audience is invited to make it. The making is the alternative to the cry of 21:14.

The verse is therefore not a description of a past event; it is a provocation. It says to the audience: the wrongdoers eventually confessed; what will you confess, and when? The verse expects the audience to recognise that they have not yet confessed as they should; it expects the recognition to lead to a confession that is in time. The cry of 21:14 is therefore both a description and a warning. It describes what the wrongdoers did; it warns the audience that the same cry is available to them, and that the time for it is now.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:15

> They kept repeating their cry until We mowed them down, ˹leaving them˺ lifeless

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Cry That Becomes the Whole of Their Speech**

The previous verse recorded the cry; this verse records that the cry did not stop. *Mā zālū yaṭṭūḥūna* — they did not cease to cry out, to call out, to supplicate — uses the verb *ṭawḥ*, which names a sustained, repeated cry. The wrongdoers did not say the words once and then say something else; they kept repeating the same cry. The verse is therefore not about a single utterance; it is about a single utterance repeated until it became the entire content of their speech.

The repetition is part of the verse's polemic. A cry that is repeated is a cry that has been overtaken by the situation. The wrongdoers are not saying something new each time they open their mouths; they are saying the same thing because the situation has not changed and they have nothing else to say. The cry becomes a kind of speech that has been emptied of its performative force. They are not confessing any longer; they are simply continuing to make the noise of confession because the alternative is silence.

**"Until We Mowed Them Down" — The Verb of Cutting**

The verb *ḥasarnāhum* — we mowed them down — uses the root that names the reaping of a ripe crop. The verb carries in Arabic the same image as the English: the wrongdoers are cut down like ripe wheat, the way a field is harvested. The image is agricultural, but it is not pastoral. The field is not a place of rest; it is a place of cutting. The harvest is not a celebration; it is the termination of growth. The wrongdoers had grown into something ripe for cutting, and the cutting is now done.

The verb is in the first person plural. *We mowed them down.* The voice returns to the divine first person that has been the source of the punishment throughout the sūrah. The wrongdoers had rejected the messengers; the messengers had been saved; the wrongdoers had been promised a reckoning; the reckoning has now arrived. The cutting is not an external event; it is the divine response to the entire sequence of choices. The verse does not describe the punishment as something that happens to the wrongdoers; it describes it as something done to them. The agency is consistent with the warnings of the earlier verses.

**"Leaving Them Lifeless" — Khāmidīn and the Word for Stilled**

The Arabic *khāmidīn* is a rare and powerful word. It names a kind of stillness that is more than motionlessness. The same root is used for an extinguished flame, for a settled dust, for the silence of a thing that has finished its active motion. Al-Qurṭubī notes that the verb *khamada* can also mean simply to die. The verse therefore does not say that the wrongdoers were slain in the violence of an attack; it says that they were left in a stillness that is itself a form of death. The stillness is the final state of those who have been mowed down; they are not moving any more.

The translation's "lifeless" captures this. The wrongdoers are not described as bodies; they are described as the absence of life. The verse does not dwell on the bodies or the scene of their destruction; it moves quickly to the stillness. The stillness is the verdict. The wrongdoers had been full of words; the words have now stopped. The stillness is the equivalent in the moral order of the cutting down in the physical order.

**The Closing Question — "Divine Amusement?"**

The verse ends with one of the Qur'an's most piercing rhetorical questions: *afa-lāhin* — is it divine amusement? The question is in the negative form; the expected answer is no. The verse is asking the audience to confirm that what has just been described — the destruction of a community whose only remaining act was a sustained cry — is not how God treats His creatures for amusement. The question is therefore not a question about the event; it is a question about the meaning of the event. The event happened; the question is whether the audience will read it as evidence of divine play.

The question *afa-lāhin* is sharp because the previous verses of the sūrah have already addressed the audience's accusation of play. The audience had said in 21:2 that they listened to the message *laʿiban* — in play. The Qur'an will return to the theme in 21:16: *We did not create the heavens and the earth in play* (21:16). The closing question of 21:15 therefore connects the audience's earlier accusation with the present destruction. The destruction was not play; the audience's accusation that the message was play is being returned to them. The verse names the connection without spelling it out. The audience is left to do the work of recognising that the accusation they had made against the message is precisely the accusation they cannot make against God.

**A Verse That Reframes the Audience's Accusation**

The audience had accused the Prophet of producing confused dreams, fabricated speech, and poetry (21:5). The Qur'an now reframes that accusation. The Prophet is not the one whose work is in question; the audience's own framework is in question. The audience had assumed that the message was a kind of entertainment; the verse says that entertainment is precisely what God does not produce. The destruction of the wrongdoers is not entertainment; the creation of the heavens and the earth (which the next verse will name) is not entertainment. The only entertainment in the sequence is the entertainment the audience had imagined the message to be. The audience is being asked to recognise the inversion: they had accused the message of being play; the verdict at 21:15 is that play is the wrong word for what has happened.

This reframing is one of the most powerful rhetorical moves in the Qur'an. The verse does not argue with the audience about whether the message is play; it simply describes an event and asks the audience to apply the word *laʿib* to that event. The application is impossible. The audience is forced to recognise that the word does not fit. In recognising that, they are also forced to recognise that the word never fit the message either. The accusation collapses; the audience is left holding a category that no longer applies to anything in view.

**The Verse's Function for the Reader Who Has Not Yet Been Mowed Down**

The verse is read today by people who are not in the process of being cut down. The verse therefore does its work not as a prediction but as a re-framing. The reader is asked to consider whether the categories they have used to dismiss the message — play, sport, amusement — survive a reading of 21:15. If they do not survive, the reader is asked to consider what other categories they have been using, and whether those categories have any firmer foundation.

The verse is also a warning about the cost of the cry. The wrongdoers cried until they were cut down. The cry was real, but the cry was too late. The verse is therefore a call to make a different kind of cry — one that comes before the cutting, in the time when cries can still be heard. The closing question — *afa-lāhin* — invites the reader to enter the time when cries are still heard, and to cry then, in the way that is appropriate to the situation. The verse's invitation is not casual; it is the closing of a long argument that has been running since 21:1.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:16

> We did not create the heavens and the earth and everything in between for sport

**Expanded Commentary**

**Not in Sport — The Reframing of Creation**

The verse answers, with the divine voice, the question that closed the previous verse. *Mā khalaqnā al-samāwāti wa al-arḍa wa mā baynahumā lāʿibīn* — We did not create the heavens and the earth and what is between them as ones who play. The verb *khalaqa* is the Qur'an's standard verb for creation; the negative *lāʿibīn* — as players, in sport — is the same word that has appeared in 21:2 and 21:15. The repetition is the point. The audience had accused the message of being play; the Qur'an now says that creation is not play.

The verse therefore refuses two uses of the word. It refuses to apply *laʿib* to the divine act, and it refuses to allow the audience's previous use of the word to remain uncontested. The word has now been defined in the strongest possible way: it is the wrong word for what God does. The audience's earlier accusation was therefore not a description; it was a category mistake. The verse does not say the audience is mistaken about a fact; it says they have applied a word that does not fit.

**The List That the Verse Names**

The verse names three things: the heavens, the earth, and what is between them. The tripartite formulation is one of the Qur'an's most common descriptions of the totality of created reality. *Al-samāwāt* — the heavens — names what is above; *al-arḍ* — the earth — names what is below; *mā baynahumā* — what is between them — names the in-between, the spatial and temporal order in which human beings live. The three together exhaust the field. The verse is saying that there is nothing in existence that is the product of divine play. Whatever exists is not the work of a divine sport.

This is a foundational claim. The verse is not a piece of polemic; it is a piece of ontology. It states what kind of world the audience inhabits. The world is not a divine joke; the world is a creation. The difference between these two views of the world is total. A world that is a divine joke is a world in which seriousness is finally impossible; a world that is a creation is a world in which seriousness is the appropriate response to what is there.

**The Polemical Edge That Does Not Need to be Spoken**

The verse's polemical edge is silent but precise. The audience had been treating the message as play (21:2). The audience is now told that creation is not play. The implication is that if the audience can recognise that creation is not play, they must also recognise that the message — which is itself part of what has been revealed within creation — is not play. The audience's framework cannot coherently treat creation as serious and the message as sport. The verse forces the audience to choose. The choice is not between the message and creation; the choice is between a coherent worldview and an incoherent one.

This is the work that the verse does without saying it explicitly. The Qur'an rarely needs to spell out the implication of its claims; the claims are usually tight enough that the implications follow. The audience is invited to do the inference. The inference is not difficult. The audience that refuses to do it is the audience that is already hardened, and the verse has not been written for hardened audiences to comfort them.

**A Verse That Contrasts Two Frames**

The verse contrasts the divine frame with the human frame. The divine frame is the creation of the heavens and the earth; the human frame is the audience's assumption that what is happening is sport. The contrast is total. The divine frame is large, structured, ordered, and serious; the human frame is small, distracted, and playful. The verse asks the audience to consider which frame is more adequate to the world they inhabit. The answer is implicit. The audience that lives in the heavens and the earth has no business treating those heavens and that earth as the stage of a divine joke.

The verse is also a critique of a particular kind of piety. There is a form of piety that imagines that God is mysterious in a way that exempts Him from the moral order. The verse refuses this form. God is not mysterious in the sense of arbitrary; God is mysterious in the sense that His purposes are not exhausted by human categories. The refusal of *laʿib* is part of that refusal. To say that God creates in play is to say that God's purposes are arbitrary. The verse says that God's purposes are not arbitrary. The world has been created with a seriousness that the audience cannot match.

**Connection to the Larger Argument of the Sūrah**

The verse is the centre of a passage that runs from 21:1 to 21:30. The passage is the sūrah's polemic against the Meccan audience's refusal of the message. The polemic moves from accusation (21:2–5) to historical reminder (21:6–9) to direct address (21:10) to narrative of destruction (21:11–15). The verse at 21:16 is the pivot. After the verse, the argument turns to the metaphysical foundations of the message — creation, divine purpose, the nature of God. The shift is deliberate. The historical polemic has reached its climax in the destruction of the wrongdoers; the next step is to ground the message in the structure of creation itself.

The verse is therefore not just one polemical move among many; it is the move that allows the rest of the polemic to happen. The audience is told that the world is not play. From this foundation, the audience can be told about the purpose of creation, about the responsibility of human beings, about the impossibility of polytheism, and about the necessity of worship. Each of these arguments depends on the foundation that the verse at 21:16 has laid. The verse is the floor on which the rest of the argument walks.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:17

> Had We intended to take ˹some˺ amusement, We could have found it in Our presence, if that had been Our Will

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Conditional That Denies Its Own Condition**

The verse is a conditional statement whose condition is denied. *Law aradnā an nattakhidha lahwān* — if We had wanted to take amusement — is the structure of a hypothesis that is then refused. The Qur'an uses this structure throughout to make claims about divine action by specifying what would not have been done. Here, the claim is that God does not take amusement. The denial of the condition is the teaching of the verse.

The verb *ittakhadha* — to take, to acquire, to adopt — is the same verb used elsewhere for taking a consort, taking a child, taking a partner. The word *lahw* in the verse means diversion, play, amusement, the kind of activity one undertakes to escape more serious things. The Qur'an's choice of *ittakhadha lahwān* — to take amusement as one would take a companion — emphasises that amusement is treated here as a thing one might have done. The verse says: this is not something we have done.

**"In Our Presence" — The Location of the Hypothetical Amusement**

The second half of the verse says *la-ijtadaynāhu min ladunnā* — we would have found it from what is with Us, in Our presence. The hypothetical amusement, had it been sought, would not have been sought in creation. It would have been sought in something closer to God. The commentators have read this in several ways. Al-Ṭabarī reads it as saying that if God had wanted to play, He would not have created the world at all; the world is not the place of divine play. Other commentators read it as a reference to the kinds of companions and consorts that the polytheists imagined God had — the consort, the child, the partner.

Both readings are consistent with the verse's larger polemic. The polytheists of Mecca had accused God of having offspring and of needing companions; the verse says that if God had wanted such a thing, He would have sought it from His own presence, not from creation. The implication is that God has no need of companions, consorts, or amusement. The world was not created to provide what God lacks.

**The Verb "Law" — A Conditional of Refusal**

The Arabic *law* — if — is the conditional that opens a hypothesis. The hypothesis here is *if We had wanted* — *law aradnā*. The structure continues *la-ittakhadhnāhu* — we would have taken it. The use of the *law* construction is a recognised Qur'anic device for denying an implication without having to state the denial explicitly. The denial is performed by the failure of the condition: since the condition has not occurred, the consequent has not occurred either. The audience is invited to infer that the consequent has not occurred; the inference is the verse's work.

This is one of the most economical rhetorical structures in the Qur'an. A whole claim is made in one conditional: God does not take amusement. The conditional is necessary because the verse is simultaneously denying the claim and refusing to engage with the framework that produced the claim. The audience had assumed that God might take amusement; the verse does not argue against the assumption. The verse shows what would have been the case if the assumption had been right, and shows that the assumption is not. The audience is left with the assumption dismantled but unaddressed.

**A Verse That Addresses the Charge of Divine Need**

The audience had, in various ways, accused God of having needs that the created world supplies. The verse is the systematic refusal of that accusation. God does not need amusement; God does not need consorts; God does not need children; God does not need partners. The world is not the place where God meets His needs; the world is the place where creatures meet the test of how they will respond to a God who has no needs.

This is one of the most theologically demanding claims in the Qur'an. A God who needs is a God who can be manipulated, bargained with, and induced. A God who has no needs is a God whose actions cannot be predicted from the perspective of what He might want from His creatures. The verse is therefore not just a denial of divine play; it is a denial of the framework that makes play, companionship, and acquisition coherent categories when applied to God. The verse, by denying that God takes amusement, denies the whole anthropology of the divine that the polytheists had imported into their theology.

**"If That Had Been Our Will" — The Closing Caveat**

The verse ends with *in kunnā fāʿilīn* — if We were to do so. The phrase is the closing caution. It says: if We had willed to do so, we would have done so; but We have not willed. The caveat is not an opening for future divine play; it is a refusal of any suggestion that God might yet take amusement in some unforeseen circumstance. The past perfect of the conditional has already settled the matter; the closing phrase reinforces the closure. The verse is saying: this is not something We have done, this is not something We will do, and this is not the kind of being We are.

The audience is therefore being asked to recognise that their framework — God as a being who might have needs that creation supplies — is incoherent. The verse does not argue for a different framework; it asks the audience to consider the consequences of the framework they have. The framework produces the charge of divine play. The verse refuses the charge. The audience is invited to consider what kind of God would not be subject to the charge. The answer is the God the Qur'an has been describing — the God who created in seriousness, who has no need of amusement, who governs the world for purposes the creature can only partially trace.

**The Verse's Place in the Anti-Polytheistic Argument**

The verse is part of a passage that will culminate in the radical monotheistic claims of 21:21–23. The progression is deliberate: creation is not play (21:16); if it had been play, it would not have required the world (21:17); the truth is that truth is hurled against falsehood and falsehood is crushed (21:18); the beings in the heavens and the earth belong to God (21:19); the beings near God do not tire of worshipping Him (21:20); the gods imagined on earth cannot raise the dead (21:21); if there were other gods, the heavens and earth would be corrupted (21:22); God cannot be questioned, but all others will be questioned (21:23). The verse at 21:17 is therefore one move in a longer polemic that is dismantling the polytheistic framework piece by piece.

The dismantling is not polemical in the argumentative sense; it is structural. Each verse removes one support of the polytheistic framework. The verse at 21:17 removes the support that depends on divine need. Without divine need, there is no reason to imagine that God took a consort, begot a child, or set up partners. The polytheistic worldview loses its foundation; the rest of the passage clears away the remaining structures. The verse is therefore not just a denial; it is a demolition that allows the construction of the next argument.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:18

> In fact, We hurl the truth against falsehood, leaving it crushed, and it quickly vanishes. And woe be to you for what you claim

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Image of Truth Against Falsehood**

The verse offers one of the Qur'an's most compact images. *Naqdhifu al-ḥaqqa ʿalā al-bāṭili* — We hurl the truth against falsehood. The verb *naqdhifu* names a forceful, casting motion. The truth is not presented; it is cast. The casting is against falsehood, and the result is the crushing of falsehood. The Arabic *yadmaghuhu* — to crush, to smash — is unusually violent. The same root appears in the word for the brain (*dimāgh*); the verb conveys the image of breaking a skull open to reach the brain. The verse is saying: when the truth is cast at falsehood, the falsehood is not gently displaced; it is destroyed at its core.

This is the Qur'an's fundamental ontology of the relationship between truth and falsehood. They are not co-equal forces that balance each other; they are not complementary perspectives that can both be true. The truth is the active agent; the falsehood is the recipient of the casting. The result is not a debate in which two positions are weighed; it is a combat in which one is victorious and the other is destroyed. The image refuses the relativistic picture of competing claims; the picture it offers is of a moral order in which truth is, by its nature, victorious.

**"And It Quickly Vanishes"**

The verse continues *fa-idhā huwa zāhiqun* — and behold, it vanishes. The verb *zahiqa* in classical Arabic names the passing away of something that cannot remain. The vanishing is not a slow decay; it is the disappearance of something that has no capacity to persist in the face of what has been cast at it. The image completes the work of the verse. The truth has been cast; the falsehood has been crushed; the falsehood now vanishes. The three moments — casting, crushing, vanishing — are the rhythm of the moral order.

The vanishing has a temporal qualifier. *Idhā* — and behold, then — is the particle that signals an immediate consequence. The vanishing is not delayed; it is the immediate result of the crushing. The audience is invited to imagine the sequence as taking place in a single motion. The truth is cast; the falsehood is crushed; the falsehood is gone. The audience cannot intervene in the sequence; the sequence is complete by the time the audience is asked to witness it.

**The Closing Woe — "Woe Be to You for What You Claim"**

The verse ends with *wa waylun lakum mimmā taṣifūn* — and woe to you for what you claim. The verb *waṣafa* — to describe, to claim, to ascribe — is the same verb that appears in the audience's accusations of witchcraft and fabrication in 21:3 and 21:5. The closing line therefore returns to the audience. The audience had accused the Prophet of producing confused dreams, fabricated speech, and poetry. The verse is now saying: the truth has been cast at the falsehood, and the falsehood has vanished; woe to you for the descriptions you have been making.

The link is precise. The audience's descriptions are among the falsehoods that the truth has been casting itself against. The descriptions were the verbal form of the audience's refusal. The truth has now been cast against those very descriptions. The descriptions are part of what will vanish. The audience is therefore being warned that the descriptions they have been making are not merely mistaken; they are part of the falsehood that is being destroyed. The warning is direct: woe to you, because what you have been describing is on the side of what is being crushed.

**The Verse's Relation to Other Passages**

The relationship between truth and falsehood is a recurring theme in the Qur'an. 17:81 says: *Truth has come, and falsehood has vanished.* 8:8 says: *So that He may verify the truth and prove falsehood to be false.* 34:49 says: *Truth has come, and falsehood originates naught, nor restores.* 42:24 says: *God wipes out falsehood and verifies the truth through His Words.* The verse at 21:18 is the most violent of these images. The verb *damaghahu* — to crush — is not present in any of the other passages. The violence of the image is appropriate to the moment. The audience has been called to confess; the audience has refused; the casting of truth against falsehood is the divine response to the refusal. The response is not gentle.

The accumulation of these passages is also significant. The Qur'an is not content with one statement of the relationship between truth and falsehood; it returns to the theme in many forms. The repetition is part of the polemic. The audience is asked to consider the relationship from many angles; each angle makes the same point. The point is that truth is, by its nature, the active force in the moral order, and falsehood is the recipient. The audience's refusal does not change this order; it only places the audience on the side of falsehood.

**The Verse As Self-Application**

The verse is, of course, itself part of the truth that it describes. The recitation is part of what is being cast against falsehood. The audience is being asked to recognise that the verse they are hearing is itself an instance of the casting it describes. The truth of the verse is not an abstract proposition; it is the active presence of the casting itself. The verse is doing what it is talking about. The audience is therefore not hearing a description of something happening elsewhere; they are hearing an instance of the happening.

This self-application is one of the recurrent features of Qur'anic rhetoric. The verse does not just say that truth is cast against falsehood; the verse is itself the casting. The audience's recognition of this self-application is part of what the verse is asking for. The audience is being invited to see themselves inside the verse — not as outside observers of the casting but as the objects of it. The objects of the casting do not remain as they were. The casting is the cause of the vanishing. The audience is being asked to recognise that they are themselves in the position of what is being crushed.

**A Woe That Precedes the Woes That Follow**

The verse ends with the first of a series of woes in this sūrah. The audience will hear *waylun* again at 21:46, 54:8, and many other places. The woe is the Qur'an's standard word for the divine verdict pronounced on those who have refused the truth. The verse at 21:18 introduces the word into the sūrah; the audience is warned that the verdict is being prepared. The woe will come; the question is whether the audience will hear it as a warning in time, or as a verdict after the time. The verse at 21:18 is the warning; the verses that follow will determine which kind of hearing the audience has.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:19

> To Him belong all those in the heavens and the earth. And those nearest to Him are not too proud to worship Him, nor do they tire

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Verse That Names the Owner of All That Exists**

The verse opens with a claim that the rest of the verse presupposes. *Wa lahu man fī al-samāwāti wa al-arḍ* — to Him belongs whoever is in the heavens and the earth. The construction *lahu man* — to Him belongs whoever — is one of the Qur'an's most expansive ownership formulas. It does not say that He owns some of those in the heavens and the earth; it says He owns whoever is in them. The possession is exhaustive. Every being in every realm belongs to Him, and the belonging is the basic fact about those beings.

This is the foundation of the rest of the verse. Once ownership is established, the rest of the verse can describe the conduct of those who are owned. The ownership is not a metaphor; it is the reality that determines what is possible for those who belong to God. To belong to God is to be in a position of acknowledged dependence; the dependence is the basis of worship. Without ownership, worship is one option among many; with ownership, worship is the appropriate response to the fact of belonging.

**"Those Nearest to Him" — The Proximity That Is Worship**

The second half of the verse specifies a particular group: *wa alladhīna ʿindahu* — those who are with Him, those in His presence, those nearest to Him. The phrase has been understood by al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī to refer to the angels — the beings whose existence is the most direct response to the divine presence. The verse is therefore making a claim about the highest order of created beings. If even the highest order worships, the implication for lower orders is firm.

The verse does not say that those nearest to God have been ordered to worship. It says they are not too proud to worship; they do not tire. The form of the claim is significant. Worship is not something imposed on those who are reluctant; it is something they embrace without fatigue. The verse thereby refuses a model in which worship is the reluctant performance of obligation. The model it offers is one in which the highest beings, who are nearest to the source of being, do not merely accept worship but never tire of it. Worship is not a burden to them; it is the expression of what they are.

**"Not Too Proud" — The Refusal of a Specific Vice**

The Arabic *lā yastakbirūna* — they do not behave with arrogance, they are not too proud — names a specific vice. Pride is not the only obstacle to worship; fear, fatigue, confusion, and distraction are also obstacles. The verse names the most theologically dangerous obstacle, which is the refusal to acknowledge the order of being. The beings nearest to God could, if they wished, claim a status that exempted them from worship. They could claim to be too exalted to prostrate, too close to the source to need to express gratitude. The verse says they do not. They are not too proud to worship.

This is one of the Qur'an's most pointed statements about the nature of the highest beings. The verse is not just saying that angels worship; it is saying that the refusal of worship would be a possibility for them, and that they have refused the possibility. The implication is that the refusal of worship is a possibility for any created being; the choice not to refuse it is itself a moral victory. The verses will return to the refusal by humans (Iblīs in 21:22 is not named here, but the contrast is implicit). The audience is invited to consider which side of the contrast they are on.

**"Nor Do They Tire" — The Permanence of Their Worship**

The verse adds *wa lā yastaʿsūn* — they do not tire, they do not become exhausted. The verb *istaʿsa* names the kind of fatigue that comes from long-continued effort. The verse is therefore making a claim about the duration of the worship of those nearest to God. The worship is not a passing engagement; it is a permanent posture. The beings nearest to God worship without fatigue, and they continue to worship without fatigue.

The commentators note that this kind of unfatiguing worship is possible only for the angels. Human beings tire; the verse is not making a claim about humans. The claim is about the highest created order; the claim is being made for the purpose of contrasting what the highest order does with what lower orders sometimes refuse to do. The contrast is implicit. The audience is being asked to consider that if the highest order does not tire, then the question of whether to tire of worship is a question about the disposition of the worshipper, not about the burden of the worship itself.

**A Verse That Provides the Standard Against Which Human Worship Is Measured**

The verse is a standard. It says: this is what worship looks like at the highest level. The standard is not coercive; the verse does not command the audience to match it. The standard is presented as the description of a fact about the highest created order. The audience is invited to measure their own worship against that description. The measurement is not flattering. Human beings tire; human beings grow proud; human beings find reasons to withhold the worship that is owed. The verse places the audience's own conduct in a wider field in which other beings, nearer to God and more exalted in the divine economy, do not behave that way.

The verse also provides the implicit basis for the condemnation of those who refuse to worship. If those who are nearest to God worship without pride and without fatigue, then those who are further from God and yet refuse to worship are choosing a position that is below the position of the highest created order. The refusal is therefore not just a refusal of duty; it is a refusal of the position that the refuser has been given. The refuser is asking to be lower than the angel; the asking is the refusal itself.

**A Verse That Continues the Polemic Against the Audience**

The verse is part of the polemic against the Meccan audience. The audience had refused to acknowledge that the heavens and the earth belong to God; the audience had instead imagined that God had partners who shared His dominion. The verse names what the audience's framework has missed. The framework had imagined God as one among several powers, with consorts and partners. The verse says: all that exists, in the heavens and the earth, belongs to God, and those who are nearest to God do not refuse to worship Him. The audience's framework is being dismantled. Each verse removes another support. The verse at 21:19 removes the support that depends on the divine nature. The audience had imagined God as one whose nature would make Him reluctant to demand worship. The verse says: those nearest to God do not find worship reluctant; the audience's imagining is wrong.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:20

> They glorify ˹Him˺ day and night, never wavering

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Continuity of Angelic Worship**

The verse continues the description of the highest order of created beings. *Yusabbiḥūna al-layla wa al-nahāra lā yafturūn* — they glorify by night and day, without interruption, without wavering. The verb *sabbiḥa* — to glorify, to declare transcendent, to remove from imperfection — is the Qur'an's most characteristic word for the worship of the highest order. The same verb names the human practice of *tasbīḥ*; the verse is using the verb to describe what angels do continuously.

The temporal specification — *al-layla wa al-nahāra* — by night and by day — is a standard Qur'anic way of expressing totality of time. To say that the angels glorify by night and by day is to say that no part of the time is exempt from their glorification. Their worship is not occasional; it is continuous. The verse does not allow for any moment at which the angels are not engaged in the act of declaring God's transcendence.

**"Never Wavering" — The Unfailing Continuation**

The phrase *lā yafturūn* — they do not cease, they do not interrupt, they do not waver — closes the verse. The verb *fatra* names a gap or interruption in an otherwise continuous activity. The verse says there is no such gap in the angelic worship. The worship is uninterrupted; the angels do not take a break; they do not alternate between glorification and other activities; the glorification is the steady state of their existence.

This is the second time the verse has emphasised the continuity of the angelic worship. The previous verse (21:19) said they do not tire; this verse says they do not waiver. The two claims are related but distinct. Not to tire is to have the energy for the activity; not to waiver is to remain committed to the activity. The angels are not just able to continue; they continue without interruption. The combination of the two claims is what makes the angelic worship a model of the permanent posture that the verse is describing.

**The Relation to 41:38**

The verse echoes 41:38, which says: *Those who are near to Him do not boast against each other, and they do not tire.* The two passages together form one of the Qur'an's recurring descriptions of the highest order of created beings. The echo is not accidental; the Qur'an returns to the same description because the description is foundational. The point is not to give biographical information about the angels; the point is to describe a permanent feature of the moral order. The moral order includes beings whose worship is the steady state of their existence.

This is one of the most demanding features of the Qur'anic worldview. The reader is asked to imagine a level of being in which worship is not the response to an occasional summons but the constant posture. The reader is also asked to imagine that this level of being is not exhausting for those who inhabit it. The reader is being introduced to a kind of existence in which worship is the natural expression of what the worshipper is.

**A Model for Human Worship Without Coercion**

The verse presents the angelic worship as a model, but the model is not coercive. The Qur'an does not command the audience to worship without wavering; the command would be impossible for human beings. The verse is therefore doing something subtler than commanding imitation. The verse is presenting the angelic worship as the description of the highest order; the audience is invited to consider the gap between its own practice and that description. The invitation is not a command; it is an exercise in moral location.

The gap is the gap between wavering and unwavering, between tiring and not tiring, between occasional engagement and continuous posture. The verse is asking the audience to consider whether the gap can be closed. The closing is not the work of a single decision; it is the slow work of forming the kind of being for whom worship is the steady state. The verse therefore points toward a project of self-formation that the audience may or may not undertake. The project is open-ended; the description of the highest order is the criterion against which the project measures itself.

**The Polemical Edge of the Description**

The description of the angelic worship also has a polemical edge. The audience of al-Anbiyāʾ had refused to worship God with the seriousness that worship requires. The verse describes the highest order of beings as those who worship without wavering. The contrast is implicit. The audience is being asked to consider where they fall on the spectrum between the unwavering worship of the angels and the wavering refusal of those who treat the message as play. The polemic is silent but precise. The audience is being invited to choose its company.

This is a recurring Qur'anic strategy. The audience is not confronted directly with a command; the audience is presented with a description of the highest order, and the audience is invited to recognise that the choice it is making places it below the highest order. The recognition is the work of the polemic. The recognition does not coerce; it presents the choice as it is, and asks the audience to make the choice with the relevant facts in view. The relevant facts include the existence of beings who do not waver. The audience is invited to be such a being or to recognise that it has chosen not to be.

**What "Glorifying" Means in This Verse**

The verb *sabbiḥa* has been theologically rich throughout the Qur'anic vocabulary. To glorify God is to declare that He is beyond all imperfection; to declare that no category of creaturely limitation applies to Him; to declare that He is utterly other. The angels, by their permanent glorification, are therefore declaring something about their own relationship to God: they are not God, they are not equal to God, they are not competing with God. The declaration is the form their existence takes. The angels exist as the declaration of divine otherness.

For the audience of the Qur'an, the same declaration is available, even if the form of the declaration differs. The audience is invited to join the declaration, not by becoming angels but by recognising what the angels have already recognised. The recognition is the beginning of the *tasbīḥ* in human life. The verse does not promise that human *tasbīḥ* will be as continuous as the angels'; the verse promises only that the declaration, when it is made, will be made in the same direction. The audience is being invited to declare, with whatever continuity they can sustain, that God is beyond all imperfection. The declaration is the substance of worship. The audience is invited to make it.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:21

> Or have they taken gods from the earth, who can raise the dead

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Question That Poses Its Own Answer**

The verse is one short question, and the question carries its answer in its grammar. *Or have they taken gods from the earth?* The construction *a-ittakhadhū* — have they taken — uses the verb of choosing, adopting, taking as partners. The audience is being asked whether they have, in fact, chosen gods who are from the earth. The question is rhetorical, but the rhetorical force depends on what follows. The gods they have chosen cannot do what gods must do. The verse specifies one thing gods must do: *who can raise the dead*.

The raising of the dead is the test. Anything that can raise the dead has the kind of power that belongs to God alone. Anything that cannot raise the dead is not God. The audience's choice of gods — idols, statues, angels treated as intercessors, deified beings of one kind or another — fails this test. The test is not a test of theological sophistication; it is a test of power. The verse is asking: have you taken gods who can do what only God can do?

**Why Resurrection Is the Litmus Test**

The verb *baʿatha* — to send forth, to raise, to bring back to life — names the central divine act that no created being can perform. The Qur'an elsewhere describes the challenge of the resurrection explicitly: *And they say, "When we are bones and crumbled dust, will we be raised as a new creation?"* (17:49; 17:98); *They say, "Will we indeed be returned to the original state of creation?"* (79:10). The Meccan audience had struggled with the doctrine of bodily resurrection; the verse at 21:21 is built around the same struggle. The audience doubted that God would raise the dead; the audience is now being asked whether their gods could do what they doubted God would do.

Theological struggles cluster around the doctrine of resurrection for a reason. Resurrection is the most clearly visible demonstration that God has power over His creation in a way that nothing else has. A god who could not raise the dead would not be God; the audience's gods cannot raise the dead; therefore the audience's gods are not God. The verse does not need to argue the theological claim; it just needs to ask the empirical question. The empirical answer is no. The verse's polemic is therefore not built on argument but on observation.

**"From the Earth" — The Origin That Disqualifies**

The phrase *ālihatan min al-arḍi* — gods from the earth — specifies the origin of the gods the audience has taken. They are not transcendent; they are terrestrial. They came from the earth; they are made of the earth; they will return to the earth. The gods of the Meccan pantheon were often stones, trees, or statues carved from materials of the earth. The verse's specification of "from the earth" is the description of their origin and a quiet dismissal of their status. Gods that are from the earth are part of the earth's furniture; they are not its rulers.

This is also a refusal of the polytheistic worldview on a metaphysical level. The polytheists had imagined that the gods were at a distance from the world, occasionally intervening. The verse is saying that the gods they have taken are not at a distance; they are from the same place they inhabit. The gods are part of the world; they do not transcend it; they cannot raise anyone from the world because they are part of the world. The audience's framework has assigned transcendence to beings that have none.

**A Verse That Reframes the Polemic Against Idolatry**

The verse is doing part of the Qur'an's larger polemic against idolatry. The polemic does not only argue that idols are useless; it argues that idols are misclassified. They have been called gods; they are not. They have been given the role of gods; they cannot perform it. The argument is not that the idols are worthless but that they are misidentified. The audience has called them gods; the verse is asking whether the calling fits.

The reframing is important because the audience did not, in many cases, think of their idols as literal rivals to God. They thought of them as intercessors, as higher beings who could approach God on their behalf, as functional deities within the divine economy. The verse refuses this functional theology. There is no functional deity; there is only God. The idols cannot raise the dead; the angels who are sometimes treated as intercessors are creatures like the audience; nothing in creation has the power that the audience is looking for. The looking is in the wrong place.

**The Resurrection Symbol in the Image of Bringing the Earth Back to Life**

The commentators note that the imagery of biological renewal — the revival of dead land by rain — is treated elsewhere as a symbol of resurrection (35:9). The verse at 21:21 fits within a wider Qur'anic argument that takes the revival of dead land as a small-scale model of the larger resurrection. The audience that sees land die and revive every season has a sign of the resurrection before its eyes. The verse's question — *who can raise the dead?* — is therefore not addressed to a community that lacks the data for an answer. The community has the data; the community has been watching the earth die and revive; the community has been told that the same hand that revives the land will revive the dead.

The data is offered; the verse asks whether the audience has integrated it. The integration is the response the verse is asking for. The audience is being asked to recognise that what is impossible for their terrestrial gods is not impossible for the God whose signs are visible in the recurring death and life of the earth. The audience's gods are tied to the earth; the God of the verse is the one who gives life to the earth and who will give life again to those who have been buried in it. The verse does not say this in full; the verse's question is enough to invite the inference.

**A Question That Asks for the Audience's Reorientation**

The verse ends in a question; the question is the work. The audience is asked to reorient. The reorientation is from the idols they have been calling gods to the God who can raise the dead. The verse does not command the reorientation; it poses the question and waits. The audience that answers the question honestly has begun the work the verse is asking for; the audience that refuses to answer has refused the verse. The verse's force is in the question; the audience is being invited to recognise the question as addressed to them, and to answer it in the only way it can be answered.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:22

> Had there been other gods besides Allah in the heavens or the earth, both ˹realms˺ would have surely been corrupted. So Glorified is Allah, Lord of the Throne, far above what they claim

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Argument from Corruption**

The verse makes a structural argument for monotheism. *Law kāna fīhimā ālihatun illā Allāhu la-fasadatā* — if there were in them gods other than God, they would have been corrupted. Any system with multiple independent authorities would not hold together. Each god would have a will; each will would seek to implement itself; the implementation of multiple wills would result in contradiction, and the contradiction would tear the system apart.

The argument is not a proof in the philosophical sense; it is closer to a recognition of what would have to be the case if there were more than one god. The recognition has been described as a *burhān* — a demonstration — by the commentators. The heavens and the earth are intact. The heavens revolve; the orbits hold; the seasons continue; the earth supports life; the system works. The system works because there is a single will at the centre, and the existence of the system is itself the demonstration. The system could not work if multiple wills were competing for its governance.

**"In Them" — The Two Realms Addressed**

The verse uses the dual pronoun *fīhimā* — in them — to refer to the heavens and the earth. The dual form is precise. The verse is not saying that the corruption would have occurred in some general sense; it is saying that it would have occurred in both realms. Each realm would have been corrupted by its competing authorities; the corruption would have been visible in the heavens as disorder in the celestial mechanics, and in the earth as disorder in the natural order. Neither disorder is observed; the absence of the observed disorder is the demonstration.

A reader can step outside at night, see the stars in their places, watch the moon wax and wane, and consider whether the observed order is what would be expected if there were competing authorities for the governance of the sky. The observed order is not what would be expected; therefore the assumption of competing authorities is false.

**The Closing Declaration of Transcendence**

The verse closes with *subḥāna Allāhi rabbi al-ʿarshi ʿammā yasifūn* — glory be to God, Lord of the Throne, above what they ascribe. The declaration of divine transcendence is the response to the ascriptions of partners, consorts, and children that the polytheists have made. The verb *sabbaha* — to declare transcendent, to glorify — is the same verb used of the angels' continuous worship (21:20). The audience is being asked to join the angels in declaring divine transcendence against the false ascriptions.

The phrase *rabb al-ʿarsh* — Lord of the Throne — names God in one of His most exalted titles. The Throne (*al-ʿarsh*) is, in the Qur'anic vocabulary, the seat of divine governance; the Lord of the Throne is the one in whose governance the heavens and the earth are held. The verse is therefore not just naming God in the abstract; it is naming God as the one whose governance is the condition of the heavens and the earth being intact. The Throne is the locus of the oneness that has just been argued for.

**Other Verses That Make the Same Argument**

The verse is one of several in the Qur'an that argue for monotheism from the structural integrity of the world. The same argument recurs in different forms: each passage uses a different aspect of the same intuition, which is that the world as we observe it is what we would expect from a single will, not from multiple competing wills. If there were other gods, each would seek to implement its own purposes; the competing purposes would manifest as visible disorder; the visible order we observe is therefore evidence for a single divine will at the centre.

The repetition of the argument across multiple passages is not redundancy; it is the consistent application of a method. The Qur'an reads the natural order as evidence for the doctrine of divine unity. The reading is offered repeatedly because the audience is invited to verify it for itself. The verification is not difficult; the audience can look at the sky and ask whether the order they see is the order that would obtain under competing authorities. The empirical answer is clear; the empirical answer is what the Qur'an has been pointing to.

**The Spiritual Reading That Extends the Argument**

The Sufi commentators offered a reading in which the verse is understood symbolically. The "two realms" are also the two orders of the soul; the multiple gods are the competing impulses within a divided heart; the corruption is the corruption that occurs when the heart is governed by more than one intention. The reading is consistent with the verse's polemical force. A heart that is divided between competing loyalties cannot hold itself together; the heart must be unified and detached from entanglements in the world to remain sound.

The verse is therefore also a description of a spiritual reality. The heart that tries to serve two masters is corrupted; the heart that has a single object of devotion is held together. The argument from the heavens and the earth is also the argument from the integrity of the self. A self that tries to be governed by multiple competing wills cannot hold; the self that is held in a single direction can. The verse's argument is empirical at both scales; the empirical observation at both scales supports the same conclusion.

**The Verse As the Centre of the Anti-Polytheistic Argument**

The verse is the centre of the sūrah's anti-polytheistic argument. The verses before it (21:19–21) established that the heavens and the earth belong to God, that those nearest to Him worship without wavering, and that gods from the earth cannot raise the dead. The verse at 21:22 makes the structural argument that follows from these premises: if there were gods other than God, the order of the world would have been corrupted. The verses after it (21:23 onward) will draw the consequences: God is not questioned, but all others will be questioned; the audience is challenged to bring its proof; the messengers have always proclaimed the same monotheistic message.

The verse's centrality is not incidental. The argument from corruption is the one that makes the others coherent. The verses that say God is the only one in the heavens and the earth are not just claims; they are claims that follow from a structural argument about the kind of governance the world requires. The argument sits at the centre of the polemic, and the polemic proceeds outward from the centre. The audience is invited to recognise that the centre is the foundation of everything else they have been hearing.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:23

> He cannot be questioned about what He does, but they will ˹all˺ be questioned

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Asymmetry of Accountability**

The verse establishes a fundamental asymmetry. *Lā yuʾsalu ʿammā yafʿalu wa hum yuʾsalūn* — He is not questioned about what He does, but they will be questioned. The verb *suʾāl* — to question, to hold to account — is the same verb that has appeared in 21:13 and 21:14; here it is given its sharpest application. God is not answerable to any external authority; creatures are answerable to Him. The asymmetry is not an arbitrary preference; it is the structural feature of the relationship between the Creator and the created.

The verse does not say that God is not subject to questioning because His actions are necessarily just; it says He is not subject to questioning. The non-questionability is ontological, not moral. God is not subject to questioning because the structure of being places the Creator outside the jurisdiction of the created. A creature that questions God is asking the kind of question that the structure does not accommodate. The question has no forum in which it can be properly addressed.

**The Theological Stakes of the Claim**

The verse touches directly on some of the most contested theological questions in the history of religious thought. If God is not answerable for what He does, what becomes of human demands for justice, for reasons, for explanations? The commentators have approached the question in several ways. Al-Rāzī notes that the verse forecloses any demand that God provide reasons for His actions that a creature could verify. The reasons for what God does are not available to creatures; the lack of availability is part of the structure. A creature that demands reasons is asking for a kind of access that the structure does not provide.

The verse also touches on the question of free will versus predestination. If God cannot be questioned for what He does, then the question of why one person is made a believer and another a disbeliever cannot be addressed to God; it can only be addressed to the human being, who will be questioned. The Muʿtazilite theologians tried to preserve human moral agency by limiting God's action to what is just; the verse refuses this approach. God acts as He acts; the acting is not subject to the moral categories that govern creaturely action.

**Why This Is the Foundation of Worship**

The verse is the foundation of the kind of worship the Qur'an describes. Worship that is conditioned on understanding the reasons for what God does is not the worship the Qur'an describes. The Qur'an's worship is the response of the creature to a God who is not conditioned by creaturely reasons. The response is owed because of the structure, not because of the reasons. The structure is the basis of the obligation; the reasons are not required.

This is one of the most demanding aspects of the Qur'anic worldview. A creature that demands reasons before worshipping is asking for a God whose worship is conditional. The verse at 21:23 says that the God the Qur'an describes is not that God. The God the Qur'an describes acts as He acts, and the creature's response is owed before the creature has understood the act. The response is the appropriate posture of the creature before the Creator; the verse is grounding the appropriateness in the structure of being.

**The Reverse Asymmetry — They Will Be Questioned**

The verse's second half — *wa hum yuʾsalūn* — but they will be questioned — restores the asymmetry the first half has established. The creatures will be questioned; the questioning will be done by the One who is not questioned. The forum is therefore not symmetric; the forum is one-sided. The creature stands before the Creator and is questioned; the Creator does not stand before the creature and answer.

The verse does not specify what the creatures will be questioned about. The commentators have inferred that the questioning will be about the deeds, the words, the intentions, the moments of life, and the choices made. The Qur'an elsewhere specifies some of the content of the questioning (the five greater things in the hadith tradition; the moments of standing before God in 36:65; the recording of every word and deed in 36:12; the weighing of deeds in 21:47). The verse at 21:23 is the general principle of which these specifications are applications.

**The Verse As the Refusal of a Particular Kind of Arrogance**

The verse also refuses a particular kind of intellectual arrogance. There is a temptation, especially among the religiously educated, to imagine that the questioning of God is a sophisticated posture — that to ask why God does what He does is a sign of intellectual seriousness. The verse says the opposite. The questioning of God is the failure to recognise the structure. The failure is not sophisticated; it is fundamental. The sophisticated posture is to recognise that the structure is what it is, and to worship accordingly.

The audience of al-Anbiyāʾ had not been engaged in philosophical questioning; they had been engaged in practical refusal. They had refused to worship, and they had produced arguments for the refusal. The verse at 21:23 addresses the arguments at their root. The arguments have been based on the assumption that the creature can question the Creator. The verse says: that assumption is the structure of the wrong; the question has no forum; the creature will be questioned, not the Creator.

**A Verse That Makes the Subsequent Demands Coherent**

The verse is the foundation for what the rest of the sūrah will demand of the audience. The audience will be challenged to bring proof of their claims (21:24); they will be told that messengers have always proclaimed the same message (21:25); they will be told that the things they have taken as partners are servants, not gods (21:26–29). None of these demands would be coherent if the creature could question the Creator. The demands are coherent only because the forum has been set by the verse at 21:23. The audience is being asked to recognise that the forum is not theirs; the forum is God's; the audience will be the ones questioned in it.

This is one of the most important theological moves in the Qur'an. The verse refuses to enter the creature's forum. The creature has imagined that the appropriate response to the message is to question the message's source. The verse says: the source is not to be questioned; you are. The forum is therefore the forum of accountability, not the forum of intellectual debate. The audience is invited to recognise the forum and to act accordingly. The recognition is the work the verse is asking for.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:24

> Or have they taken other gods besides Him? Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “Show ˹me˺ your proof. Here is ˹the Quran,˺ the Reminder for those with me; along with ˹earlier Scriptures,˺ the Reminder for those before me.” But most of them do not know the truth, so they turn away

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Demand for Proof**

The verse returns to the question of partners and asks for evidence. *Or have they taken other gods besides Him?* The verse is a challenge: if the audience has taken gods besides God, let them show their proof. The verb *taʿrif* — show, produce, demonstrate — names the work of bringing forward what would be required to establish the claim. The audience is being asked to produce what they have.

The challenge is one of the Qur'an's recurring devices. The audience is invited to bring the evidence that supports their position. The invitation is courteous in form and devastating in implication. The audience has been maintaining the position for some time; the position must have evidence somewhere. The verse is asking the audience to produce it. The audience cannot. The Qur'an knows the audience cannot; the verse is structured so that the audience's inability to produce evidence becomes the demonstration of the position's emptiness.

**"The Reminder for Those With Me" — The Qur'an in Its Own Voice**

The Prophet is asked to say: *this is the Reminder for those with me*. The Arabic *dhikr* — reminder, mention, remembrance — is one of the Qur'an's self-designations. What the *dhikr* is for the community is precisely the Qur'an. The word names the function the Book performs for those who receive it: it reminds them of what they had been created to know and to do. The designation is not boastful; it is descriptive. The Qur'an does what a *dhikr* does. The audience is invited to recognise what they have.

The verse is unusual in the Qur'an because it puts words in the Prophet's mouth in the middle of the divine address. The verse is part of the divine address; the Prophet is being asked to speak within it. The effect is to make the Prophet's speech continuous with the divine address. The audience cannot distinguish the Prophet's words from the words of the One who sent him. The continuity is the authentication. The audience is being asked to receive the Prophet's speech as they receive the divine address; the verse is making the reception the same act.

**"The Reminder for Those Before Me" — The Earlier Scriptures**

The second *dhikr* is the Reminder for those before the Prophet. The verse is referring to the Torah, the Gospel, and the other revealed books that preceded the Qur'an. The *dhikr* is the same function in a different community; the function is universal. The earlier communities also had a Reminder; the Reminder for them is the same kind of book as the Reminder for the present community. The verse is therefore not claiming that the Qur'an is the only *dhikr*; it is claiming that the Qur'an is the *dhikr* for this community, just as the Torah was the *dhikr* for the previous community.

This is one of the most significant claims in the Qur'anic self-understanding. The Qur'an is not a replacement for the previous Scriptures; it is the continuation of the same function in a new community. The previous Scriptures are not invalidated; they are the *dhikr* of the communities that received them. For that community, the *dhikr* they have received is the Qur'an itself. The implication is that the message is one, even if the communities are different. The verse is therefore part of the Qur'an's argument against the polemical claims of both polytheists and particularist religious communities. The message is not the property of one group; it is the property of the human community as it has been addressed by revelation through history.

**The Audience's Ignorance — "Most of Them Do Not Know the Truth"**

The verse's closing observation is sharp: *but most of them do not know the truth, so they turn away*. The Arabic *lā yaʿlamūna* — they do not know — is not the ignorance of those who have never heard. It is the ignorance of those who have heard and have refused to receive. The audience has had access to the message; the audience has had the proof that the verse has just asked for; the audience has chosen not to recognise what it has. The ignorance is therefore not intellectual; it is volitional.

The verb *muʿriḍūn* — they turn away — describes the gesture of those who have heard and have chosen not to engage. The turning away is not from one specific claim; it is from the truth as such. The audience has been given the truth; the truth is the Reminder for those with them and the Reminder for those before them; the audience has turned away. The verse names the turning as the consequence of the ignorance, and the ignorance as the cause of the turning. The cycle is closed; the audience has been moving in it.

**A Verse That Connects Two Testaments**

The verse is one of the most explicit Qur'anic statements of continuity between the Qur'an and the earlier Scriptures. The *dhikr* is one; the communities are multiple; the function is the same. The verse is therefore a refusal of the kind of religious particularism that would treat the Qur'an as a competitor with the Torah and the Gospel. The Qur'an is the continuation of what they began; the verse is the explanation of how the continuation works. The audience is invited to recognise that the previous Scriptures, if they had been received properly, would have welcomed the Qur'an as their own continuation. The audience that turns away from the Qur'an is turning away from what their own Scriptures had promised.

This is part of the broader Qur'anic argument that the previous religious communities had been informed, in their own Scriptures, of the coming of the Prophet. The verse at 21:24 is not making that argument in detail; it is making the general claim that the Reminder is one and that the present Reminder is the same kind of thing as the previous Reminder. The detail of the argument is elsewhere; the principle is here.

**A Verse That Refuses to Enter the Audience's Forum**

The verse's challenge — *show me your proof* — is itself a refusal to enter the audience's forum. The audience had been demanding proof from the Prophet; the verse inverts the demand. The audience is asked to bring its own proof. The inversion is not a rhetorical trick; it is the recognition that the audience has been making claims that need to be supported by evidence. The audience has been claiming that its gods are gods, that its partners are partners, that its intercessors are intercessors. The verse asks for the evidence. The audience cannot produce the evidence; the inability is the verdict.

In context, the verse functions as the polemic counterpart to 21:23. The verse at 21:23 refused to allow the audience to question God; this verse refuses to allow the audience to make unsupported claims about gods. The two refusals together close the avenues by which the audience might have attempted to continue its position. The audience is left with the position, but without the forum in which to defend it. The closure is the work of the verse.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:25
