> We never sent a messenger before you ˹O Prophet˺ without revealing to him: “There is no god ˹worthy of worship˺ except Me, so worship Me ˹alone˺.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Unchanging Message Across Messengers**

The verse makes one of the Qur'an's most theologically significant claims: every messenger has been sent with the same essential message. *Wa mā arsalnā qablaka min rasūlin illā nūḥī ilayhi annahu lā ilāha illā ana fa-ʿbudūnī* — We did not send any messenger before you except that We revealed to him that there is no god except Me, so worship Me. The verse uses the conditional structure *illā* — except that — to make the claim absolute. Every messenger; every revelation; the same message.

This is one of the strongest formulations in the Qur'an of the unity of revelation. The messengers had different laws, different specific commands, different historical situations. The message they brought, at its core, was the same. The verse is therefore not just making a historical claim; it is making a theological claim about the structure of revelation. The structure is monotheistic; the content is the call to worship the One God; the form varies but the substance does not.

**"There Is No God Except Me" — The First-Person Voice**

The verse puts the message in the first person of God. *Lā ilāha illā ana* — there is no god except I. The first-person speech is the speech of the One who is being addressed. The structural ambiguity of the Arabic is significant: *ana* — I — refers back to the One speaking in the verse, who is God. The saying of the message is the saying of the divine utterance; the message is not the Prophet's composition; it is the divine self-disclosure.

The first-person voice is also a refusal of mediation. The message is not transmitted through a chain of intermediaries; it is spoken directly by God. The Prophet is the recipient of the message, not the author. The earlier messengers were also recipients, not authors. The verse is therefore establishing the protocol of revelation: God speaks; the messenger receives; the messenger conveys. The protocol is the same for every messenger. The shape of revelation is consistent across the history of messengers.

**"So Worship Me" — The Conclusion of Monotheism**

The second half of the message — *fa-ʿbudūnī* — worship Me — is the practical conclusion of the monotheistic claim. The first half says there is no god except God; the second half says therefore worship God. The two halves are connected by a single inference. If there is no other god, then there is no-one else to worship. The conclusion is not added; it is implied. The verse is explicit about the implication because the audience had not always drawn it.

The verb *ʿabada* in Arabic covers the full range of worship: ritual acts, inward devotion, the entire orientation of a life toward God. The verse's command therefore encompasses everything. To worship God is to orient one's life toward God in all its dimensions. The orientation is the practical form of monotheism. Monotheism that does not issue in worship is incomplete; worship that is not directed to the One God is misdirected. The verse is naming both halves of the requirement.

**What This Verse Says About Other Religions**

The verse's claim is one of the most demanding in the Qur'anic treatment of other religions. The Qur'an is not saying that all religions are true, or that all paths lead to the same destination. It is saying that the messages of the messengers have been, at their core, monotheistic. The verse is therefore a critique of any later religious tradition that has added partners, consorts, or intermediaries to the original monotheistic message. The additions are described in the Qur'an as later corruptions of what was originally given.

This is also why the Qur'an can address both the polytheists of Mecca and the People of the Book with a similar critique. The polytheists had added partners to God; some communities of the People of the Book had added sons and divine persons to God. The verse is saying that the original message to both communities was the same: there is no god except God; worship God. The additions are not part of the original message; they are part of the later history. The critique is therefore not of the religions as such but of the additions to them.

**The Uniform Content, the Varied Forms**

The verse is consistent with the Qur'anic recognition that different messengers had different specific commands. The Qur'an elsewhere acknowledges that the laws given to different communities were different (5:48). The uniformity of the verse at 21:25 is not a uniformity of all the details; it is a uniformity of the central message. The details are the work of the *sharīʿah*; the message is the work of the *dīn*. The verse is about the *dīn*.

The distinction is important for understanding the rest of the Qur'an's treatment of other religions. The Qur'an can say, at the level of the *dīn*, that all messengers brought the same message. The Qur'an can also say, at the level of the *sharīʿah*, that the specific commands given to different communities differed. The two claims are not contradictory. The verse at 21:25 is making the first claim; the verses that follow will work out the implications of both claims together.

**A Verse That Establishes the Continuity for the Audience**

The verse's audience, the Meccans, knew about earlier messengers. What the verse is doing here is not introducing a new doctrine; it is recalling a familiar one. The audience had heard the stories of Noah, Abraham, Moses, and the others. The verse is telling the audience what the content of those messengers' messages had been. The audience is being asked to recognise that the messages of those messengers, at their core, are the same as the message the audience is now receiving from Muhammad.

The recognition is the work the verse is asking for. The audience that recognises the continuity will be unable to refuse the present message as if it were new or strange. The message is not new; it is the same message; the audience has heard it before in the mouths of earlier messengers. The audience's refusal is therefore not a refusal of something unfamiliar; it is a refusal of something they have already been told, in many forms, by messengers whose authority the audience would, in other contexts, have acknowledged. The verse is closing off the avenues of refusal by reminding the audience what they have already heard.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:26

> And they say, “The Most Compassionate has offspring!” Glory be to Him! In fact, those ˹angels˺ are only ˹His˺ honoured servants

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Audience's Most Theologically Specific Accusation**

The verse records an accusation of particular precision: *The Most Compassionate has taken a child*. The Arabic *ittakhadha waladan* — has taken a child — is the same construction used in 21:17 of taking amusement. The accusation is therefore placed alongside the previous polemic. The audience had accused God of having offspring; the verse responds with the same kind of refusal it has been making throughout.

The accusation is not casual. In the pre-Islamic Arabian context, the accusation that God had offspring referred to the belief that the angels were the daughters of God. The Quraysh had imagined that the angels, as intercessors, were the daughters of God and that worshipping them was a way of approaching God. The verse addresses this belief directly. The accusative construction — *al-Raḥmānu* — uses the most beautiful name of God, the name that emphasises His mercy. The audience had taken the most beautiful name and attached to it the most theologically problematic claim. The juxtaposition is the verse's polemical force.

**"Glory Be to Him!" — The Transcendence Refused**

The verse replies with *subḥānahu* — glory be to Him — declaring divine transcendence against the ascription. The verb is the same one used by the angels in their continuous worship (21:20). The audience is being asked to join the angels in declaring that God is utterly transcendent over the ascription. The declaration is the response; the ascription is the error.

This is one of the Qur'an's characteristic moves. The polemic against false ascriptions is conducted by declaring divine transcendence. The audience is not asked to argue against their own claim; they are asked to declare the alternative. The alternative is the declaration of divine perfection. The declaration is the appropriate response to a false ascription; the ascription dissolves in the declaration.

**The Identity of the "Children"**

The verse identifies what the audience has been calling God's children: the angels. *In hum illā ʿibādun mukramūn* — they are nothing but honoured servants. The verb *inn* — verily — is restrictive. The verse is restricting what the angels are: they are honoured servants. The construction refuses any other description. They are not children; they are not partners; they are not intercessors in the sense the audience has been imagining. They are servants of God, honoured by God, and nothing more.

The identification is the central polemical move. The audience had taken the angels and elevated them to divine status; the verse returns them to their proper status. The angels are not divine by virtue of being called God's children; they are honoured by virtue of being servants. The two descriptions are not equivalent; the verse is making that non-equivalence explicit. The audience's framework has confused the categories; the verse is sorting them out.

**The Multiple Targets of the Verse**

The verse addresses two distinct theological errors. The first is the Arabian polytheistic belief that the angels are the daughters of God. The second is the Christian doctrine that Jesus is the Son of God. Both errors are forms of divine filiation; both involve the ascription of offspring to God; both are addressed by the same verse. The Qur'an does not typically single out one error at a time; it often addresses multiple errors in a single verse, because the verses are about categories of error rather than specific historical instances.

The commentators have noted that some Muslim theologians do not object to calling God "Father" in a narrow metaphorical sense. The objection is to the ontologisation of the metaphor. To say that God has a child in the way that human beings have children is to import a category of generation into the divine nature; the importation produces a theology that the Qur'an refuses. The verse is not forbidding the metaphor; it is forbidding the ontology.

**The Status of Honoured Servants**

The verse's alternative description — *ʿibādun mukramūn* — honoured servants — is carefully chosen. The angels are honoured, not divine; servants, not partners. The honour is the honour of being servants of God; it is not the honour of being God's offspring. The distinction is precise. To be honoured by God is to be given a place in the divine economy; to be God's offspring would be to share in the divine nature. The verse is rejecting the second and affirming the first.

This is one of the most carefully nuanced descriptions of the angels in the Qur'an. The angels are not diminished by being called servants; they are honoured by being called servants. The honour comes from the service, not from a metaphysical status. The verse is therefore also a lesson in how to think about the highest created order. The highest created order is not the order of beings that share in the divine nature; the highest created order is the order of beings that serve God with perfection. The service is the honour.

**A Verse That Connects to the Refusal of Polytheism**

The verse is the next move in the sūrah's polemic against polytheism. The audience had taken partners; the partners had been given ontological status they did not have. The verse at 21:26 names the partners: the angels. The angels are honoured servants; they are not gods; they are not offspring; they are not partners. The audience is being asked to recognise that the beings they had been treating as partners are, in fact, servants. The recognition is the conversion the verse is asking for.

The conversion is not a private matter; it is a public reorientation. The audience that had been worshipping the angels is being asked to stop. The angels do not need the worship; the worship is owed to the One whom the angels themselves serve. The verse is therefore not just a description of what the angels are; it is a prescription for what the audience is to do. The audience is to recognise the angels as honoured servants and to direct their worship to the One whom the angels serve. The recognition and the redirection are the work of the verse.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:27

> who do not speak until He has spoken, ˹only˺ acting at His command

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Silence of the Angels Before God**

The verse specifies the conduct of the angels in their honoured service. *Lā yasbiqūnahu bi-qawlin* — they do not precede Him in speech. The verb *sabaqa* — to precede, to go before — names the kind of initiative the angels do not take. The angels do not speak first; they wait. The waiting is part of their honoured status. To wait for God to speak is to acknowledge that speech begins with God. The angels, who are nearest to God, demonstrate the posture that all of creation is asked to demonstrate.

The verb is in the negative. The angels do not speak first. The negation is not a denial of speech; the angels do speak. The negation is about the timing and the initiative. Initiation never comes from the angels; they only respond. The response is the form their speech takes. To speak in response to God is to speak as a servant; to speak before God is to speak as a rival. The distinction is explicit in the verse's grammar.

**"Acting at His Command" — The Source of the Angels' Action**

The verse continues *wa bi-amrihi yaʿmalūn* — and they act at His command. The verb *yaʿmalu* — to act, to do — names the active dimension of the angels' service. The angels act; the acts are at God's command; the command is the source. What the verse describes, in other words, is a chain of obedience: God commands; the angels act; the acts follow from the command. The chain does not run in the other direction; the angels do not command and have God act.

The chain refuses a particular kind of piety that imagines the angels as intercessors who can independently intervene in the divine economy. The angels act at God's command; the command is the source of the action; the action is not initiated by the angels. To imagine the angels as independently intervening is to imagine a chain of obedience that does not exist.

**The Relation to 21:19–20**

The verse continues the description of the angels begun at 21:19. The angels are nearest to God (21:19); they do not tire of worshipping God (21:19); they glorify God continuously (21:20). What 21:27 adds is a specific feature of their conduct: they do not speak before God speaks. That feature is the source of the appropriateness of their worship. The angels' worship is appropriate because it follows the divine initiative; the angels do not worship on their own initiative and then expect God to ratify it. The order is the other way around.

The chain of obedience is also the basis of the angels' honour. The honour is not a function of the angels' nature; it is a function of the angels' posture. The posture is the waiting; the honour is what comes to those who wait properly. Any audience that wishes to be honoured by God is being invited to demonstrate the same posture: not to speak before God speaks, to act at God's command.

**The Spiritual Reading of the Verse**

The Sufi commentators offered a reading in which the verse is understood as an instruction for the inner life. The angels' posture of waiting for God to speak becomes the model for the human being who wishes to align the inner life with the divine will. The instruction is twofold: do not speak except as God has given speech; do not act except in accordance with the divine command. That twofold instruction is the substance of *ittibāʿ* — following — in its deepest sense. To follow is to wait for the command and then to act on it.

This reading is consistent with the verse's polemical force. The audience had been treating the angels as intercessors who could be invoked independently. The verse insists that the angels do not act independently. The angels are models of the dependent posture; the audience is being asked to learn the same posture. The angels demonstrate what it means to be a creature; the audience is being asked to recognise itself in that demonstration. The recognition is the conversion.

**A Refusal of Independent Intercession**

The verse is one of the Qur'anic foundations for the doctrine of intercession. The doctrine of intercession in the Qur'an is carefully bounded. Intercession can occur only with God's permission; the permission is specified in 21:28; the permission cannot be assumed. On their own initiative, the angels never intercede. They do not have an independent store of intercessory power. Their action, including any intercessory action, follows from the divine command. The verse is the foundation for the careful Qur'anic treatment of intercession: the angels are honoured servants; their honour does not include the right to intercede independently; the intercession is a matter of divine permission.

That foundation is one of the most distinctive features of the Qur'anic doctrine of intercession. The doctrine is not a denial that intercession can occur; the doctrine is a careful specification of when and how it can occur. The verse at 21:27 is one of the specifications: the angels do not initiate; they do not precede God in any act; they wait for the command. The intercession, when it occurs, follows the same pattern. The angels intercede at God's command; they do not intercede on their own initiative.

**The Verse That Frames the Angels' Service**

What 21:27 frames is what the angels' service looks like in practice. The previous verses had described the angels as those nearest to God and as those who glorify without tiring. The verse at 21:27 specifies the practical form of that service. The angels do not speak first; they wait; they act on the command. The practical form is what makes the angelic service possible. A service without the waiting would not be service; it would be initiative. A service without the acting would be silent witness; the angels act as well as wait. The combination of the two is the form of the honoured service.

For any reader seeking to understand the angelic order, this is one of the most important verses in the Qur'an. The angels are not just glorified beings; they are obedient beings. The obedience is not the obedience of those who have no choice; it is the obedience of those who have chosen the right posture and maintain it. The verse describes the angels as moral agents, not as automatic performers. The angels could speak first; they have chosen not to. The choice is what makes the angels the honoured servants.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:28

> He ˹fully˺ knows what is ahead of them and what is behind them. They do not intercede except for whom He approves, and they tremble in awe of Him

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Scope of God's Knowledge of the Angels**

The verse specifies the scope of God's knowledge of the angels. *Yaʿlamu mā bayna aydīhim wa mā khalfahum* — He knows what is before them and what is behind them. The phrase *mā bayna aydīhim* — what is before them — names the future; the phrase *mā khalfahum* — what is behind them — names the past. The verse is therefore saying that God encompasses the entire temporal existence of the angels. The future and the past are both within God's knowledge.

The commentators have noted that the phrase can also refer to the Hereafter and this world. *Mā bayna aydīhim* — what is before them — is the Hereafter; *mā khalfahum* — what is behind them — is this world. The verse would then be saying that God knows the angels in both their eschatological and worldly dimensions. The reading is consistent with the verse's other references to the Hereafter; the verse is naming both dimensions of angelic existence.

Either reading reinforces the verse's central claim: the angels are not outside God's knowledge in any dimension of their existence. The future is not hidden from God; the past is not preserved only in the angels' memory; the Hereafter is not beyond the divine scope. The angels are within God's knowledge at every point. The knowledge is total; the angels are not exempt.

**The Limited Scope of Intercession**

The verse specifies the scope of intercession. *Wa lā yashfaʿūna illā liman irtaḍā* — they do not intercede except for whom He is pleased with. The verse is therefore bounding the doctrine of intercession. The angels do intercede; the intercession is not denied. The intercession is limited to those with whom God is pleased. The limitation is the divine choice; the angels do not get to choose for whom they intercede. The choice belongs to God.

This is one of the most carefully stated Qur'anic verses on intercession. The verse does not say that the angels never intercede; it says they do not intercede for anyone except those with whom God is pleased. The implication is that the recipients of angelic intercession are those whom God has already approved. The intercession is therefore not a way of changing God's mind; it is a way of expressing God's already-existing pleasure. The intercession confirms; it does not persuade.

The verse has been the subject of extensive theological debate. The Muʿtazilite theologians restricted the category of those with whom God is pleased to those who have not committed major sins. The verse does not specify the category in those terms; the verse specifies the category by God's approval alone. The category is therefore broader or narrower than any human classification can make it. The verse is bounding the doctrine without resolving all the questions the bound raises.

**"They Tremble in Awe of Him" — The Posture of the Angels**

The verse ends with *wa hum min khashyatihi mushfiqūn* — and they are, from the fear of Him, anxious, wary, trembling. The verb *khashiya* — to fear with reverence — names a specific kind of fear. The angels fear God; the fear is not the fear of punishment but the fear of awe. The verse is therefore describing the angels as those who, even in their honoured status, are aware of the divine majesty in a way that produces trembling.

The trembling is significant. The angels are the highest order of created beings. The verse is saying that even they tremble in awe of God. The implication is that the human audience, who are lower than the angels, have even more reason to tremble. The verse is not making the trembling an obligation; it is making the trembling the natural response of any creature that recognises the divine majesty. The recognition produces the trembling; the trembling is the appropriate response.

**The Verse As a Model for Human Posture**

The verse is one of the most explicit Qur'anic descriptions of how the highest created order responds to God. The response is the combination of two features: obedience to the divine command (21:27), and awe of the divine majesty (this verse). The combination is what honoured service looks like. The angels serve because they are commanded; they tremble because they recognise the majesty of the one who commands. The two features are not contradictory; the two features together constitute the honoured service.

The audience is invited to consider the same combination in its own life. The audience has been commanded; the audience has not always obeyed. The audience has had access to the divine majesty; the audience has not always recognised it with the trembling the recognition deserves. The verse is not asking the audience to become angels; the verse is asking the audience to learn the angels' posture. The audience is being invited to tremble in awe of the One who has commanded the message; the trembling is the appropriate response to the message.

**The Connection to the Polemic Against the Audience's View of the Angels**

The verse is the third in a passage describing the angels (21:26–29). The passage has been dismantling the audience's view of the angels as divine partners or as beings who can be invoked independently. The verse at 21:28 contributes to the dismantling by specifying the limitations on angelic intercession. The angels do not intercede for everyone; they intercede only for those with whom God is pleased. The limitation places the intercession within the divine economy; the intercession does not escape the economy.

The verse is also a quiet rebuke to those who imagine that invoking the angels is a way of bypassing the divine judgement. The angels do not intercede for those whom God has not approved; the invocation of the angels does not produce automatic intercession. The audience is being asked to recognise that the relationship with the angels is mediated by the relationship with God; the relationship with God cannot be bypassed by going to the angels. The bypassing does not work; the angels themselves recognise that they do not bypass the divine will.

**A Verse That Reframes the Meaning of Fear**

The verse's description of the angels' fear is one of the most theologically significant reframings in the Qur'an. The fear is not the fear of punishment; the fear is the fear of awe. The angels, who are sinless, do not fear punishment. They fear the majesty. The fear is the appropriate response to majesty; the response is not conditional on the possibility of punishment. The fear is part of the worship; the fear is part of the service.

This reframing is important for the audience's understanding of its own religious life. The audience is invited to fear God, but the fear is not to be the fear of punishment alone. The fear is to be the fear of awe — the recognition that the One who has commanded the message is the One whose majesty the appropriate response is trembling. The trembling is the response; the response is the substance of the worship. The verse is teaching the audience what worship looks like at the highest level.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:29

> Whoever of them were to say, “I am a god besides Him,” they would be rewarded with Hell by Us. This is how We reward the wrongdoers

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Hypothetical That Defines a Category**

The verse is constructed as a hypothesis about what would happen if any of the angels were to claim divinity. *Wa man yaqul minhum innī ilāhun min dūnihi* — whoever of them says, "I am a god besides Him" — *fa-dhālika najzīhi jahannam* — that one We will requite with Hell. The verse is not saying that any angel has made this claim; the verse is specifying what would happen if one did. The specification is the substance of the verse.

The commentators have noted that the verse can be read as a specific reference to Iblīs, who is sometimes considered to have been among the angels before his refusal. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī record this reading. The reading is not necessary; the verse is structured as a hypothesis that defines a category, and the category can apply to any being who might make the claim. The application to Iblīs is one instance of the category; the category is broader than the instance.

**"They Would Be Rewarded" — The Logic of the Requital**

The verb *najzīhi* — We will requite him — uses the root * jazāʾ*, which names the appropriate return for an act. The requital is Hell; the Hell is the appropriate return for claiming divinity. The verse is therefore describing a moral order in which claims of divinity are matched with a specific punishment. The matching is not arbitrary; it is the structural consequence of what the claim denies.

A being that claims divinity denies the structure of being. The structure is the oneness of God; the claim of divinity by a creature denies the structure; the denial requires a correction. The correction is Hell. Hell is not added to the denial as an external punishment; Hell is the form the denial takes when its consequences are worked out. The denial is the act; Hell is the form the act assumes when it has run its course. The verse is describing this sequence in compact form.

**The Wrongdoers — Who They Are**

The verse ends with *kadhālika najzī al-ẓālimīn* — thus do We requite the wrongdoers. The verb *ẓalama* — to wrong — is the same verb used in 21:14 of the wrongdoers who confessed too late. The wrongdoers are the ones who have put something in the wrong place. The category is precise. A being that claims divinity has put itself in the wrong place; the being has usurped the position of the One who alone is entitled to that position. The wrong is therefore a specific kind of usurpation. The punishment is appropriate to the kind.

The verse is also making a connection between the angels and the audience. The verse says that if any of the angels were to claim divinity, they would be punished. The audience, who have been treating the angels as divine partners, have effectively been encouraging the kind of claim that the verse condemns. The audience is being asked to recognise that the framework in which they have been operating is the framework in which the angels, if they were to make the claim, would be punished. The audience is in the wrong framework; the wrong framework produces the wrong outcome.

**A Refusal of Any Divine Status for the Angels**

The verse is one of the strongest Qur'anic refusals of any divine status for the angels. The angels have been described as honoured servants (21:26); as those who do not speak before God (21:27); as those whose intercession is bounded by God's approval (21:28); and now as those who, if they were to claim divinity, would be punished with Hell. The descriptions form a single portrait. The portrait is the portrait of the highest created order, and the portrait refuses to elevate the highest created order into the divine nature.

This is one of the most significant features of the Qur'an's theology of the highest created order. The theology refuses to divinise the angels; the refusal is consistent across the sūrah and the Qur'an. The refusal is not a demotion of the angels; the angels remain honoured servants. The refusal is a clarification of the category. The angels are in the category of servants; they are not in the category of divinity. The verse is therefore performing a clarification of the category by stating what the consequences would be of confusing the categories.

**The Verse As a Warning to the Audience**

The verse is also a warning to the audience. The audience has been treating the angels as partners or intercessors who share in the divine nature. The verse is saying: this is the kind of claim that gets punished with Hell. The audience is being asked to recognise that the framework in which they have been operating is the framework that produces this punishment. The audience is being asked to abandon the framework before the framework abandons them to its consequences.

The warning is not arbitrary. The audience had been making claims about the angels that the angels themselves, if they were to make the same claims, would be punished for making. The audience is therefore in the position of encouraging in the angels what the angels would be punished for doing. The position is the position of the wrongdoer. The wrongdoer is the one who has put things in the wrong place. The audience has put the angels in the wrong place; the putting is the wrong; the wrong requires correction.

**A Closing of the Polemic Against Polytheism**

The verse closes the polemic against polytheism begun at 21:19. The polemic has removed one support after another from the polytheistic framework. The polytheists had imagined the angels as divine partners; the verses have shown that the angels are honoured servants who do not speak before God, whose intercession is bounded, who tremble in awe, and who would be punished with Hell if they were to claim divinity. The framework has been dismantled piece by piece. The verse at 21:29 is the final dismantling. The framework has been removed; the audience is invited to recognise what they have been left with.

What they have been left with is the oneness of God, the honoured status of the angels as servants, and the moral order in which claims of divinity are punished. The audience is invited to live within the oneness, to honour the servants, and to recognise the moral order. The invitation is the work the verses have been doing since 21:19. The work is complete in this verse; the verses that follow will turn to other themes.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:30

> Do the disbelievers not realize that the heavens and earth were ˹once˺ one mass then We split them apart? And We created from water every living thing. Will they not then believe

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Question About Whether the Audience Has Noticed**

The verse opens with a question that contains its own accusation. *Awa lam yara alladhīna kafarū* — have the disbelievers not seen, not noticed, not realised? The verb *raʾā* — to see, to notice, to perceive — is the verb of empirical observation. The verse is asking whether the audience has failed to notice something that should have been obvious. The verse is not asking whether the audience has heard the news; the verse is asking whether the audience has looked at the sky.

The verse's use of the verb of sight is theologically significant. The audience is being asked to consider something that is available to the eye. The data is not hidden in a book that the audience cannot read; the data is in the heavens and the earth. The audience has access to the data every moment; the audience has been failing to use the data. The failure is not the failure of those who cannot see; it is the failure of those who refuse to see what they can see.

**The Single Mass That Was Split Apart**

The verse specifies what the audience should have noticed. *An al-samāwāti wa al-arḍa kānatā ratqan fa-fataqnāhumā* — the heavens and the earth were a single mass, then We split them apart. The verb *ratq* names a closed, sealed, joined state; *fataqa* names the act of splitting open. The verse is describing an original state of unity and a subsequent act of separation. The description corresponds to one of the most theologically significant images in the Qur'anic cosmology: the heavens and the earth were joined, and God split them apart.

This image is one of the few Qur'anic cosmological descriptions that admits of empirical investigation. Modern cosmology describes an original state of extreme density and a subsequent expansion; the verse describes an original state of unity and a subsequent splitting. The two descriptions are not identical in their language; they are close enough that the verse can be read as pointing toward what cosmology has described. The reading is not the verse's primary meaning; the verse is primarily a theological claim about God's act of creation. The reading is, however, a contemporary extension that the verse can support.

**"And We Created from Water Every Living Thing"**

The second half of the verse names another feature of creation: *wa khalaqnā min al-māʾi kulla shayʾin ḥayyin* — and We created from water every living thing. The verse is making a biological claim about the dependence of life on water. The claim is empirically verifiable; the verse is therefore making another empirical observation that the audience should have noticed. Every living thing requires water; the verse names water as the substance from which life is created.

This is one of the most theologically rich empirical observations in the Qur'an. The verse is saying that water is the common substance of life; the observation is correct; the observation points to a creator who chose water as the basis of biological existence. The audience is being asked to recognise the choice and the chooser. The recognition is the work the verse is asking for; the failure to recognise is the failure the verse is accusing.

**"Will They Not Then Believe?"**

The verse ends with *afalā yuʾminūn* — will they not then believe? The closing question is the conclusion of the empirical argument. The audience has the data; the data points to a creator; the creator has acted in specific ways; the audience is asked to draw the conclusion. The conclusion is belief. The verse is not asking the audience to believe on the basis of revelation alone; the verse is asking the audience to believe on the basis of what the audience can see.

The closing question is rhetorical, but the rhetorical force is not against the audience's intelligence. The verse is accusing the audience of having refused to use their intelligence on what the intelligence has access to. The intelligence has access to the original state of the heavens and the earth and to the role of water in biological existence; the audience has refused to draw the conclusion. The refusal is the accusation. The audience is being asked to consider what the refusal has cost.

**The Verse As Empirical Theology**

The verse is one of the most explicit Qur'anic examples of empirical theology. The verse does not ask the audience to believe on the basis of revelation alone; the verse asks the audience to look at the world and to infer the conclusion. The conclusion is belief; the inference is open to anyone who will do it; the refusal to do it is the disbelief the verse is addressing.

This is one of the most distinctive features of the Qur'anic approach to its audience. The Qur'an does not require the audience to abandon reason; the Qur'an requires the audience to use reason. The verses that ask the audience to look at the world are inviting the audience to do exactly what reason does. The refusal to do it is what reason would call unreasonable; the verse is accusing the audience of failing to do the reasoning that is available.

**The Theological Implications of the Single Mass**

The verse's description of an original state of unity followed by splitting has deep theological implications. The original state is described as a single mass; the single mass is what existed before the differentiation of the heavens and the earth. The verse is therefore saying that the present order — with the heavens above and the earth below, with the celestial bodies in their orbits and the terrestrial bodies on their surface — is the result of an act of God. The act is not ongoing; the act is described as a past event that brought the present order into being.

This is the Qur'anic version of the doctrine of creation out of nothing. The single mass was the original state; the act of God produced the differentiation. The differentiation required an act of God; the act is the source of the differentiation. The present order is the result of the act; the act is not necessary to the present order; the present order is contingent on the act. The verse is therefore a refusal of any account of the present order as self-sustaining or self-explanatory. The present order requires the act; the act is what produced it.

**The Verse's Connection to the Cosmological Argument**

The verse is one of the foundations of the cosmological argument in Islamic theology. The argument runs: the heavens and the earth are here; they were not always in their present form; the change from the original state to the present state required an act; the act is the act of God. The verse is the explicit Qur'anic form of this argument. The argument is offered not as a philosopher's demonstration but as an invitation to see what the audience can see. The seeing is the foundation of the argument; the argument is the foundation of the belief the verse is asking for.

The verse's empirical basis is what makes it so durable. The argument does not depend on any particular philosophical framework; the argument depends on what the audience can see. The audience that sees can draw the conclusion; the audience that refuses to see is the audience the verse is addressing. The refusal is not the failure of an argument; the refusal is the failure to look.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:31

> And We have placed firm mountains upon the earth so it does not shake with them, and made in it broad pathways so they may find their way

**Expanded Commentary**

**Mountains That Stabilise What Could Not Hold Itself**

The verse extends the cosmological argument begun at 21:30. The heavens and the earth were split apart (21:30); now the earth has been given stabilising mountains and the pathways that make it habitable. The verse names a specific feature of the earth's design: *rawāsiya* — firm, fixed, anchored mountains — that prevent the earth from shaking its inhabitants. The verb *an taḥīfa* — to shake, to totter, to be in motion — names a state the verse says the earth is protected from by the mountains.

The image is not a poetic flourish; it is a geophysical claim. The earth is described as needing anchors. The mountains are the anchors. Without the anchors, the earth would shake; with the anchors, the earth is stable. The Qur'an returns to this image at 16:15, 31:10, and elsewhere, treating the mountains as a structural feature of the earth's design that allows life to continue. The image is consistent with what geology describes: the continents ride on tectonic plates whose behaviour is shaped in part by the mountainous regions along their margins.

**The Image of a Ship on the Sea**

The commentators, including al-Rāzī, preserve an image in which the earth floats upon water and would move about as if it were a ship at sea, were it not for the firm mountains anchoring it. The image is cosmological in the premodern sense, but it expresses something that survives the change of cosmological framework: the earth is held in place by features of its design, and those features are part of what makes life possible. The mountains are not accidents of geology; they are the stabilising elements without which the surface of the earth would not be a fit place for sustained life.

This is one of the recurring features of the Qur'anic cosmology: the natural features are not just the result of physical processes; they are the result of a design that has the support of life as one of its purposes. The mountains are part of that design. The earth was created for life; the earth's surface needs stabilising features; the stabilising features are provided. The audience is invited to recognise that what they take for granted — the stability of the ground beneath them — is part of a designed order.

**The Broad Pathways Between the Mountains**

The verse names a second feature of the design: *subulan fawājiḥ* — broad pathways, wide tracts. The Arabic *fijāj* — wide, broad, open — names the kind of pathway that allows passage between the mountains. The verse says that broad pathways have been made so that people may be guided. The verb *yahtadūn* — they may be guided, they may find their way — names both physical and spiritual guidance. The pathways are physically navigable; the navigability is also a sign that points beyond itself.

The commentators have noted that the guidance intended here includes both kinds. The pathways make physical movement possible; the design that made the pathways points to a designer. The audience that uses the pathways is invited to recognise that the design of the pathways is part of the divine economy. The guidance is not just the guidance of someone who has lost their way; it is the guidance of someone who has begun to recognise that the order they are moving through has a source.

**The Spiritual Reading**

The Sufi commentators, including al-ʿAjlūnī, offer a reading in which the natural features of the verse become metaphors for the architecture of the soul. The stabilising mountains are understood as the intelligence that anchors the inner life; the broad pathways are understood as the spiritual movements that allow the soul to find its way toward God; the entire arrangement is read as a description of an inner order that mirrors the outer one. The reading is not a replacement for the cosmological one; it is an extension. The verse can be read at both scales; the reading at both scales supports the same conclusion.

The reading at the interior scale is consistent with what the verse asks of the audience. The audience is invited to recognise that what holds the inner life together is part of the divine economy. The interior stabilising features are not accidents; they are part of the design. The audience is being asked to recognise the design at the interior scale just as the audience can recognise the design at the exterior scale. The two recognitions are part of one work.

**A Verse That Continues the Empirical Argument**

The verse continues the empirical argument begun at 21:30. The verse asks the audience to look at the world and to draw the conclusion. The mountains are there; the pathways are there; the design is observable. The verse is asking the audience to integrate the observation with the inference. The integration is the substance of belief. The refusal to integrate is the substance of the disbelief the verse is addressing.

The empirical argument is cumulative. 21:30 named the splitting of the heavens and the earth and the dependence of life on water; 21:31 names the stabilising features of the earth's surface and the pathways that make the surface habitable. The verses are not isolated observations; they are cumulative evidence for a designed order. The design is the evidence; the designer is the inference; the inference is the substance of the belief the verses are asking for.

**A Verse That Asks for Gratitude**

The verse is also a call to gratitude. The stabilising features of the earth and the pathways that make it habitable are part of what the audience takes for granted. The verse is asking the audience to recognise that the taken-for-granted is not accidental. The audience that recognises the design is being invited to respond with gratitude. The gratitude is the appropriate response to a gift; the gift is the entire designed order. The audience is being asked to recognise the gift and to respond accordingly.

This is one of the recurring Qur'anic moves. The empirical observation is offered; the inference is drawn; the audience is asked to respond. The response is gratitude; the gratitude is the form of worship that the verse at 21:25 has already demanded. The empirical verses and the demanding verses are not separate; they are part of the same argument. The argument is the unity of the designed order; the appropriate response to the unity is the worship of the designer.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:32

> And We have made the sky a well-protected canopy, still they turn away from its signs

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sky As a Ceiling, Protected**

The verse offers a single, compact image. *Wa jaʿalnā al-samā'a saqfan maṣūnān* — and We made the sky a protected ceiling. The verb *jaʿala* — to make — is the same verb used throughout the cosmological passage. The sky is one of the things that has been made. The verse names what the sky has been made into: a ceiling, a roof, a canopy. The image is architectural. The sky is the upper boundary of the inhabited world; the sky covers the earth; the sky is the canopy under which life is possible.

The Arabic *maṣūn* — protected, preserved, guarded — names a specific feature of the canopy. The canopy is not just a covering; it is a protected covering. The commentators, including al-Rāzī, note that the protection is from the satans; the heavens are protected from demonic incursion. Al-Ṭabarī reads the protection as physical: the sky is held in place, prevented from falling down to the earth. The two readings are not exclusive; the verse can be describing both kinds of protection. The canopy protects what is below it from what is above; the protection is both physical and moral.

**"Still They Turn Away from Its Signs"**

The verse's second half records the audience's response. *Wa hum ʿan āyātihā muʿriḍūn* — and they, from its signs, turn away. The signs of the sky include the sun, the moon, the stars, the alternation of night and day, the rain, the colour of the sky at different times. The verse says the audience turns away from these signs. The turning away is a recurring theme in the sūrah (21:1, 21:24). The audience has refused to look at what is in front of them; the audience continues to refuse.

The verse's polemic is silent but precise. The signs are there; the signs are observable; the audience is invited to look. The audience has chosen not to look. The choosing is the verse's accusation. The audience is not unable to see; the audience has decided that the seeing will not count. The decision is the refusal the verse is naming.

**The Image of a Canopy Is Both Literal and Architectural**

The Arabic *saqf* — ceiling, roof — is an architectural word. The sky is being described in the language of the building arts. The image is consistent with the rest of the Qur'anic cosmology: the sky is above; the earth is below; the design is that of a covered space in which life is possible. The image is not mythological; the image is the description of what the inhabitant of the earth sees when the inhabitant looks up. The inhabitant sees a covering. The question is whether the covering is recognised as part of a design.

The architectural image is also theologically significant. A ceiling is something that holds back what is above from what is below; a ceiling is a barrier; a ceiling is a boundary. The verse is saying that the sky is a boundary that has been designed for the protection of what is below it. The boundary is part of the design; the design has the inhabitants of the earth in view; the inhabitants are being asked to recognise what the design has done for them.

**The Sky As Object of Refusal**

The verse names the sky as the object of refusal. The audience has been turning away from the signs of the sky. The signs are visible; the refusal is volitional. The verse is therefore asking the audience to consider what the turning away has cost. The sky has been a canopy; the canopy has protected the audience; the audience has refused to recognise the protection. The refusal is the loss. The audience has been given a gift and has declined to receive it.

This is one of the most quietly devastating features of the Qur'anic polemic. The verses do not threaten the audience with deprivation; the verses point out that the audience has been given everything and has refused to recognise what it has been given. The refusal is the deprivation. The audience has not been denied the sky; the audience has been denied the recognition of the sky. The denial is the loss; the loss is the audience's own doing.

**A Verse That Connects to the Broader Cosmological Argument**

The verse is one in a sequence that begins at 21:30 and continues through 21:33. The verses are presenting a cumulative case for the designed order of the world. The heavens and the earth were split apart (21:30); the earth has stabilising mountains and pathways (21:31); the sky is a protected canopy (21:32); the night, day, sun, and moon move in their orbits (21:33). The verses are not isolated claims; they are a cumulative demonstration. The demonstration is what the audience has been asked to look at.

The cumulative demonstration is the empirical foundation of the theological claim. The theological claim is that there is a designer; the empirical foundation is the designed order of the world. The verses are inviting the audience to integrate the observation with the inference. The integration is the substance of belief. The refusal to integrate is the substance of the disbelief the verses are addressing.

**The Closing Refusal That Closes the Verse**

The verse ends with the audience's refusal. The closing word is *muʿriḍūn* — they turn away. The word closes the verse as a verdict. The verse has described the sky as a protected canopy; the verdict on the audience is that they turn away from it. The turning away is the verdict on the audience; the verdict is what the audience has chosen. The verse is asking the audience to consider what it would mean to stop turning away. The stopping would be the beginning of the recognition. The recognition is what the verse is offering.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:33

> And He is the One Who created the day and the night, the sun and the moon—each travelling in an orbit

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Four Features Named in the Verse**

The verse names four of the most fundamental features of the observable cosmos. *Al-layla wa al-nahāra* — the night and the day — name the alternation that organises life on earth. *Al-shamsa wa al-qamara* — the sun and the moon — name the two principal celestial bodies whose interaction produces the alternation and the months. The four together cover the temporal and the luminous, the regular and the recurrent, the visible and the measurable. The verse is naming what the audience lives inside.

Each of the four is named with a definite article. The definite article in Arabic can mark either definiteness in the sense of being known, or it can mark the totality of the species. The verse's use of the definite article covers both senses. The night and the day are the night and the day that the audience experiences; the sun and the moon are the sun and the moon that the audience has always seen. The verse is naming what is most familiar in order to ask what the audience has made of the familiarity.

**"Each Travelling in an Orbit"**

The verse specifies a feature of the celestial bodies: *kullun fī falakin yasbāḥūn* — each swimming, gliding, travelling in an orbit. The verb *sabaḥa* names a kind of motion that is steady and continuous; the noun *falak* names the path or sphere of the motion. The verse is describing the celestial bodies as moving along trajectories that are not random and not arbitrary. The motion is regular; the regularity is the signature of design.

The commentators note that the verb *sabaḥa* in classical Arabic can also mean to swim. The image of swimming is appropriate to a fluid medium; the Qur'an is here using an image from the natural world to describe motion that the audience cannot directly observe. The audience does not see the sun swimming through the heavens; the audience sees the sun rise and set. The verse is telling the audience that the rising and the setting are the visible expression of a continuous motion through a path. The path is the *falak*.

**The Cosmology the Verse Implies**

The verse is one of the most theologically significant cosmological statements in the Qur'an. The verse is saying that the celestial bodies are in motion; the motion is along trajectories; the trajectories are part of the design. The verse is therefore implying that the cosmos is not static, that the celestial bodies are not fixed in place, and that the order of the heavens is dynamic rather than stationary. The dynamic order is the order the verse is naming.

The commentators have noted that premodern Islamic cosmology often pictured the heavenly bodies as moving within spheres of an ethereal or subtle substance. The verse does not require this picture; the verse requires only that the motion is along trajectories. Some commentators, such as al-Ḍaḥḥāk, have read the verse more simply as saying that the celestial bodies move along their trajectories through the heavens. The reading is consistent with what the verse says; the verse does not require a specific cosmological model. The verse requires the design; the design is what the verse is asking the audience to recognise.

**A Recognition That Requires No Telescope**

The verse's empirical claim does not require any instrument beyond the eye. The alternation of night and day is observable without equipment; the sun and the moon are observable without equipment; the trajectories of the sun and the moon are inferable from observations over time. The verse is asking the audience to make observations the audience already makes, and to draw the inference the observations support. The inference is that the celestial bodies are in designed motion; the design is the signature of a designer; the designer is the One whose design the audience has been inhabiting.

This is one of the most distinctive features of the Qur'anic empirical argument. The argument does not require the audience to acquire new data; the argument requires the audience to interpret the data the audience already has. The interpretation is the work the verse is asking for; the work is the substance of the belief the verse is calling for. The audience that interprets the data well has begun the work; the audience that refuses to interpret has refused the verse.

**The Verse As a Recurrence of an Earlier Pattern**

The verse's pairing of night and day, sun and moon, is one of the most common Qur'anic formations. The same combination appears at 36:40, 39:5, 71:16, and other places. The recurrence is not redundancy; it is the consistent application of a method. The method is to ask the audience to look at what is most familiar and to recognise the design in the familiarity. The audience has been looking at the night and the day for an entire life; the verse is asking the audience to see the design in what they have been looking at.

This is one of the most demanding features of the Qur'anic empirical argument. The audience cannot escape the data; the data is what the audience lives inside. The argument is therefore not one the audience can postpone; the argument is one the audience is inside of at every moment. The argument is also one the audience cannot avoid by ignoring it; the ignoring is the very refusal the verse is naming. The audience has been given the data; the audience has been given the inference; the audience is being asked to integrate the two.

**What "Orbit" Specifies and What It Does Not**

The verse says *falak* — orbit, sphere, trajectory. The Arabic word does not specify the shape of the trajectory; the word specifies only that there is a trajectory. The verse is therefore not committing to any particular cosmological model; the verse is committing only to the fact of ordered motion. The ordered motion is what the verse is naming; the specific shape of the orbits is left to human investigation.

This restraint is consistent with the broader Qur'anic approach to cosmology. The Qur'an names the order; the Qur'an does not specify the mechanism. The mechanism is for human beings to investigate; the order is for human beings to recognise. The investigation is a form of human responsibility; the recognition is the form of human response. The verse is asking for the response and leaving the investigation to the responsible labour of the human community. The labour and the response are both parts of what the verse is asking for.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:34

> We have not granted immortality to any human before you ˹O Prophet˺: so if you die, will they live forever

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Prophet's Mortality As a Sign**

The verse makes a structural claim: *We have not granted immortality to any human being before you*. The Arabic *khuld* — immortality, perpetuity, everlastingness — is the same word used elsewhere for the state of the inhabitants of Paradise or Hellfire. What 21:34 sets out is that the Prophet, like every human being before him, has not been given the kind of perpetuity that would exempt him from death. The Prophet is mortal. The mortality is not a deficiency; it is the human condition.

The verse functions as a sign for the audience. The audience had hoped, or feared, that the Prophet's death would either vindicate or invalidate the message. What the verse is preparing the audience for is an event that will, in fact, occur — the death of the Prophet. The preparation is part of the verse's task; the task is to remove the symbolic weight the audience had loaded onto the Prophet's death.

**Question That Reframes the Audience's Hope**

The second half of the verse asks the rhetorical question: *fa-in mita fa-hum al-khālidūn* — if you die, will they abide forever? The question is addressed to the Prophet, but its target is the audience. The audience had imagined that the Prophet's death would vindicate their refusal; what the question reveals is that the audience's vindication is not what they imagine. If the Prophet dies, the audience does not thereby live forever. The same mortality that will take the Prophet will take the audience. The death of the messenger does not exempt those who refused the message.

The question's force is therefore not in the question itself but in the answer the question assumes. The answer is no. The audience will not live forever. The audience will die. The death of the Prophet is the death of one mortal among mortals; the death of the audience will be the death of others. The two deaths are part of the same human condition; neither death has the symbolic weight the audience had hoped to load onto it.

**The Word "Khuld" As a Theological Category**

The Arabic *khuld* — perpetuity, immortality — is one of the Qur'an's most theologically loaded words. The same word is used for the state of the inhabitants of Paradise (3:15, 4:57, 5:119) and the inhabitants of Hell (7:36, 43:74). The verse's use of *khuld* for the kind of life the Prophet has not been given is therefore precise: the Prophet has not been given the eschatological perpetuity that belongs to the next world; the Prophet has been given the mortality that belongs to this world. The two are different orders; the verse is making the distinction explicit.

What the verse clarifies is what the Prophet is and is not. The Prophet is not a divine being exempted from death; the Prophet is a human being who has been given a message. The message is not dependent on the Prophet's perpetual life; the message is dependent on the Prophet's transmission. The transmission has occurred; the transmission does not require the perpetuity of the transmitter. The perpetuity belongs to the message; the mortality belongs to the messenger. The distinction is the substance of the verse.

**Why Some Believed the Prophet Would Not Die**

The commentators record that some of the audience had believed that the Prophet would not die; the verse is addressed to that belief. The belief is part of the audience's resistance to the message; if the messenger does not die, the message can be postponed indefinitely; if the messenger dies, the postponement is over. The postponement is what the verse is addressing; the postponement is based on a misconception about the nature of the messenger.

The commentators also record that the verse was revealed because the disbelievers expressed delight at the prospect of the Prophet's misfortune and death. The delight was the audience's way of asserting that the message would end with the messenger. What the verse refuses is precisely that assertion. The message will not end with the messenger; the message will continue after the messenger; the audience's delight is therefore premature. The audience that delights in the prospect of the Prophet's death is the audience that does not yet understand what the Prophet's death will mean.

**Junayd's Spiritual Reading**

The Sufi commentator Junayd offered a striking reading: *Whosoever is between the two vanishings is now vanishing*. The two vanishings are the state of nonexistence that precedes earthly life and the death at the end of earthly life. Between them, the human being is in a constant state of dying. The reading extends the verse into the interior life. The interior life is a constant process of letting go of what has been, in anticipation of what is to come. The letting go is the form the vanishing takes. The human being who has understood this has begun to live in the way the verse is asking for. The human being who has not yet understood is still trying to make the world a permanent residence. The residence is not permanent; the residence is a passage; the passage is the substance of the human life.

**The Verse As a Refusal of Two Errors**

The verse refuses two errors at once. The first is the error of treating the Prophet as a divine being exempted from death. The second is the error of imagining that the audience itself is exempt from death. What the verse is saying is that the Prophet will die; the audience will die; the death is the same kind for both. The framework in which the Prophet's death would vindicate the audience is therefore refused, as is the framework in which the audience's life would outlast the Prophet. Both frameworks are based on misconceptions; both are being corrected.

The correction is part of the sūrah's larger polemic. The audience had been trying to find ways to defer the consequences of their refusal. What the verse makes explicit is that there is no deferment. The Prophet will die; the audience will die; the consequences of refusal will arrive; the only variable is whether the audience will respond to the message before the death that is coming for both. The variable is the variable the verse is asking the audience to consider.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:35

> Every soul will taste death. And We test you ˹O humanity˺ with good and evil as a trial, then to Us you will ˹all˺ be returned

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Universality of Death**

The verse opens with one of the Qur'an's most insistent universal claims. *Kullu nafsin dhāʾiqatu al-mawti* — every soul will taste death. The word *kull* — every — is the words's most emphatic universal. The verb *dhāqa* — to taste — names an experiential encounter; the noun *al-mawt* — death — names the encounter's content. The verse is saying that every soul, without exception, will experience death. The universality is not qualified by rank, by piety, by prophetic office, by divine election. Every soul will die.

This is one of the Qur'an's most theologically significant universals. The verse at 21:34 said that no human being had been granted immortality; this verse extends the same observation to every soul. The audience cannot exempt itself from the observation; the audience is part of the universality. The death that will take the Prophet will take the audience; the death will take the believers; the death will take the disbelievers. The universality is total.

**"Taste" — The Verb of Limited Experience**

The verb *dhāqa* — to taste — is not the verb of total experience. To taste is to encounter a small portion; to taste is to take a sample; to taste is to encounter what is sufficient to know what is being encountered. The verse uses the verb of limited experience deliberately. The death that is being named is not the whole of what comes after life; the death is the threshold of what comes after life. The tasting is at the threshold; the crossing is beyond the verse.

The use of *dhāqa* is also a way of saying that death is not annihilation. To taste is to encounter; the encounter implies that what is being encountered has a content. The content is the discontinuity between this life and whatever comes after. Death is the discontinuity; the verse is naming the discontinuity by naming the encounter. The encounter is universal; the discontinuity is what every soul experiences.

**The Test by Good and Evil**

The verse continues: *wa nabluwukum bi-l-sharri wa al-khayri fitnatan* — and We test you with evil and good as a trial. The verb *balā* — to test, to try, to put to proof — names the activity of examining how the tested will respond. The means of the test are named in two opposing pairs: *al-sharr* — evil, harm, difficulty — and *al-khayr* — good, benefit, ease. The verse is saying that both the harmful and the beneficial are means by which God tests the human being.

This is one of the most theologically demanding features of the Qur'anic worldview. The test is not confined to what the human being would consider bad; the test includes what the human being would consider good. Al-Ṭabarī notes that the test by evil is whether the human being will be patient; al-Rāzī adds that the test by good is whether the human being will be grateful. Both responses are forms of worship; both are being tested by the Qur'anic revelation. The test is not, in other words, the bad things that happen to bad people; the test is the entire range of human experience, evaluated by how the human being responds.

**"Then to Us You Will Be Returned"**

The verse closes with *wa ilaynā turjaʿūn* — then to Us you will be returned. The verb *rājaʿa* — to return — names the eventual return of every soul to God. The return is the consequence of the test; the return is also the completion of the testing. The verse is therefore describing a complete arc: every soul will taste death; every soul is being tested in the interval before death; every soul will be returned to God for the assessment. The arc is the substance of the moral order the verse is describing.

The venue of the return is not specified in this verse; the venue is described in 21:47, where the scales of justice are set up. The scales will weigh what was done in the testing; the return will be the moment when the testing is finally evaluated. The verse at 21:35 is the principle of which 21:47 is the application. The principle is that the testing has a purpose; the purpose is the return; the return is the moment when the testing is resolved.

**A Verse That Frames the Human Situation**

The verse is one of the Qur'an's most concise frames for the human situation. Every soul will die; every soul is being tested; every soul will return. The three are the three acts of the human drama. The first act is the universal condition; the second act is the task; the third act is the resolution. The frame is consistent across the Qur'an; the verses that describe the frame are not in dispute with each other; the frame is one.

The verse is asking the audience to consider which act they are in. Every audience member is in the second act. The first act has already occurred; the third act has not yet occurred. The second act is the only one in which the audience can act. The verse is asking the audience to act well in the second act; the acting well is the substance of the response the verse is calling for.

**The Polemic Edge of the Universality**

The universality is also a polemic. What the audience had imagined is that the audience itself is at the centre of its own world; the verse is reminding the audience that the audience is one among the souls that will taste death. In the audience's own imagining, prosperity was a sign of divine favour; the verse is reminding the audience that prosperity is part of the test, not a verdict on the test. The audience had imagined that the message could be deferred until later; the verse is reminding the audience that the deferment is not guaranteed. The audience had imagined many things; the verse is collapsing all of them into the single fact that the audience will die and will be returned.

This collapse is one of the verse's most powerful rhetorical moves. The audience had been constructing a world in which the deferred, the prosperous, and the postponed could all coexist. The verse is saying: the world you have constructed is not the world you live in. The world you live in is one in which you will die and will be returned. The verse is not asking the audience to abandon its world in favour of another; the verse is asking the audience to recognise the world it actually lives in.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:36

> When the disbelievers see you ˹O Prophet˺, they only make fun of you, ˹saying,˺ “Is this the one who speaks ˹ill˺ of your gods?” while they disbelieve at the mention of the Most Compassionate

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Scene at the Encounter**

The verse records a recurring scene: the Prophet encounters the disbelievers; the disbelievers mock him. *Wa idhā raʾāka alladhīna kafarū* — when those who disbelieve see you. The verb *raʾā* — to see — names the visual encounter. The scene is not hypothetical; the verse is reporting what happens when the audience sees the Prophet. The reporting is part of the verse's task. The audience is being shown itself as the audience appears when it sees the Prophet.

The verse's use of the verb *raʾā* is also polemical. The audience has been introduced to a cosmological argument (21:30–33) and to the universal fact of mortality (21:34–35). The audience has been asked to look at the world and to recognise the design. Now the audience is shown in the act of looking at the Prophet and of mocking. The two acts of looking are juxtaposed. The audience can look at the world and recognise the design; the audience can look at the Prophet and produce mockery. The contrast is the verse's polemic.

**"Is This the One Who Speaks Ill of Your Gods?"**

The audience's mock-question is recorded. *A-hādha al-ladhī yadhkuru ālihatakum* — is this the one who mentions your gods? The verb *yadhkuru* — to mention, to remember, to bring to mind — is the same root as *dhikr* — remembrance, reminder. The verb can be used in either a positive or a negative sense. The audience uses it in the negative sense: the Prophet has been mentioning their gods in a way that the audience considers insulting. The mocking question is built on the implicit claim that the Prophet has been insulting what the audience holds sacred.

The verse does not directly dispute the audience's framing of the Prophet's speech. The verse does not say the Prophet has been praising or insulting; the verse says that the audience has been construing the Prophet's speech as insulting. The construal is part of the mocking. The mocking is not just a reaction to what the Prophet has said; the mocking is a posture the audience adopts toward the Prophet. The posture is what the verse is naming.

**"While They Disbelieve at the Mention of the Most Compassionate"**

The verse adds a sharp corrective: *wa hum bi-dhikri al-Raḥmāni hum kāfirūn* — and they, at the mention of the Most Compassionate, are disbelievers. The verse is reframing the mockery. The audience had been mocking the Prophet for mentioning their gods; the verse is reminding the audience that the audience, in turn, disbelieves at the mention of the Most Compassionate. The reciprocal structure is deliberate. The audience mocks what the Prophet says about their gods; the audience disbelieves what the Prophet says about the Most Compassionate. The two refusals are parallel; both are refusals of the same kind.

The use of *al-Raḥmān* — the Most Compassionate — is significant. The verse uses the most beautiful name of God, the name that emphasises mercy. The audience disbelieves at the mention of mercy. The verse is asking the audience to consider the contrast: the audience is sensitive about being mocked about its gods, but the audience mocks the mention of the Most Compassionate. The asymmetry is the verse's polemical force.

**The Reciprocity of Mockery and Disbelief**

The verse is one of the most explicit Qur'anic formulations of the reciprocity of refusal. The audience refuses the message; the audience mocks the messenger; the messenger continues to deliver the message; the audience continues to mock. The verse breaks into this reciprocity to point out that the audience's mocking is not just a response to the Prophet; it is a response to the Most Compassionate. The mocking is therefore not a private behaviour of the audience; the mocking is a public verdict on the Most Compassionate.

The verse is also a warning. The audience that disbelieves at the mention of the Most Compassionate is the audience that has refused the most basic orientation of the religious life. The orientation is the orientation toward mercy; the orientation is what the Most Compassionate has offered; the audience has refused. The refusal is the substance of the disbelief the verse is naming. The naming is not abstract; the naming is the diagnosis of what the audience has been doing every time it has mocked the Prophet.

**A Verse That Connects to the Earlier Polemic**

The verse connects back to the sūrah's opening polemic. In 21:5, the audience had accused the Prophet of being a poet, a fabricator, a producer of confused dreams. In 21:36, the audience is shown in the act of mocking the Prophet for what he says about their gods. The accusations and the mocking are part of the same posture. The posture is the audience's settled disposition toward the message; the disposition is what the verses have been describing from the opening of the sūrah.

The verse is therefore a continuation of the sūrah's diagnostic project. The sūrah is not just arguing with the audience; the sūrah is describing the audience to itself. The audience is being shown what it looks like from the outside; the audience is being shown what its behaviour means in the larger moral order the sūrah has been describing. The showing is the work of the verse. The audience is invited to recognise what it has been doing and to consider whether the doing is what the audience wants to be remembered for.

**The Most Compassionate As the Object of Disbelief**

The verse's specification that the audience disbelieves at the mention of the Most Compassionate is one of the most theologically pointed moments in the sūrah. The name *al-Raḥmān* emphasises the divine mercy; the divine mercy is what the audience is rejecting. The rejection is the worst kind of rejection, because the rejection is of what is most worthy of acceptance. The audience is not just rejecting a claim; the audience is rejecting the divine mercy itself. The rejection is therefore not just an intellectual failure; the rejection is a moral failure of the deepest kind.

The verse is asking the audience to consider what it would mean to receive the mention of the Most Compassionate with belief instead of disbelief. The reception would be the substance of the response the verse is calling for. The calling is what the verse has been doing since the opening of the sūrah. The calling is consistent; the response the verse is calling for is the same response the verse has been calling for throughout. The verse is asking for belief at the mention of the Most Compassionate. The asking is the work of the verse; the response is the audience's.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:37

> Humankind is made of haste. I will soon show you My signs, so do not ask Me to hasten them

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Composition of the Human Being**

The verse makes a claim about the composition of the human being. *Al-insānu khuliqa min ʿajal* — the human being was created of haste, from haste. The Arabic *ʿajal* — haste, hurry, speed — names a quality of the human constitution. The verse is saying that the human being is constituted in such a way that haste is part of what the human being is. The haste is not a moral failing; the haste is a feature of the human makeup.

The commentators have read the verse in several ways. Al-Qurṭubī notes that the verse is similar to 30:54 — *God is He Who created you from weakness* — and 4:28 — *Man was created weak*. The verses are part of a Qur'anic pattern that describes the human being as created with a particular constitutional feature that the human being must learn to manage. The management is part of the moral task. The Qur'an does not deny the constitutional feature; the Qur'an names it so that the management can begin.

**"I Will Soon Show You My Signs"**
