The verse continues with a divine promise: *saʾurīkum āyātī* — I will soon show you My signs. The verb *arā* — to show, to cause to see — names an act of making visible. The divine act of showing is the substance of the verse's promise. The audience will be shown the divine signs; the showing is imminent. The imminence is itself part of the address: the audience does not need to wait long.

The Arabic *sawfa* — soon, before long — marks a near future. The verse is saying that the signs will be shown in the near future, not in some remote horizon. The nearness is part of the address's force. The audience had been asking for signs (21:5); the audience is now being told that signs will be shown in the near future. The audience does not need to demand the signs; the signs are already on the way. The demanding is therefore unnecessary.

**"So Do Not Ask Me to Hasten Them"**

The verse closes with *fa-lā tastaʿjilūnī* — so do not ask Me to hasten them. The verb *istaʿjala* — to seek to hasten, to demand that something come sooner — names a specific kind of demand. The audience had been demanding that the punishment arrive; the audience is now being told not to make that demand. The demand is part of the audience's haste; the haste is part of the audience's constitution; the constitution is what the verse is naming at the opening.

The verse is therefore tightly constructed. The opening names the constitutional feature; the middle promises the imminence of the signs; the closing warns against the demand for hastening. The three parts are connected. The constitutional feature is the source of the demand; the demand is a misuse of the constitutional feature; the misuse is what the verse is correcting. The correction is not a denial that the signs will come; the correction is a redirection of how the audience should wait for them.

**Why Hastening Is Forbidden**

The audience might wonder why the demand for hastening is forbidden if the signs are coming soon. The reason is that the demand expresses a particular posture toward the divine will. The audience that demands hastening is treating the signs as a punishment the audience has authority to schedule. The audience is therefore taking a position of authority over the divine schedule. The position is the position of those who do not recognise that the schedule belongs to God. The recognition is what the verse is asking for.

This is also why the demand is forbidden rather than simply futile. A futile demand would not need to be forbidden; a forbidden demand is a demand that is in itself a moral failure. The audience's demand for hastening is a failure because the demand is an expression of the audience's posture of refusal. The audience wants the punishment to come quickly so that the audience can see whether the messenger is right; the audience's wanting the verification is itself a kind of testing of God. The testing is the failure. The verse is forbidding the testing.

**The Haste That Is Permitted and the Haste That Is Not**

The verse makes a distinction the commentators have noted. The fact that the human being is constituted of haste is not itself a sin; the Qur'an is describing what the human being is. The sin is the misuse of the haste; the sin is the demand that God conform to the human being's timing. The verse is therefore not asking the audience to deny the haste; the verse is asking the audience to recognise the haste and to refrain from acting on it in the wrong direction. The direction is what the verse is specifying.

This is one of the most distinctive features of the Qur'anic moral psychology. The Qur'an does not ask the human being to be other than what the human being is; the Qur'an asks the human being to recognise what the human being is and to direct it appropriately. The recognition is part of the moral task; the directing is the rest of the task. The verse is asking for the recognition and the directing. The asking is the work of the verse.

**The Signs That Will Be Shown**

The verse's promise — *I will soon show you My signs* — does not specify which signs. The commentators have noted that the verse could refer to signs in this world, signs in the eschatological sense, or both. The verse does not need to specify; the verse is asking for a posture of expectant waiting, not a precise calculation. The audience that waits expectantly will receive the signs; the audience that calculates will miss them. The waiting is the substance of the verse's call.

This is one of the most demanding features of the Qur'anic ethical life. The audience is being asked to wait for signs whose timing is not specified. The waiting is not idle; the waiting is the active orientation of the soul toward what is to come. The orientation is what the verse is asking for. The audience that waits actively is the audience that has understood the verse. The audience that demands the timing has not yet understood.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:38

> They ask ˹the believers˺, “When will this threat come to pass if what you say is true?”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Question That Is Its Own Refusal**

The verse records the audience's challenge. *Wa yaqūlūna matā hādha al-waʿdu in kuntum ṣādiqīn* — and they say, when will this promise come to pass, if you are truthful? The challenge is one of the recurring Qur'anic refrains. The audience had been making the same demand throughout the sūrah; the verse is now recording the most explicit form of the demand. The audience wants the promise to come to pass in a time the audience can see.

The verse's polemic is structural. The question is asked in the conditional: *if you are truthful*. The conditional contains the audience's verdict already. The audience is not genuinely asking when; the audience is asserting that the messengers are not truthful. The question is the assertion in interrogative form. The verse is recording the assertion; the verse is not engaging with the question as if it were a genuine inquiry.

**"This Promise" — The Promise the Audience Is Refusing**

The phrase *hādha al-waʿd* — this promise — refers to the promise that has been made throughout the sūrah. The promise includes the punishment of the disbelievers and the reward of the believers; the promise is the eschatological resolution the sūrah has been describing. The audience is asked to specify when the promise will come; the audience is being told, in effect, that the specification is not the audience's to make.

The verse is therefore not refusing to give a date. The verse is refusing to give the audience the kind of date the audience is demanding. The audience wants the date in order to verify the message; the audience wants the verification so that the audience can decide whether to believe. The order of priorities the audience has constructed is one in which the audience decides on the basis of the verification. The Qur'an refuses that order. The Qur'an asks the audience to believe before the verification. The believing is the substance of the response the Qur'an is asking for.

**The Challenge as Excess of Haste**

The previous verse named haste as part of the human constitution; this verse records what haste looks like when it is acted on. The audience's question is the form haste takes when it is allowed to dictate the religious posture. The audience is in a hurry; the audience wants the promise now; the audience wants the verification now; the audience is not willing to wait. The not-willingness-to-wait is what the previous verse has just warned against. The audience's question is the violation of the warning.

This is one of the most pointed juxtapositions in the sūrah. The previous verse warned against hastening; this verse records the audience's hastening. The juxtaposition is not accidental; the verses are showing the audience exactly what the warning was about. The audience is being shown its own haste; the audience is being shown what the haste looks like in practice. The showing is the polemic. The audience is being asked to recognise itself in the showing.

**A Question That Asks for the Wrong Thing**

The audience's question is one that asks for the wrong thing. The audience wants the promise to come to pass in the audience's timeframe. The audience's timeframe is the timeframe of the audience's patience, which is the patience of those who have not yet believed. The audience is therefore asking the messenger to accommodate the audience's lack of belief. The accommodation would be the wrong response; the verse is refusing to give it.

The refusal is itself the substance of the test. The audience's test is whether the audience will believe without the visible verification; the messenger's response is to refuse the visible verification in the timeframe the audience demands. The refusal is the form the test takes. The audience that believes without the verification has passed the test; the audience that refuses to believe without the verification has failed. The refusal of the messenger is the condition of the test; the condition is the work the verse is doing.

**The Verb of Refusal — "Yaqūlūna"**

The verse begins with *wa yaqūlūna* — and they say. The verb is in the simple past; the saying has already happened. The verse is reporting the saying as a fact. The audience has made the challenge; the challenge is recorded; the recording is part of the verse's polemic. The recording is also part of the larger Qur'anic project of documenting the audience's refusals so that future generations can see what the audience said and how the messenger responded.

The verb's tense is significant. The saying is past tense from the perspective of the recitation. The recitation is reporting what the audience said; the audience's saying is now part of the historical record. The audience that asked the question has been recorded as the audience that asked the question. The asking has become part of the moral record. The verse is reminding the audience that its refusals are not private; the refusals are part of what the verse has been recording. The recording is the substance of the warning.

**A Verse That Returns to the Earlier Polemic**

The verse returns to the sūrah's earlier polemic against the audience's demand for signs. In 21:5, the audience had asked for a tangible sign like those sent before; in 21:6, the Qur'an had responded with the empirical observation that no community that had received the signs had believed after receiving them. In 21:38, the audience is again asking for a sign — this time asking when the promised sign will come. The verse is therefore not breaking new ground; the verse is recapitulating the demand in a new form. The recapitulation is the recurrent pattern of the sūrah's polemic.

The pattern is what the audience must recognise. The audience has been making the same demand in different forms throughout the sūrah; the audience has been answered in different ways; the audience has continued to make the demand. The continuation is the refusal; the refusal is the substance of the audience's posture. The audience is being shown, in this verse, what the posture looks like; the audience is being asked to recognise the posture and to consider what the recognition requires.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:39

> If only the disbelievers knew that a time will come when they will not be able to keep the Fire off their faces or backs, nor will they be helped

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Conditional That Has No Sound Completion**

The verse opens with a conditional whose conclusion is implied rather than stated. *Law yalʿamūna* — if they knew. Al-Rāzī and al-Zamakhsharī note that the verse's completion is implicit: "if they knew, they would not act as they are acting." The conditional names the condition that would change the audience's behaviour; the verse does not specify the change. The specification is left to the audience's imagination. The imagination is part of the verse's work.

The Arabic *law* — if — marks a counterfactual hypothesis. The verse is saying that the audience does not know; the not-knowing is the condition of their behaviour; the behaviour would change if the not-knowing were reversed. The verse is not saying that the audience can never know; the verse is saying that the audience has not yet known. The implication is that the not-yet can be reversed; the reversal is what the verse is asking for.

**"A Time Will Come When They Will Not Be Able to Keep the Fire Off"**

The verse specifies what the audience would know if they knew. *Lā yakhfūna ʿalā wujūhihim al-nāra wa lā ʿalā ẓuhūrihim* — they will not be able to keep the fire from their faces or from their backs. The verb *khaffa* — to withhold, to keep from — names an action that the verse says will be impossible. The audience is being told that there will be a time when the audience's attempts to deflect the fire will fail. The attempts will fail from the front and from behind; the fire will surround them.

The image is specific. The fire comes from in front of them and from behind them; the faces and the backs are the parts of the body that are exposed. What the verse is asserting here is that the audience's exposure will be total. The audience will not be able to hide; the audience will not be able to deflect; the audience will be unable to prevent what is coming. The inability is the substance of the punishment.

**"Nor Will They Be Helped"**

The verse adds *wa lā hum yuṣarūn* — and they will not be helped. The verb *naṣara* — to help, to support — names an action the verse says will be unavailable. The audience's expectation that someone will come to their aid is named and refused. The refusal is part of the verse's polemic. The audience had been postponing the reckoning; the postponement had implicitly assumed that someone would intervene on the audience's behalf. The verse is saying: no one will intervene. The reckoning will not be postponed by intervention; the reckoning will arrive.

This is one of the most demanding moments in the Qur'anic treatment of the eschatological punishment. What the audience is hearing in this verse is that there is no escape, no intervention, no help. The telling is not arbitrary; the telling is the form the moral order takes when the audience has refused to recognise what has been offered. The audience that does not accept the offer cannot expect the offerer to intervene when the consequences arrive. The intervention is not part of the moral order the audience has chosen.

**The Recognising That Would Change the Behaviour**

The verse's opening conditional — *if they knew* — implies that knowledge would change the audience's behaviour. What the verse is pointing toward is the audience's current behaviour — the behaviour of those who do not know. The audience mocks (21:36); the audience demands hastening (21:37); the audience demands to know when the promise will come (21:38). All of these behaviours are the behaviours of those who do not know. The not-knowing is the condition of the behaviours; the behaviours would change if the knowing came.

The verse is therefore asking the audience to consider what it would mean to know. The knowing is not just information; the knowing is the kind of knowledge that issues in behaviour. The audience that knew would not mock; the audience that knew would not demand; the audience that knew would not postpone. The knowing is the substance of the response the verse is asking for. The asking is the verse's call.

**A Verse That Makes the Reckoning Concrete**

The verse is one of the most concrete descriptions of the eschatological punishment in the sūrah. The punishment is not abstract; the punishment is specific. The fire will be on the faces and the backs; the audience will be unable to keep it from them; the audience will not be helped. The specificity is part of the verse's polemic. The audience had imagined the reckoning as something distant; the verse is saying that the reckoning will be concrete, embodied, and irreversible. The imagination of distance is part of the problem the verse is correcting.

The concreteness is also part of the verse's call. The audience that hears the concreteness is being asked to imagine the reckoning in a way that the audience's previous abstractions had not allowed. The imagining is the work the verse is asking for. The work is the substance of the response the verse is calling for. The audience that imagines the reckoning well has begun the change the verse is asking for. The audience that refuses to imagine has refused the verse.

**A Verse That Returns to the Universal Mortality of 21:35**

The verse implicitly connects to the previous claim that every soul will taste death (21:35). The audience will die; the audience will be returned to God (21:35); the audience will face the reckoning. The reckoning is what the verse is describing. The description is concrete; the concreteness is part of the verse's polemic. The audience is being asked to recognise what 21:35 named in universal terms; the verse is naming what the universal terms will look like in practice.

The connection is part of the sūrah's cumulative argument. The verses have been moving from the universal to the specific; the universal is the universality of death and return; the specific is the concrete description of what the return will involve. The cumulative argument is the verse's call. What the verse is calling for here is that the audience integrate the universal with the specific; the integration is the substance of the response the verse is asking for.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:40

> In fact, the Hour will take them by surprise, leaving them stunned. So they will not be able to avert it, nor will it be delayed from them

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Suddenness of the Hour**

The verse names the suddenness of the Hour. *Bal taʾtīhim baghtatan* — rather, it will come upon them suddenly, unexpectedly. The Arabic *baghta* — suddenness, unexpectedness — names the form of the arrival. The Hour will not announce itself; the Hour will arrive in the way that the audience has been postponing. The arrival is the form the postponement takes when the postponement is exhausted.

The verse's use of *bal* — rather — marks a contrast. The audience had asked when the promise will come (21:38); the verse is saying that the request itself is based on a false premise. The premise is that the Hour will come in a way the audience can predict; the verse is denying the premise. The Hour comes suddenly. The coming cannot be predicted. The audience's request for a date is therefore a request for the impossible. The verse is refusing the request by describing what the actual arrival will be like.

**"Leaving Them Stunned"**

The verse continues *wa tubhithuhum* — and will stun them, will confound them, will leave them in disarray. The verb *bahasat* — to be stunned, to be confounded, to be unable to respond — names the state the audience will be in when the Hour arrives. The audience will not be able to organise a response; the audience will not be able to argue; the audience will not be able to defend. The confounding is the form the Hour takes for those who have refused to prepare.

This is one of the most distinctive features of the Qur'anic treatment of the Hour. The Hour arrives as a kind of total disruption; the disruption is not just an external event; the disruption is the form the audience's inner unpreparedness takes when the unpreparedness is exposed. The audience will be confounded because the audience has refused to be ready; the refusal is what is exposed. The exposure is the substance of the confounding.

**"They Will Not Be Able to Avert It"**

The verse adds *fa-lā yastaṭīʿūna raddahā* — so they will not be able to turn it back. The verb *istaṭāʿa* — to be able, to have the capacity — names an ability the verse says will be absent. The audience will not be able to turn the Hour back; the audience will not be able to postpone it; the audience will not be able to deflect it. The inability is the form the Hour takes in relation to those who have refused.

The verb is in the same root as the noun *ṭāʿa* — obedience — and the broader semantic field of capacity and power. The verse is saying that the audience's capacity will be exhausted. The audience's capacity to act, to defer, to negotiate — the entire repertoire of postponement the audience had been using — will be revealed as having had a limit. The limit is the limit the verse is naming. The audience that has lived within the limit will be exposed by the limit.

**"Nor Will It Be Delayed From Them"**

The verse closes *wa lā hum yunẓarūn* — and they will not be given respite. The verb *anẓara* — to grant respite, to delay — names an action the verse says will be refused. The audience that had been hoping for postponement is being told that the postponement will not come. The postponement is what the audience has been counting on; the verse is removing the count.

This is one of the most devastating moments in the sūrah. The audience's strategy of postponement — the strategy of refusing now and then saying they will consider later — has been the audience's main posture throughout the sūrah. The verse is saying that the strategy will not work. The audience will not be granted the postponement; the audience will be exposed to the Hour; the exposure will be sudden, confounding, irreversible. The strategy is being removed in a single sentence.

**The Connection to Other Verses on Suddenness**

The verse is one of several in the Qur'an that describe the suddenness of the Hour. 6:31 says: *If only you could see when they are set before the Fire and say, "Would that we could be returned and not deny the signs of our Lord!"* 7:95 says: *Then We shall turn them out from wherever they were, just as We brought forth the first creation.* 22:55, 26:202, 29:53, 39:55, 43:66, 47:18 — each of these verses describes the suddenness in a different idiom. The recurrence is the consistent application of a method: the audience that has been postponing is being told, in many forms, that the postponement will not be granted.

The recurrence is also the recognition that the audience's postponement is a recurring strategy. The audience returns to the strategy in every age; the Qur'an's response is to return to the refutation in every age. The recurrence is the response. The audience is being told that the strategy has been tried and refused before; the audience is being asked to recognise the strategy and to abandon it before the strategy abandons the audience.

**The Verse As a Refusal of the Audience's Posture**

The verse is the climax of the sūrah's treatment of the audience's postponement. The previous verses (21:37–39) had warned against hastening and named what the audience would face if they knew; this verse names what the actual arrival will be like. The arrival is sudden; the arrival is confounding; the arrival cannot be averted; the arrival cannot be delayed. The audience that has been building a worldview on the postponement is being told that the worldview is built on a refusal that will not be granted.

The refusal is the substance of the verse's call. The audience is being asked to recognise that the postponement is the basis of the audience's behaviour; the postponement is being removed; the audience is being asked to consider what the behaviour will look like without the postponement. The behaviour without the postponement is what the verse is naming. The naming is the verse's polemic; the recognition is the verse's call.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:41

> ˹Other˺ messengers had already been ridiculed before you ˹O Prophet˺, but those who mocked them were overtaken by what they used to ridicule

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Pattern Repeated Before**

The verse tells the Prophet that he is not the first messenger to face mockery. *La-qad istuhziʾa bi-rusulin min qablika* — messengers were certainly mocked before you. The verb *istahzā'a* — to mock, to ridicule, to make fun of — names the form the earlier refusals took. The pattern is the same pattern the audience has been following in Mecca. The Prophet is being reminded that the pattern is not original; the pattern has been tried before. The reminder is part of the verse's consolation and part of its polemic.

The consolation is for the Prophet. The Prophet has been mocked by his people; the verse is reminding him that earlier prophets faced the same mockery. The consolation is not abstract; the consolation is grounded in a recurring historical pattern. The pattern is what the verse is asking the Prophet to recognise. The recognition will not stop the mockery, but the recognition will give the mockery its proper weight. The mockery is not unique; the mockery is part of a recurring dynamic.

**"Overtaken by What They Used to Ridicule"**

The second half of the verse specifies the consequence: *fa-aḥāṭa bi-alladhīna sakhirū minhum mā kānū bihi yastahziʾūn* — so there surrounded those who mocked of them what they used to mock. The verb *aḥāṭa* — to surround, to encompass, to beset — names the form the consequence takes. The mockery is overtaken by the very thing the mockers mocked. The audience that mocked the promise of punishment is overtaken by the punishment; the audience that mocked the warning is overtaken by the warning's fulfilment.

This is one of the most theologically pointed patterns in the Qur'an. The recurrence is at 6:10, 11:8, 16:34, 39:48, 40:83, 45:33, 46:26. The pattern is the same in each case: those who mock are overtaken by what they mocked. The pattern is the form the moral order takes when the mockers have refused to recognise what they were mocking. The mockers thought they were dismissing a possibility; the dismissal became the very mechanism by which the possibility became real.

**The verse is also recorded in 6:10 with similar wording.**

The substance of the pattern is that the mockers cannot keep their mockery at a distance from themselves. The mockery was meant to be at the messenger; the mockery ends up being about the mocker. The thing the mockers mocked is the thing that overtakes them. The mockers had treated the warning as a remote possibility; the warning arrived as the present reality. The arrival is the form the warning takes when it has been sufficiently delayed.

**The Prophet's Comfort and the Audience's Warning**

The verse is addressed to the Prophet, but the audience is also being addressed. The Prophet is being comforted; the audience is being warned. The comfort is in the recognition that earlier prophets faced the same treatment; the warning is in the recognition that the earlier mockers were overtaken. The audience is being asked to consider which side of the pattern it wants to be on. The audience that mocks is the audience that will be overtaken; the audience that believes is the audience that has joined the prophets.

The verse's structure makes the two sides of the choice explicit. The mocking side has been described; the believing side is implied by the contrast. The audience is being asked to recognise that the choice is available and that the choice has consequences. The consequences are not abstract; the consequences are the consequences the verse has just described. The audience that mocks will be overtaken; the audience that believes will be with those who warned.

**The Verse As a Recurrence of an Earlier Theme**

The verse is one of several in the Qur'an that connect the Prophet's experience to that of earlier messengers. 13:32 says: *Messengers before you were mocked, but I gave respite to those who disbelieved, then I seized them*. 15:11 says: *There did not come to them any messenger except that they used to mock him*. 36:30 says: *How many a generation have We destroyed before them, and they cried out when it was too late for escape*. The recurrence is the consistent application of a method: the Prophet is not alone in his experience; the experience is part of the prophetic calling.

The recurrence is also a teaching about the nature of the prophetic calling. The calling is to deliver a message that will be mocked; the message is delivered knowing that it will be mocked; the mocking is part of the test. The Prophet who knows the pattern has been initiated into the meaning of the calling. The audience that hears the pattern is being invited to recognise that the message is genuine — even the rejection of it follows the pattern. The pattern is evidence for the message as much as it is evidence against the audience.

**The Mockery That Recovers Its Object**

The verse's claim that the mockers are overtaken by what they mocked is one of the most arresting features of the Qur'anic moral psychology. The mockers had thought that the mockery put the mocked thing at a distance from them; the verse is saying that the mockery brought the mocked thing closer. The distance is illusory; the closeness is real. The mocked warning is the warning the mockers themselves will hear; the mocked punishment is the punishment the mockers themselves will receive. The mockery did not protect the mockers; the mockery exposed them.

This is the form the moral order takes in the Qur'anic worldview. Nothing can be mocked at a safe distance. The mockers' mockery is itself an act in the moral order; the act has consequences; the consequences are the very things the mockers had tried to mock at a distance. The mocking is the moral act that produces the exposure; the exposure is the form the moral order takes when the mocking is complete. The verse is therefore not just recording what happened to earlier mockers; it is describing a structure that operates at every moment.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:42

> Ask ˹them, O Prophet,˺ “Who can defend you by day or by night against the Most Compassionate?” Still they turn away from the remembrance of their Lord

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Question That Closes Off Every Escape**

The verse asks a question whose answer is rhetorically predetermined. *Man yajiruukum min al-Raḥmāni* — who will protect you from the Most Compassionate? The verb *jāra* — to protect, to take refuge with, to defend — names a specific form of action. The verse is asking who, if anyone, will defend the audience against the Most Compassionate. The answer is no one; the verse is constructed to make the answer obvious. The question is therefore not a genuine inquiry; it is a polemical move. The polemic is to close off the escape routes the audience had been imagining.

The specification of *al-Raḥmān* — the Most Compassionate — is significant. The name that emphasises divine mercy is the name under which the protection is being asked. The audience might have imagined that mercy exempts them from consequences; the verse is saying that mercy does not exempt; mercy is the very attribute under which the protection must be sought. The audience that refuses to seek protection cannot assume that the mercy will operate without the seeking.

**"By Day or By Night" — The Coverage of the Question**

The verse specifies *al-layla wa al-nahāra* — by night and by day. The temporal specification names the totality of the audience's existence. The audience is being asked who will protect them at every moment of their lives; the verse is asking who will protect them in any moment of their lives. The question covers the entire span. The audience cannot escape the question by retreating into some private moment of safety; the moment of safety is itself covered.

This is one of the recurring features of the Qur'anic polemic. The verses that ask the audience to consider their situation ask the audience to consider it across the entire span of their lives. The span is not arbitrary; the span is the span in which the audience has been postponing. The postponement has been covered by days and nights; the protection is being asked for across the same span. The audience cannot hide from the question in any part of the span.

**"Still They Turn Away from the Remembrance of Their Lord"**

The verse's closing observation is sharp. *Bal hum ʿan dhikri rabbihim muʿriḍūn* — rather, they turn away from the remembrance of their Lord. The verb *ʿaraḍa* — to turn away — is the same verb used throughout the sūrah (21:1, 21:24, 21:32). The audience has been turning away from the very remembrance that would constitute the answer to the question. The audience's turning away is the source of the audience's vulnerability; the audience has refused the protection because the audience has refused the one from whom the protection comes.

The verse therefore refuses the audience's implicit assumption that they are safe. The safety the audience has been assuming is the safety of those who are not yet overtaken. The audience has assumed that the protection is unnecessary; the verse is saying that the assumption is based on the audience's turning away from the very source of protection. The source is the remembrance; the remembrance has been refused; the protection has therefore not been arranged. The audience is exposed because of what the audience has refused.

**The Name "The Most Compassionate" As Polemic**

The verse's use of *al-Raḥmān* is one of the most theologically pointed choices in the sūrah. Al-Rāzī notes that the name is used here because it causes one to reflect on the fact that God encompasses all things with His mercy. The name is the name of mercy; the mercy is what the audience is being asked to seek refuge from; the paradox is the verse's polemic. The audience that has been calling on God as the Most Compassionate has been assuming that the mercy is protection; the verse is saying that the mercy is also the source of consequences, because the mercy is part of a structure in which the merciful One is also the just One.

The polemic is not a denial of mercy. The polemic is a refusal to allow mercy to be reduced to a comfortable idea. The mercy of the Most Compassionate is operative in the entire moral order; the mercy is not an exemption from the order. The audience that wishes to be the recipient of mercy must seek it through the order, not against it. The order is the remembrance; the audience that turns away from the remembrance cannot expect the mercy to operate as if the remembrance had been honoured. The verse is therefore not denying the mercy; the verse is specifying the conditions under which the mercy operates.

**A Verse That Returns to the Audience's Posture**

The verse is a return to the diagnosis the sūrah has been making of the audience's posture. The audience has been turning away from the remembrance (21:1); the audience has been listening jokingly (21:2); the audience has been mocking (21:36). The verse at 21:42 is the diagnosis of all those postures in a single line. The turning away is the consistent feature; the consistent feature is what the verse is naming. The naming is the diagnostic.

The diagnostic is not a defeat. The diagnostic is an invitation to recognise the posture. The audience that recognises the posture has begun the work of changing it. The audience that refuses to recognise has refused the verse. The refusal is not the end of the verse's call; the refusal is the form the call takes when it has not yet been answered. The call continues; the verses continue; the audience is being asked to consider whether the continued call will eventually be heard.

**A Verse That Invites Reflection on Divine Awareness**

Al-Qurṭubī observes that the verse functions as an implicit reminder that the divine awareness reaches the disbelievers continuously, both day and night, even while they remain oblivious to that awareness. The reminder is part of the verse's polemic. The audience has been imagining that they are not being watched; the verse is saying that the watching is continuous. The continuous watching is the form the divine vigilance takes; the vigilance is not contingent on the audience's recognition; the vigilance is the structure in which the audience lives. The audience that recognises the vigilance has begun to recognise what it means to live in the divine presence.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:43

> Or do they have gods—other than Us—that can protect them? They cannot ˹even˺ protect themselves, nor will they be aided against Us

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Question About Other Gods**

The verse returns to the question of gods other than God. *Am lahum ālihatun min dūninā* — or do they have gods besides Us, that can protect them? The construction *min dūninā* — other than Us, besides Us — is the Qur'an's standard way of referring to the supposed partners of God. The verse is asking whether the audience's supposed partners have the capacity to defend them. The question is constructed so that the answer is no.

The verse is a continuation of the argument begun at 21:21 and 21:22. The audience had been asked about gods that could raise the dead (21:21); the audience was told that multiple gods would corrupt the heavens and the earth (21:22). The verse at 21:43 is asking about a different capacity — the capacity to protect. The protection is what the audience's supposed partners are supposed to provide. The verse is saying that the partners cannot provide what they are supposed to provide.

**"They Cannot Protect Themselves"**

The verse continues *lā yastaṭīʿūna naṣra anfusihim* — they cannot protect themselves. The verb *istaṭāʿa* — to have the capacity — names the capacity the verse says is absent. The audience's supposed partners cannot protect themselves. The incapacity is the foundation of the verse's polemic. If the partners cannot protect themselves, the partners cannot protect the audience. The argument is not that the partners are weaker than the audience; the argument is that the partners are not the kind of beings that can protect anyone, including themselves.

This is one of the most distinctive features of the Qur'anic polemic against idols. The idols are not merely weaker than the audience imagines them to be; the idols are the wrong kind of beings entirely. The idols cannot do what the audience wants them to do. The incapacity is the form the false gods take; the form is the substance of the criticism.

**"Nor Will They Be Aided Against Us"**

The verse adds *wa lā hum minnā yuṣḥabūn* — and they will not be aided against Us. The verb *yuṣḥabūn* is from *ṣaḥiba* — to be a companion, to accompany, to be present with. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī note that the phrase can be read as "they will not have Us as their Companion" or "they will not be given protection against Us." Either reading reinforces the verse's polemic. The idols will not be aided by God against God. The reading refuses the polytheistic assumption that the idols have some standing in the divine economy that would protect them.

The polemic is precise. The audience had been treating the idols as beings who could intervene in the moral order. The verse is saying that the idols are not in the moral order in the way the audience imagines. The idols are not protected against God; the idols are not companions of God. The moral order does not include them in the way the audience's polytheism assumed. The order is monotheistic; the monotheism is the form the moral order takes.

**A Verse That Names the Powerlessness of the Idols**

The verse is one of the clearest Qur'anic statements of the powerlessness of the supposed partners. The same point is made at 7:192, 7:197, and elsewhere. The repetition is the consistent application of a method: the idols are not what the audience has imagined them to be. The idols cannot protect themselves; the idols cannot protect the audience; the idols will not be aided against God. The idols are not part of the protective framework the audience has constructed; the idols are part of what the audience has been mistaken about.

The verse is therefore part of the diagnostic project the sūrah has been pursuing. The sūrah is not just arguing against the audience; the sūrah is showing the audience what its framework actually looks like. The framework looks like a collection of partners who cannot protect themselves and cannot protect the audience. The audience is being asked to recognise the framework and to consider whether the framework is what the audience wants to trust.

**A Refusal of the Implicit Bargain**

The audience's polytheistic framework had implicitly been a bargain. The audience had imagined that the idols could be invoked for protection; the audience had imagined that the protection was available because the idols had standing in the divine economy. The verse is refusing the bargain. The idols have no standing; the protection is not available; the bargain cannot be struck. The audience that has been relying on the bargain is the audience that has been relying on a non-existent protection.

The refusal of the bargain is one of the most theologically significant moments in the sūrah. The verse is not just denying that the idols can protect; the verse is denying that the protection can come from the direction the audience has been imagining. The protection, if it is to come, must come from the One the audience has been turning away from. The direction is the audience's choice; the verse is asking the audience to reconsider the choice. The reconsideration is the verse's call.

**A Verse That Connects to the Broader Polemic**

The verse connects back to the sūrah's earlier polemic against the supposed partners. The partners were unable to raise the dead (21:21); the partners would have corrupted the heavens and the earth (21:22); the partners are honoured servants who do not speak first (21:27). The verse at 21:43 adds that the partners cannot protect themselves or the audience. The cumulative picture is one of partners who are unable to do any of the things the audience had been expecting of them. The picture is the form the audience's polytheism takes when examined; the examination is the work the verses have been doing.

The examination is not a defeat of the audience. The examination is an invitation to recognise the picture and to reconsider the framework that produced it. The reconsideration is the work the verses are asking for; the work is the substance of the response the verses are calling for. The audience that does the work has begun the change the verses have been calling for. The audience that refuses to do the work has refused the verses. The refusal is what the verses have been recording throughout.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:44

> In fact, We have allowed enjoyment for these ˹Meccans˺ and their forefathers for such a long time ˹that they took it for granted˺. Do they not see that We gradually reduce ˹their˺ land from its borders? Is it they who will then prevail

**Expanded Commentary**

**Enjoyment That Becomes Self-Evident**

The verse opens with an observation about the audience's history. *Bal mattaʿnā hāʾulāʾi wa ābāʾahum ḥattā ʿāla ʿalayhim al-ʿumuru* — rather, We gave enjoyment to these and their fathers, until time grew long upon them. The verb *mattaʿa* — to give enjoyment, to provide with the means of enjoyment — names what the audience has been given. The audience has been given enjoyment; the enjoyment has continued; the continuation has become the substance of the audience's life.

The verse is not praising the enjoyment. The verse is observing what the enjoyment has produced. The continuation has become a taken-for-granted. The audience has been receiving the enjoyment so long that the receiving has become invisible. The enjoyment has ceased to be recognised as a gift and has become recognised as a fact. The fact is what the verse is naming. The naming is part of the verse's polemic.

**"Until Time Grew Long Upon Them"**

The Arabic *ḥattā ʿāla ʿalayhim al-ʿumuru* — until time grew long upon them — names the duration. The duration is what made the enjoyment invisible as a gift. The audience had been receiving so long that the receiving had become part of the natural order. The natural order is what the verse is asking the audience to reconsider. The reconsideration is the substance of the verse's call.

The Arabic phrase has a particular weight. The verb *ʿāla* — to grow long, to be prolonged — names a passive experience of time. The Meccans did not choose to have the time grow long; the time grew long upon them. The passivity is part of the verse's polemic. The audience has been treated as the recipients of a long duration; the duration has not been within their control; those who have been passive about the duration cannot claim credit for what the duration has produced.

**The Gradual Reduction of the Land**

The verse continues *afa-lā yarawna annā naṭīʾu al-arḍa naqṣuhā min atrāfihā* — do they not see that We come upon the land, reducing it from its borders? The verb *naṭaʾa* — to come upon, to approach, to encroach — names the form of the action. The verb *naqṣa* — to reduce, to diminish, to take from — names what the action produces. The land is being reduced; the reduction is gradual; the audience is being asked to notice the reduction.

The reduction is the form the consequences take. What has been enjoyed over the long duration is now contracting. The contraction is gradual; the gradualness is part of the verse's argument. The audience cannot point to a single dramatic event; the only available observation is the slow contraction over time. The noticing is the substance of the verse's call. Whoever notices has begun to recognise what the contraction means.

**Multiple Interpretations of "Reduction"**

The commentators have offered several interpretations of the reduction. Some read it as a reference to the loss of territory through military conquest — a reading that fits the Meccan context if the verse is read as anticipating the Muslim conquests. Others read it as the loss of the population and blessings of a community — a general reading that fits any period of decline. Still others read it as the loss of the learned sages of a community, citing a hadith about God removing knowledge by removing the learned. The interpretations are not exclusive; the verse can be read in any of the ways, and the verse can be read in all of them at once.

The interpretive range is itself part of the verse's polemic. The audience that has been enjoying the duration is being told that the duration produces consequences; the consequences can take many forms; the audience is being asked to notice whatever form the consequences are taking in the audience's own case. The noticing is the substance of the verse's call. The audience that notices has begun to recognise that the duration is not permanent; the recognition is the beginning of the change the verse is asking for.

**"Is It They Who Will Prevail?"**

The verse closes with *afahum al-ghālibūn* — are they the ones who will prevail? The question is rhetorical. The audience is not going to prevail. The audience that has been enjoying the duration is not going to prevail against the One who has been giving the enjoyment. The question is the verse's polemic. The audience is being asked to recognise what the audience's actual position is in the moral order. The audience's actual position is not the position of those who prevail.

This is one of the recurring moves in the sūrah's polemic. The audience is asked to consider what its actual position is, in light of the verses that have been describing the moral order. The audience's actual position is not the position of partners of God; the audience's actual position is not the position of those who can demand signs (21:5); the audience's actual position is not the position of those who will prevail. The audience's actual position is the position of those who have been receiving a gift and have failed to recognise it as a gift. The recognition is what the verse is asking for.

**A Verse That Combines Long-Suffering and Gradual Judgment**

The verse combines two registers of the divine economy. The first register is the long-suffering — the divine patience that has allowed the audience to enjoy the duration without interruption. The second register is the gradual judgment — the divine response that reduces the land from its borders. The two registers are not contradictory; the two registers are the form the moral order takes over time. The patience provides the duration; the judgment provides the consequence. The audience is being asked to read both registers and to integrate what they see.

The integration is the substance of the response the verse is asking for. What the audience that has only read the patience has constructed is a worldview in which the patience is permanent. What the audience that has only read the judgment has constructed is a worldview in which the patience is illusory. The verse is asking the audience to read both and to recognise that the moral order includes both. The recognition is what the verse is offering.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:45

> Say, ˹O Prophet,˺ “I warn you only by revelation.” But the deaf cannot hear the call when they are warned

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Prophet's Task Defined**

The verse defines the Prophet's task in a single line. *Innamā undhirukum bi-l-waḥyi* — I warn you only by revelation. The verse is putting words in the Prophet's mouth; the words describe what the Prophet does. The Prophet warns; the warning is by revelation; the warning is not by personal opinion, not by inference from nature, not by deduction from moral principles. The warning is by revelation. The verse is therefore a statement about the source of the warning.

The verb *waḥy* — revelation — is the Qur'anic technical term for the act by which God communicates with the Prophet. The term is used throughout the Qur'an to describe the Prophet's reception of the message. The verse is therefore placing the Prophet's warning within the framework of revelation. The warning is not the Prophet's invention; the warning is the Prophet's reception. The reception is what the verse is naming; the naming is the verse's polemic against the audience's accusations.

**"But the Deaf Cannot Hear"**

The verse continues *wa la yuḥīʾa al-ṣummu al-duʿāʾa idhā mā nudhirū* — but the deaf cannot hear the call when they are warned. The verse is using the verb *aṣamma* — to be deaf — in its figurative sense. The audience is not literally deaf; the audience is spiritually deaf. The deafness is the audience's refusal to listen; the refusal is what the verse is naming.

This is one of the recurring images of the Qur'anic treatment of audience resistance. The audience that has been refusing the message is described as deaf, blind, and dead — the three images of those who have chosen not to receive. The images are not metaphors of physiological incapacity; the images are descriptions of a chosen posture. The audience has chosen not to hear; the audience is therefore described as deaf. The description is the diagnosis.

**The Verb "Idhā Mā Nudhirū" — The Conditional of Warning**

The phrase *idhā mā nudhirū* — when they are warned — names the condition under which the deaf cannot hear. The condition is being warned. The deaf cannot hear the warning. The verse is therefore not just describing the audience's general condition; the verse is specifying the condition under which the condition manifests. The condition is the warning; the warning is what the audience cannot hear.

This is one of the most pointed moments in the sūrah's polemic. The Prophet is warning; the audience cannot hear the warning. The inability is the form the warning takes when the warning has been sufficiently refused. The audience that has refused the warning cannot hear it; the cannot-hearing is what the verse is naming. The naming is the verse's diagnosis of the audience's posture.

**The Reading That Addresses the Prophet**

Some commentators, including al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī, read the verb in the second person: *Thou canst not cause the deaf to hear when they are warned*. The reading addresses the Prophet directly and is consistent with other verses that remind the Prophet that his task is to deliver the message, not to make the audience hear (2:272; 16:35). The reading is not exclusive of the first reading; the two readings are compatible. The Prophet is warning; the audience cannot hear the warning. The two statements describe the same situation from two angles.

The reading that addresses the Prophet is also a comfort. The Prophet is not failing; the audience's deafness is not the Prophet's responsibility. The Prophet's task is to deliver; the audience's hearing is the audience's responsibility. The verse is reminding the Prophet that the responsibility has limits. The limits are the limits of the prophetic task; the limits are part of what the prophetic calling is.

**A Verse That Closes Off One Form of Audience Demand**

The audience had been demanding that the Prophet produce signs (21:5); the audience had been demanding that the Prophet demonstrate his credentials (21:24); the audience had been demanding the timing of the promise (21:38). The verse at 21:45 is a refusal of one of those demands in particular: the demand that the Prophet's warning come with something other than revelation. The Prophet warns by revelation; the revelation is what the Prophet has; the audience's demand for something other than revelation is a demand for something the Prophet does not have.

The refusal is not arbitrary. The Prophet's warning by revelation is the appropriate form of the warning. The audience's demand for something other than revelation is a demand that the warning take a different form; the demand is a demand for a different kind of prophet. The verse is saying that the demand is being made of the wrong person; the audience that wishes to hear a different kind of warning is asking the wrong prophet. The asking is not answered; the asking is the form the audience's refusal takes.

**The Deafness As a Spiritual Category**

The verse's treatment of deafness is part of the broader Qur'anic category of spiritual deafness, which is invoked at 2:18, 5:71, 6:25, 31:7, and elsewhere. The category names a state of being in which the senses operate but the inner reception has been blocked. The blocking is a chosen state; the chosen state is what the verse is naming. The audience has chosen to refuse the warning; the choice has produced the deafness; the deafness is what the warning encounters.

The spiritual category is important because it allows the verse to describe the audience's condition without accusing the audience of having no access to the warning. The audience has access; the access has been refused; the refusal has produced the deafness. The deafness is not the audience's nature; the deafness is the audience's posture. The posture can be changed; the change is the substance of the response the verse is asking for. The audience that recognises the posture can begin the change; the audience that refuses to recognise has refused the verse.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:46

> If they were touched by even a breath of your Lord’s torment, they would certainly cry, “Woe to us! We have really been wrongdoers.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Conditional and Its Implied Completion**

The verse opens with a conditional whose completion is implicit. *Wa la-in massahum nafḥatun min ʿadhābi rabbika* — if there touched them a breath of your Lord's torment. The conditional is completed by what follows: they would certainly cry out. The construction is one of the Qur'an's recurring patterns for describing what would happen if the audience were to experience the consequences. The consequences are described as hypothetical; the hypothesis is constructed so that the audience can recognise what it would be.

The Arabic *nafḥa* — breath, whiff, puff — is a precise term. It does not name the full force of the torment; it names what comes from the torment at a distance. A breath is what one encounters without approaching the source. The verse is saying that even the slightest encounter with the torment would produce the cry. The implication is that the full force of the torment would produce something more than a cry; the slightest encounter produces the cry. The full force produces what the full force produces.

**"Woe to Us! We Have Been Wrongdoers"**

The verse records the cry that would come. *Ya waylanā innā kunnā ẓālimīn* — woe to us, we have indeed been wrongdoers. The cry is the same cry that the wrongdoers of 21:14 uttered when the punishment arrived. The verse is connecting the hypothetical cry to the cry that was recorded earlier; the connection is part of the verse's polemic. The cry that the audience would utter in the hypothetical is the same cry the wrongdoers actually uttered. The audience and the wrongdoers are the same kind of people; the cry is the cry that the kind of people utter when the consequences arrive.

The verb *ẓalama* — to wrong — is the same verb used throughout the sūrah (21:14, 21:29). The wrongdoers are those who have put something in the wrong place; the wrong is specific; the wrongdoers know what the wrong is. The cry is therefore not a general expression of distress; the cry is the specific acknowledgement that the cry-er has been a wrongdoer. The acknowledgement is the substance of the cry.

**"If Only the Wrongdoers Knew"**

The verse is one of several in the Qur'an that connect the hypothetical experience of punishment to the recognition that would come. 67:10–11 says: *And they will say, "We would have been better off if we had listened and used our reason."* 39:48 says: *The evil of what they sent before them will appear to them, and they will be encompassed by what they used to mock.* Each of these passages describes the recognition that comes when the consequences arrive. The recognition is too late; the consequences are not reversed; the recognition is part of the consequences.

The verse at 21:46 is therefore part of the Qur'an's consistent treatment of the belated recognition. The recognition is real; the lateness is the substance of the recognition's inadequacy. The audience that recognises too late has not recognised in time. The recognising in time is what the verse is asking for; the audience is being shown what the recognising in time would look like by contrast with the recognising too late. The contrast is the verse's polemic.

**A Verse That Names the Audience's Future**

The verse is implicitly naming the audience's future. The audience will one day cry out; the cry will be the cry of those who have been wrongdoers; the cry will come too late. The verse is not predicting this future; the verse is describing what the future will be like if the audience continues in its present course. The description is the verse's call. The audience is being asked to recognise the description and to change the course so that the description does not apply.

This is one of the most demanding features of the Qur'anic treatment of the audience's situation. The verses are not just describing what the audience is doing; the verses are describing what the audience will become if the audience continues. The verses are offering the audience the chance to recognise the description and to change. The recognition is the substance of the response the verses are asking for; the response is what the audience is being invited to give.

**The Breath As a Symbol of Subtlety**

The Arabic *nafḥa* — breath — is one of the most carefully chosen words in the verse. The breath is what one encounters at a distance; the breath is what reaches one without approaching the source. The verse is saying that the audience would cry out even at the slightest encounter. The slightest encounter is the breath; the breath is enough to produce the cry. The implication is that the slightest encounter is enough to reveal what the audience really is.

The revelation is the substance of the verse's polemic. The audience that has been maintaining its composure in the absence of any encounter with the consequences would not maintain that composure in the presence of even a breath. The composure is therefore not the composure of those who have nothing to fear; the composure is the composure of those who have not yet encountered what they should be fearing. The composure would be lost at the breath; the loss is what the verse is naming; the naming is the verse's call.

**A Verse That Returns to the Theme of Belated Recognition**

The verse is the second time the sūrah has recorded the cry *ya waylanā*. The first was at 21:14, where the wrongdoers cried out after the punishment arrived. The verse at 21:46 is the hypothetical version of the same cry. The hypothetical is what the actual would have looked like before it became actual; the hypothetical is the warning; the warning is the substance of the verse's call.

The recurrence of the cry is the verse's polemic. The audience that has been refusing the message will one day cry out; the cry will be the same cry. The audience is being shown, in advance, what the cry will be. The showing is not cruelty; the showing is mercy. The audience that recognises the cry in advance has been given the chance to avoid the circumstances that would produce it. The chance is the substance of the verse's call; the call is the substance of the response the verse is asking for.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:47

> We will set up the scales of justice on the Day of Judgment, so no soul will be wronged in the least. And ˹even˺ if a deed is the weight of a mustard seed, We will bring it forth. And sufficient are We as a ˹vigilant˺ Reckoner

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Scales of Justice**

The verse names the apparatus of the eschatological reckoning. *Wa naḍaʿu al-mawāzīna al-qista li-yawmi al-qiyāmati* — and We will set up the scales of justice for the Day of Resurrection. The verb *waḍaʿa* — to set up, to place, to establish — names the act of establishing the scales. The noun *mawāzīn* is the plural of *mīzān* — scale, balance. The adjective *qist* — justice, equity, fairness — qualifies the scales. The reckoning will be done with just scales; the scales will be the apparatus of the reckoning.

The use of the plural *mawāzīn* — scales — is significant. Some commentators understand the plural as referring to the scales for each soul or the scales for each kind of action. Others understand the plural as the plural of intensification — the scales will be the most balanced possible. The plurality, in either reading, is part of the verse's emphasis on the thoroughness of the reckoning. The reckoning will not be a single weighing; the reckoning will be a thoroughgoing assessment using whatever apparatus is required for the justice the verse is naming.

**"No Soul Will Be Wronged in the Least"**

The verse continues *fa-lā tuẓlamu nafsun shayʾan* — so no soul will be wronged in anything. The verb *ẓalama* — to wrong — is the same verb used throughout the sūrah. No soul will be the victim of wrong in the eschatological reckoning. The reckoning will be done with such precision that no soul will be the recipient of any injustice. The verb is in the passive — the soul will not be wronged — because the wronging would have to be done by some agent, and the verse refuses to specify the agent. The wronging will not occur; the reason is the precision of the scales.

This is one of the most theologically significant moments in the sūrah. The eschatological reckoning will be free of the kind of injustice that is so common in human reckoning. Human courts can err; human courts can be bribed; human courts can be partial. The divine reckoning described in the verse is free of all these defects. The audience is being asked to recognise that the reckoning they will face is the reckoning that does not err; the recognition is part of the response the verse is asking for.

**"Even if it be the Weight of a Mustard Seed"**

The verse continues *wa in kāna miṣqāla ḥabbatin min khardalin aṭaynā bihā* — and if it be the weight of a mustard seed, We will bring it forth. The mustard seed is the smallest object commonly known in the ancient world; it is used here to represent the smallest possible unit of action. No deed, no matter how small, will escape the reckoning. The mustard seed is the threshold; the threshold is so small that no deed can fall below it. The reckoning will catch everything.

The verse is connected to a recurring Qur'anic theme. 31:16 says: *If a mustard seed were hidden in a rock, or in the heavens, or in the earth, God would bring it forth*. 99:7–8 says: *Whoever has done an atom's weight of good will see it; and whoever has done an atom's weight of evil will see it*. The recurrence is the consistent application of the principle that no deed is too small to be reckoned. The audience is being asked to recognise the principle; the recognition is part of the response the verse is calling for.

**"Sufficient Are We as a Reckoner"**

The verse closes *wa kaḥnā bi-ḥāsibīn* — and sufficient are We as Reckoners. The verb *ḥasaba* — to reckon, to account, to consider — names the action of the reckoning. God is sufficient as the Reckoner; no other reckoning is needed. The sufficiency is part of the verse's polemic. The audience had been relying on the idols as intercessors; the divine Reckoner is sufficient. The sufficiency removes the need for any other reckoning.

This is one of the most theologically pointed moments in the sūrah. The divine Reckoner is sufficient, and that sufficiency is part of the verse's polemic against any imagined need for other reckoners. The verse is correcting the assumption that mediation by the supposed partners is necessary.

**The Image of the Scales**

The commentators have offered different readings of the image of the scales. Some have taken the image literally, describing the scales as being held by Gabriel and bearing some resemblance to the two-pan balance. Others have taken the image symbolically, understanding the scales as a reference to the divine weighing of actions according to the divine justice. The two readings are not exclusive. The literal image is the visible representation of the symbolic weighing.

The image places the reckoning at the level of weights and measures. The metaphor is taken from commerce and from law; the scales are the instruments by which amounts are determined. The metaphor is not casual; the metaphor is the form the verse uses to communicate the precision the divine justice requires. The precision is what the audience is being asked to recognise.

**The Verse As a Refusal of the Idols' Mediation**

The verse is implicitly a refusal of the audience's assumption that the idols might mediate the reckoning. The audience had been treating the idols as intercessors; the divine Reckoner is sufficient. The sufficiency removes the ground for the idols' intercession. The audience that continues to invoke the idols is invoking mediators who are not needed; the invocation is a misunderstanding of the divine economy. The verse is correcting the misunderstanding.

The correction is part of the sūrah's larger polemic. The sūrah has been removing the supports of the polytheistic framework one by one. The verse at 21:47 removes the support that depends on the supposed insufficiency of the divine Reckoner. The removal is precise; the sufficiency of the Reckoner is the substance of the removal.

**A Verse That Connects to the Earlier Universal Mortality**

The verse is connected to 21:35, which stated that every soul will taste death and will be returned. The verse at 21:47 specifies what the return will involve. The return will be a reckoning; the reckoning will be done with scales of justice; no deed will escape the reckoning. The return is therefore not an arbitrary event; the return is the moment when the universal mortality is followed by the universal reckoning. The verse at 21:47 is the specification of what the return means in practice. The specification is the substance of the verse's call; the call is the audience's invitation to take the specification seriously.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:48

> Indeed, We granted Moses and Aaron the standard ˹to distinguish between right and wrong˺—a light and a reminder for the righteous

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Furqān Given to Moses and Aaron**

The verse announces the giving of the *furqān* — the criterion, the standard, the means by which truth is distinguished from falsehood. *Wa la-qad āṭaynā Mūsā wa Hārūna al-furqān* — and We gave Moses and Aaron the criterion. The use of *la-qad* — certainly, indeed — marks the verse as a decisive declaration. The criterion was given; the giving is part of the divine economy. The giving is to two specific messengers — Moses and Aaron — who are mentioned together here as elsewhere in the Qur'an (23:45; 25:35; 37:114).

The Arabic *furqān* comes from the root *f-r-q* — to divide, to separate, to distinguish. The criterion is the means of distinguishing. The Qur'an uses the term to refer to what distinguishes truth from falsehood in revelation itself. The Torah is described elsewhere as the criterion (2:53). The verse is therefore naming the Torah as the criterion given to Moses and Aaron; the naming is part of the verse's polemic against the audience who would deny that God has given such criteria to His messengers.

**The Identification of the Criterion**

The commentators have offered different identifications of the *furqān*. Al-Ṭabarī reads it as referring to the Torah itself. Al-Rāzī reads it as referring to the help that God gave Moses in his mission — the parting of the sea, the destruction of Pharaoh, and other signs. The two readings are not exclusive. The Torah is the criterion in the sense that it distinguishes truth from falsehood; the help that God gave is the criterion in the sense that it demonstrated the truth of Moses' message. The verse can be read in both ways.

This is also one of the verses that connects the Qur'an's self-presentation to the Torah's. The Qur'an elsewhere describes itself as a criterion (25:1). The verse at 21:48 is therefore part of a broader Qur'anic argument that God has given criteria to His messengers; the criteria are what the messengers have; the messengers have been using the criteria to distinguish truth from falsehood in their respective communities. The giving of the criterion is therefore a recurring feature of the prophetic calling.

**"A Light and a Reminder"**

The verse continues *wa ḍiyāʾan wa dhikrā lil-muttaqīn* — a light and a reminder for the God-fearing. The light and the reminder are two specifications of what the criterion produces. The light is what enables the God-fearing to see; the reminder is what calls to mind what the God-fearing might otherwise forget. The two together are the form the criterion takes for those who receive it properly.

The pairing of light and reminder is a recurring Qur'anic theme. The Qur'an itself is described as a light (5:15; 7:157; 64:8) and as a reminder (15:9; 21:2, 21:10). The verse at 21:48 is therefore part of the Qur'anic self-presentation that places the Qur'an in continuity with the Torah. The Torah was a light and a reminder; the Qur'an is a light and a reminder. The two revelations have the same function; the function is what the verse is naming.

**The God-Fearing — The Audience for the Criterion**

The verse specifies the audience for the criterion: *lil-muttaqīn* — for the God-fearing, for those who are in awe of God. The verse is saying that the criterion is for a specific kind of recipient. Not everyone can receive the criterion properly; the criterion is for those who fear God. The fear is what makes the criterion operative. The God-fearing hear the criterion and respond to it; those who do not fear God hear the criterion but do not respond. The criterion's effectiveness depends on the recipient's posture.

This is one of the most distinctive features of the Qur'anic treatment of revelation. The revelation is not a neutral object that operates regardless of the recipient. The revelation is a sign that operates differently for different recipients. The God-fearing receive the revelation as light and reminder; the others receive the revelation as something to be mocked or ignored. The same revelation produces different responses; the responses depend on the recipients. The verse is asking the audience to consider which kind of recipient they are.

**A Verse That Begins a New Section**

The verse is the first in a series of verses (21:48–91) that narrate the stories of various prophets. The series begins with Moses and Aaron, then moves to Abraham (21:51–73), Lot (21:74–75), Noah (21:76–77), David and Solomon (21:78–82), Job (21:83–84), Ishmael, Enoch, and Dhu'l-Kifl (21:85–86), Jonah (21:87–88), and Zachariah, John, and Mary (21:89–91). The series of stories is one of the longest in the Qur'an.

The verse is therefore a transition. The previous verses (21:1–47) have been the sūrah's polemic against the Meccan audience and its cosmology. What the verse at 21:48 does here is introduce the new section that will narrate the prophetic histories. The criterion given to Moses and Aaron is the bridge: the criterion is what the messengers have; the messengers are the ones whose stories the sūrah is about to tell. The criterion is the doorway into the prophetic histories.

**The Continuity of the Prophetic Calling**

The verse is part of the Qur'an's argument for the continuity of the prophetic calling. The calling has been given to messengers throughout history; the calling takes different forms in different messengers, but the calling is one. The audience of the Qur'an is being invited to recognise the calling as one; the recognition is the substance of the response the verse is asking for. The audience that recognises the calling has begun to recognise that the message it is receiving is not new; the message is the same calling that has been given to messengers since the beginning.

This is one of the most theologically significant features of the Qur'anic presentation of prophecy. The Qur'an does not present the prophetic calling as a series of unrelated events; the Qur'an presents the calling as a single, continuous calling that has been given to messengers throughout history. The calling is the same; the messengers are different; the communities are different. One of the verses that makes this continuity explicit is precisely the verse at 21:48. The continuity is the substance of the verse's polemic; the polemic is the substance of the verse's call.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:49

> who are in awe of their Lord without seeing Him, and are fearful of the Hour

**Expanded Commentary**

**Two Features of the God-Fearing**

The verse describes the God-fearing whom the previous verse mentioned. The description has two parts. First, *alladhīna yukhshawna rabbahum bi-l-ghaybi* — who fear their Lord in the unseen, who are in awe of their Lord without seeing Him. Second, *wa hum min al-sāʿati mushfiqūn* — and they are wary of the Hour. The verse is therefore specifying two qualities of the God-fearing: the fear of God in the absence of visible confirmation, and the fear of the eschatological reckoning. The two qualities together describe the inner posture of those for whom the criterion is intended.

The first quality is the more demanding. To fear God in the unseen — *bi-l-ghaybi* — is to fear God in the absence of any visible evidence that the fear is warranted. The God-fearing do not see God; the God-fearing do not see the consequences of fearing or not fearing God in this world; the God-fearing nevertheless maintain the fear. The fear is therefore a posture of the heart, not a calculation of consequences. The God-fearing have interiorised the fear; the interiorisation is what makes the fear operative.

**"Bi-l-Ghaybi" — The Meaning of "In the Unseen"**

The Arabic *bi-l-ghaybi* — in the unseen, in the absence — is a precise specification. The verse is not saying that the God-fearing have no evidence for their fear; the verse is saying that the fear does not depend on the visible evidence. The God-fearing could be reassured by visible signs; they have not been reassured; they have maintained the fear. The maintenance is the substance of the fear.

The commentators have offered two readings of *bi-l-ghaybi*. Al-Rāzī reads it as referring to the God-fearing being absent from other people, praying in seclusion. Al-Qurṭubī reads it as referring to the fact that the God-fearing are not present before God and the realities of the Hereafter; they cannot perceive God directly, but they know Him through faith and reflection. The two readings are not exclusive. The seclusion from other people is one form of the unseen; the absence from the direct perception of God is another. Both readings emphasise the same point: the fear does not depend on visible confirmation.

**"Wary of the Hour"**

The second quality — *wa hum min al-sāʿati mushfiqūn* — and they are wary of the Hour — specifies the eschatological dimension of the God-fearing's posture. The verb *ashfaqa* — to be wary, to be anxious, to be on guard — names a specific kind of fear. The God-fearing are not terrified by the Hour; the God-fearing are wary. The wariness is the appropriate response to an event of enormous significance; the event is not yet arrived; the wariness is the form the response takes.

The combination of the two qualities is significant. The God-fearing fear God in the unseen and fear the Hour. The two fears are not contradictory; the two fears are mutually reinforcing. The fear of God in the unseen prepares the God-fearing for the Hour; the fear of the Hour deepens the fear of God in the unseen. The two together constitute the inner posture of those who have received the criterion properly.

**The Verse As a Specification of the Audience's Invitation**

The verse is implicitly an invitation to the audience. The audience is being asked to consider whether it has the qualities of the God-fearing. The audience is being asked to consider whether it fears God in the unseen. What the audience is being asked to consider is whether it is wary of the Hour. The audience's current posture is the opposite: the audience is not fearing God in the unseen; the audience is not wary of the Hour. The audience's posture is the posture of those who have refused the criterion.

The invitation is the verse's call. What the verse is asking for here is that the audience change the posture. The change is the substance of the response the verse is asking for. The change does not require visible signs; the change is interior. The change is the willingness to fear God without seeing, to be wary of the Hour without its arrival. The willingness is what the verse is asking the audience to develop.

**A Verse That Names the Standard**

The verse names a standard against which the audience's posture can be measured. The God-fearing have a specific set of qualities; the qualities are described in the verse; the audience is invited to measure itself against them. The measurement is not arbitrary; the measurement is the form the verse's call takes. The audience that measures itself has begun the work of changing. The audience that refuses to measure has refused the verse.

This is one of the most demanding features of the Qur'anic treatment of the audience's life. The verses do not just command; the verses describe standards. The standards are what the audience is asked to measure itself against; the measurement is the work the verse is asking for. The audience that measures has begun to change. The audience that refuses to measure has refused the verse.

**The Verse As a Connection to the Earlier Cosmological Argument**
