The verse connects to the cosmological argument of 21:30–33. The argument was that the heavens and the earth are designed; the audience is invited to recognise the design. The verse at 21:49 is asking for the recognition to issue in a specific kind of posture: the fear of God in the unseen. The posture is what the recognition is supposed to produce. The audience that recognises the design but does not develop the posture has not yet integrated the recognition with the response. The integration is what the verse is asking for.

This is one of the most theologically significant moves in the sūrah. The cosmological argument is not just a logical argument; it is a call for a particular kind of life. The life is the life of those who fear God in the unseen. The argument without the life is incomplete; the life without the argument is ungrounded. The verse is asking the audience to integrate the argument and the life. The integration is the substance of the response the verse is asking for.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:50

> And this ˹Quran˺ is a blessed reminder which We have revealed. Will you ˹pagans˺ then deny it

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Qur'an As a Blessed Reminder**

The verse names the Qur'an as a blessed reminder. *Wa hādha dhikrun mubārun unazzalnāhu* — and this is a blessed reminder We have revealed. The Arabic *dhikr* — reminder, remembrance — is one of the Qur'an's self-designations (15:9; 21:2, 21:10, 21:48). The verse is using the Qur'an's own word for itself. What is offered here is a blessed reminder; the blessed reminder has been revealed.

The use of *dhikr* — remembrance, reminder — is significant. The word is the same word used in 21:2 for the previous reminders; the word is the same word used in 21:24 for the Reminder of those before the Prophet; the word is the same word used in 21:48 for the Torah given to Moses and Aaron. The Qur'an is therefore being placed in a continuous series: each revelation is a reminder; each reminder is in continuity with the others; the continuity is what the verse is naming.

**"Blessed" — The Arabic "Mubārak"**

The Arabic *mubārak* — blessed — is a word that means not only "full of blessing" but also "a source of blessing." What is offered is not just blessed; it is a source of blessing. The blessing flows from the Qur'an to those who receive it. The receiving is the form the blessing takes. The verse is therefore not just making a claim about the Qur'an; the verse is making a claim about the audience's relationship to the Qur'an. Whoever receives the Qur'an will be blessed; whoever refuses will have refused the source of blessing.

The word *mubārak* also has connotations of being sustained and perpetuated. A blessed thing is a thing whose effects continue beyond the moment of its origin. The Qur'an's effects continue beyond the moment of revelation. The effects are what the audience is being asked to consider. Whoever considers the effects has begun to receive the Qur'an; whoever refuses to consider has refused the blessing.

**"Which We Have Revealed" — The Source of the Reminder**

The verse continues *unazzalnāhu* — We have revealed it. The verb *nazzala* — to reveal, to send down — is the Qur'an's standard verb for the divine act of revelation. The use of the first person plural is the divine speech; the divine speech is the source of the reminder. What is presented here is not the Prophet's composition; it is what God has revealed. The revealing is the form the source takes.

This is one of the most significant theological claims in the Qur'an. The Qur'an is what God has revealed; the audience is being asked to receive it on that basis. Whoever refuses on the basis that it is the Prophet's composition has refused the source of the Qur'an. Whoever receives the Qur'an as the revelation of God has received the source. The verse is asking the audience to recognise the source; the recognition is the substance of the response the verse is asking for.

**"Will You Then Deny It?"**

The verse closes with *afa-antum lahu munkirūn* — will you then deny it? The verb *ankara* — to deny, to refuse to acknowledge, to be ungrateful — names the form the audience's refusal would take. The audience is being asked whether it will be ungrateful for the reminder; the asking is the verse's call. The call is rhetorical; the verse expects the audience to recognise the ungratefulness as ungratefulness and to change.

The use of *anta* — you — is in the second person plural. The verse is addressing the audience directly. The audience is being asked, as a community, whether it will deny the reminder. The asking is a public act; the public act is the form the call takes. Whoever acknowledges the reminder has begun the response the verse is asking for; whoever denies has refused the verse.

**A Verse That Returns to the Sūrah's Central Theme**

The verse returns to one of the sūrah's central themes. The sūrah has been asking the audience to recognise what has been given: the messengers (21:7–9); the criterion (21:48); the reminder (21:2, 21:10, 21:24, 21:48). The verse at 21:50 adds the Qur'an itself to the list. What is presented here is a blessed reminder; it has been revealed; the audience is being asked whether it will deny what has been revealed.

This is one of the most focused moments of the sūrah's polemic. The polemic has been building; the focus is now on the Qur'an itself. The audience is being asked to consider the Qur'an as a blessed reminder; the audience is being asked to consider the Qur'an as a divine revelation; the audience is being asked to consider whether the denial makes sense in light of what has been described. The question is the verse's call. The call is the substance of the response the verse is asking for.

**A Verse That Connects to the Reader of the Qur'an**

The verse is one of the most reflexive in the Qur'an. The verse is part of the Qur'an; the verse describes the Qur'an; the verse is asking the reader of the Qur'an to recognise what the reader is reading. The reflexivity is not accidental. Every generation of readers is being asked to consider what they are receiving. The receiving is the form the response takes. The verse is asking the readers to receive the Qur'an as a blessed reminder; the receiving is what the verse is calling for.

The reflexivity is also a form of comfort. Whoever receives the Qur'an is participating in the moment that the verse is naming. The verse is not just speaking to a past audience; the verse is speaking to every audience that has been receiving the Qur'an. The receiving is the same receiving across the centuries; each generation is asked to make the receiving explicit. The making-explicit is the substance of the response the verse is asking for. The response is the same response; the generations are different; the substance is one.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:51

> And indeed, We had granted Abraham sound judgment early on, for We knew him well ˹to be worthy of it˺

**Expanded Commentary**

**Sound Judgment Before the Office**

The verse announces a gift that preceded the prophetic office. *Wa la-qad āṭaynā Ibrāhīma rushdahu min qablu* — and We had given Abraham his sound judgment before. The Arabic *rushd* — right guidance, sound judgment, mature comprehension — names a quality of the intellect and the will. The gift is the gift of being rightly oriented before the formal office of prophethood was conferred. The verse is therefore making a distinction between the gift of orientation and the gift of office. Both come from God; the order is significant.

The commentators, including al-Ṭabarī and al-Rāzī, have offered different readings of the timing. Al-Ṭabarī reads it as referring to Abraham's right guidance before Moses and Aaron; al-Rāzī and al-Qurṭubī read it as referring to Abraham's right guidance even as a child, before the formal conferral of prophethood. The readings are not exclusive. The verse is making the general point that Abraham was given the orientation before the office; the specification of the timing is secondary to the point.

**"We Knew Him" — The Divine Knowledge As Ground**

The verse continues *wa kunnā bihi ʿālimīn* — and We knew him well. The verb *ʿalima* — to know — is being used here in the divine sense: the comprehensive knowledge that grounds the giving. The gift of sound judgment was given because God knew that Abraham would use it well. The knowing precedes the giving; the giving is grounded in the knowing. The verse is therefore presenting a particular divine economy: the gifts are given in response to the recipients who will use them; the recipients are known before the gifts are given.

This is one of the most theologically significant features of the Qur'anic treatment of the divine economy. The gifts are not given arbitrarily; the gifts are given to those who will use them properly. The using is known in advance; the knowledge is what makes the giving appropriate. The verse is therefore not just saying that Abraham received a gift; the verse is saying that Abraham was the kind of recipient who would receive the gift well.

**A Transition Verse**

The verse is the door into the longest narrative section of the sūrah. The verses that follow (21:52–73) narrate the story of Abraham's confrontation with his people and the idols. The verse at 21:51 prepares the audience for the story by naming Abraham's qualification. The qualification is the sound judgment; the sound judgment is what will enable Abraham to recognise the absurdity of the idols and to articulate it. The recognition and the articulation are what the rest of the narrative describes.

The transition is also a typological move. The Prophet of Islam has been the recipient of similar gifts; the audience of the Qur'an is being invited to consider what the gifts are and what they imply. The story of Abraham is the model for the prophetic calling; the model is what the audience is being asked to recognise. The recognition is part of the response the verses are asking for.

**The Sound Judgment As Intellectual and Moral**

The Arabic *rushd* names more than the correct opinion. It names the sound judgment that includes the intellectual capacity to recognise the truth, the moral disposition to act on the recognition, and the practical capacity to implement the action. The gift is therefore comprehensive; the gift touches the whole person. The whole person is what God has given Abraham; the giving is what the verse is naming.

This comprehensive reading is consistent with the rest of the Abraham narrative. Abraham is not just a thinker; he is a doer. He thinks the truth about the idols; he also acts on the truth by smashing them; he also defends the action before his people. The thinking, the acting, and the defending are all parts of the same sound judgment. What the verse at 21:51 is offering is the gift that produces all three. The gift is comprehensive; the products are the rest of the narrative.

**A Refusal of the Inheritance Argument**

The verses that follow will record the audience of Abraham's complaint: "We found our fathers worshipping them" (21:53). The complaint is the argument from inheritance. The complaint says that the worship is justified because the fathers did it. The verse at 21:51 is implicitly a refusal of the inheritance argument. The sound judgment that Abraham received was given to him before he inherited anything from his fathers; the judgment came from God, not from inheritance. The inheritance is what produced the error; the gift from God is what produced the truth.

The refusal is significant because the inheritance argument was the audience's main defence throughout the sūrah. The audience had been saying, implicitly, that they could not be blamed for following what they had been given by their fathers. The verse is saying that what the fathers gave can be wrong; what God gives is what produces the truth. The audience is being asked to recognise that the inheritance argument is not a defence against the truth; the inheritance argument is part of the error.

**A Verse That Sets the Stage for the Rest of the Sūrah**

The verse is the first in the series of prophetic narratives that will dominate the second half of the sūrah. The series will move from Abraham to Lot, Noah, David and Solomon, Job, Ishmael, Enoch, Dhu'l-Kifl, Jonah, Zachariah, John, and Mary. Each of the figures will be introduced with the gift that God gave them. What the verse at 21:51 becomes is the model for those later introductions. The model is: God gave; God knew; the gift was appropriate to the recipient. The introduction is the form the divine economy takes when applied to the prophetic calling.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:52

> ˹Remember˺ when he questioned his father and his people, “What are these statues to which you are so devoted?”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Question That Begins the Argument**

The verse opens the Abraham narrative. *Idh qāla li-abīhi wa qawmihi mā hādhihi al-tamāthīlu allatī antum lahā ʿākidūn* — when he said to his father and his people, what are these statues to which you are devoted? The Arabic *tamāthīl* — statues, likenesses, images — names the objects of the audience's worship. The verb *ʿakada* — to cleave to, to be devoted to, to adhere to — names the form the worship takes. The verse is asking what these objects are and why they are the objects of such devotion.

The question is the form the Abraham narrative takes. The narrative is not presented as a long philosophical disproof of idolatry; the narrative is presented as a question. The question is the substance of the argument. The argument is the form the invitation takes.

**The Question to His Father and His People**

The verse specifies the addressees: *li-abīhi wa qawmihi* — to his father and to his people. The father is mentioned first; the people are mentioned second. The order is significant. Abraham's confrontation is not just a public argument with a community; the confrontation is a familial argument. The father and the community are addressed together; the familial and the public are joined. The argument cannot be separated from the personal; the personal is part of the public.

The specification of *qawmihi* — his people — also reminds the audience of the Qur'anic claim that messengers are sent to their own communities. Abraham is sent to his own people; the sending is the form the prophetic calling takes in this narrative. The Qur'an returns to this pattern throughout the prophetic narratives. The pattern is what the verse is reminding the audience of; the reminder is part of the verse's purpose.

**"What Are These Statues?" — The Question As Beginning**

The question — *mā hādhihi al-tamāthīl* — what are these statues? — is a question that requires an answer. The audience's worship of statues is taken as given; the verse is asking what the statues are. The question is not a request for information; the question is a request for justification. The audience is being asked to justify the devotion. The justification will be tried in the verses that follow.

The verse is also doing something more subtle. The question takes the audience's worship as the object of investigation. The audience had been treating the worship as self-justifying; the verse is refusing that treatment. The worship can be questioned; the worship must be justified; the audience is being asked to do the justifying. The asking is the form the verse's call takes; the call is the substance of the response the verse is asking for.

**"To Which You Are So Devoted"**

The closing phrase — *allatī antum lahā ʿākidūn* — to which you are devoted — specifies the form of the audience's relationship to the statues. The Arabic *ʿakada* — to cling, to devote, to be devoted — is a strong word for attachment. The verse is saying that the audience's relationship to the statues is not casual; the relationship is the form the audience's life has taken. The asking is therefore not just about the statues; the asking is about the audience's life. The life is what the verse is asking the audience to reconsider.

The reconsideration is the substance of the verse's call. The audience is being asked to look at what it has been devoted to and to consider whether the devotion is appropriate. The consideration is not abstract; the consideration is the form the audience's self-examination takes when the audience is asked to look at its own attachments. The looking is the work the verse is asking for; the work is the substance of the response the verse is calling for.

**Connection to Other Qur'anic Versions of the Abraham Narrative**

The same question appears in other Qur'anic accounts of Abraham. 6:75 says: *And thus We showed Abraham the kingdom of the heavens and the earth, that he might be of those possessing certainty*. 19:42 says: *Mention in the Book Abraham; he was a man of truth, a prophet*. 26:70 says: *And when he said to his father and his people, "What do you worship?"* 37:85 says: *When he said to his father and his people, "What do you worship?"* 43:26 says: *And when Abraham said to his father Azar, "Do you take idols as gods?"*

The recurrence of the same question is part of the Qur'anic treatment of the Abraham narrative. The same question is asked in slightly different forms in different sūrahs; the differences are part of the variations in the narratives. The asking is consistent; the audience is being asked the same question in many forms. The asking is what the Qur'an is doing throughout; the asking is the substance of the call to Abraham; the call is what the audience is being invited to share.

**A Verse That Begins the Argument the Audience Will Continue**

The verse begins an argument that the Abraham narrative will continue. The argument is not just Abraham's; the argument is the form the divine economy takes in this sūrah. The argument will proceed through the smashing of the idols (21:58), the dialogue with his people (21:59–67), and the command to burn him alive (21:68). Each move in the argument is part of the larger polemic against the audience's worship of idols. The argument is what the narrative is about; the narrative is what the sūrah is doing.

The argument is also a model for the audience's situation. The audience of the Qur'an is in a position analogous to Abraham's audience. The audience has been worshipping what has no right to be worshipped. The audience is being asked to consider the worship; the asking is the form the call takes. The call is the substance of the response the sūrah is asking for; the response is the same response Abraham's audience was asked to give. The asking continues; the audience is being invited to respond.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:53

> They replied, “We found our forefathers worshipping them.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Argument from Inheritance**

The verse records Abraham's people's reply. *Qālū wajadnā ābāʾanā lahā ʿābidīn* — they said, we found our fathers worshipping them. The Arabic *wajadnā* — we found — names the form of the report. The worship was not invented by the audience; the worship was inherited. The audience is therefore presenting the worship as something they received, not something they chose. The argument is the argument from inheritance: we do what we were taught to do.

This is one of the most common arguments the Qur'an has been refuting throughout. 5:104 records the same defence: *We found our fathers doing this*. 7:28 says: *And when they commit an immorality, they say, "We found our fathers doing this."* 31:21 says: *And when it is said to them, follow what God has revealed, they say, "Rather, we will follow that which we found our fathers doing."* The recurrence is the consistent application of the Qur'anic refusal: the inheritance argument is not a defence against the truth.

**The Defensive Structure of the Reply**

The reply is defensive. The audience is not asked to justify the worship in principle; the audience is asked to justify the worship by reference to what they inherited. The justification is the form the audience's resistance takes. The audience does not say why the worship is appropriate; the audience says that the worship is what the fathers did. The saying is the form the refusal takes; the refusal is what the verse is recording.

The defensive structure is significant because it reveals the audience's posture. The audience does not have an argument for the worship; the audience has an excuse for the worship. The argument would need to address the question Abraham asked (what are these statues?); the excuse only addresses the source of the practice (we got it from our fathers). The verse is exposing the audience's posture by recording the deflection. The deflection is the form the audience's resistance takes.

**The Precedent of Similar Refusals**

The Qur'an records similar arguments from inheritance in multiple places. 43:22–23 records the polytheists' response to the Prophet: *We found our fathers following a religion, and we are, upon their footsteps, rightly guided*. 2:170 records: *And when it is said to them, follow what God has revealed, they say, "Rather, we will follow that which we found our fathers doing."* The recurrence is the consistent application of the principle that the inheritance argument is the argument of those who have refused to think for themselves.

The pattern is also significant because the same defence was given to earlier messengers. The audience of Moses, the audience of Noah, the audience of every prophet in the Qur'anic narrative gave the same defence. The defence is the form the refusal takes in every age. The Qur'an is therefore not just refuting a specific Meccan defence; the Qur'an is refuting the universal form of the defence. The refutation is part of the sūrah's polemic; the polemic is what the audience is being asked to recognise.

**A Refusal Without Argument**

The reply is not an argument; the reply is a report. The audience does not offer reasons for the worship; the audience offers the historical fact of the worship. The fact is being offered as a substitute for reasons. The substitution is the form the refusal takes. The audience is saying: we do not need to give reasons because the practice is established. The saying is the form the audience's resistance to thinking takes.

The verse is therefore asking the audience to do something the audience is not willing to do: to give reasons. The reasons would require examining the practice; the examination would require looking at the statues and considering what they are. The audience has refused to do the examination; the refusal is what the verse is recording. The recording is part of the verse's polemic. The audience is being shown what its refusal looks like when stated explicitly.

**The Verse As an Invitation to Examine**

The verse is implicitly an invitation to examine the inheritance argument. The audience has offered the inheritance; the audience is being asked to consider whether the inheritance is sufficient. The asking is the form the verse's call takes. The audience that considers the inheritance is being asked to consider what the inheritance actually consists of. The consideration is the form the audience's self-examination takes.

The self-examination is one of the recurring demands of the Qur'anic address. The audience is being asked to look at what it has been doing and to consider whether the doing makes sense. The making-sense is the substance of the response the verses are asking for. The audience that does the self-examination has begun the response; the audience that refuses has refused the verses.

**A Bridge to the Rest of the Abraham Narrative**

The verse is the audience's contribution to the Abraham dialogue. The dialogue will continue in 21:54 with Abraham's reply: *Certainly you and your fathers have been in manifest error*. The reply will refuse the inheritance argument; the refusal will be the form the prophetic argument takes. The argument will proceed through the smashing of the idols and the trial. The trial is the culmination of the argument; the argument is the substance of the narrative.

The verse is therefore a bridge between Abraham's question (21:52) and Abraham's reply (21:54). The bridge is the audience's refusal to think; the refusal is what the narrative is documenting. The documentation is part of the sūrah's polemic against the contemporary audience; the polemic is what the audience is being asked to recognise. The recognition is the substance of the response the sūrah is asking for.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:54

> He responded, “Indeed, you and your forefathers have been clearly astray.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Direct Refusal of the Inheritance Argument**

The verse is Abraham's reply to the inheritance argument. *Qāla la-qad kuntum antum wa ābāʾukum fī ḍalālin mubīn* — he said, indeed you and your fathers have been in manifest error. The reply is direct; the directness is the form the prophetic speech takes. Abraham does not soften the diagnosis; he states it. The heirs of a wrong practice are wrong; the fathers were wrong; the wrong has been transmitted.

The Arabic *ḍalāl* — error, misguidance, going astray — names the kind of error the verse is naming. The error is not a minor misstep; the error is *mubīn* — manifest, clear, evident. The verse is not saying that the audience might have been mistaken in some subtle way; the verse is saying that the audience was clearly mistaken. The clear-mistaken is the substance of the verse's diagnosis.

**The Plural "You and Your Fathers"**

The verse names both the audience and the fathers. *Antum wa ābāʾukum* — you and your fathers. The plural includes both the present worshippers and the past ones. The verse is refusing the inheritance argument by extending the error across generations. The error is not something the audience picked up recently; the error is something the audience has been receiving for a long time. The long reception does not validate the practice; the long reception makes the error deeper.

The plural is also a refusal of the assumption that the fathers could be wrong but the children are not. The verse is saying that the error is the same in both generations; the same error has been transmitted; the transmission does not break the error. The inheritance argument presupposes that the transmission preserves the practice; the verse is saying that the transmission preserves the error. The preservation is the substance of the verse's polemic.

**The Manifestness of the Error**

The Arabic *mubīn* — manifest, evident, clear — names a specific quality of the error. The error is not hidden; the error is evident to anyone who looks. The manifestness is part of the verse's polemic. The audience had been treating the error as something only the heretics would notice; the verse is saying that anyone who looks will see it. The looking is what the verse is asking for; the seeing is what the looking produces.

The manifestness also has implications for the audience's responsibility. The audience cannot claim that the error was hidden. The error was evident; the audience was surrounded by evidence; the audience chose the error anyway. The choosing is what the verse is naming; the naming is part of the verse's polemic. The audience that has chosen the evident error is the audience that has done so with the evidence before it. The choice is what the audience is being asked to reconsider.

**A Refusal of the Imitation Argument**

The verse is implicitly a refusal of the imitation argument that 5:104 records: *We found our fathers doing this*. The imitation is the substance of the inheritance argument. The verse is saying that imitation does not validate what is imitated. The imitated practice can be wrong; the imitation is wrong to the extent that it perpetuates the wrong. The perpetuation is what the verse is naming; the naming is the substance of the polemic.

This is one of the recurring Qur'anic themes. The Qur'an refuses to accept the imitation of error as a defence. The audience that imitates is responsible for the imitation; the responsibility cannot be transferred to the imitated model. The audience is asked to consider what it is doing, not what its fathers did. The consideration is the form the verse's call takes; the call is the substance of the response the verse is asking for.

**A Verse That Connects to the Broader Qur'anic Treatment of the Inheritance Argument**

The verse is part of the broader Qur'anic treatment of the inheritance argument. 7:28 records: *And when they commit an immorality, they say, "We found our fathers doing this." God has certainly enjoined immorality and disbelief to them.* 31:21 records: *And when it is said to them, follow what God has revealed, they say, "Rather, we will follow that which we found our fathers doing."* 43:23 records: *And We found our fathers following a religion, and we are, in their footsteps, rightly guided.*

The recurrence is the consistent application of the principle: the inheritance argument is not a defence against the truth. The principle is what the verse is asking the audience to recognise. The recognition is the form the response takes; the response is what the verse is asking for.

**A Verse That Begins the Argument That Will Continue**

The verse begins the argument that will continue through 21:55–73. The argument will include the smashing of the idols (21:58), the dialogue about who smashed them (21:59–67), and the attempt to burn Abraham alive (21:68). The argument will end with Abraham being saved by the fire (21:69). The argument is the form the Abraham narrative takes; the form is what the verse is beginning.

The verse is therefore a transitional moment. The audience has been asked a question (21:52); the audience has offered a defence (21:53); Abraham has refused the defence (21:54). The refusal sets up the rest of the argument. The argument is what the narrative is about; the narrative is what the sūrah is using to address its own audience. The audience of the Qur'an is being asked to consider the Abraham narrative as a mirror. The mirror is what the verse is offering; the considering is what the response requires.

The argument that follows will also be a model for the audience's situation. The audience of the Qur'an is in a position analogous to Abraham's audience. The audience has been worshipping what has no right to be worshipped; the audience is being asked to consider the worship. The asking is the form the call takes; the call is the substance of the response the sūrah is asking for; the response is the same response Abraham's audience was asked to give.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:55

> They asked, “Have you come to us with the truth, or is this a joke?”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Audience's Reclassification**

The verse records Abraham's people's response to his diagnosis. *Qālū a-jīʾtanā bi-l-ḥaqqi am anta min al-lāʿibīn* — they said, have you brought us the truth, or are you among those who jest? The verse shows the audience reclassifying what Abraham has said. The earlier accusation (in the sūrah's polemic against the Meccan audience) was that the message is play (21:2). The same reclassification is happening here. Abraham has said something that does not fit the audience's framework; the audience is responding by reclassifying it as either truth (which the audience would not want to accept) or play (which the audience can dismiss).

The reclassification is the form the audience's resistance takes. The audience does not engage with what Abraham has actually said; the audience places what Abraham has said in a category that allows the dismissal. The category is either truth or play; the audience prefers the category of play because play can be ignored. The ignoring is what the verse is documenting; the documentation is part of the verse's polemic.

**The Connection to the Recurring Pattern**

The commentators note that the same pattern recurs in the story of Moses, where a difficult command was met with the accusation of mockery. The pattern is the same: a messenger delivers a difficult or unwelcome message; the audience accuses the messenger of mocking. The accusation is the form the resistance takes; the resistance is what the verse is recording.

The recurrence is the consistent application of the principle that the audience's resistance takes the form of accusing the messenger of play. The principle is what the verse is asking the audience of the Qur'an to recognise. The recognition is the form the response takes; the response is what the verse is calling for.

**Truth or Play — The Audience's Binary**

The binary — truth or play — is the binary the audience has been using throughout the sūrah. The audience had earlier accused the Prophet of producing confused dreams, fabricated speech, or poetry (21:5). The accusations were ways of avoiding the truth of the message. The reclassification at 21:55 is doing the same work. The audience is offering Abraham the choice: admit you are jesting, or claim to be telling the truth and be dismissed as making extraordinary claims. The choice is rigged; the rigging is the form the audience's defence takes.

The rigging is what the verse is exposing. What we see here is the audience working with a binary that excludes the possibility that the messenger is telling the truth and the audience is mistaken. The exclusion is the substance of the audience's resistance. The audience is being asked to consider whether the binary is appropriate; the consideration is the form the verse's call takes.

**"Or Are You Among Those Who Jest?"**

The question — *am anta min al-lāʿibīn* — is one of the most theologically pointed in the Abraham narrative. The audience is accusing Abraham of jesting because the audience cannot fit what Abraham has said into the framework. The framework cannot accommodate a messenger who diagnoses the audience's worship as clear error. The diagnosis is therefore reclassified as jest; the reclassification is what allows the dismissal.

The dismissal is what the verse is recording. What we witness is the audience in the act of dismissing what cannot be dismissed. The dismissal is the form the resistance takes. The audience is being asked to consider what the dismissal has cost. The cost is the cost of refusing to hear the diagnosis; the cost is the form the resistance takes when it has been sustained for a long time.

**A Verse That Reframes the Sūrah's Theme of Play**

The verse returns to the theme of play that has been running through the sūrah. 21:2 described the audience as listening jokingly; 21:15 asked whether the destruction of the wrongdoers was divine amusement; 21:16 stated that God did not create the heavens and the earth in play. The verse at 21:55 is the audience's return to the theme of play. The audience is accusing Abraham of playing; the audience is using the vocabulary of play to dismiss what cannot be dismissed.

The Qur'an has been refusing the vocabulary of play throughout. The vocabulary is the form the audience's resistance takes; the vocabulary cannot accommodate the truth. The refusal is the form the sūrah's polemic takes. What the vocabulary does is shut the audience off from the recognition the verse is calling for; the call requires the recognition to be freed.

**A Verse That Invites a Different Response**

The verse is implicitly an invitation to a different response. The audience is asked to consider whether Abraham has come with the truth. The asking is the form the verse's call takes. The audience that considers the question is the audience that has begun to respond. The response is the substance of what the verse is asking for; the asking is the form the call takes.

The invitation extends to the audience of the Qur'an. The audience of the Qur'an is in the same position as Abraham's audience. The audience has been receiving the message; the audience is being asked to consider whether the message is true or whether it is play. The consideration is the form the response takes. The audience that considers has begun the response; the audience that refuses has refused the verse.

**The Cost of Treating the Messenger As a Jester**

The verse leaves the audience with a question they will spend the rest of the narrative answering. If Abraham is jesting, then his smashing of the idols is a prank; the prank can be punished and the matter can be closed. If Abraham is telling the truth, then the worship of the idols is a manifest error; the error requires more than punishment. The audience that has chosen to call him a jester has chosen the path that closes the matter; the audience that considers the alternative has opened the matter. The opening is what the demonstration is for; the demonstration is the form the prophetic argument takes.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:56

> He replied, “In fact, your Lord is the Lord of the heavens and the earth, Who created them ˹both˺. And to that I bear witness.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Direct Affirmation**

The verse is Abraham's reply to the question of truth or play. *Qāla bal rabbukum rabbu al-samāwāti wa al-arḍi alladhī faṭarahunna wa anā ʿalā dhālika min al-shāhidīn* — he said, rather, your Lord is the Lord of the heavens and the earth, who originated them, and I am among those who bear witness to this. Abraham is not answering the question as posed; he is reframing the entire conversation. The reframing is the substance of the reply.

The Arabic *bal* — rather — is the same particle that overturns an earlier claim. The earlier claim was "truth or play"; the *bal* refuses both alternatives and proposes a third. The third is that God is the Lord of the heavens and the earth. The third is what Abraham has been trying to communicate; the refusal of the binary is the form the communication takes.

**"Your Lord Is the Lord of the Heavens and the Earth"**

The verse names the Lord as the Lord of the heavens and the earth. The naming is a polemical move. The audience had been worshipping statues (21:52); the statues are not the Lord of the heavens and the earth. The Lord of the heavens and the earth is the One who originated them; the originating is the form the Lordship takes. The worship of statues is therefore not just a different choice; it is a refusal of the actual Lord.

The verse is also a repetition of the theme that has been running through the sūrah. 21:19 said: *To Him belong all those in the heavens and the earth*. 21:22 said: *If there had been gods other than God in them, they would have been corrupted*. 21:30 said: *Do they not see that the heavens and the earth were a single mass, then We split them apart*. The verse at 21:56 is Abraham's form of the same theme. The form is what the verse is asking the audience of the Qur'an to recognise.

**"Who Created Them" — The Act of Origination**

The Arabic *faṭara* — to originate, to create, to bring into being — names the act by which God brought the heavens and the earth into existence. The act is the foundation of the Lordship. The One who originated the heavens and the earth is the Lord of the heavens and the earth. The audience that worships statues is refusing to acknowledge the one who originated; the refusal is the form the audience's error takes.

This is one of the most theologically significant moments in the Abraham narrative. Abraham is not just saying that God is great; Abraham is saying that God is the originator of the heavens and the earth. The origination is the form the divine uniqueness takes. The audience that worships statues has refused to recognise the uniqueness; the refusal is what Abraham is addressing. The addressing is the substance of the prophetic argument.

**"And to That I Bear Witness"**

The verse closes with *wa anā ʿalā dhālika min al-shāhidīn* — and I am among those who bear witness to this. Abraham places himself among those who witness to the Lordship. The witnessing is the form the prophetic calling takes. Abraham is not just stating the truth; Abraham is testifying to it. The testimony is what makes his speech prophetic.

The verb *shāhid* — witness — is one of the Qur'an's most theologically loaded words. The witness is one who has seen; the seeing is what makes the testimony authoritative. Abraham has seen the truth; the seeing is what qualifies him to bear witness. The audience is being asked to consider the authority of the witness. The consideration is the form the verse's call takes.

**A Verse That Reframes the Audience's Question**

The audience had asked: truth or play? Abraham reframes: not truth or play, but recognition or refusal of the actual Lord. The reframing is one of the most powerful rhetorical moves in the Qur'anic narrative. The audience's question presupposed a binary that excludes the recognition of the actual Lord. The reframing breaks the binary and forces the audience to consider a third option. The third option is the recognition; the recognition is the form the response takes.

This is one of the recurring features of the prophetic address in the Qur'an. The messengers do not enter the framework of the audience's questions; the messengers reframe the framework. The reframing is the substance of the prophetic speech. The audience that accepts the reframing has begun the response; the audience that refuses has refused the speech.

**A Verse That Returns to the Qur'an's Central Theme**

The verse is one of the most explicit formulations of the Qur'an's central theme: the oneness of God as the Lord of the heavens and the earth. The verse will be repeated and varied throughout the Qur'an. The recurrence is the consistent application of the principle: the audience is being asked to recognise the Lord of the heavens and the earth, and the recognition is the substance of the response the Qur'an is asking for. The asking is the form the call takes; the call is what the verse is offering the audience.

**The Witness Who Stands Inside the Truth**

The Arabic *shāhid* — witness — implies more than a bystander. The witness is one who has standing to testify because of direct acquaintance with what is attested. Abraham places himself among those who know what they are talking about; he is not reporting a received opinion, he is naming something he has seen. The audience's framework cannot accommodate this kind of authority because their framework has no category for a messenger who has seen what he speaks of. The framework only has categories for received opinions, ancestral reports, or fabrications. Abraham is none of those.

The polemic is therefore not just about the content of the testimony; it is about the standing of the witness. The standing is the kind that comes from a direct encounter with what is being witnessed. The audience of the Qur'an in every age is invited to consider what it would mean to be a witness in this sense — not just to repeat the testimony but to stand inside what is being attested. That standing is the form of response the verse is asking for; the asking is the substance of the call.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:57

> ˹Then he said to himself,˺ “By Allah! I will surely plot against your idols after you have turned your backs and gone away.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Plan That Begins in Private**

The verse records Abraham's private decision. *Wa ta Allāhi la-akīdanna aṣnāmakum baʿda an tuwallū mudbirīn* — and by God, I will surely plot against your idols after you have turned your backs and gone away. The Arabic *la-akīdanna* — I will surely scheme, I will surely plan — names a determined intention. The determination is sworn by God; the swearing is part of what makes the determination binding. The plan is not a momentary thought; the plan is a resolution.

The verse is unusual in the Qur'anic narrative because it records an interior decision. Most of the verses that record prophetic speech record what the prophet said to the audience. This verse records what the prophet said to himself. The interior is being shown; the showing is part of what the narrative is doing. The audience is being invited to see the interior life of the messenger; the seeing is what the verse is offering.

**"After You Have Turned Your Backs"**

The verse specifies the timing: *baʿda an tuwallū mudbirīn* — after you have turned your backs. The verb *wallā* — to turn — and the active participle *mudbirīn* — turning away — name the form the audience's departure will take. The audience will leave; the idols will be unattended; the plan can be executed. The plan depends on the departure; the departure is the form the timing takes.

The verse is also implicitly characterising the audience. The audience turns its back on the idols; the turning is what makes the idols vulnerable. The audience that worships the idols does not stay to defend them; the audience goes away. The going is what the verse is naming; the naming is part of the polemic. The audience's relationship to the idols is not the relationship of those who stay; the relationship is the relationship of those who turn their backs.

**Why the Plan Is Necessary**

The commentators note that Abraham's verbal argument had not been successful. The audience had refused to listen to the diagnosis; the audience had reclassified the diagnosis as play. The verbal argument had reached its limit. The plan is therefore not a refusal of the verbal argument; the plan is what follows from the verbal argument's limit. The limit is what the verse is implicitly acknowledging.

This is one of the most distinctive features of the Qur'anic treatment of the prophetic calling. The prophet is not just a speaker; the prophet is also a doer. The verbal argument is the first part of the prophetic task; the action is the second part. The action is appropriate when the verbal argument has been exhausted. The exhausting is what the verse is documenting; the action is the form the prophetic calling takes in this case.

**"By God, I Will Surely Plot"**

The swearing — *wa ta Allāhi* — is the form the determination takes. The determination is sworn by God; the swearing is the binding. The binding is part of what makes the plan executable. The plan is not a tentative thought; the plan is a sworn resolution. The resolution is what the verse is recording; the recording is what makes the resolution part of the narrative.

The swearing also has a polemical edge. Abraham swears by the God whom the audience refuses to acknowledge. The swearing is a confession of faith in the very God whose existence the audience had been treating as a matter of jest. The swearing is therefore a continuation of the prophetic speech; the swearing is the form the speech takes when it has been refused. The refusal has not silenced the prophet; the refusal has only changed the form the speech takes.

**The Plan As Demonstration**

The commentators, including al-Qurṭubī, note that after having tried verbal argument, Abraham was now planning to use a different mode of demonstration. The plan is a demonstration in the literal sense: it is meant to show something. What the plan is meant to show is the inability of the idols to defend themselves. The smashing of the idols is the demonstration; the demonstration is what the plan is for.

This is one of the most theologically significant features of the narrative. The prophet is not just telling the audience that the idols are wrong; the prophet is showing the audience that the idols cannot defend themselves. The showing is the form the prophetic demonstration takes; the demonstration is the substance of the argument against the idols. The argument is what the verses that follow will develop.

**A Verse That Connects to the Qur'an's Broader Treatment of Demonstration**

The verse is part of the broader Qur'anic treatment of demonstration as a form of prophetic speech. The Prophet Muhammad had been asked for demonstrations; the Qur'an has refused many of the requests. The refusal is the form the Qur'anic treatment of demonstration has taken; the demonstration that is part of the prophetic task is different from the demonstration the audience demands. The Abraham narrative shows what the prophetic demonstration looks like; the audience is being invited to recognise the difference between the demanded demonstration and the prophetic one. The recognition is the form the response takes.

**The Interiority of the Resolution**

The verse is one of the few in the Qur'an that records the private resolve of a prophet before the action is taken. The resolve is recorded because the resolve is part of what the narrative is teaching. The teaching is that the prophetic task involves both the verbal address and the interior preparation for what follows when the verbal address has been refused. The interior preparation is what 21:57 makes visible; the visibility is itself part of the verse's contribution. The audience of the Qur'an is being shown that the prophets are not just speakers; the prophets are also those who have prepared themselves for what speaking will cost. The preparing is part of the prophetic vocation.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:58

> So he smashed them into pieces, except the biggest of them, so they might turn to it ˹for answers˺

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Demonstration in Action**

The verse describes the execution of the plan. *Fajaʿalahum juḍādhān illā kabīran lahum la-ʿallahum ilayhi yarjiʿūn* — and he made them broken pieces, except for the largest of them, that perhaps they would return to it. The verb *jaʿala* — to make — and the noun *juḍādh* — broken pieces, fragments — name the form the action took. The idols were not just damaged; the idols were shattered into pieces. The destruction was total for all but one.

The verse's structure is precise. The action is described in a single sentence; the exception is named; the purpose of the exception is stated. The structure mirrors the rhetorical strategy of the demonstration. The demonstration is meant to force the audience to think; the exception is the part of the demonstration that forces the thinking.

**"Save the Largest of Them"**

The exception is *kabīran lahum* — the largest of them. The largest idol was preserved. The preservation is the rhetorical centre of the demonstration. By preserving the largest idol while smashing the others, Abraham created a situation in which the audience would have to ask who smashed the idols. The answer could only be the largest idol. The audience would have to recognise that the largest idol, which they had treated as the most valuable, was the only candidate for the action. The asking is the form the demonstration takes; the answer is the substance of the demonstration.

The commentators, including al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī, record that Abraham took the axe with which he smashed the smaller idols and hung it around the neck of or leaned it against the largest one. The detail is not in the Qur'an; the detail is part of the commentators' elaboration. The elaboration is consistent with the verse's logic. The largest idol is the only one left; the largest idol is the only candidate for the smashing; the largest idol could have smashed the others only if the largest idol had power. The power is what the demonstration denies.

**"That Perhaps They Would Return to It"**

The verse's stated purpose — *la-ʿallahum ilayhi yarjiʿūn* — that perhaps they would return to it — is one of the most theologically pointed phrases in the narrative. The verb *rajaʿa* — to return — names the form the hoped-for response would take. The audience would return to the largest idol; the returning would be the form the questioning would take. The audience would have to ask the largest idol what happened; the asking is the substance of the demonstration.

Some commentators, including al-Rāzī and al-Ṭabarī, note that the phrase can also be translated "that perhaps they would return" — referring not to the idol but to the truth. The audience would return to the true faith; the returning is the form the demonstration is meant to produce. The two readings are not exclusive. The audience would return to the idol to ask what happened; the returning would expose the idol's inability to answer; the exposure would be the form the recognition takes.

**The Demonstration As Rhetoric**

The demonstration is a piece of rhetoric. The smashing is not an attack on the idols; the smashing is an argument against the idols. The argument is made by leaving one idol intact and destroying the others. The intact idol is the only candidate for the action; the action is what the audience would have to attribute to the idol. The attribution would force the audience to choose between recognising the idol's power (and therefore granting it what they had not granted it before) or recognising the idol's helplessness (and therefore abandoning the worship). The choice is the substance of the demonstration.

This is one of the most sophisticated pieces of prophetic rhetoric in the Qur'anic narrative. The demonstration is not destructive; the demonstration is argumentative. The smashing is the form the argument takes; the argument is the form the prophetic speech takes when verbal argument has reached its limit. The limit is what 21:55 had recorded; the demonstration is the form the next step takes.

**A Verse That Forces Recognition**

The verse forces the audience to recognise what their worship implies. The audience that worships the idols must, after the demonstration, either grant the largest idol power it had not been credited with or recognise that the idol has no power at all. The forcing is the form the demonstration takes. The audience is being asked to choose between granting absurd powers and abandoning the worship. The choice is the substance of the verse's call.

This is one of the most distinctive features of the Qur'anic prophetic narrative. The messengers do not just argue with the audience; the messengers create situations in which the audience's own framework forces the audience to a choice. The choice is between granting the audience's framework more than the audience had been willing to grant it or abandoning the framework. The choice is what the verses are asking for; the asking is the form the call takes.

**A Verse That Connects to the Rest of the Narrative**

The verse is the action; the verses that follow are the dialogue about the action. The action forces the dialogue; the dialogue will produce the confrontation; the confrontation will produce the fire. The narrative moves from action to dialogue to confrontation to deliverance. The movement is what the Abraham story is about. The story is the form the sūrah's polemic takes; the polemic is what the audience of the Qur'an is being asked to recognise. The recognition is the substance of the call. The sūrah has been steadily pressing the audience toward this recognition; the pressing is the form the prophetic call takes.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:59

> They protested, “Who dared do this to our gods? It must be an evildoer!”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Outrage That Opens the Inquiry**

The verse records the audience's reaction to the demonstration. *Qālū man faʿala hādha bi-ālihatinā innahu la-min al-ẓālimīn* — they said, who has done this to our gods? He is surely among the wrongdoers. The Arabic *man faʿala* — who has done — names the form the inquiry takes. The audience is asking who did it; the asking is the form the demonstration was meant to produce. The demonstration has done its work.

The verse is one of the most rhetorically satisfying moments in the Abraham narrative. The smashing was the action; the inquiry is the response. The response is exactly what the smashing was meant to produce. The smashing was not random violence; the smashing was a designed argument. The argument has produced the inquiry; the inquiry is the form the demonstration takes when it has been successful.

**"Who Has Done This to Our Gods?"**

The question — *man faʿala hādha bi-ālihatinā* — names the form the inquiry takes. The audience has not yet identified the perpetrator. The audience is asking who did it. The asking is the form the demonstration's first stage takes. The audience that asks is the audience that has begun to engage with the demonstration; the engaging is what the smashing was meant to produce.

The question is also a confession. The audience's reference to *ālihatinā* — our gods — shows that the audience continues to identify with the idols. The identification is what the demonstration is meant to disrupt. The disruption has begun; the audience has begun to question what has happened to the idols; the questioning is the form the disruption takes.

**"He Is Surely Among the Wrongdoers"**

The verse's second clause — *innahu la-min al-ẓālimīn* — he is surely among the wrongdoers — names the verdict the audience has already pronounced. The audience has decided that whoever did it is a wrongdoer. The decision is the form the audience's continued loyalty to the idols takes. The decision is also premature; the demonstration has not yet been explained. The audience is passing judgment before the evidence has been examined.

This is one of the most theologically pointed moments in the narrative. The audience is treating the smashing as obviously wrong without yet asking who did it. The treatment is the form the audience's framework takes when the framework has been disrupted. The framework produces immediate condemnation; the condemnation is what the audience is offering. The offering is what the verses that follow will address.

**A Verse That Continues the Demonstration's Work**

The demonstration has produced the inquiry; the inquiry will produce the dialogue; the dialogue will produce the recognition. The verse is the first moment of the dialogue. The dialogue will continue in 21:60–67. The continuation is the form the demonstration's work takes. The demonstration is not yet complete; the demonstration is in process. The process is what the verses are documenting.

The documenting is part of the sūrah's polemic. The audience of the Qur'an is being shown how a demonstration works. The demonstration begins with an action; the action produces an inquiry; the inquiry produces a dialogue; the dialogue produces a recognition. The pattern is the form the prophetic demonstration takes. The audience is being asked to recognise the pattern. The recognition is what the verse is offering.

**The Audience's Premature Verdict**

The audience's verdict — that whoever did it is a wrongdoer — is premature in a way the narrative is exposing. The audience has not yet heard the explanation for the smashing; the audience has already pronounced the verdict. The verdict is therefore based on the audience's framework, not on the evidence. The framework is what the demonstration is meant to disrupt; the disruption has begun; the audience is still operating within the framework.

The audience of the Qur'an is being asked to consider whether they have done the same thing. The audience that has been treating the message as wrong without examining the message has been doing what Abraham's audience is doing at 21:59. The verse is asking the audience to consider the parallel. The consideration is the form the call takes; the call is what the verse is offering.

**A Verse That Returns to the Theme of Wrongdoing**

The verse returns to one of the sūrah's recurring themes: the wrongdoers. The verb *ẓalama* — to wrong — is the same verb used throughout the sūrah (21:14, 21:29, 21:47). The wrongdoers are those who have put something in the wrong place. The audience is using the word to condemn whoever smashed the idols. The verses that follow will show that the smashing was not the wrong; the wrong was the worship of things that cannot defend themselves. The framework of wrongdoing is being turned against the audience. The turning is the form the demonstration's polemic takes.

**The Rhetoric of the Outcry**

The audience's outcry is rhetorical even before it is rational. The Arabic word order in *man faʿala hādha bi-ālihatinā* — who has done this to our gods — places the audience's possessive relationship to the idols at the centre of the inquiry. The audience is not asking *what happened*; the audience is asking *who did this to our gods*. The possessive pronoun has already done the interpretive work before any candidate is named. The gods are *ours*; the action against them is therefore an action against *us*. The framing is what prepares the audience to identify the perpetrator as an evildoer rather than as someone who has revealed something important about the gods.

The framing is the form the audience's commitment takes when it has been disturbed. The commitment cannot survive without framing the disturbance as wrongdoing. The framing is what the demonstration has provoked. The provocation is the form the demonstration's polemic takes; the verses that follow will determine whether the provocation leads to reflection or to retaliation.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:60

> Some said, “We heard a young man, called Abraham, speaking ˹ill˺ of them.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Naming of the Perpetrator**

The verse records the identification of the perpetrator. *Qālū samiʿnā fatan yadhkuruhum yuqālu lahu Ibrāhīm* — they said, we heard a young man mentioning them; he is called Abraham. The verse is the second step in the demonstration: the audience has asked who did it (21:59); the audience is now naming who they think did it. The naming is the form the inquiry takes when the inquiry has reached its next stage.

The Arabic *fatan* — young man — is the term used for Abraham. The use of *fatan* is significant. The word can mean young man, servant, or one who is being tried. The polysemy is part of what the verse is doing. The audience is identifying Abraham as a young man; the audience is also, without knowing it, identifying him as one who is being tried. The trial is what the rest of the narrative will describe.

**"We Heard Him Mentioning Them"**

The verb *dhakara* — to mention — is the same verb used elsewhere in the sūrah for the Prophet's speech about the audience's gods. The verse is presenting the audience's report of what Abraham said. The audience heard Abraham mention the idols in a way that the audience considered insulting. The mentioning is what the verses earlier recorded (21:52, 21:54). The audience is now identifying the source of the mentioning.

The identification is the form the inquiry takes when the inquiry has been answered. The audience has asked who did it; the audience is now saying it must have been Abraham. The answer is what the verses will test in the dialogue that follows. The testing will reveal whether the answer leads to recognition or to condemnation. The recognition would require the audience to engage with what Abraham actually said; the condemnation would require the audience to skip the engagement and proceed to judgment. The judgment will come; the engagement will not.

**"He Is Called Abraham"**

The naming — *yuqālu lahu Ibrāhīm* — is the formal identification. The audience has identified the young man as Abraham. The identification is the form the inquiry takes when the inquiry has moved from the question to the answer. The audience now has a candidate; the candidate will be interrogated. The interrogation will take the form of a trial; the trial is what the next verses will describe.

The naming is also part of the polemic. The audience had been treating Abraham as an anonymous young man; the naming places him in the tradition. The audience that has heard Abraham's arguments and has named him is the audience that has begun to engage with the prophetic calling. The engaging is what the verses are recording; the recording is part of what the sūrah is doing.

**A Verse That Sets Up the Trial**

The verse is the setup for what will follow. The audience has identified the perpetrator; the audience is now demanding that the perpetrator be brought for the public confrontation. The confrontation will be the climax of the Abraham narrative; the narrative is what the verses are setting up.

The trial will be a confrontation between Abraham's argument and the audience's framework. The confrontation will culminate in Abraham's sarcastic suggestion that the largest idol had smashed the others (21:63). The suggestion will expose the audience's framework; the exposure is the form the prophetic argument takes when it has reached its climax. The trial is the form the demonstration takes when it has been pushed to its limit.

**The Young Man As a Figure of the Trial**

The polysemy of *fatan* — young man, servant, one being tried — is part of what the verse is doing. Abraham is the young man the audience identifies; Abraham is also the one being tried. The trial is not just the audience's trial of Abraham; the trial is also the trial of the audience by the demonstration Abraham has set up. The form the trial takes is the form the demonstration has been designed to produce.

This is one of the most theologically significant features of the Abraham narrative. The messenger is being tried by the audience; the audience is being tried by the messenger's demonstration. The double trial is the form the prophetic calling takes when it has reached its climax. The audience that recognises the trial they are being put through has begun the recognition the verses are calling for; the audience that refuses to recognise has refused the verses.

**A Verse That Connects to the Audience of the Qur'an**

The verse is implicitly addressing the audience of the Qur'an. The audience is being asked to consider the role they play in the trial. The audience that has heard the message has been placed in the same position as Abraham's audience: the audience has heard the messenger; the audience is being asked to consider what to do about what they have heard. The doing is the substance of the response the verses are asking for; the asking is the form the call takes.

**The "Mentioning" That Is Already a Polemic**

The audience reports Abraham as *yadhkuruhum* — mentioning them. The same root *dhikr* is what the Qur'an elsewhere calls itself (21:50). The audience uses the word in a pejorative sense: Abraham has been speaking against the gods. The pejorative sense is the audience's; the pejorative sense assumes the gods deserve defending. The assumption is what the demonstration has begun to disturb. The disturbance will continue as the dialogue unfolds; the unfolding is what the narrative is about.

The audience of the Qur'an reads this verse and recognises the rhythm: the mention of truth that is treated as an insult because the framework cannot accommodate the truth. The recognition is what the verse is offering the audience across the centuries; the offering is the form the call takes.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:61

> They demanded, “Bring him before the eyes of the people, so that they may witness ˹his trial˺.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Public Demand**

The verse records the audience's demand that Abraham be brought before the public. *Qālū fa-taḥālaw lahu bi-ṣirāḥin la-ʿallahum yashhadūn* — they said, then bring him, with clear authority, that they may witness. The Arabic *taḥāla* and *ṣirāḥ* — together meaning to bring with clear proof — name the form the demand takes. The audience wants to make the trial public. The public trial is the form the audience's response to the demonstration takes.

Al-Ṭabarī notes that the audience wanted either proof or public punishment. The public punishment is what the trial is producing. The trial is not just the audience's private resolution of the question; the trial is the public spectacle the audience has chosen. The choosing is what the verse is documenting; the documentation is part of what the narrative is doing.

**"So That They May Witness"**

The purpose clause — *la-ʿallahum yashhadūn* — names what the public trial is meant to produce. The people will witness; the witnessing is the form the trial is meant to take. The witnessing is not just observation; the witnessing is the public ratification of the audience's verdict. The verdict has already been reached (21:59); the public trial is the public confirmation.

This is one of the recurring patterns of the Qur'anic prophetic narrative. The messengers are tried publicly; the trials are meant to confirm the verdict the audience has already reached. The pattern is the form the audience's resistance takes when the resistance has been sufficiently developed. The development is what the verse is recording; the recording is part of the sūrah's polemic.

**A Verse That Sets Up the Climax**

The verse is the setup for the climax of the Abraham narrative. The trial that begins here will culminate in 21:68–69 with the attempt to burn Abraham alive and the divine intervention that saves him. The verse is preparing the audience for what is to come; the preparation is part of the narrative's rhetoric. The narrative is moving toward a confrontation that will demonstrate the limits of human power when it is opposed to the divine purpose.

The confrontation is also the form the argument about the idols takes. The argument has proceeded from the verbal diagnosis (21:54) to the demonstration (21:58) to the inquiry (21:59–60) to the public trial. Each step has moved the argument closer to the point at which the audience's framework will be definitively exposed. The exposure is what the climax is for; the climax is what the verses are building toward.

**A Verse That Returns to the Sūrah's Earlier Polemic**

The verse returns to one of the sūrah's recurring themes. The audience of the Qur'an had demanded that the Prophet produce signs (21:5); the audience had demanded to know when the promise would come (21:38). The demands were attempts to set the conditions of the prophetic mission. The verse at 21:61 records the same kind of demand in the Abraham narrative. The audience wants to set the conditions; the conditions are part of the audience's framework.

The framework is what the trial is meant to expose. The audience that has set the conditions of the trial has not realised that the trial will also be a trial of the conditions. The audience's framework will be exposed by the trial itself. The exposure is the form the prophetic argument takes; the argument is what the narrative is about.

**A Verse That Connects to the Audience of the Qur'an**

The verse is implicitly addressing the audience of the Qur'an. The audience is being asked to consider what it means to demand a public trial of the messenger. The demand has been made throughout the sūrah; the demand has been refused; the refusal is part of the sūrah's polemic. The audience is being asked to consider whether the demand is appropriate; the consideration is the form the verse's call takes.

The verse also asks the audience to consider what the public trial would produce. The trial would expose the audience's framework; the exposure would force the audience to recognise what the framework has produced. The recognising is what the verse is offering; the offering is the form the call takes.

The phrase *la-ʿallahum yashhadūn* — that they may witness — uses the same root *shuhūd* that has been central to Abraham's argument. The audience had demanded that Abraham bring forward witnesses; the audience had been unable to defend the idols in argument. The witnesses the audience now wants to gather are not neutral observers; the witnesses are being recruited into the audience's framework. The recruitment is part of the audience's strategy: the more witnesses there are, the harder it will be for anyone to break from the framework. The verse is therefore exposing the rhetorical work the audience is doing; the exposure is part of what the sūrahic narrative accomplishes by recording these small details.

The audience of the Qur'an can recognise the dynamic: when an argument is failing, the natural move is to recruit more people into the framework so that the framework appears to be supported by numbers. The recruitment does not change the substance of the framework; the substance is what the demonstration has exposed. The exposure is what the verse is asking the audience of every age to recognise; the recognition is the form the response takes.

**The Trial as a Mirror for Every Audience**

The trial Abraham faced is recorded in the Qur'an precisely because it mirrors the trial every messenger faces when the messenger has undermined the framework the audience had been trusting. The audience that had asked for a public trial wanted to make the verdict of condemnation a community event; the community event would strengthen the framework the demonstration had been threatening. The Qur'an records the trial not just to tell us what happened to Abraham but to show every subsequent audience what their own behaviour looks like when it is being reflected back to them in the mirror of the Abraham narrative. The mirror is what the verse is offering; the offering is the form the call takes to every generation that hears this recitation.

**The Quiet Polemic of the Detail**

The verse is doing a quiet polemic in the details of its narration. The detail about the public trial is not incidental; the detail is part of what the narrative is showing. The audience that wants witnesses, that wants public condemnation, that wants the trial to be a community event — that audience is the audience that has made the framework into a community possession. The framework is no longer a private opinion; the framework is the public identity of the community. The smashing of the idols threatened not just the religious framework but the community identity built on it. The exposure is part of what the verse is asking the audience to recognise.

**What the Verse Asks of the Audience Today**

The verse is asking the audience of the Qur'an to recognise the trial dynamics in their own lives. Every reader has, at some point, resisted an argument by gathering witnesses rather than by engaging with the argument itself. Every reader has faced a moment when their framework has been challenged and has chosen to make the framework a community possession rather than to consider whether the framework might be wrong. The verse is showing that pattern in its earliest scriptural form; the showing is what the verse is offering to every generation. The audience that recognises the pattern has begun the response the verse is asking for; the audience that refuses to recognise has refused the verse.

**A Bridge Between Verse and Reader**

The verse is also doing the work of bridging between the historical narrative and the contemporary reader. The reader who has never faced a public trial can still recognise the dynamics of public resistance to private argument. The reader can recognise the recruitment of witnesses, the impatience for verdicts, the making of community identity into a defence for the framework. The recognition is what the verse is asking for; the asking is the form the call takes. The call is what the verse is offering to every generation that encounters this recitation; the offering is part of what the Qur'an continues to do for those who hear it.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:62

> They asked, “Was it you who did this to our gods, O Abraham?”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Direct Confrontation**

The verse is the formal confrontation. *Qālū a-antum faʿalta hādha bi-ālihatinā yā Ibrāhīm* — they said, was it you who did this to our gods, O Abraham? The verse is the moment the inquiry becomes a direct accusation. The audience has identified the perpetrator (21:60); the audience has demanded a public trial (21:61); the audience is now addressing the perpetrator directly. The addressing is the form the trial takes.

The Arabic *a-antum* (here *anta*) — was it you? — is a question that demands a yes or no answer. The verse is asking Abraham to confirm or deny. The asking is the form the public trial takes; the trial will determine the audience's verdict. The verdict will not be a recognition of what Abraham has demonstrated; the verdict will be the verdict the audience had already reached before the trial began.

**"O Abraham" — The Naming That Frames the Question**

The address — *yā Ibrāhīm* — is the formal naming of the accused. The audience is no longer speaking about the young man who mentioned them (21:60); the audience is now addressing Abraham by name. The naming is the form the trial takes. The trial is no longer about an anonymous perpetrator; the trial is about a named person. The person is being asked to take responsibility for what he has done.

The naming is also part of the polemic. The audience that has named the perpetrator has done the work that the demonstration was designed to produce. The demonstration forced the naming; the naming is the form the demonstration's first phase takes. The naming will lead to the trial; the trial will lead to the climax; the climax is what the narrative is about.

**The Question That Is Not Neutral**

The question is not neutral. The audience is not genuinely asking whether Abraham did it; the audience is accusing Abraham and demanding a confession. The form of the question presupposes the answer. The audience has decided that Abraham did it; the question is the form the decision takes. The decision has been made before the trial began; the trial is the public confirmation.

This is one of the recurring patterns of the Qur'anic prophetic narrative. The audience's questions are not inquiries; the questions are accusations in interrogative form. The form is the substance of the audience's resistance; the resistance is what the verse is recording. The recording is part of the verse's polemic; the polemic is what the audience of the Qur'an is being asked to recognise.

**A Verse That Begins the Trial's Verbal Phase**

The verse is the opening of the trial's verbal phase. The trial will proceed through the question here, Abraham's reply in 21:63, the audience's momentary recognition in 21:64, their retreat in 21:65, Abraham's final polemic in 21:66–67, and the verdict in 21:68. The progression is the form the trial takes. The trial will move from the direct accusation through Abraham's reductio ad absurdum, through the audience's brief recognition, through the retreat into the framework, to the verdict of burning.

The progression is what the narrative is documenting. The documentation is part of the sūrah's polemic. The audience of the Qur'an is being shown the dynamics of a prophetic confrontation; the dynamics are what the audience of the Qur'an is being asked to recognise. The recognition is the substance of the response the verses are asking for.

**A Verse That Connects to the Earlier Polemic**

The verse connects to the broader sūrahic polemic against the audience of the Qur'an. The Meccan audience had been asking the Prophet questions; the questions were forms of accusation. The questions had been answered by the Qur'anic verses; the answers were forms of the prophetic argument. The verse at 21:62 is part of the same structure. The questions and the answers are the form the prophetic confrontation takes.

The confrontation will end with the audience's inability to defend its framework. The inability is what the dialogue is producing. The producing is the form the demonstration's argument takes. The argument is what the sūrah is offering the audience of every age; the offering is the substance of the verse's call.

**The Trial as a Mirror for Every Audience**

The trial Abraham faced is recorded in the Qur'an precisely because it mirrors the trial every messenger faces when the messenger has undermined the framework the audience had been trusting.
