The audience that had asked for a public trial wanted to make the verdict of condemnation a community event; the community event would strengthen the framework the demonstration had been threatening. The Qur'an records the trial not just to tell us what happened to Abraham but to show every subsequent audience what their own behaviour looks like when it is being reflected back to them in the mirror of the Abraham narrative. The mirror is what the verse is offering; the offering is the form the call takes to every generation that hears this recitation.

**The Quiet Polemic of the Detail**

The verse is doing a quiet polemic in the details of its narration. The detail about the public trial is not incidental; the detail is part of what the narrative is showing. The audience that wants witnesses, that wants public condemnation, that wants the trial to be a community event — that audience is the audience that has made the framework into a community possession. The framework is no longer a private opinion; the framework is the public identity of the community. The smashing of the idols threatened not just the religious framework but the community identity built on it. The exposure is part of what the verse is asking the audience to recognise.

**What the Verse Asks of the Audience Today**

The verse is asking the audience of the Qur'an to recognise the trial dynamics in their own lives. Every reader has, at some point, resisted an argument by gathering witnesses rather than by engaging with the argument itself. Every reader has faced a moment when their framework has been challenged and has chosen to make the framework a community possession rather than to consider whether the framework might be wrong. The verse is showing that pattern in its earliest scriptural form; the showing is what the verse is offering to every generation. The audience that recognises the pattern has begun the response the verse is asking for; the audience that refuses to recognise has refused the verse.

**A Bridge Between Verse and Reader**

The verse is also doing the work of bridging between the historical narrative and the contemporary reader. The reader who has never faced a public trial can still recognise the dynamics of public resistance to private argument. The reader can recognise the recruitment of witnesses, the impatience for verdicts, the making of community identity into a defence for the framework. The recognition is what the verse is asking for; the asking is the form the call takes. The call is what the verse is offering to every generation that encounters this recitation; the offering is part of what the Qur'an continues to do for those who hear it.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:63

> He replied ˹sarcastically˺, “No, this one—the biggest of them—did it! So ask them, if they can talk!”

**Expanded Commentary**

**Sarcasm That Exposes**

The verse records Abraham's reply. *Qāla bal faʿalahu kabīruhum hādha fa-sʾalūhum in kānū yanṭiqūn* — he said, rather, the largest of them did this, so ask them, if they can speak. The reply is one of the most rhetorically pointed moments in the entire Qur'an. Abraham turns the question against the audience by suggesting that the largest idol — the only one he left intact — did the smashing. The suggestion is obviously false; the obviousness is the form the argument takes.

The verb *qāla* — he said — and the particle *bal* — rather — are doing significant work. The *bal* overturns the implicit accusation; Abraham does not answer the direct question. The overturning is the form the prophetic argument takes. The argument is the reductio: if the idols can do what gods are supposed to do, then they could have smashed each other; if they cannot speak, they cannot have done anything; the smashing was done by the largest, and the largest is the only one left to ask. The reductio is what the verse is doing.

**"So Ask Them, If They Can Talk"**

The closing phrase — *fa-sʾalūhum in kānū yanṭiqūn* — so ask them, if they can speak — is the apex of the demonstration. The phrase forces the audience to recognise the absurdity of what they had been doing. The audience has been worshipping things that cannot speak. The audience has been asking things that cannot answer. The absurdity is the substance of the demonstration.

The conditional *in kānū yanṭiqūn* — if they can speak — is rhetorical. The idols cannot speak; the audience knows it; Abraham knows the audience knows it. The conditional forces the audience to consider what they have been doing. The considering is the form the demonstration's argument takes. The argument is what the verse is asking the audience to integrate.

**The Commentators' Discussion of the "Liar"**

The commentators, including al-Qurṭubī and al-Rāzī, have noted that a hadith attributed to the Prophet identifies this as one of three occasions on which Abraham spoke something other than the literal truth. The occasions are: his saying "I am sick" (37:89), his saying that Sarah was his sister (Genesis 20:2, cited in the Qur'anic tradition), and his suggestion here that the largest idol did the smashing. The commentators disagree on whether this counts as a lie. Al-Qurṭubī suggests that Abraham may have been speaking while still a child. Al-Rāzī argues that the statement is not a lie in the proper sense because the audience could not have believed that the idol did the smashing. The argument was meant to demonstrate a truth by uttering words that no one present could have believed literally.

The discussion is important because it shows that the commentators recognised that the prophetic speech is not just any speech; the prophetic speech is the speech that serves the prophetic argument. The argument is what the speech is for; the speech is the form the argument takes. The form is what the verse is asking the audience to consider.

**Using Mockery for Argument**

The verse is one of the most striking examples of prophetic mockery in the Qur'an. Abraham mocks the audience by suggesting that the largest idol did the smashing. The mocking is not cruelty; the mocking is argument. The argument is meant to expose the absurdity of the audience's worship. The exposure is the form the prophetic speech takes when the speech has reached its climax.

This is one of the most distinctive features of the Qur'anic treatment of the prophetic calling. The messengers are not just patient speakers; the messengers are also capable of pointed mockery. The mockery is reserved for the moments when the audience's framework has been exposed as untenable; the mockery is the form the exposure takes. The form is what the verse is offering the audience of every age; the offering is part of the sūrah's polemic.

**The Trial's Climactic Moment**

The verse is the climax of the trial's verbal phase. The argument that began with the inquiry (21:59) has now reached the point at which the audience's framework is exposed. The exposure is the form the demonstration has been designed to produce. The exposure will lead to the audience's brief recognition in 21:64, the retreat into the framework in 21:65, and the verdict of burning in 21:68. The progression is what the narrative is about.

The audience of the Qur'an is being shown the dynamics of the prophetic argument. The argument begins with a verbal diagnosis, proceeds through a demonstration, culminates in mockery, and ends with the audience's verdict against the messenger. The dynamics are the form the prophetic confrontation takes. The form is what the audience of every age is being asked to recognise; the recognition is the substance of the response the verses are asking for.

**The Trial as a Mirror for Every Audience**

The trial Abraham faced is recorded in the Qur'an precisely because it mirrors the trial every messenger faces when the messenger has undermined the framework the audience had been trusting. The audience that had asked for a public trial wanted to make the verdict of condemnation a community event; the community event would strengthen the framework the demonstration had been threatening. The Qur'an records the trial not just to tell us what happened to Abraham but to show every subsequent audience what their own behaviour looks like when it is being reflected back to them in the mirror of the Abraham narrative. The mirror is what the verse is offering; the offering is the form the call takes to every generation that hears this recitation.

**The Quiet Polemic of the Detail**

The verse is doing a quiet polemic in the details of its narration. The detail about the public trial is not incidental; the detail is part of what the narrative is showing. The audience that wants witnesses, that wants public condemnation, that wants the trial to be a community event — that audience is the audience that has made the framework into a community possession. The framework is no longer a private opinion; the framework is the public identity of the community. The smashing of the idols threatened not just the religious framework but the community identity built on it. The exposure is part of what the verse is asking the audience to recognise.

**What the Verse Asks of the Audience Today**

The verse is asking the audience of the Qur'an to recognise the trial dynamics in their own lives. Every reader has, at some point, resisted an argument by gathering witnesses rather than by engaging with the argument itself. Every reader has faced a moment when their framework has been challenged and has chosen to make the framework a community possession rather than to consider whether the framework might be wrong. The verse is showing that pattern in its earliest scriptural form; the showing is what the verse is offering to every generation. The audience that recognises the pattern has begun the response the verse is asking for; the audience that refuses to recognise has refused the verse.

**A Bridge Between Verse and Reader**

The verse is also doing the work of bridging between the historical narrative and the contemporary reader. The reader who has never faced a public trial can still recognise the dynamics of public resistance to private argument. The reader can recognise the recruitment of witnesses, the impatience for verdicts, the making of community identity into a defence for the framework. The recognition is what the verse is asking for; the asking is the form the call takes. The call is what the verse is offering to every generation that encounters this recitation; the offering is part of what the Qur'an continues to do for those who hear it.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:64

> So they came back to their senses, saying ˹to one another˺, “You yourselves are truly the wrongdoers!”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Moment of Recognition**

The verse records the audience's brief moment of recognition. *Farajaʿū ilā anfusihim fa-qālū innakum antum al-ẓālimūn* — then they returned to themselves and said, indeed you are the wrongdoers. The phrase *rajāʿū ilā anfusihim* — they returned to themselves — names the form the recognition takes. The audience has been carried away by the framework; the framework has been momentarily suspended; the audience has returned to themselves. The returning is the form the recognition takes.

The recognition is one of the most striking moments in the entire Qur'anic narrative. The audience of Abraham, who had been demanding that the perpetrators be identified and punished, has now turned the verdict against itself. The verdict is the form the recognition takes; the recognition is the form the demonstration's argument has produced.

**"You Yourselves Are Truly the Wrongdoers"**

The audience's confession — *innakum antum al-ẓālimūn* — you yourselves are the wrongdoers — is the substance of the recognition. The audience has recognised that the wrong is not in what Abraham did; the wrong is in what the audience had been doing. The recognition is brief; the recognition is significant; the recognition will not last. The verses that follow will show that the recognition was too brief to produce the change it seemed to promise.

This is one of the most theologically pointed moments in the Qur'anic narrative. The audience that has worshipped idols has been brought to the point of recognising that the worship is wrong. The recognition has been produced by an argument; the argument has done what the verbal diagnosis (21:54) could not do. The argument has done it by being sharper than the verbal diagnosis; the sharpness is the form the demonstration takes.

**Al-Ṭabarī's Reading of the Recognition**

Al-Ṭabarī records a reading in which the recognition is understood to mean that some of the audience thought they were wronging Abraham by accusing him. The reading softens the recognition; the audience is not yet recognising the idolatry as wrong, only the false accusation as wrong. The softer reading is consistent with the verses that follow; the verses will show that the recognition did not extend to the underlying framework.

The reading is part of the commentators' effort to make the narrative coherent with the verses that follow. The verses that follow (21:65) record the retreat into the framework; the retreat is easier to understand if the recognition had not gone all the way. The reading therefore serves a hermeneutical purpose; the purpose is to make the narrative's progression coherent. The progression is what the narrative is documenting.

**The Briefness of the Recognition**

The verse records a recognition that will be retracted in 21:65. The retraction is the form the audience's resistance takes when the resistance has been momentarily broken. The moment of recognition is real; the moment will not sustain itself. The sustaining would require the audience to do something the audience is not yet willing to do: to acknowledge that the framework itself was wrong.

This is one of the recurring patterns of the Qur'anic prophetic narrative. The audience has moments of recognition; the moments do not last; the audience returns to the framework. The pattern is the form the audience's resistance takes when the resistance has been sufficiently developed. The development is what the verses are documenting; the documentation is part of the sūrah's polemic.

**A Verse That Shows What the Demonstration Did**

The verse is the demonstration's moment of success. The demonstration was designed to produce recognition; the recognition has been produced. The production is what the verse is recording; the recording is part of the demonstration's polemic. The demonstration has shown what it was meant to show; the showing is the form the demonstration takes.

The showing is also a teaching. The audience of the Qur'an is being shown what happens when the demonstration works. The working is what the demonstration has produced; the production is what the verse is recording. The recording is part of the sūrah's polemic; the polemic is what the audience of the Qur'an is being asked to recognise. The recognition is the substance of the response the verses are asking for.

**A Verse That Returns to the Theme of Wrongdoing**

The verse returns to one of the sūrah's recurring themes: the wrongdoers. The Arabic *ẓālimūn* — wrongdoers — is the same word used throughout the sūrah (21:14, 21:29, 21:47, 21:59). The audience that has just called itself the wrongdoers is the audience that has reached the edge of recognition. The edge is what the verse is documenting; the documenting is part of the polemic. The audience of the Qur'an is being asked to consider how often the recognition reaches only the edge and does not go further. The considering is the form the verse's call takes; the call is the substance of the response the verse is asking for.

**The Trial as a Mirror for Every Audience**

The trial Abraham faced is recorded in the Qur'an precisely because it mirrors the trial every messenger faces when the messenger has undermined the framework the audience had been trusting. The audience that had asked for a public trial wanted to make the verdict of condemnation a community event; the community event would strengthen the framework the demonstration had been threatening. The Qur'an records the trial not just to tell us what happened to Abraham but to show every subsequent audience what their own behaviour looks like when it is being reflected back to them in the mirror of the Abraham narrative. The mirror is what the verse is offering; the offering is the form the call takes to every generation that hears this recitation.

**The Quiet Polemic of the Detail**

The verse is doing a quiet polemic in the details of its narration. The detail about the public trial is not incidental; the detail is part of what the narrative is showing. The audience that wants witnesses, that wants public condemnation, that wants the trial to be a community event — that audience is the audience that has made the framework into a community possession. The framework is no longer a private opinion; the framework is the public identity of the community. The smashing of the idols threatened not just the religious framework but the community identity built on it. The exposure is part of what the verse is asking the audience to recognise.

**What the Verse Asks of the Audience Today**

The verse is asking the audience of the Qur'an to recognise the trial dynamics in their own lives. Every reader has, at some point, resisted an argument by gathering witnesses rather than by engaging with the argument itself. Every reader has faced a moment when their framework has been challenged and has chosen to make the framework a community possession rather than to consider whether the framework might be wrong. The verse is showing that pattern in its earliest scriptural form; the showing is what the verse is offering to every generation. The audience that recognises the pattern has begun the response the verse is asking for; the audience that refuses to recognise has refused the verse.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:65

> Then they ˹quickly˺ regressed to their ˹original˺ mind-set, ˹arguing,˺ “You already know that those ˹idols˺ cannot talk.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Retreat Into the Framework**

The verse records the audience's rapid retreat from the recognition. *Thumma nukisū ʿalā ruʾūsihim* — then they were turned upside down, they reverted — and *la-qad ʿalimta mā hāʾulāʾi yanṭiqūn* — you know that these do not speak. The Arabic *nukisū ʿalā ruʾūsihim* is one of the Qur'an's most striking phrases. The image is of being turned head-over-heels; the previous recognition is overturned; the previous framework reasserts itself.

The retreat is one of the most psychologically accurate descriptions of audience resistance in the Qur'an. The audience has reached the edge of recognition; the recognition has been uncomfortable; the audience has chosen to return to the framework. The returning is the form the resistance takes when the resistance has been momentarily broken. The breaking is what the demonstration produced; the production is what the verse is recording.

**"You Already Know That These Cannot Talk"**

The audience's argument — *la-qad ʿalimta mā hāʾulāʾi yanṭiqūn* — you know that these do not speak — is a return to the framework dressed as a continuation of the dialogue. The audience is acknowledging what Abraham's argument had made obvious. The acknowledgement is the form the audience's response takes when the response has been forced. The forcing is what Abraham's argument produced.

The argument is also a refusal to engage with the implications. The audience has acknowledged that the idols cannot speak. The audience had been arguing that the largest idol did the smashing (because of Abraham's sarcastic suggestion). The argument collapses if the idols cannot speak; the audience cannot have it both ways. The audience chooses the framework; the framework is restored. The restoring is the form the retreat takes.

**Al-Ṭabarī's Three Readings**

Al-Ṭabarī records three readings of the retreat. The first is that the audience was overcome by the madness of evil; the second is that the audience was momentarily convinced by Abraham's argument; the third is that the audience went back to its previous thinking because it could not sustain the recognition. The readings are not exclusive; the readings are three ways of describing what happens when an audience that has been operating within a framework encounters an argument that breaks the framework. The framework is restored; the restoration is what the verse is documenting.

The readings are part of the commentators' effort to understand the dynamics of the audience's retreat. The dynamics are what the verse is recording; the recording is part of the sūrah's polemic. The audience of the Qur'an is being shown what the retreat looks like; the showing is what the sūrah is offering; the offering is the substance of the verse's call.

**A Verse That Documents the Failure of the Recognition**

The verse documents the failure of the brief recognition of 21:64. The failure is part of the narrative's realism. The audience does not stay at the edge of recognition; the audience retreats. The retreat is the form the resistance takes when the resistance has been sufficiently developed. The development is what the verses are documenting; the documentation is part of the sūrah's polemic.

The documentation is also a teaching. The audience of the Qur'an is being shown that recognition is not the same as change. The audience can recognise something and still not change. The recognition can be retracted; the retraction is the form the resistance takes. The audience of the Qur'an is being asked to consider whether the recognition has gone all the way; the considering is the form the verse's call takes.

**A Verse That Returns to the Theme of False Argument**

The verse records the audience's false argument. The audience knows the idols cannot speak; the audience uses this knowledge as an argument. The argument is false because it presupposes that the inability to speak is a minor defect; the inability to speak is the very point. The argument does not engage with the implication; the implication is what the argument refuses to engage with.

This is one of the recurring patterns of the Qur'anic prophetic narrative. The audience uses false arguments to defend the framework. The arguments are often technically correct; the arguments are false because they accept the wrong premise. The wrong premise is what the demonstration is meant to expose. The exposure is what the verse is asking the audience of the Qur'an to consider. The considering is the form the verse's call takes.

**A Verse That Connects to the Audience of the Qur'an**

The verse is implicitly addressing the audience of the Qur'an. The audience is being asked to consider whether it has ever retreated from a recognition it had reached. The retreat is the form the resistance takes; the resistance is what the audience is being asked to recognise. The recognition is what the verse is offering; the offering is the substance of the verse's call. The call is the form the response takes.

**The Trial as a Mirror for Every Audience**

The trial Abraham faced is recorded in the Qur'an precisely because it mirrors the trial every messenger faces when the messenger has undermined the framework the audience had been trusting. The audience that had asked for a public trial wanted to make the verdict of condemnation a community event; the community event would strengthen the framework the demonstration had been threatening. The Qur'an records the trial not just to tell us what happened to Abraham but to show every subsequent audience what their own behaviour looks like when it is being reflected back to them in the mirror of the Abraham narrative. The mirror is what the verse is offering; the offering is the form the call takes to every generation that hears this recitation.

**The Quiet Polemic of the Detail**

The verse is doing a quiet polemic in the details of its narration. The detail about the public trial is not incidental; the detail is part of what the narrative is showing. The audience that wants witnesses, that wants public condemnation, that wants the trial to be a community event — that audience is the audience that has made the framework into a community possession. The framework is no longer a private opinion; the framework is the public identity of the community. The smashing of the idols threatened not just the religious framework but the community identity built on it. The exposure is part of what the verse is asking the audience to recognise.

**What the Verse Asks of the Audience Today**

The verse is asking the audience of the Qur'an to recognise the trial dynamics in their own lives. Every reader has, at some point, resisted an argument by gathering witnesses rather than by engaging with the argument itself. Every reader has faced a moment when their framework has been challenged and has chosen to make the framework a community possession rather than to consider whether the framework might be wrong. The verse is showing that pattern in its earliest scriptural form; the showing is what the verse is offering to every generation. The audience that recognises the pattern has begun the response the verse is asking for; the audience that refuses to recognise has refused the verse.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:66

> He rebuked ˹them˺, “Do you then worship—instead of Allah—what can neither benefit nor harm you in any way

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Final Argument Before the Verdict**

The verse is Abraham's final argument before the audience pronounces its verdict. *Qāla afa-taʿbudūna min dūni Allāhi mā lā yanfaʿukum shayʾan wa lā yaḍurrukum* — he said, do you worship besides God that which neither benefits you in anything nor harms you? The verse is the climax of the verbal phase of the trial. The argument is reduced to its essentials; the essentials are the question.

The question — *afa-taʿbudūna* — do you worship? — is one of the most direct in the Abraham narrative. The verb *ʿabada* — to worship — names the action the audience has been performing; the question is asking the audience to consider what the action has meant. The meaning is what the question is exposing. The exposure is the form the argument takes.

**"What Can Neither Benefit Nor Harm You"**

The specification — *mā lā yanfaʿukum shayʾan wa lā yaḍurrukum* — that which neither benefits you in anything nor harms you — is the substance of the question. The Arabic *nafaʿa* and *ḍarara* — benefit and harm — name the two basic forms of agency a worshipper would expect from a god. The audience's gods can do neither. The gods cannot benefit; the gods cannot harm. The gods are not gods; the gods are nothing. The verse is naming the nothing.

This is one of the most pointed statements in the entire Qur'anic treatment of idolatry. The Qur'an does not deny that idols have a kind of existence; the Qur'an denies that the existence is the kind of existence that could ground worship. The idols exist; the existence does not confer agency; the agency is what the worship presupposes; the presupposition is what the verse is denying. The denial is the form the verse's argument takes.

**Connection to Other Qur'anic Statements**

The verse echoes similar statements throughout the Qur'an. 5:76 says: *You have taken idols besides God in vain; between yourselves in the life of this world; but on the Day of Resurrection you will disown one another*. 6:71 says: *We have been warned against this; and woe to those who associate others with God*. 10:18 says: *They worship besides God that which neither harms them nor benefits them*. 13:16 says: *They have taken besides Him gods; they do not create anything, but are themselves created*. 22:12 says: *He calls, besides God, that which neither harms him nor benefits him*. 25:55 says: *They serve, besides God, that which neither benefits them nor harms them*.

The recurrence is the consistent application of the principle that the idols are not gods. The principle is what the verse is asking the audience to recognise. The recognition is the form the response takes; the response is what the verse is asking for.

**A Verse That Returns to the Sūrah's Central Polemic**

The verse is part of the sūrah's polemic against the polytheistic framework. The audience of Abraham had been treating the idols as partners of God; the verse is denying that the idols have the agency that would make them partners. The denying is the form the polemic takes. The polemic is what the sūrah has been doing since 21:19. The verse at 21:66 is the Abrahamic form of the polemic; the form is what the audience of the Qur'an is being asked to recognise.

The recognition is also an invitation to recognise what the Qur'anic treatment of idolatry has been doing in every age. The Qur'an is not just arguing with the audience of Abraham; the Qur'an is showing what the argument looks like when it has been done thoroughly. The doing is what the verse is offering the audience of the Qur'an; the offering is the substance of the verse's call.

**A Verse That Precedes the Final Polemic**

The verse is the second-to-last of Abraham's arguments. The next verse (21:67) will be the final polemic — *fie upon you and upon what you worship besides God*. The two verses together form the climax of the verbal phase. The climax is what the trial has been building toward; the climax is what the verses that follow will respond to. The response will be the verdict of burning (21:68). The verdict is what the audience has chosen in preference to the recognition. The choosing is what the verse is documenting; the documentation is part of the sūrah's polemic.

The audience of the Qur'an is being shown what the choice looks like when it has been made fully. The choosing is what the verses are asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the verse's call takes; the call is the substance of the response the verses are asking for.

**The Trial as a Mirror for Every Audience**

The trial Abraham faced is recorded in the Qur'an precisely because it mirrors the trial every messenger faces when the messenger has undermined the framework the audience had been trusting. The audience that had asked for a public trial wanted to make the verdict of condemnation a community event; the community event would strengthen the framework the demonstration had been threatening. The Qur'an records the trial not just to tell us what happened to Abraham but to show every subsequent audience what their own behaviour looks like when it is being reflected back to them in the mirror of the Abraham narrative. The mirror is what the verse is offering; the offering is the form the call takes to every generation that hears this recitation.

**The Quiet Polemic of the Detail**

The verse is doing a quiet polemic in the details of its narration. The detail about the public trial is not incidental; the detail is part of what the narrative is showing. The audience that wants witnesses, that wants public condemnation, that wants the trial to be a community event — that audience is the audience that has made the framework into a community possession. The framework is no longer a private opinion; the framework is the public identity of the community. The smashing of the idols threatened not just the religious framework but the community identity built on it. The exposure is part of what the verse is asking the audience to recognise.

**What the Verse Asks of the Audience Today**

The verse is asking the audience of the Qur'an to recognise the trial dynamics in their own lives. Every reader has, at some point, resisted an argument by gathering witnesses rather than by engaging with the argument itself. Every reader has faced a moment when their framework has been challenged and has chosen to make the framework a community possession rather than to consider whether the framework might be wrong. The verse is showing that pattern in its earliest scriptural form; the showing is what the verse is offering to every generation. The audience that recognises the pattern has begun the response the verse is asking for; the audience that refuses to recognise has refused the verse.

**A Bridge Between Verse and Reader**

The verse is also doing the work of bridging between the historical narrative and the contemporary reader. The reader who has never faced a public trial can still recognise the dynamics of public resistance to private argument. The reader can recognise the recruitment of witnesses, the impatience for verdicts, the making of community identity into a defence for the framework. The recognition is what the verse is asking for; the asking is the form the call takes. The call is what the verse is offering to every generation that encounters this recitation; the offering is part of what the Qur'an continues to do for those who hear it.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:67

> Shame on you and whatever you worship instead of Allah! Do you not have any sense?”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Final Polemic**

The verse is Abraham's final polemic before the verdict. *Uffun lakum wa limā taʿbudūna min dūni Allāhi afalā taʿqilūn* — fie upon you and upon what you worship besides God; have you no sense? The verse is the climax of the verbal argument; the argument is being closed. The closing is the form the prophetic speech takes when the speech has done what it can do.

The Arabic *uff* — fie, an exclamation of annoyance and disgust — is unusually strong language in the Qur'an. The same word appears in 17:23 in the verse that forbids even saying *uff* to one's parents. The use of *uff* here, against the idols the audience worships, is a striking rhetorical move. The rhetorical move is meant to communicate the intensity of Abraham's reaction to what the audience has been doing. The intensity is the form the polemic takes.

**"You and Whatever You Worship Instead of God"**

The construction — *uffun lakum wa limā taʿbudūna min dūni Allāhi* — names both the audience and the idols. The audience is included; the idols are included. The fie is addressed to both. The addressing is the form the polemic takes; the polemic is meant to make the audience recognise that the worship of the idols is part of what the audience is being judged for. The worship is not separate from the audience; the worship is what the audience has been doing.

The phrase *min dūni Allāhi* — besides God, other than God — is the standard Qur'anic way of referring to the supposed partners of God. The phrase has been used throughout the sūrah (21:43, 21:53, 21:55, 21:57, 21:58, 21:66). The recurrence is the consistent application of the principle that the partners are not partners. The principle is what the verse is asking the audience to recognise; the recognition is the form the response takes.

**"Do You Not Have Any Sense?"**

The closing question — *afalā taʿqilūn* — have you no sense? — is the same kind of question that has appeared throughout the sūrah. 21:10 asked: *Will you not then understand?* 21:30 asked: *Will they not then believe?* The question is the form the sūrah's call takes. The call is the audience's invitation to use the minds they have. The minds are what the audience is being asked to exercise; the exercise is what the question is offering.

The question is also the recognition that the audience has not been using the minds they have. The minds have been subordinated to the framework; the framework has been operating without examination; the examination is what the question is asking the audience to do. The doing is the form the response takes; the response is what the verse is asking for.

**A Verse That Closes the Verbal Phase of the Trial**

The verse closes the verbal phase of the trial. The next verse (21:68) will record the audience's verdict: *Burn him, and help your gods*. The transition from argument to verdict is the form the trial takes when the argument has been completed. The completing is what the verse is documenting; the documenting is part of the sūrah's polemic.

The transition is also a teaching. The audience of the Qur'an is being shown what happens when the argument has been exhausted. The exhaustion is the form the verbal phase takes when the verbal phase has been concluded; the verdict is what follows the exhaustion. The verdict is the form the audience's resistance takes when the resistance has been given its full opportunity. The opportunity is what the argument has provided; the verdict is what the audience has chosen. The choosing is what the verses are documenting.

**A Verse That Returns to the Sūrah's Refusal of the Inheritance Argument**

The verse implicitly returns to one of the sūrah's recurring refutations. The audience had argued that they were worshipping because their fathers had worshipped (21:53). The verse's question — *do you not have any sense?* — is asking the audience to consider whether the inherited practice is justified by any sense they can recognise. The sense is the form the recognition takes; the recognition is the form the response takes.

The verse is therefore not just an expression of disgust; the verse is an appeal to the intellect. The appeal is the last thing the argument has to offer. The appeal is what the verse is asking the audience to take seriously. The seriousness is the form the response takes; the response is what the verse is asking for.

**A Verse That Connects to the Audience of the Qur'an**

The verse is implicitly addressing the audience of the Qur'an. The audience is being asked whether it has any sense; the audience is being asked to use the minds it has been given. The asking is the form the verse's call takes. The call is what the verse is offering the audience; the offering is the substance of the response the verses are asking for. The response is the response Abraham's audience refused to give; the refusal is what the verse is documenting in the verses that follow.

**The Trial as a Mirror for Every Audience**

The trial Abraham faced is recorded in the Qur'an precisely because it mirrors the trial every messenger faces when the messenger has undermined the framework the audience had been trusting. The audience that had asked for a public trial wanted to make the verdict of condemnation a community event; the community event would strengthen the framework the demonstration had been threatening. The Qur'an records the trial not just to tell us what happened to Abraham but to show every subsequent audience what their own behaviour looks like when it is being reflected back to them in the mirror of the Abraham narrative. The mirror is what the verse is offering; the offering is the form the call takes to every generation that hears this recitation.

**The Quiet Polemic of the Detail**

The verse is doing a quiet polemic in the details of its narration. The detail about the public trial is not incidental; the detail is part of what the narrative is showing. The audience that wants witnesses, that wants public condemnation, that wants the trial to be a community event — that audience is the audience that has made the framework into a community possession. The framework is no longer a private opinion; the framework is the public identity of the community. The smashing of the idols threatened not just the religious framework but the community identity built on it. The exposure is part of what the verse is asking the audience to recognise.

**What the Verse Asks of the Audience Today**

The verse is asking the audience of the Qur'an to recognise the trial dynamics in their own lives. Every reader has, at some point, resisted an argument by gathering witnesses rather than by engaging with the argument itself. Every reader has faced a moment when their framework has been challenged and has chosen to make the framework a community possession rather than to consider whether the framework might be wrong. The verse is showing that pattern in its earliest scriptural form; the showing is what the verse is offering to every generation. The audience that recognises the pattern has begun the response the verse is asking for; the audience that refuses to recognise has refused the verse.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:68

> They concluded, “Burn him up to avenge your gods, if you must act.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Verdict of Burning**

The verse records the audience's verdict. *Qālū ḥarrijūhu wa-nṣurū ālihatakum in kuntum fāʿilīn* — they said, burn him and help your gods, if you would act. The verb *ḥarrajūhu* — burn him — is a rare and violent word. The verb is in the imperative; the audience is giving an order. The order is the form the verdict takes. The verdict is the audience's response to the entire argument.

This is one of the most theologically pointed moments in the entire Qur'anic narrative. The audience has just heard a sustained argument about the absurdity of idolatry. The argument has been reduced to a final polemic. The audience's response is not engagement with the argument; the audience's response is the order to burn. The response is the form the resistance takes when the resistance has been fully developed.

**"Help Your Gods"**

The verb *naṣara* — to help, to support — is being used here in its basic sense. The audience is being asked to help the gods; the gods cannot help themselves. The asking is the form the audience's continued commitment to the framework takes. The commitment has survived the entire argument; the commitment is now being asked to express itself in action against the messenger.

This is one of the most psychologically revealing moments in the narrative. The audience that has been told by its own argument that the idols cannot speak is now being asked to help them. The asking is not addressed to the idols; the asking is addressed to the audience members. The audience members are being asked to act on behalf of gods who cannot act on their own behalf. The asking is the form the absurdity takes when the absurdity has been exposed but the audience will not recognise the exposure.

**"If You Would Act"**

The conditional — *in kuntum fāʿilīn* — if you would act — names the form the audience's commitment takes. The audience has been talking about defending the gods; the audience is now being asked to actually do something. The doing is the form the commitment takes when the commitment has been exposed by the argument. The argument has shown that the gods cannot act; the audience is being asked to substitute their action for the gods'. The substitution is the form the absurdity takes.

The conditional is also a challenge. The audience is being asked to put up or shut up. The challenge is the form the trial takes when the trial has reached its climax. The audience has been arguing; the audience is now being asked to act. The acting is what the verdict will demonstrate; the demonstration is the form the audience's resistance takes.

**A Refusal of the Accusation of Nimrod**

The commentators, including al-Rāzī, note that the popular stories attached to this incident — particularly the assertion that it was Nimrod who ordered the burning — have no direct basis in the Qur'an. The Qur'an does not name the king; the Qur'an only records the audience's verdict. The verdict is the form the audience's resistance takes; the resistance is what the verse is documenting.

The comment is significant because it shows that the Qur'anic narrative is more austere than the elaborations the tradition has added. The verse records the audience's verdict; the verse does not name the king. The naming is not necessary for the narrative's argument; the argument is what the verse is asking the audience to consider. The considering is the form the response takes.

**A Verse That Sets Up the Miracle**

The verse is the setup for the miracle at 21:69. The audience has pronounced its verdict; the verdict will be the occasion for the divine intervention. The intervention is the form the argument has been preparing for. The argument has not just been verbal; the argument has been moving toward the moment when the divine power will be visibly demonstrated. The demonstration is what the verses that follow will record.

The demonstration is also part of the sūrah's polemic. The audience that has just ordered the burning of the messenger will be shown that the burning cannot harm the messenger. The showing is the form the divine intervention takes. The intervention is what the verse is preparing; the preparation is the form the narrative's polemic takes.

**A Verse That Returns to the Sūrah's Theme of the Wrongdoers**

The verse returns to one of the sūrah's recurring themes: the wrongdoers. The audience that has just pronounced the verdict of burning is the audience that the sūrah has been describing throughout. The wrongdoers are those who put something in the wrong place; the verdict places the messenger in the wrong place (the fire) and the audience's gods in the wrong place (as those who need help). The placing is the form the wrong takes when the wrong has been fully developed. The development is what the verses are documenting; the documentation is part of the sūrah's polemic.

The audience of the Qur'an is being asked to consider what it means to be in the position of the audience that is about to order the burning. The considering is the form the verse's call takes; the call is the substance of the response the verses are asking for.

**The Trial as a Mirror for Every Audience**

The trial Abraham faced is recorded in the Qur'an precisely because it mirrors the trial every messenger faces when the messenger has undermined the framework the audience had been trusting. The audience that had asked for a public trial wanted to make the verdict of condemnation a community event; the community event would strengthen the framework the demonstration had been threatening. The Qur'an records the trial not just to tell us what happened to Abraham but to show every subsequent audience what their own behaviour looks like when it is being reflected back to them in the mirror of the Abraham narrative. The mirror is what the verse is offering; the offering is the form the call takes to every generation that hears this recitation.

**The Quiet Polemic of the Detail**

The verse is doing a quiet polemic in the details of its narration. The detail about the public trial is not incidental; the detail is part of what the narrative is showing. The audience that wants witnesses, that wants public condemnation, that wants the trial to be a community event — that audience is the audience that has made the framework into a community possession. The framework is no longer a private opinion; the framework is the public identity of the community. The smashing of the idols threatened not just the religious framework but the community identity built on it. The exposure is part of what the verse is asking the audience to recognise.

**What the Verse Asks of the Audience Today**

The verse is asking the audience of the Qur'an to recognise the trial dynamics in their own lives. Every reader has, at some point, resisted an argument by gathering witnesses rather than by engaging with the argument itself. Every reader has faced a moment when their framework has been challenged and has chosen to make the framework a community possession rather than to consider whether the framework might be wrong. The verse is showing that pattern in its earliest scriptural form; the showing is what the verse is offering to every generation. The audience that recognises the pattern has begun the response the verse is asking for; the audience that refuses to recognise has refused the verse.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:69

> We ordered, “O fire! Be cool and safe for Abraham!”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Divine Intervention**

The verse records the divine intervention. *Qulnā yā nāru kūnī bardan wa-salāman ʿalā Ibrāhīm* — We said, O fire, be coolness and peace for Abraham. The verb *qāla* is in the first person plural — We said — and the address is to the fire itself. The fire is being addressed as an agent; the fire is being commanded to do something contrary to its nature. The command is the form the divine intervention takes.

The verse is one of the most famous in the Qur'an. The intervention has become a paradigm of divine deliverance; the deliverance is what the verse is recording. The recording is part of the sūrah's larger treatment of the prophets; the treatment is what the verses have been preparing for. The preparation is what the argument has been doing; the argument is what the previous verses have been building toward.

**"Be Coolness and Peace for Abraham"**

The constructive — *kūnī bardan wa-salāman* — be coolness and peace — names the form the command takes. The fire is commanded to be two things: coolness and peace. The coolness removes the heat; the peace removes the harm. The double specification is the form the divine command takes; the command is meant to do two things, and the two things are named.

The commentators note that without the specification of *salāma* — peace — the coolness alone might have been a problem. Al-Ṭabarī records that without the peace, the coolness would have been so extreme that Abraham would have been harmed by the cold. The peace is what makes the coolness safe; the safety is what the verse is asking the audience to recognise.

**Al-Rāzī's Three Possibilities**

Al-Rāzī records three ways in which the fire could have become coolness and peace. The first is that God removed the heat and the burning quality while leaving the illumination. The second is that God changed the nature of Abraham's body so that the fire could not affect him. The third is that God placed a barrier between Abraham and the fire. The three are possibilities; the verse does not specify which. The specification is not necessary for the verse's argument; the argument is what the verse is doing.

The possibilities are part of the commentators' effort to make the miracle intelligible. The intelligibility is not the point of the verse; the verse is making a different point. The point is that the fire, which was meant to be the instrument of the audience's verdict, has been transformed into the instrument of Abraham's deliverance. The transformation is the form the divine intervention takes.

**A Verse That Returns to the Sūrah's Theme of Deliverance**

The verse is one of several in the sūrah that record divine deliverances. 21:9 promised: *Then We fulfilled Our promise to them, saving them along with whoever We willed*. 21:71 will record: *They had sought to harm him, but We made them the worst losers*. The verse at 21:69 is the specific instance of the promise; the instance is what the verse is recording.

The recording is part of the sūrah's polemic. The audience of the Qur'an is being shown what divine deliverance looks like when it is needed. The looking is what the verse is offering; the offering is part of the polemic. The polemic is what the sūrah has been doing throughout; the polemic is the substance of the call the verses are making to the audience.

**A Verse That Reverses the Audience's Verdict**

The verse reverses the audience's verdict. The audience ordered the fire; the fire has been commanded to spare Abraham. The reversal is the form the divine intervention takes; the intervention is the response to the verdict. The response is not just the sparing of Abraham; the response is the transformation of the fire into the instrument of the sparing. The transformation is what the verse is asking the audience to consider.

This is one of the most theologically pointed moments in the entire Qur'an. The audience had used the fire as an instrument of punishment; the fire has been made an instrument of preservation. The use is what the audience had controlled; the preservation is what God has now produced. The production is the form the divine power takes; the power is what the verse is asking the audience to recognise.

**A Verse That Connects to the Audience of the Qur'an**

The verse is implicitly addressing the audience of the Qur'an. The audience is being asked to consider what it means to be in the position of those who face trials on account of their faith. The verse is offering the comfort that divine deliverance is possible; the comfort is the form the verse's call takes. The call is what the verse is making to the audience; the response is what the audience is being asked to give.

The audience that has been wondering whether the messenger of God is sincere is being shown the kind of deliverance that follows sincere messengers. The showing is the form the verse's evidence takes; the evidence is what the verse is asking the audience to consider. The considering is the form the response takes; the response is what the verse is asking for.

**The Trial as a Mirror for Every Audience**

The trial Abraham faced is recorded in the Qur'an precisely because it mirrors the trial every messenger faces when the messenger has undermined the framework the audience had been trusting. The audience that had asked for a public trial wanted to make the verdict of condemnation a community event; the community event would strengthen the framework the demonstration had been threatening. The Qur'an records the trial not just to tell us what happened to Abraham but to show every subsequent audience what their own behaviour looks like when it is being reflected back to them in the mirror of the Abraham narrative. The mirror is what the verse is offering; the offering is the form the call takes to every generation that hears this recitation.

**The Quiet Polemic of the Detail**

The verse is doing a quiet polemic in the details of its narration. The detail about the public trial is not incidental; the detail is part of what the narrative is showing. The audience that wants witnesses, that wants public condemnation, that wants the trial to be a community event — that audience is the audience that has made the framework into a community possession. The framework is no longer a private opinion; the framework is the public identity of the community. The smashing of the idols threatened not just the religious framework but the community identity built on it. The exposure is part of what the verse is asking the audience to recognise.

**What the Verse Asks of the Audience Today**

The verse is asking the audience of the Qur'an to recognise the trial dynamics in their own lives. Every reader has, at some point, resisted an argument by gathering witnesses rather than by engaging with the argument itself. Every reader has faced a moment when their framework has been challenged and has chosen to make the framework a community possession rather than to consider whether the framework might be wrong. The verse is showing that pattern in its earliest scriptural form; the showing is what the verse is offering to every generation. The audience that recognises the pattern has begun the response the verse is asking for; the audience that refuses to recognise has refused the verse.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:70

> They had sought to harm him, but We made them the worst losers

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Reversal of the Verdict**

The verse completes the reversal. *Wa arādu bihi kaydan fa-jaʿalnāhum al-akhsarīn* — they intended against him a plan, but We made them the greatest losers. The verb *arāda* — to intend, to plan — names the audience's action. The verb *jaʿala* — to make — names God's response. The response is the form the verse's argument takes; the argument is what the verse is asking the audience to recognise.

The verse is one of the most rhetorically compact in the entire narrative. The audience intended harm; God made them the greatest losers. The intending is human; the making is divine. The making is the form the divine economy takes; the economy is what the verse is documenting.

**"We Made Them the Greatest Losers"**

The Arabic *al-akhsarīn* — the greatest losers — is the Qur'an's strongest expression of failure. The audience has attempted to harm the messenger; the attempt has been transformed into the audience's own greatest loss. The loss is the form the divine response takes; the response is what the verse is recording.

This is one of the most theologically significant moments in the sūrah. The audience that ordered the burning is the audience that is now the greatest loser. The reversal is total; the totality is the form the divine response takes. The response is not just the sparing of Abraham; the response is the conversion of the audience's verdict into the audience's own punishment. The punishment is what the verse is asking the audience to consider.

**Connection to 21:9**

The verse echoes the promise made earlier in the sūrah: *Then We fulfilled Our promise to them, saving them along with whoever We willed and destroying the transgressors*. The verse at 21:70 is the specific instance of the general promise. The destruction of the transgressors is now recorded as the greatest losers. The recording is what the sūrah has been preparing for; the preparation is what the earlier verses have been doing.

The connection is part of the sūrah's structure. The sūrah is moving from the general promise to the specific instances. The instances are what the verses are now documenting. The documentation is part of the polemic; the polemic is what the sūrah is offering the audience of the Qur'an. The offering is the substance of the response the verses are asking for.

**A Verse That Returns to the Theme of Scheming**

The verse returns to one of the sūrah's recurring themes: scheming. The Arabic *kaid* — scheming, plotting, planning — is the same word that Abraham used for his own plan against the idols (21:57). The audience is now doing what Abraham had done: planning against something. The planning is the form the resistance takes; the resistance is what the verses are documenting.

The verse's use of *kaid* for both the messenger and the audience creates a striking parallel. Both Abraham and the audience had planned; the plans had different outcomes. Abraham's plan was a demonstration; the audience's plan was an attempt to harm. The plans are being judged by their outcomes. The judging is the form the divine economy takes; the economy is what the verse is asking the audience to recognise.

**A Verse That Connects to the Qur'an's Broader Treatment**

The verse is part of the broader Qur'anic treatment of divine deliverance. The prophets are tried; the prophets are delivered; the deliverances are recorded. The recording is the form the Qur'anic narrative takes; the narrative is what the Qur'an is offering the audience. The offering is the substance of the argument the Qur'an is making; the argument is what the verses are asking the audience to consider.

The audience of the Qur'an is being asked to consider what it would mean to be in the position of those who have tried to harm the messengers. The considering is the form the verse's call takes; the call is what the verse is offering the audience; the offering is the substance of the response the verses are asking for.

**A Verse That Closes the Abraham Narrative of the Sūrah**

The verse closes the Abraham narrative within the sūrah. The verses that follow will move to Lot (21:74–75) and then to other prophets. The closure is the form the Abraham narrative takes; the narrative has moved from the verbal diagnosis to the demonstration to the trial to the verdict to the deliverance. The progression is what the narrative is about. The about-ness is the substance of the sūrah's polemic.

The audience of the Qur'an is being asked to integrate the Abraham narrative into their understanding of the divine economy. The integrating is the form the response takes; the response is what the verses are asking for. The asking is the substance of the verse's call.

**The Trial as a Mirror for Every Audience**

The trial Abraham faced is recorded in the Qur'an precisely because it mirrors the trial every messenger faces when the messenger has undermined the framework the audience had been trusting. The audience that had asked for a public trial wanted to make the verdict of condemnation a community event; the community event would strengthen the framework the demonstration had been threatening. The Qur'an records the trial not just to tell us what happened to Abraham but to show every subsequent audience what their own behaviour looks like when it is being reflected back to them in the mirror of the Abraham narrative. The mirror is what the verse is offering; the offering is the form the call takes to every generation that hears this recitation.

**The Quiet Polemic of the Detail**

The verse is doing a quiet polemic in the details of its narration. The detail about the public trial is not incidental; the detail is part of what the narrative is showing. The audience that wants witnesses, that wants public condemnation, that wants the trial to be a community event — that audience is the audience that has made the framework into a community possession. The framework is no longer a private opinion; the framework is the public identity of the community. The smashing of the idols threatened not just the religious framework but the community identity built on it. The exposure is part of what the verse is asking the audience to recognise.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:71

> Then We delivered him, along with Lot, to the land We had showered with blessings for all people

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Deliverance to the Blessed Land**

The verse records Abraham's deliverance along with Lot. *Wa najjaynāhu wa Lūṭan ilā al-arḍi allatī bāraknā fīhā lil-ʿālamīn* — and We delivered him and Lot to the land that We blessed for all peoples. The verb *najjā* — to deliver, to save — is the same verb used throughout the sūrah for divine deliverance. The deliverance has brought Abraham and Lot to a specific land; the land has been blessed for all peoples.

The commentators differ on which land is meant. Al-Ṭabarī reads it as the Levant (Shām); al-Qurṭubī reads it as Mecca, where Abraham went to build the Kaʿbah with Ishmael (2:127). The land from which they were delivered is reputed to be Mesopotamia. The reading is part of the commentators' effort to place the Abraham narrative in a specific geography; the geography is not essential to the verse's argument, but the blessing is.

**"Blessed for All Peoples"**

The phrase *allatī bāraknā fīhā lil-ʿālamīn* — that We blessed for all peoples — names the form the blessing takes. The blessing is not for Abraham alone; the blessing is for all peoples. The blessing is therefore a universal blessing; the universality is part of what the verse is asking the audience to recognise. The land has been made a place of blessing for the entire human community; the recognition of the blessing is part of the response the verse is asking for.

The same vocabulary appears elsewhere in the Qur'an in connection with other prophets and other lands. The recurrence is the consistent application of the principle that divine blessing has been placed on specific lands for the benefit of all peoples. The principle is what the verse is asking the audience to recognise.

**Lot's Companionship in Deliverance**

Lot is mentioned here as a companion in the deliverance. *Lūṭan* — Lot — is the nephew of Abraham in the Qur'anic narrative (11:74–81); his wife was a disbeliever who stayed behind when the cities were destroyed. The companionship of Lot in the deliverance shows that the divine deliverance is not reserved for one person; the deliverance extends to those who have aligned themselves with the messenger.

The companionship is also part of the polemic against the audience. The audience of the Qur'an had been demanding that the message be restricted to a particular kind of recipient. The verse is showing that the deliverance extends across the lines the audience had been drawing. The drawing is what the verse is asking the audience to reconsider; the reconsideration is the form the verse's call takes.

**A Verse That Returns to the Sūrah's Theme of Deliverance**

The verse returns to the sūrah's theme of divine deliverance. The deliverance has been promised (21:9); the deliverance has been manifested in Abraham's case (21:69–70); the deliverance has now been extended to a specific land. The progression is what the sūrah is documenting. The documenting is the form the divine economy takes; the economy is what the verses are asking the audience to consider.

The audience of the Qur'an is being shown what divine deliverance looks like when it has been completed. The completion is the form the verse is recording; the recording is part of what the sūrah is offering to the audience; the offering is the substance of the response the verses are asking for. The response is the audience's recognition that the deliverance has been and is available; the recognition is what the verse is asking for.

**A Verse That Sets Up the Following Narrative**

The verse is the bridge between the Abraham narrative and the verses that will follow. The next verses will describe Abraham's descendants (21:72), his role as imam (21:73), Lot's deliverance (21:74–75), Noah's deliverance (21:76–77), David and Solomon's judgment (21:78–80), and the rest of the prophetic narratives. The bridge is what the verse is providing; the providing is the form the narrative's coherence takes.

The coherence is part of what the sūrah is doing. The sūrah is not just listing prophets; the sūrah is showing how the prophetic calling has continued from one messenger to the next. The continuation is the form the divine economy takes; the economy is what the audience of the Qur'an is being asked to recognise. The recognition is the substance of the response the verses are asking for; the asking is the form the call takes.

**The Trial as a Mirror for Every Audience**

The trial Abraham faced is recorded in the Qur'an precisely because it mirrors the trial every messenger faces when the messenger has undermined the framework the audience had been trusting. The audience that had asked for a public trial wanted to make the verdict of condemnation a community event; the community event would strengthen the framework the demonstration had been threatening. The Qur'an records the trial not just to tell us what happened to Abraham but to show every subsequent audience what their own behaviour looks like when it is being reflected back to them in the mirror of the Abraham narrative. The mirror is what the verse is offering; the offering is the form the call takes to every generation that hears this recitation.

**The Quiet Polemic of the Detail**

The verse is doing a quiet polemic in the details of its narration. The detail about the public trial is not incidental; the detail is part of what the narrative is showing. The audience that wants witnesses, that wants public condemnation, that wants the trial to be a community event — that audience is the audience that has made the framework into a community possession. The framework is no longer a private opinion; the framework is the public identity of the community. The smashing of the idols threatened not just the religious framework but the community identity built on it. The exposure is part of what the verse is asking the audience to recognise.

**What the Verse Asks of the Audience Today**

The verse is asking the audience of the Qur'an to recognise the trial dynamics in their own lives. Every reader has, at some point, resisted an argument by gathering witnesses rather than by engaging with the argument itself. Every reader has faced a moment when their framework has been challenged and has chosen to make the framework a community possession rather than to consider whether the framework might be wrong. The verse is showing that pattern in its earliest scriptural form; the showing is what the verse is offering to every generation. The audience that recognises the pattern has begun the response the verse is asking for; the audience that refuses to recognise has refused the verse.

**A Bridge Between Verse and Reader**

The verse is also doing the work of bridging between the historical narrative and the contemporary reader. The reader who has never faced a public trial can still recognise the dynamics of public resistance to private argument. The reader can recognise the recruitment of witnesses, the impatience for verdicts, the making of community identity into a defence for the framework. The recognition is what the verse is asking for; the asking is the form the call takes. The call is what the verse is offering to every generation that encounters this recitation; the offering is part of what the Qur'an continues to do for those who hear it.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:72

> And We blessed him with Isaac ˹as a son˺ and Jacob ˹as a grandson˺, as an additional favour—making all of them righteous

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Gift of Descendants**

The verse records the gift of descendants. *Wa wahabnā lahu Isḥāqa wa Yaʿqūba nāfilatan* — and We bestowed upon him Isaac and Jacob as an additional gift. The verb *wahaba* — to bestow, to grant as a gift — names the form the giving takes. The giving is not earned; the giving is gift. The gift is the substance of the verse's announcement; the announcement is what the verse is making.

The Arabic *nāfila* — additional gift, supererogatory gift — is a precise term. The commentators differ on what it modifies. Al-Ṭabarī reads it as referring to Jacob alone, on the reasoning that Abraham had prayed for Isaac (37:100) but not for a grandson. Al-Qurṭubī reads it as applying to both: the initial gift was Isaac, and the additional gift was Jacob. The reading is not essential to the verse's argument; the additionalness is what the verse is asking the audience to consider. The gift is bigger than what was prayed for.

**"Making All of Them Righteous"**

The verse continues *wa jaʿalnāhum salihīn* — and We made them righteous. The verb *jaʿala* — to make — names the act of making. The act is divine; the righteousness is the gift. The gift is what the verse is asking the audience to recognise. The recognition is the form the response takes.

The making of the prophets and their descendants righteous is one of the most theologically significant features of the Qur'anic narrative. The righteousness is not produced by the prophets themselves; the righteousness is given by God. The giving is the form the divine economy takes; the economy is what the verse is asking the audience to recognise.

**A Verse That Returns to the Sūrah's Theme of Divine Gifts**

The verse returns to the sūrah's recurring theme of divine gifts. 21:51 said: *And We had given Abraham his sound judgment before*. 21:48 said: *We gave Moses and Aaron the Criterion*. The verse at 21:72 is the next instance of the same theme. The gifts are what God has given to the prophets; the giving is what the sūrah is documenting; the documenting is part of what the verses are asking the audience to consider.

The audience of the Qur'an is being asked to consider what divine gifts they have received. The consideration is the form the verse's call takes; the call is what the verses are offering the audience; the offering is the substance of the response the verses are asking for. The response is the audience's recognition of the gifts they have received; the recognition is what the verses are asking for.

**A Verse That Extends the Abrahamic Narrative**

The verse extends the Abrahamic narrative by adding his descendants. The narrative had focused on Abraham himself (21:51–71); the verse now extends to Isaac and Jacob. The extension is the form the divine economy takes; the economy is what the verse is asking the audience to consider. The consideration is the substance of the response the verses are asking for.

The extension is also part of the broader Qur'anic argument about the continuity of the prophetic calling. The calling has been passed from one messenger to the next; the calling is the form the divine economy takes. The verses that follow (21:73) will extend the argument further by making Abraham and his descendants imams who guide by God's command. The extension is what the sūrah is doing; the doing is part of what the verses are asking the audience to recognise.

**The Trial as a Mirror for Every Audience**

The trial Abraham faced is recorded in the Qur'an precisely because it mirrors the trial every messenger faces when the messenger has undermined the framework the audience had been trusting. The audience that had asked for a public trial wanted to make the verdict of condemnation a community event; the community event would strengthen the framework the demonstration had been threatening. The Qur'an records the trial not just to tell us what happened to Abraham but to show every subsequent audience what their own behaviour looks like when it is being reflected back to them in the mirror of the Abraham narrative. The mirror is what the verse is offering; the offering is the form the call takes to every generation that hears this recitation. The pattern is not confined to Abraham's audience; the pattern has recurred wherever a community has built its identity on a framework that has been shown to be fragile.
**The Quiet Polemic of the Detail**

The verse is doing a quiet polemic in the details of its narration. The detail about the public trial is not incidental; the detail is part of what the narrative is showing. The audience that wants witnesses, that wants public condemnation, that wants the trial to be a community event — that audience is the audience that has made the framework into a community possession. The framework is no longer a private opinion; the framework is the public identity of the community. The smashing of the idols threatened not just the religious framework but the community identity built on it. The exposure is part of what the verse is asking the audience to recognise. The polemic works because the audience that hears the detail is the audience that has been making the framework; the detail is what the framework cannot absorb without contradiction.
**What the Verse Asks of the Audience Today**

The verse is asking the audience of the Qur'an to recognise the trial dynamics in their own lives. Every reader has, at some point, resisted an argument by gathering witnesses rather than by engaging with the argument itself. Every reader has faced a moment when their framework has been challenged and has chosen to make the framework a community possession rather than to consider whether the framework might be wrong. The verse is showing that pattern in its earliest scriptural form; the showing is what the verse is offering to every generation. The audience that recognises the pattern has begun the response the verse is asking for; the audience that refuses to recognise has refused the verse. The verse is not asking for a feeling; the verse is asking for a reordering of what the audience trusts when the audience is under pressure.
**A Bridge Between Verse and Reader**

The verse is also doing the work of bridging between the historical narrative and the contemporary reader. The reader who has never faced a public trial can still recognise the dynamics of public resistance to private argument. The reader can recognise the recruitment of witnesses, the impatience for verdicts, the making of community identity into a defence for the framework. The recognition is what the verse is asking for; the asking is the form the call takes. The call is what the verse is offering to every generation that encounters this recitation; the offering is part of what the Qur'an continues to do for those who hear it. The bridge is what the Qur'an continues to do for every generation; the building of the bridge is part of what the recitation is for.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:73

> We ˹also˺ made them leaders, guiding by Our command, and inspired them to do good deeds, establish prayer, and pay alms-tax. And they were devoted to Our worship

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Making of Imams**

The verse records the making of the prophetic descendants into leaders. *Wa jaʿalnāhum aʾimmatan yahdūna bi-amrinā* — and We made them imams guiding by Our command. The noun *imam* — leader, one who is followed — names the role; the verb *jaʿala* — to make — names the act. The making is divine; the leading is the form the role takes; the role is what the verse is asking the audience to recognise.

The commentators, including al-Ṭabarī and al-Rāzī, note that the making into imams means that they were the ones who were followed and imitated. They taught and guided people in matters of religion and good deeds. The guidance is the substance of the role; the role is what the divine economy produces. The producing is the form the verse's argument takes.

**"Guiding by Our Command"**

The phrase *yahdūna bi-amrinā* — guiding by Our command — specifies the form the guidance takes. The guidance is not by personal opinion or by inherited tradition; the guidance is by divine command. The guidance is therefore authoritative in a way that human guidance cannot be. The authority is the form the guidance takes; the guidance is what the verse is offering the audience to consider.

This is one of the most theologically significant features of the verse. The imams are not just leaders; the imams are leaders whose leading is by divine command. The leading is therefore not susceptible to the criticisms that human leadership is susceptible to; the leading is what God has commanded. The commanding is what the verse is asking the audience to recognise; the recognition is the form the response takes.

**The Three Inspired Actions**

The verse continues *wa awḥaynā ilayhim fiʿla al-khayrāti wa iqāma al-ṣalāti wa ītāʾi al-zakāti* — and We inspired them to do good deeds, to establish prayer, and to give alms. The three actions are the practical specifications of the prophetic calling. The good deeds are the general form of righteous action; the prayer is the form of devotion; the alms is the form of charity. The three are what the prophetic calling produces in those who follow it.

The verse is doing two things at once. The verse is describing what the prophets and their descendants did; the verse is also telling the audience what the audience is being asked to do. The good deeds, the prayer, and the alms are not just historical descriptions; they are invitations to the audience. The invitations are what the verse is offering; the offering is the substance of the response the verses are asking for.

**"They Were Worshippers of Ours"**

The verse closes *wa kānū lanā ʿābidīn* — and they were worshippers of Ours. The verb *ʿabada* — to worship — names the form of relationship the prophets and their descendants had with God. The relationship is described as worship; the worship is the form the relationship takes. The relationship is what the verse is asking the audience to consider.

The use of the first person plural — *lanā* — for Ours is significant. The worship is described as worship of God in the first person; the first person is the form the divine speech takes throughout the Qur'an. The form is what the verse is asking the audience to recognise. The recognition is the form the response takes; the response is the audience's recognition that the worship is owed; the worship is the substance of what the verse is asking for.

**A Verse That Connects to the Sūrah's Earlier Polemic**

The verse connects to the sūrah's earlier polemic against polytheism.
