## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:83

> And ˹remember˺ when Job cried out to his Lord, “I have been touched with adversity, and You are the Most Merciful of the merciful.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**Job's Cry**

The verse records Job's cry to God. *Wa Ayyūb idhā nādā rabbahu annī massaniya al-ḍurru wa anta arḥamu al-rāḥimīn* — and Job, when he called his Lord, "Truly affliction has touched me, and You are the Most Merciful of the merciful." The verb *nādā* — to call, to cry out — names the form the prayer takes. The prayer is the substance of the verse's announcement; the announcement is what the verse is asking the audience to consider.

Job's prayer combines two movements. The first is the acknowledgment of affliction — *massaniya al-ḍurru*, affliction has touched me. The second is the acknowledgment of God's mercy — *wa anta arḥamu al-rāḥimīn*, and You are the Most Merciful of the merciful. The two movements are not in tension; the two movements are part of the same prayer. The prayer acknowledges what has happened and asks for what can happen.

**The Affliction As a Test**

The commentators transmit much of the Job narrative from sources other than the Qur'an. The Qur'an itself names the affliction but does not specify its full nature; the verse simply says affliction has touched Job. The unspecified is part of the verse's argument; the argument is that affliction can come upon the righteous as well as upon the wicked, and the response to the affliction is what matters.

This is one of the most theologically significant features of the verse. The righteous are not exempt from affliction; the righteous are afflicted as the wicked are. The distinction is in the response. Job responded by crying out to God; the crying is the form the response takes. The form is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the substance of the response the verses are asking for.

**"The Most Merciful of the Merciful"**

Job's title for God — *arḥamu al-rāḥimīn* — is the most intense form of the Arabic superlative. The construction places Job's address of God at the very peak of divine names. The peak is part of what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.

This is one of the most distinctive features of the Job prayer. The prayer combines the acknowledgment of affliction with the recognition of divine mercy. The combination is the form the prayer takes when it is offered in the right spirit; the spirit is what the verse is asking the audience to consider. The consideration is the substance of the response the verses are asking for.

**A Verse That Returns to the Theme of Prayer in Distress**

The verse returns to one of the sūrah's recurring themes: prayer in distress. Noah cried out to God (21:76); Abraham pleaded with God in various ways; Zachariah will cry out to God (21:89); the prayer is the form the relationship with God takes when the relationship has been pressed. The pressing is what the verses are documenting; the documenting is part of what the sūrah is offering the audience; the offering is the substance of the response the verses are asking for.

The audience of the Qur'an is being asked to consider what their own prayer looks like when they face affliction. The consideration is the form the verse's call takes; the call is what the verse is offering the audience. The offering is the substance of the response the verses are asking for; the asking is the form the call takes.

**A Verse That Connects to the Broader Job Narrative**

The verse is part of the broader Qur'anic treatment of Job, which is told more fully in 38:41–44. The treatment in both sūrahs emphasises the prayer, the answer, and the restoration. The verse at 21:83 is the prayer; the next verse will be the answer. The verse is asking the audience to integrate the prayer and the answer; the integration is the form the response takes.

**The Trial as a Mirror for Every Audience**

The trial Abraham faced is recorded in the Qur'an precisely because it mirrors the trial every messenger faces when the messenger has undermined the framework the audience had been trusting. The audience that had asked for a public trial wanted to make the verdict of condemnation a community event; the community event would strengthen the framework the demonstration had been threatening. The Qur'an records the trial not just to tell us what happened to Abraham but to show every subsequent audience what their own behaviour looks like when it is being reflected back to them in the mirror of the Abraham narrative. The mirror is what the verse is offering; the offering is the form the call takes to every generation that hears this recitation.

**The Quiet Polemic of the Detail**

The verse is doing a quiet polemic in the details of its narration. The detail about the public trial is not incidental; the detail is part of what the narrative is showing. The audience that wants witnesses, that wants public condemnation, that wants the trial to be a community event — that audience is the audience that has made the framework into a community possession. The framework is no longer a private opinion; the framework is the public identity of the community. The smashing of the idols threatened not just the religious framework but the community identity built on it. The exposure is part of what the verse is asking the audience to recognise.

**What the Verse Asks of the Audience Today**

The verse is asking the audience of the Qur'an to recognise the trial dynamics in their own lives. Every reader has, at some point, resisted an argument by gathering witnesses rather than by engaging with the argument itself. Every reader has faced a moment when their framework has been challenged and has chosen to make the framework a community possession rather than to consider whether the framework might be wrong. The verse is showing that pattern in its earliest scriptural form; the showing is what the verse is offering to every generation. The audience that recognises the pattern has begun the response the verse is asking for; the audience that refuses to recognise has refused the verse.

**A Bridge Between Verse and Reader**

The verse is also doing the work of bridging between the historical narrative and the contemporary reader. The reader who has never faced a public trial can still recognise the dynamics of public resistance to private argument. The reader can recognise the recruitment of witnesses, the impatience for verdicts, the making of community identity into a defence for the framework. The recognition is what the verse is asking for; the asking is the form the call takes. The call is what the verse is offering to every generation that encounters this recitation; the offering is part of what the Qur'an continues to do for those who hear it.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:84

> So We answered his prayer and removed his adversity, and gave him back his family, twice as many, as a mercy from Us and a lesson for the ˹devoted˺ worshippers

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Answer to Job**

The verse records God's answer to Job's prayer. *Fa-istajabnā lahu wa kashafnā mā bihi min ḍurrin wa āṭaynāhu ahlahu wa mithlahum maʿahum raḥmatan min ʿindinā wa dhikrā lil-ʿābidīn* — so We answered him and removed what was of affliction in him, and We gave him his family and the like of them with them, as a mercy from Us and a reminder to the worshippers. The verb *istajāba* — to answer — is the same verb used for the answer to Noah's prayer (21:76). The answering is the form the divine response takes; the response is what the verse is recording.

The prayer in 21:83 was brief; the answer in 21:84 is specific. The specific removes the affliction, restores the family, doubles the household, and gives the restoration as a mercy and a reminder. The specific is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the consideration is the form the response takes.

**"We Gave Him His Family, Twice as Many"**

The Arabic *wa āṭaynāhu ahlahu wa mithlahum maʿahum* — and We gave him his family and the like of them with them — specifies the form the restoration takes. The family is restored, and the family is doubled. The doubling is not just a recovery; the doubling is an addition. The addition is what the divine mercy produces; the producing is the form the verse's argument takes.

This is one of the most theologically significant features of the verse. The divine restoration is not just restoration to the previous state; the restoration is restoration to a state of increase. The increase is what the divine mercy does; the doing is what the verse is asking the audience to consider.

**"As a Mercy from Us and a Lesson for the Worshippers"**

The verse closes *raḥmatan min ʿindinā wa dhikrā lil-ʿābidīn* — a mercy from Us and a reminder to the worshippers. The two terms specify two dimensions of what has happened. The mercy is the source; the reminder is the function. The source is the form the divine action takes; the function is the form the divine action serves.

This is one of the most distinctive features of the verse. The divine action is described in terms of both source and function. The source is the mercy; the function is the reminder. The reminder is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes. The response is what the verse is asking for.

**A Verse That Returns to the Theme of Divine Response**

The verse returns to one of the sūrah's recurring themes: divine response to prayer. Noah prayed and was answered (21:76–77); Job prayed and was answered (21:83–84). The recurrence is the consistent application of the principle that the one who calls out to God in the right spirit will be answered. The answering is the form the divine economy takes; the economy is what the verses are asking the audience to consider.

The audience of the Qur'an is being asked to consider what their own prayer life looks like. The consideration is the form the verse's call takes; the call is what the verse is offering the audience. The offering is the substance of the response the verses are asking for; the asking is the form the call takes.

**A Verse That Connects to the Broader Job Narrative**

The verse is part of the broader Qur'anic treatment of Job, which is told more fully in 38:41–44. The treatment in both sūrahs emphasises the prayer, the answer, and the restoration. The verse at 21:83 was the prayer; this verse is the answer. The pair forms the shape of what the prophetic call looks like when it has been pressed by affliction and answered by God. The shape is what the verses are asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.

**The Trial as a Mirror for Every Audience**

The trial Abraham faced is recorded in the Qur'an precisely because it mirrors the trial every messenger faces when the messenger has undermined the framework the audience had been trusting. The audience that had asked for a public trial wanted to make the verdict of condemnation a community event; the community event would strengthen the framework the demonstration had been threatening. The Qur'an records the trial not just to tell us what happened to Abraham but to show every subsequent audience what their own behaviour looks like when it is being reflected back to them in the mirror of the Abraham narrative. The mirror is what the verse is offering; the offering is the form the call takes to every generation that hears this recitation.

**The Quiet Polemic of the Detail**

The verse is doing a quiet polemic in the details of its narration. The detail about the public trial is not incidental; the detail is part of what the narrative is showing. The audience that wants witnesses, that wants public condemnation, that wants the trial to be a community event — that audience is the audience that has made the framework into a community possession. The framework is no longer a private opinion; the framework is the public identity of the community. The smashing of the idols threatened not just the religious framework but the community identity built on it. The exposure is part of what the verse is asking the audience to recognise.

**What the Verse Asks of the Audience Today**

The verse is asking the audience of the Qur'an to recognise the trial dynamics in their own lives. Every reader has, at some point, resisted an argument by gathering witnesses rather than by engaging with the argument itself. Every reader has faced a moment when their framework has been challenged and has chosen to make the framework a community possession rather than to consider whether the framework might be wrong. The verse is showing that pattern in its earliest scriptural form; the showing is what the verse is offering to every generation. The audience that recognises the pattern has begun the response the verse is asking for; the audience that refuses to recognise has refused the verse.

**A Bridge Between Verse and Reader**

The verse is also doing the work of bridging between the historical narrative and the contemporary reader. The reader who has never faced a public trial can still recognise the dynamics of public resistance to private argument. The reader can recognise the recruitment of witnesses, the impatience for verdicts, the making of community identity into a defence for the framework. The recognition is what the verse is asking for; the asking is the form the call takes. ## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:85

> And ˹remember˺ Ishmael, Enoch, and Ⱬul-Kifl. They were all steadfast

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Group of Patient Prophets**

The verse names three figures in a single sentence. *Wa Ismāʿīla wa al-Idrīsa wa Dhū al-Kifli kullun min al-ṣābirīn* — and Ishmael, and Idrīs, and Dhu'l-Kifl, each of them was among the patient. The verse is asking the audience to remember three specific prophets; the remembering is the form the call takes.

Ishmael's mention is significant. Ishmael is the son of Abraham and Hagar, the ancestor of the Arab peoples through whom the Prophet Muhammad would come. The Qur'an elsewhere mentions Ishmael with his father in the founding of the Kaʿbah (2:127). The mention here is in a list of patient prophets; the patience is what the verse is asking the audience to consider.

**Idrīs and Dhu'l-Kifl**

Idrīs is usually identified with the Biblical Enoch. The identification is consistent with what the Qur'an elsewhere says about Idrīs being raised to a high station (19:57). The mention here is brief; the brief is part of the verse's compression. The compression is what the verse is asking the audience to integrate with the longer treatments elsewhere.

Dhu'l-Kifl is a more obscure figure. He is also mentioned in 38:48. The traditional accounts identify him variously: as an Israelite entrusted with responsibility, as a prophet under various other names. Some Indian Muslim scholars have identified him with the Buddha. The Qur'an itself does not specify; the Qur'an only names him as one of the patient. The patience is what the verse is asking the audience to consider.

**"Each of Them Was Among the Patient"**

The verse's specification — *kullun min al-ṣābirīn* — is one of the most pointed in the entire sūrah. The Arabic *ṣābirīn* — the patient — is an intensive form. The patience is not just the patience of those who happen to wait; the patience is the patience of those who have made endurance their defining characteristic. The characteristic is what the verse is asking the audience to consider.

This is one of the most theologically significant features of the verse. The prophets named here are not just messengers; they are examples of patience. The patience is the quality the verse is offering the audience; the offering is the substance of the response the verses are asking for. The response is the audience's recognition that patience is a prophetic quality; the recognition is what the verse is asking for.

**A Verse That Returns to the Theme of the Patient**

The verse returns to one of the Qur'an's recurring themes: the patient. The patient are those who endure what they are given; the enduring is the form the patience takes. The Qur'an elsewhere describes the patient as those who will be rewarded without measure (39:10). The verse at 21:85 is asking the audience to consider what the patience looks like in specific prophetic lives. The considering is the form the response takes.

**A Verse That Connects to the Audience of the Qur'an**

The verse is implicitly addressing the audience of the Qur'an. The audience is being asked to consider what it would mean to be counted among the patient. The counting is the form the response takes; the response is what the verse is asking for. The asking is the form the call takes; the call is what the verse is offering the audience of every age.

The prophets named in the verse come from different times and different communities. The unifying quality is patience. The unification is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the consideration is the form the response takes; the response is what the verse is asking for.

**The Trial as a Mirror for Every Audience**

The trial Abraham faced is recorded in the Qur'an precisely because it mirrors the trial every messenger faces when the messenger has undermined the framework the audience had been trusting. The audience that had asked for a public trial wanted to make the verdict of condemnation a community event; the community event would strengthen the framework the demonstration had been threatening. The Qur'an records the trial not just to tell us what happened to Abraham but to show every subsequent audience what their own behaviour looks like when it is being reflected back to them in the mirror of the Abraham narrative. The mirror is what the verse is offering; the offering is the form the call takes to every generation that hears this recitation. The pattern is not confined to Abraham's audience; the pattern has recurred wherever a community has built its identity on a framework that has been shown to be fragile.
**The Quiet Polemic of the Detail**

The verse is doing a quiet polemic in the details of its narration. The detail about the public trial is not incidental; the detail is part of what the narrative is showing. The audience that wants witnesses, that wants public condemnation, that wants the trial to be a community event — that audience is the audience that has made the framework into a community possession. The framework is no longer a private opinion; the framework is the public identity of the community. The smashing of the idols threatened not just the religious framework but the community identity built on it. The exposure is part of what the verse is asking the audience to recognise. The polemic works because the audience that hears the detail is the audience that has been making the framework; the detail is what the framework cannot absorb without contradiction.
**What the Verse Asks of the Audience Today**

The verse is asking the audience of the Qur'an to recognise the trial dynamics in their own lives. Every reader has, at some point, resisted an argument by gathering witnesses rather than by engaging with the argument itself. Every reader has faced a moment when their framework has been challenged and has chosen to make the framework a community possession rather than to consider whether the framework might be wrong. The verse is showing that pattern in its earliest scriptural form; the showing is what the verse is offering to every generation. The audience that recognises the pattern has begun the response the verse is asking for; the audience that refuses to recognise has refused the verse. The verse is not asking for a feeling; the verse is asking for a reordering of what the audience trusts when the audience is under pressure.
**A Bridge Between Verse and Reader**

The verse is also doing the work of bridging between the historical narrative and the contemporary reader. The reader who has never faced a public trial can still recognise the dynamics of public resistance to private argument. The reader can recognise the recruitment of witnesses, the impatience for verdicts, the making of community identity into a defence for the framework. The recognition is what the verse is asking for; the asking is the form the call takes. The call is what the verse is offering to every generation that encounters this recitation; the offering is part of what the Qur'an continues to do for those who hear it. The bridge is what the Qur'an continues to do for every generation; the building of the bridge is part of what the recitation is for.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:86

> We admitted them into Our mercy, for they were truly of the righteous

**Expanded Commentary**

**Admission Into Mercy**

The verse records the admission of the three prophets into God's mercy. *Wa adkhalnāhum fī raḥmatinā innahum min al-ṣāliḥīn* — and We admitted them into Our mercy; indeed they were of the righteous. The verb *adkhala* is the same verb used for Lot's admission (21:75); the admission is what the verse is asking the audience to consider.

The admission is into the divine mercy; the mercy is the form the divine action takes. The action is what the verse is recording; the recording is part of the sūrah's argument that the prophets' lives are templates for the divine response to righteousness. The templates are what the verses are asking the audience to consider.

**"They Were Truly of the Righteous"**

The verse's specification — *innahum min al-ṣāliḥīn* — is one of the recurring descriptions in the sūrah. The righteous are those who have aligned their lives with the divine order; the aligning is the form the righteousness takes. The three prophets named in 21:85 are confirmed as being among the righteous; the confirmation is what the verse is offering the audience.

This is one of the most theologically significant features of the verse. The righteousness is not just a description; the righteousness is the basis for the admission into mercy. The admission is grounded in the righteousness; the grounding is what the verse is asking the audience to consider. The considering is the form the response takes; the response is what the verse is asking for.

**A Verse That Closes the Group of Three**

The verse closes the brief treatment of Ishmael, Idrīs, and Dhu'l-Kifl. The next verses will move to Jonah (21:87–88). The closure is the form the treatment takes; the treatment has named the three prophets and described them as patient and righteous. The description is what the verses are offering the audience; the offering is the substance of the response the verses are asking for.

The audience of the Qur'an is being asked to integrate the description into their understanding of the prophetic tradition. The integrating is the form the response takes; the response is what the verse is asking for.

**A Verse That Connects to the Audience of the Qur'an**

The verse is implicitly addressing the audience of the Qur'an. The audience is being asked to consider what it would mean to be admitted into the divine mercy. The admission is what the verses are offering; the offering is the substance of the response the verses are asking for. The asking is the form the call takes; the call is what the verse is making to the audience of every age.

The verse is also asking the audience to consider what it would mean to be counted among the righteous. The counting is the form the response takes; the response is what the verse is asking for.

**A Verse That Returns to the Theme of Mercy**

The verse returns to one of the sūrah's recurring themes: divine mercy. The mercy has been invoked in many forms throughout the sūrah (21:25, 21:36, 21:42, 21:75, 21:83). The recurrence is the consistent application of the principle that divine mercy is the form the divine action takes when the action is responding to righteousness. The principle is what the verse is asking the audience to recognise; the recognition is the form the response takes; the response is what the verse is asking for.

**The Trial as a Mirror for Every Audience**

The audience that had asked for a public trial wanted to make the verdict of condemnation a community event; the community event would strengthen the framework the demonstration had been threatening. The Qur'an records the trial not just to tell us what happened to Abraham but to show every subsequent audience what their own behaviour looks like when it is being reflected back to them in the mirror of the Abraham narrative. The mirror is what the verse is offering; the offering is the form the call takes to every generation that hears this recitation. The pattern is not confined to Abraham's audience; the pattern has recurred wherever a community has built its identity on a framework that has been shown to be fragile.

The verse is doing a quiet polemic in the details of its narration. The detail about the public trial is not incidental; the detail is part of what the narrative is showing. The audience that wants witnesses, that wants public condemnation, that wants the trial to be a community event — that audience is the audience that has made the framework into a community possession. The framework is no longer a private opinion; the framework is the public identity of the community. The smashing of the idols threatened not just the religious framework but the community identity built on it. The exposure is part of what the verse is asking the audience to recognise. The polemic works because the audience that hears the detail is the audience that has been making the framework; the detail is what the framework cannot absorb without contradiction.
**What the Verse Asks of the Audience Today**

The verse is asking the audience of the Qur'an to recognise the trial dynamics in their own lives. Every reader has, at some point, resisted an argument by gathering witnesses rather than by engaging with the argument itself. Every reader has faced a moment when their framework has been challenged and has chosen to make the framework a community possession rather than to consider whether the framework might be wrong. The verse is showing that pattern in its earliest scriptural form; the showing is what the verse is offering to every generation. The audience that recognises the pattern has begun the response the verse is asking for; the audience that refuses to recognise has refused the verse. The verse is not asking for a feeling; the verse is asking for a reordering of what the audience trusts when the audience is under pressure.

The reader can recognise the recruitment of witnesses, the impatience for verdicts, the making of community identity into a defence for the framework. ## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:87

> And ˹remember˺ when the Man of the Whale stormed off ˹from his city˺ in a rage, thinking We would not restrain him. Then in the ˹veils of˺ darkness he cried out, “There is no god ˹worthy of worship˺ except You. Glory be to You! I have certainly done wrong.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**Jonah's Departure**

The verse records Jonah's departure in anger. *Wa Dhu al-Nūni idhā dhahaba mughaḍiban* — and the Man of the Whale, when he went away angry. Jonah — *Dhu al-Nūn* — is so named from the whale that swallowed him. The whale incident is not told in full here; the verse assumes the audience knows the story. The assuming is part of what the verse is asking the audience to consider.

The going away in anger is significant. Jonah had been sent to Nineveh; the people had not yet believed; Jonah left. The leaving is part of what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the consideration is the form the response takes.

**"Thinking We Would Not Restrain Him"**

The verse continues *fa-ẓanna an lan naqdira ʿalayhi* — and he thought We would not straiten him. The Arabic *naqdira* — from *qadara* — can mean either to have power or to straiten or to withhold. The commentators disagree on which sense is intended. Some read it as Jonah believing God would not punish him for his sins; others read it as Jonah not believing God could end the punishment he was already suffering.

The ambiguity is part of the verse's argument. The argument is that even prophets can have moments of distress that lead to questionable choices. The choosing is what the verse is recording; the recording is part of the sūrah's broader treatment of the prophetic office as one that includes human moments. The treatment is what the verses are asking the audience to consider.

**"In the Veils of Darkness He Cried Out"**

The verse continues *fa-nādā fī al-ẓulumāti* — and he called out in the darkness. The Arabic *ẓulumāt* is in the plural form, and the commentators interpret the plural as the darkness of the belly of the fish within the darkness of the sea within the darkness of the night. The layered darkness is the form Jonah's situation had taken; the taking is what the verse is asking the audience to consider.

The prayer in the darkness — *lā ilāha illā anta subḥānaka innī kuntu min al-ẓālimīn* — there is no god but You, glory be to You, I was among the wrongdoers — is one of the most famous prayers in the Qur'an. The prayer acknowledges God and acknowledges the speaker's wrong. The acknowledging is the form the prayer takes; the prayer is what the verse is asking the audience to consider.

**A Verse That Returns to the Theme of Prayer in Distress**

The verse returns to one of the sūrah's recurring themes: prayer in distress. Noah prayed (21:76); Job prayed (21:83); now Jonah prays. The recurrence is the consistent application of the principle that prayer is the form the relationship with God takes when the relationship has been pressed. The pressing is what the verses are documenting; the documenting is part of what the sūrah is offering the audience.

**A Verse That Connects to the Audience of the Qur'an**

The verse is implicitly addressing the audience of the Qur'an. The audience is being asked to consider what it means to pray in distress. The prayer of Jonah is the prayer of one who has run from his duty, who has been swallowed by a whale, who has been pressed to the limit, and who has cried out from inside the layered darkness. The crying is the form the prayer takes; the prayer is what the verse is asking the audience to consider.

The verse is also asking the audience to consider what it means to acknowledge one's own wrongness in the prayer. The acknowledgment is part of Jonah's prayer; the acknowledgment is what the verse is offering the audience. The offering is the substance of the response the verses are asking for; the asking is the form the call takes.

**The Trial as a Mirror for Every Audience**

The trial Abraham faced is recorded in the Qur'an precisely because it mirrors the trial every messenger faces when the messenger has undermined the framework the audience had been trusting. The audience that had asked for a public trial wanted to make the verdict of condemnation a community event; the community event would strengthen the framework the demonstration had been threatening. The Qur'an records the trial not just to tell us what happened to Abraham but to show every subsequent audience what their own behaviour looks like when it is being reflected back to them in the mirror of the Abraham narrative. The mirror is what the verse is offering; the offering is the form the call takes to every generation that hears this recitation. The pattern is not confined to Abraham's audience; the pattern has recurred wherever a community has built its identity on a framework that has been shown to be fragile.
**The Quiet Polemic of the Detail**

The verse is doing a quiet polemic in the details of its narration. The detail about the public trial is not incidental; the detail is part of what the narrative is showing. The audience that wants witnesses, that wants public condemnation, that wants the trial to be a community event — that audience is the audience that has made the framework into a community possession. The framework is no longer a private opinion; the framework is the public identity of the community. The smashing of the idols threatened not just the religious framework but the community identity built on it. The exposure is part of what the verse is asking the audience to recognise. The polemic works because the audience that hears the detail is the audience that has been making the framework; the detail is what the framework cannot absorb without contradiction.
**What the Verse Asks of the Audience Today**

The verse is asking the audience of the Qur'an to recognise the trial dynamics in their own lives. Every reader has, at some point, resisted an argument by gathering witnesses rather than by engaging with the argument itself. Every reader has faced a moment when their framework has been challenged and has chosen to make the framework a community possession rather than to consider whether the framework might be wrong. The verse is showing that pattern in its earliest scriptural form; the showing is what the verse is offering to every generation. The audience that recognises the pattern has begun the response the verse is asking for; the audience that refuses to recognise has refused the verse. The verse is not asking for a feeling; the verse is asking for a reordering of what the audience trusts when the audience is under pressure.
**A Bridge Between Verse and Reader**

The verse is also doing the work of bridging between the historical narrative and the contemporary reader. The reader who has never faced a public trial can still recognise the dynamics of public resistance to private argument. The reader can recognise the recruitment of witnesses, the impatience for verdicts, the making of community identity into a defence for the framework. ## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:88

> So We answered his prayer and rescued him from anguish. And so do We save the ˹true˺ believers

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Answer and the Pattern**

The verse records God's answer to Jonah's prayer. *Fa-istajabnā lahu wa najjaynāhu min al-ghammi wa kadhalika nunjī al-muʾminīn* — so We answered him and saved him from the anguish, and thus We save the believers. The verb *istajāba* — to answer — is the same verb used for Noah (21:76) and Job (21:83). The answer is the form the divine response takes; the response is what the verse is recording.

The Arabic *ghamm* — anguish, distress, that which covers the heart — is a precise term. The distress Jonah experienced was not just physical; the distress was the kind that covers the heart. The covering is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the consideration is the form the response takes.

**"And Thus Do We Save the Believers"**

The verse continues *wa kadhalika nunjī al-muʾminīn* — and thus We save the believers. The shift from the specific (Jonah) to the general (the believers) is one of the most theologically significant moves in the verse. The same form of divine action that saved Jonah is the form the divine action always takes when the believers are in distress. The always is what the verse is asking the audience to consider.

This is one of the most distinctive features of the verse. The divine saving is not reserved for the prophets alone; the saving extends to all believers. The extending is what the verse is offering the audience; the offering is the substance of the response the verses are asking for. The response is the audience's recognition that the divine saving is available; the recognition is what the verse is asking for.

**A Verse That Returns to the Theme of Divine Response**

The verse returns to one of the sūrah's recurring themes: divine response to those who call out in distress. Noah was answered (21:76); Job was answered (21:83); Jonah is answered. The recurrence is the consistent application of the principle that the divine economy responds to those who call. The responding is what the verses are asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.

**A Verse That Connects to the Sufi Reading**

The Sufi tradition has offered a reading in which the verse is understood to teach that the divine saving comes to those who combine sincerity, awareness of their own needfulness of God, the cultivation of virtue, and an attitude of surrender. The reading extends the verse's meaning from Jonah's specific rescue to the general pattern of how the divine saving operates in the lives of all who believe. The general is what the verse is asking the audience to integrate; the integrating is the form the response takes.

**A Verse That Connects to the Audience of the Qur'an**

The verse is implicitly addressing the audience of the Qur'an. The audience is being asked to consider what it would mean to be among those whom God saves from distress. The saving is the substance of the response the verse is asking for; the asking is the form the call takes. The audience that hears the verse is being invited to recognise itself in the category of those whom God saves; the recognising is what the verse is offering; the offering is the substance of the response the verses are asking for.

The verse is also asking the audience to consider what conditions are attached to being among those whom God saves. The conditions are the conditions of sincerity, awareness of needfulness, virtue, and surrender. The considering is the form the response takes; the response is what the verse is asking for.

**The Trial as a Mirror for Every Audience**

The trial Abraham faced is recorded in the Qur'an precisely because it mirrors the trial every messenger faces when the messenger has undermined the framework the audience had been trusting. The audience that had asked for a public trial wanted to make the verdict of condemnation a community event; the community event would strengthen the framework the demonstration had been threatening. The Qur'an records the trial not just to tell us what happened to Abraham but to show every subsequent audience what their own behaviour looks like when it is being reflected back to them in the mirror of the Abraham narrative. The mirror is what the verse is offering; the offering is the form the call takes to every generation that hears this recitation. The pattern is not confined to Abraham's audience; the pattern has recurred wherever a community has built its identity on a framework that has been shown to be fragile.
**The Quiet Polemic of the Detail**

The verse is doing a quiet polemic in the details of its narration. The detail about the public trial is not incidental; the detail is part of what the narrative is showing. The audience that wants witnesses, that wants public condemnation, that wants the trial to be a community event — that audience is the audience that has made the framework into a community possession. The framework is no longer a private opinion; the framework is the public identity of the community. The smashing of the idols threatened not just the religious framework but the community identity built on it. The exposure is part of what the verse is asking the audience to recognise. The polemic works because the audience that hears the detail is the audience that has been making the framework; the detail is what the framework cannot absorb without contradiction.
**What the Verse Asks of the Audience Today**

The verse is asking the audience of the Qur'an to recognise the trial dynamics in their own lives. Every reader has, at some point, resisted an argument by gathering witnesses rather than by engaging with the argument itself. Every reader has faced a moment when their framework has been challenged and has chosen to make the framework a community possession rather than to consider whether the framework might be wrong. The verse is showing that pattern in its earliest scriptural form; the showing is what the verse is offering to every generation. The audience that recognises the pattern has begun the response the verse is asking for; the audience that refuses to recognise has refused the verse. The verse is not asking for a feeling; the verse is asking for a reordering of what the audience trusts when the audience is under pressure.
**A Bridge Between Verse and Reader**

The verse is also doing the work of bridging between the historical narrative and the contemporary reader. The reader who has never faced a public trial can still recognise the dynamics of public resistance to private argument. The reader can recognise the recruitment of witnesses, the impatience for verdicts, the making of community identity into a defence for the framework. The recognition is what the verse is asking for; the asking is the form the call takes. The call is what the verse is offering to every generation that encounters this recitation; the offering is part of what the Qur'an continues to do for those who hear it. The bridge is what the Qur'an continues to do for every generation; the building of the bridge is part of what the recitation is for.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:89

> And ˹remember˺ when Zachariah cried out to his Lord, “My Lord! Do not leave me childless, though You are the Best of Successors.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**Zachariah's Prayer**

The verse records Zachariah's prayer for an heir. *Wa Zakarīyā idhā nādā rabbahu rabbi lā tadarnī fardan wa anta khayru al-wārithīn* — and Zachariah, when he called his Lord, "My Lord, do not leave me alone, and You are the best of inheritors." The verb *nādā* — to call, to cry out — names the form the prayer takes. The prayer is the substance of the verse's announcement; the announcement is what the verse is asking the audience to consider.

Zachariah's prayer has two parts. The first is the request — *lā tadarnī fardan* — do not leave me alone, childless. The second is the doxological affirmation — *wa anta khayru al-wārithīn* — and You are the best of inheritors. The two parts together are the prayer; the prayer is what the verse is asking the audience to consider.

**"The Best of Successors"**

The Arabic *khayru al-wārithīn* — the best of inheritors — is one of the most pointed phrases in the verse. The phrase can be read as a simple affirmation of God as the ultimate heir: if there is no heir, God will be the heir. The verse is therefore asking the audience to consider what it means to entrust one's legacy to God.

This is one of the most theologically significant features of the verse. The prayer is not just a request for an heir; the prayer is an act of entrusting. The entrusting is the form the prayer takes when the prayer is offered in the right spirit. The spirit is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.

**A Verse That Returns to the Theme of Prayer**

The verse returns to one of the sūrah's recurring themes: prayer. Noah prayed (21:76); Job prayed (21:83); Jonah prayed (21:87); Zachariah prays. The recurrence is the consistent application of the principle that the prophets are not just those who speak; the prophets are also those who call out to God. The calling is the substance of the prophetic office; the office is what the verses are asking the audience to consider.

**The Higher Reading**

The Sufi tradition offers a reading in which Zachariah's prayer is interpreted at a higher level: the prayer is not just for an heir; the prayer is that Zachariah not be left bereft of intimacy with God and without a way that would lead to Him. The reading is consistent with the verse's emphasis on God as the best of inheritors. The inheritance is not just biological; the inheritance is the closeness of the divine. The closeness is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.

**A Verse That Connects to the Audience of the Qur'an**

The verse is implicitly addressing the audience of the Qur'an. The audience is being asked to consider what it would mean to entrust their legacy to God. The entrusting is the substance of the response the verses are asking for; the asking is the form the call takes. The audience of every age is being invited to recognise that the inheritance of the divine presence is the most valuable inheritance; the recognising is what the verse is asking for.

The verse is also asking the audience to consider what kind of prayer corresponds to the kind of trust Zachariah shows. The prayer is the form the trust takes when the trust has been pressed to the limit. The pressing is what the verses are documenting; the documenting is part of what the sūrah is offering the audience; the offering is the substance of the response the verses are asking for.

**The Trial as a Mirror for Every Audience**

The trial Abraham faced is recorded in the Qur'an precisely because it mirrors the trial every messenger faces when the messenger has undermined the framework the audience had been trusting. The audience that had asked for a public trial wanted to make the verdict of condemnation a community event; the community event would strengthen the framework the demonstration had been threatening. The Qur'an records the trial not just to tell us what happened to Abraham but to show every subsequent audience what their own behaviour looks like when it is being reflected back to them in the mirror of the Abraham narrative. The mirror is what the verse is offering; the offering is the form the call takes to every generation that hears this recitation. The pattern is not confined to Abraham's audience; the pattern has recurred wherever a community has built its identity on a framework that has been shown to be fragile.
**The Quiet Polemic of the Detail**

The verse is doing a quiet polemic in the details of its narration. The detail about the public trial is not incidental; the detail is part of what the narrative is showing. The audience that wants witnesses, that wants public condemnation, that wants the trial to be a community event — that audience is the audience that has made the framework into a community possession. The framework is no longer a private opinion; the framework is the public identity of the community. The smashing of the idols threatened not just the religious framework but the community identity built on it. The exposure is part of what the verse is asking the audience to recognise. The polemic works because the audience that hears the detail is the audience that has been making the framework; the detail is what the framework cannot absorb without contradiction.
**What the Verse Asks of the Audience Today**

The verse is asking the audience of the Qur'an to recognise the trial dynamics in their own lives. Every reader has, at some point, resisted an argument by gathering witnesses rather than by engaging with the argument itself. Every reader has faced a moment when their framework has been challenged and has chosen to make the framework a community possession rather than to consider whether the framework might be wrong. The verse is showing that pattern in its earliest scriptural form; the showing is what the verse is offering to every generation. The audience that recognises the pattern has begun the response the verse is asking for; the audience that refuses to recognise has refused the verse. ## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:90

> So We answered his prayer, granted him John, and made his wife fertile. Indeed, they used to race in doing good, and call upon Us with hope and fear, totally humbling themselves before Us

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Answer and the Restoration**

The verse records God's answer to Zachariah's prayer. *Fa-istajabnā lahu wa wahabnā lahu Yaḥyā wa aṣlaḥnā lahu zawjahu* — so We answered him and bestowed upon him John and made his wife sound for him. The verb *istajāba* is the same verb used throughout the sūrah for divine answering. The answering is the form the divine action takes; the action is what the verse is recording.

The Arabic *aṣlaḥnā* — we made sound, we made right — is being used here to mean making Zachariah's wife able to bear children. The commentators, including al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī, note that Zachariah's wife had been described as barren elsewhere (3:40). The verse is recording the reversal of the barrenness; the reversal is the substance of the divine answering.

**"They Used to Race in Doing Good"**

The verse continues *innahum kānū yusāriʿūna fī al-khayrāti* — indeed, they used to race in doing good. The verb *sāraʿa* — to race, to hasten — names the form the action takes. The action is racing; the racing is the substance of the verse's claim.

This is one of the most theologically significant features of the verse. The racing is in good deeds; the racing is between members of the same family; the racing is part of the family culture of the prophetic households. The culture is what the verse is offering the audience; the offering is the substance of the response the verses are asking for.

**"With Hope and Fear"**

The verse continues *wa yadʿūnānā raghaban wa rahaban* — and they used to call upon Us with desire and with fear. The Arabic *raghaban* — desire, hope, longing — and *rahaban* — fear, awe, trembling — name the two poles of the prophetic address to God. The address combines the two; the combining is the form the prayer takes when the prayer is offered in the right spirit.

This is one of the most distinctive features of the verse. The prayer is not just hope; the prayer is also fear. The fear is not the fear of punishment; the fear is the fear of awe. The awe is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.

**"Totally Humbling Themselves Before Us"**

The verse closes *wa kānū lanā khāshiʿīn* — and they were humble before Us. The Arabic *khāshiʿīn* — those who humble themselves, those who are in awe — names the form of relationship the verse is asking the audience to consider. The humbling is what the prophetic households exhibited; the exhibiting is the substance of the verse's claim.

This is one of the most pointed features of the Qur'anic treatment of the prophetic households. The households were humble before God; the humbling is part of what made the households prophetic. The humbling is what the verse is asking the audience to integrate; the integrating is the form the response takes.


The verse is part of the broader Qur'anic treatment of Zachariah, which is told more fully in 3:37–41 and 19:2–11. The treatment in all three places emphasises the prayer, the answer, and the birth of John. The verse at 21:90 is unique in its emphasis on the family dynamic of racing in good deeds, calling upon God with both hope and fear, and humbling themselves. The emphasis is what the verse is offering the audience; the offering is the substance of the response the verses are asking for.


The verse is implicitly addressing the audience of the Qur'an. The audience is being asked to consider what kind of family life would race in doing good, call upon God with hope and fear, and humble themselves before God. The considering is the form the verse's call takes; the call is what the verse is offering the audience. The offering is the substance of the response the verses are asking for; the asking is the form the call takes.

**The Trial as a Mirror for Every Audience**

The trial Abraham faced is recorded in the Qur'an precisely because it mirrors the trial every messenger faces when the messenger has undermined the framework the audience had been trusting. The audience that had asked for a public trial wanted to make the verdict of condemnation a community event; the community event would strengthen the framework the demonstration had been threatening. The Qur'an records the trial not just to tell us what happened to Abraham but to show every subsequent audience what their own behaviour looks like when it is being reflected back to them in the mirror of the Abraham narrative. The mirror is what the verse is offering; the offering is the form the call takes to every generation that hears this recitation.

**The Quiet Polemic of the Detail**

The verse is doing a quiet polemic in the details of its narration. The detail about the public trial is not incidental; the detail is part of what the narrative is showing. The audience that wants witnesses, that wants public condemnation, that wants the trial to be a community event — that audience is the audience that has made the framework into a community possession. The framework is no longer a private opinion; the framework is the public identity of the community. The smashing of the idols threatened not just the religious framework but the community identity built on it. The exposure is part of what the verse is asking the audience to recognise.

**What the Verse Asks of the Audience Today**

The verse is asking the audience of the Qur'an to recognise the trial dynamics in their own lives. Every reader has, at some point, resisted an argument by gathering witnesses rather than by engaging with the argument itself. Every reader has faced a moment when their framework has been challenged and has chosen to make the framework a community possession rather than to consider whether the framework might be wrong. The verse is showing that pattern in its earliest scriptural form; the showing is what the verse is offering to every generation. The audience that recognises the pattern has begun the response the verse is asking for; the audience that refuses to recognise has refused the verse.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:91

> And ˹remember˺ the one who guarded her chastity, so We breathed into her through Our angel, ˹Gabriel,˺ making her and her son a sign for all peoples

**Expanded Commentary**

**The One Who Guarded Her Chastity**

The verse refers to Mary. *Wa allatī aḥṣanat farjaha* — and she who guarded her chastity. The Arabic *aḥṣana* — to guard, to preserve, to keep inviolate — names the form the guarding takes. The guarding is the substance of the verse's announcement; the announcement is what the verse is asking the audience to consider.

The reference to Mary is brief here; the full story of Mary is told elsewhere (3:35–47, 19:16–35, 66:12). The brevity here is part of the verse's role in the larger sūrahic narrative. The narrative is what the verses are documenting; the documenting is part of what the sūrah is offering the audience; the offering is the substance of the response the verses are asking for.

**"We Breathed Into Her of Our Spirit"**

The verse continues *fa-nafakhnā fīhā min rūḥinā* — so We breathed into her of Our Spirit. The verb *nafakha* — to breathe, to blow — names the form the divine action takes. The action is the breathing; the breathing is the substance of the verse's claim. The claim is what the verse is asking the audience to consider.

This is one of the most theologically significant features of the verse. The breathing into Mary is what produced the pregnancy without a father; the pregnancy is one of the most discussed events in the Qur'anic narrative. The commentators, including al-Rāzī, note that the breathing into Mary parallels the breathing into Adam (38:72); the parallel is part of what the verse is asking the audience to consider.

**"Making Her and Her Son a Sign for All Peoples"**

The verse closes *wa jaʿalnāhā wa ibnāhā āyatan lil-ʿālamīn* — and We made her and her son a sign for all the worlds. The noun *āya* — sign — names the form the divine action takes. The action is the making; the making is the substance of the verse's claim.

This is one of the most distinctive features of the Qur'anic treatment of Mary and Jesus. They are presented together as a sign; the sign is for all peoples; the universality is part of what the verse is asking the audience to consider. The consideration is the form the response takes; the response is what the verse is asking for.

**A Verse That Returns to the Theme of Divine Signs**

The verse returns to one of the sūrah's recurring themes: divine signs. The heavens and the earth were signs (21:30); the cosmological order is a sign (21:30–33); now Mary and her son are a sign. The recurrence is the consistent application of the principle that the divine action produces signs that point beyond themselves. The pointing is what the verses are asking the audience to recognise; the recognising is the form the response takes.

**A Verse That Connects to the Audience of the Qur'an**

The verse is implicitly addressing the audience of the Qur'an. The audience is being asked to consider what it would mean to recognise Mary and Jesus as a sign for all peoples. The recognising is the form the verse's call takes; the call is what the verse is offering the audience of every age. The offering is the substance of the response the verses are asking for; the asking is the form the call takes.

The verse also asks the audience to consider how the divine breathing produces the most remarkable of human lives. The breathing is what the verses are asking the audience to recognise; the recognition is the form the response takes; the response is what the verse is asking for.

**The Trial as a Mirror for Every Audience**

The trial Abraham faced is recorded in the Qur'an precisely because it mirrors the trial every messenger faces when the messenger has undermined the framework the audience had been trusting. The audience that had asked for a public trial wanted to make the verdict of condemnation a community event; the community event would strengthen the framework the demonstration had been threatening. The Qur'an records the trial not just to tell us what happened to Abraham but to show every subsequent audience what their own behaviour looks like when it is being reflected back to them in the mirror of the Abraham narrative. The mirror is what the verse is offering; the offering is the form the call takes to every generation that hears this recitation. The pattern is not confined to Abraham's audience; the pattern has recurred wherever a community has built its identity on a framework that has been shown to be fragile.
**The Quiet Polemic of the Detail**

The verse is doing a quiet polemic in the details of its narration. The detail about the public trial is not incidental; the detail is part of what the narrative is showing. The audience that wants witnesses, that wants public condemnation, that wants the trial to be a community event — that audience is the audience that has made the framework into a community possession. The framework is no longer a private opinion; the framework is the public identity of the community. The smashing of the idols threatened not just the religious framework but the community identity built on it. The exposure is part of what the verse is asking the audience to recognise. The polemic works because the audience that hears the detail is the audience that has been making the framework; the detail is what the framework cannot absorb without contradiction.

Every reader has faced a moment when their framework has been challenged and has chosen to make the framework a community possession rather than to consider whether the framework might be wrong. The verse is showing that pattern in its earliest scriptural form; the showing is what the verse is offering to every generation. The audience that recognises the pattern has begun the response the verse is asking for; the audience that refuses to recognise has refused the verse. The verse is not asking for a feeling; the verse is asking for a reordering of what the audience trusts when the audience is under pressure.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:92

> ˹O prophets!˺ Indeed, this religion of yours is ˹only˺ one, and I am your Lord, so worship Me ˹alone˺

**Expanded Commentary**

**One Religion, One Lord**

The verse announces the unity of religion and the unity of lordship. *Inna hādhihi ummatukum ummatan wāḥidatan wa anā rabbukum fa-ʿbudūnī* — indeed, this community of yours is one community, and I am your Lord, so worship Me. The verse is addressed to the prophets; the address is the form the divine speech takes throughout the sūrah. The speech is what the verse is asking the audience to consider.

The Arabic *ummatan wāḥidatan* — one community — names the unity of the religious community. The unity is not just numerical; the unity is the form the prophetic message takes when the message is received in its essence. The receiving is what the verse is asking the audience to integrate; the integrating is the form the response takes.

**"I Am Your Lord, So Worship Me"**

The verse continues *wa anā rabbukum fa-ʿbudūnī* — and I am your Lord, so worship Me. The structure is one of the most pointed in the Qur'an: identity, then demand. The identity is the Lord's; the demand is for worship; the worship is the substance of the response the verse is asking for.

This is one of the most theologically significant features of the verse. The demand is grounded in the identity. The identity is what makes the demand appropriate; the appropriateness is what the verse is asking the audience to consider. The considering is the form the response takes; the response is what the verse is asking for.

**A Verse That Returns to the Sūrah's Central Theme**

The verse returns to the sūrah's most central theme: the oneness of God and the call to worship Him alone. The theme has appeared throughout (21:25, 21:36, 21:42). The verse at 21:92 is the explicit declaration: the religion is one; the Lord is one; the worship is owed to one. The declaration is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the consideration is the form the response takes.

**A Verse That Connects to the Broader Qur'anic Treatment**

The verse is part of the broader Qur'anic treatment of the unity of religion. 23:52, 42:14, and other passages develop the same theme. The treatment is consistent across the Qur'an: the religion has always been one; the messengers have always brought the same essential message; the fragmentation that has occurred is the result of human disagreement, not of divine instruction. The treating is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.

**A Verse That Connects to the Audience of the Qur'an**

The verse is implicitly addressing the audience of the Qur'an. The audience is being asked to consider what it would mean to live in recognition of the unity of the religion and the unity of the Lord. The recognising is the form the verse's call takes; the call is what the verse is offering the audience. The offering is the substance of the response the verses are asking for; the asking is the form the call takes.

The verse is also asking the audience to consider what it would mean to worship God alone. The worship is the substance of the response the verse is asking for; the asking is the form the call takes. The audience that recognises the unity of religion and the unity of the Lord has begun the response the verse is asking for; the audience that refuses has refused the verse.

**The Trial as a Mirror for Every Audience**

The trial Abraham faced is recorded in the Qur'an precisely because it mirrors the trial every messenger faces when the messenger has undermined the framework the audience had been trusting. The audience that had asked for a public trial wanted to make the verdict of condemnation a community event; the community event would strengthen the framework the demonstration had been threatening. The Qur'an records the trial not just to tell us what happened to Abraham but to show every subsequent audience what their own behaviour looks like when it is being reflected back to them in the mirror of the Abraham narrative. The mirror is what the verse is offering; the offering is the form the call takes to every generation that hears this recitation. The pattern is not confined to Abraham's audience; the pattern has recurred wherever a community has built its identity on a framework that has been shown to be fragile.
**The Quiet Polemic of the Detail**

The verse is doing a quiet polemic in the details of its narration. The detail about the public trial is not incidental; the detail is part of what the narrative is showing. The audience that wants witnesses, that wants public condemnation, that wants the trial to be a community event — that audience is the audience that has made the framework into a community possession. The framework is no longer a private opinion; the framework is the public identity of the community. The smashing of the idols threatened not just the religious framework but the community identity built on it. The exposure is part of what the verse is asking the audience to recognise. The polemic works because the audience that hears the detail is the audience that has been making the framework; the detail is what the framework cannot absorb without contradiction.

The verse is asking the audience of the Qur'an to recognise the trial dynamics in their own lives. Every reader has, at some point, resisted an argument by gathering witnesses rather than by engaging with the argument itself. Every reader has faced a moment when their framework has been challenged and has chosen to make the framework a community possession rather than to consider whether the framework might be wrong. The verse is showing that pattern in its earliest scriptural form; the showing is what the verse is offering to every generation. The audience that recognises the pattern has begun the response the verse is asking for; the audience that refuses to recognise has refused the verse. The verse is not asking for a feeling; the verse is asking for a reordering of what the audience trusts when the audience is under pressure.

The reader can recognise the recruitment of witnesses, the impatience for verdicts, the making of community identity into a defence for the framework.
