## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:93

> Yet the people have divided it into sects. But to Us they will all return

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Fragmentation**

The verse records what has happened to the unified religion. *Wa taqṭaʿū amrahum baynahum thubūr* — and they have fragmented their affair among themselves into sects. The Arabic *taqṭaʿa* — to cut into pieces, to divide — names the form the action takes. The action is the fragmentation; the fragmentation is the substance of the verse's claim.

The verse is one of several in the Qur'an that addresses the divisions within religious communities. 2:213, 3:19, 5:48, and 42:14 all develop the same theme: the religion was one; the communities divided; the division is the form the human reception of the religion takes when the reception has been corrupted. The corruption is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.

**"But to Us They Will All Return"**

The verse continues *wa ilaynā tarjiʿūn* — and unto Us they shall return. The verb *tarjiʿūn* — you will return — names the form the future takes. The future is the return to God; the return is the substance of the verse's claim.

This is one of the most theologically significant features of the verse. The fragmentation is not the final word; the return to God is the final word. The final word is what the verse is offering the audience; the offering is the substance of the response the verses are asking for. The response is the audience's recognition that the fragmentation will be reversed at the return; the recognition is what the verse is asking for.

**A Verse That Returns to the Theme of the Unified Religion**

The verse returns to the sūrah's previous declaration (21:92) that the religion is one. The declaration and the fragmentation are paired in the same passage: the religion is one (21:92); the people fragmented it (21:93). The pairing is the form the verse's argument takes; the argument is that the unity was given and the fragmentation happened. The fragmentation is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.

**A Verse That Connects to the Broader Qur'anic Treatment**

The verse is part of the broader Qur'anic treatment of religious fragmentation. The Qur'an repeatedly returns to the theme of the fragmentation of the original monotheistic message into sects and denominations. The repetition is the consistent application of the principle that the human reception of divine guidance is fragile. The fragility is what the verses are asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.

**A Verse That Connects to the Audience of the Qur'an**

The verse is implicitly addressing the audience of the Qur'an. The audience is being asked to consider what it would mean to recover the unified religion that was given and not to participate in the fragmentation. The recovering is the form the verse's call takes; the call is what the verse is offering the audience. The offering is the substance of the response the verses are asking for; the asking is the form the call takes.

The audience of the Qur'an is also being asked to consider what it would mean to return to God. The returning is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes; the response is what the verse is asking for.

**The Trial as a Mirror for Every Audience**

The trial Abraham faced is recorded in the Qur'an precisely because it mirrors the trial every messenger faces when the messenger has undermined the framework the audience had been trusting. The audience that had asked for a public trial wanted to make the verdict of condemnation a community event; the community event would strengthen the framework the demonstration had been threatening. The Qur'an records the trial not just to tell us what happened to Abraham but to show every subsequent audience what their own behaviour looks like when it is being reflected back to them in the mirror of the Abraham narrative. The mirror is what the verse is offering; the offering is the form the call takes to every generation that hears this recitation. The pattern is not confined to Abraham's audience; the pattern has recurred wherever a community has built its identity on a framework that has been shown to be fragile.
**The Quiet Polemic of the Detail**

The verse is doing a quiet polemic in the details of its narration. The detail about the public trial is not incidental; the detail is part of what the narrative is showing. The audience that wants witnesses, that wants public condemnation, that wants the trial to be a community event — that audience is the audience that has made the framework into a community possession. The framework is no longer a private opinion; the framework is the public identity of the community. The smashing of the idols threatened not just the religious framework but the community identity built on it. The exposure is part of what the verse is asking the audience to recognise. The polemic works because the audience that hears the detail is the audience that has been making the framework; the detail is what the framework cannot absorb without contradiction.
**What the Verse Asks of the Audience Today**

The verse is asking the audience of the Qur'an to recognise the trial dynamics in their own lives. Every reader has, at some point, resisted an argument by gathering witnesses rather than by engaging with the argument itself. Every reader has faced a moment when their framework has been challenged and has chosen to make the framework a community possession rather than to consider whether the framework might be wrong. The verse is showing that pattern in its earliest scriptural form; the showing is what the verse is offering to every generation. The audience that recognises the pattern has begun the response the verse is asking for; the audience that refuses to recognise has refused the verse. The verse is not asking for a feeling; the verse is asking for a reordering of what the audience trusts when the audience is under pressure.
**A Bridge Between Verse and Reader**

The verse is also doing the work of bridging between the historical narrative and the contemporary reader. The reader who has never faced a public trial can still recognise the dynamics of public resistance to private argument. The reader can recognise the recruitment of witnesses, the impatience for verdicts, the making of community identity into a defence for the framework. The recognition is what the verse is asking for; the asking is the form the call takes. The call is what the verse is offering to every generation that encounters this recitation; the offering is part of what the Qur'an continues to do for those who hear it. The bridge is what the Qur'an continues to do for every generation; the building of the bridge is part of what the recitation is for.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:94

> So whoever does good and is a believer will never be denied ˹the reward for˺ their striving, for We are recording it all

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Guarantee of Reward**

The verse guarantees that the striving of the believer who does good will not be denied. *Wa man yaʿmalu ṣāliḥan min al-muʾminīn fa-lā yakfurru ʿamalahu* — and whoever works righteousness among the believers, their work will not be denied. The verb *kafara* — in this usage to deny, to be ungrateful for — names the form the denial would take. The denial will not occur; the verse is the announcement that the denial will not occur.

The verse is connected to a recurring Qur'anic theme. 17:19 says: *And whoever desires the Hereafter, and strives for it with the effort that is its due, and is a believer — those are the ones whose striving will be appreciated*. The verse at 21:94 is the explicit declaration that the striving will be appreciated. The declaration is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.

**"We Are Recording It All"**

The verse continues *wa inna lanā la-hu kātibūn* — and indeed We are writing it down for him. The verb *kataba* — to write — names the form the divine action takes. The action is the writing; the writing is the substance of the verse's claim.

This is one of the most theologically significant features of the verse. The divine writing is the form the divine recording of deeds takes. The recording is consistent throughout the Qur'an: every deed is recorded (10:21, 36:12, 45:28–29, 78:29). The recording is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.

**A Verse That Returns to the Theme of Divine Recording**

The verse returns to one of the sūrah's recurring themes: divine recording. 21:47 said: *If a deed is the weight of a mustard seed, We will bring it forth*. The verse at 21:94 is the same theme: every deed is recorded. The recording is the consistent application of the principle that nothing done is lost. The principle is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.

**A Verse That Connects to the Audience of the Qur'an**

The verse is implicitly addressing the audience of the Qur'an. The audience is being asked to consider what it would mean to have one's striving guaranteed reward. The guarantee is what the verse is offering the audience; the offering is the substance of the response the verses are asking for. The asking is the form the call takes; the call is what the verse is making to the audience of every age.

The audience is also being asked to consider what it would mean to be among those whose striving is recorded. The recording is the form the divine economy takes; the economy is what the verse is asking the audience to recognise. The recognition is the substance of the response the verses are asking for.

**A Verse That Closes the Long Prophetic Section**

The verse closes the long prophetic narrative section of the sūrah (21:51–94). The verses that follow will move to eschatological themes: the destruction of the communities that refused the messengers, the unleashing of Gog and Magog, the coming of the true promise, the fuel of Hell. The closing is the form the narrative takes; the narrative has moved from the messengers to the consequences. The consequences are what the verses will next address; the addressing is what the sūrah is offering the audience.

**The Trial as a Mirror for Every Audience**

The trial Abraham faced is recorded in the Qur'an precisely because it mirrors the trial every messenger faces when the messenger has undermined the framework the audience had been trusting. The audience that had asked for a public trial wanted to make the verdict of condemnation a community event; the community event would strengthen the framework the demonstration had been threatening. The Qur'an records the trial not just to tell us what happened to Abraham but to show every subsequent audience what their own behaviour looks like when it is being reflected back to them in the mirror of the Abraham narrative. The mirror is what the verse is offering; the offering is the form the call takes to every generation that hears this recitation. The pattern is not confined to Abraham's audience; the pattern has recurred wherever a community has built its identity on a framework that has been shown to be fragile.
**The Quiet Polemic of the Detail**

The verse is doing a quiet polemic in the details of its narration. The detail about the public trial is not incidental; the detail is part of what the narrative is showing. The audience that wants witnesses, that wants public condemnation, that wants the trial to be a community event — that audience is the audience that has made the framework into a community possession. The framework is no longer a private opinion; the framework is the public identity of the community. The smashing of the idols threatened not just the religious framework but the community identity built on it. The exposure is part of what the verse is asking the audience to recognise. The polemic works because the audience that hears the detail is the audience that has been making the framework; the detail is what the framework cannot absorb without contradiction.
**What the Verse Asks of the Audience Today**

The verse is asking the audience of the Qur'an to recognise the trial dynamics in their own lives. Every reader has, at some point, resisted an argument by gathering witnesses rather than by engaging with the argument itself. Every reader has faced a moment when their framework has been challenged and has chosen to make the framework a community possession rather than to consider whether the framework might be wrong. The verse is showing that pattern in its earliest scriptural form; the showing is what the verse is offering to every generation. The audience that recognises the pattern has begun the response the verse is asking for; the audience that refuses to recognise has refused the verse. The verse is not asking for a feeling; the verse is asking for a reordering of what the audience trusts when the audience is under pressure.
**A Bridge Between Verse and Reader**

The verse is also doing the work of bridging between the historical narrative and the contemporary reader. The reader who has never faced a public trial can still recognise the dynamics of public resistance to private argument. The reader can recognise the recruitment of witnesses, the impatience for verdicts, the making of community identity into a defence for the framework. The recognition is what the verse is asking for; the asking is the form the call takes. The call is what the verse is offering to every generation that encounters this recitation; the offering is part of what the Qur'an continues to do for those who hear it. The bridge is what the Qur'an continues to do for every generation; the building of the bridge is part of what the recitation is for.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:95

> It is impossible for a society which We have destroyed to ever rise again

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Forbidden Return**

The verse states that a destroyed community cannot return. *Wa ḥarramunnā ʿalayhi al-qaryata al-muhlikata annahum lā yarjiʿūn* — and We have forbidden unto those towns that We have destroyed that they shall not return. The verb *ḥarrama* — to forbid — names the form the divine action takes. The forbidding is what the verse is recording; the recording is the form the verse's announcement takes.

The verse has an apparent double negative that commentators discuss. Some read it as meaning that the destroyed communities will not return to the world. Others read it as meaning that they will inevitably return to God in the Hereafter. The two readings need not be exclusive; the verse can be showing both the absence of return to the world and the certainty of return to God.

**"Towns That We Have Destroyed"**

The Arabic *al-qaryata al-muhlikata* — towns that have been destroyed — is the technical term for the communities that have been destroyed by divine punishment. The verse is referring to all the communities mentioned in the sūrah's earlier narratives: the community of Noah, of Abraham, of Lot, of Moses. The destruction is the form the divine response took; the taking is what the verse is asking the audience to consider.

This is one of the most theologically significant features of the verse. The destruction is final in this world; the destruction is the form the divine punishment takes. The finality is what the verses are offering the audience; the offering is the substance of the response the verses are asking for. The response is the audience's recognition that the destruction is final; the recognition is what the verse is asking for.

**A Verse That Returns to the Theme of Punishment**

The verse returns to one of the sūrah's recurring themes: divine punishment. The punishment has been promised throughout (21:11–15, 21:38, 21:46). The verse at 21:95 is the explicit declaration that the punishment is final. The declaration is what the verse is asking the audience to integrate; the integrating is the form the response takes.

**A Verse That Connects to the Broader Qur'anic Treatment**

The verse is part of the broader Qur'anic treatment of the finality of divine punishment. 2:217, 3:144, and other passages develop the same theme: the punishment, when it comes, is final. The treatment is consistent across the Qur'an; the finality is what the verse is asking the audience to consider.

**A Verse That Connects to the Audience of the Qur'an**

The verse is implicitly addressing the audience of the Qur'an. The audience is being asked to consider what it would mean to be among the destroyed communities whose return is forbidden. The considering is the form the verse's call takes; the call is what the verse is offering the audience. The offering is the substance of the response the verses are asking for; the asking is the form the call takes.

The audience is also being asked to consider what it would mean to have one's return to this world permanently closed. The closing is what the verse is asking the audience to recognise; the recognising is the substance of the response the verses are asking for.

**The Trial as a Mirror for Every Audience**

The trial Abraham faced is recorded in the Qur'an precisely because it mirrors the trial every messenger faces when the messenger has undermined the framework the audience had been trusting. The audience that had asked for a public trial wanted to make the verdict of condemnation a community event; the community event would strengthen the framework the demonstration had been threatening. The Qur'an records the trial not just to tell us what happened to Abraham but to show every subsequent audience what their own behaviour looks like when it is being reflected back to them in the mirror of the Abraham narrative. The mirror is what the verse is offering; the offering is the form the call takes to every generation that hears this recitation. The pattern is not confined to Abraham's audience; the pattern has recurred wherever a community has built its identity on a framework that has been shown to be fragile.
**The Quiet Polemic of the Detail**

The verse is doing a quiet polemic in the details of its narration. The detail about the public trial is not incidental; the detail is part of what the narrative is showing. The audience that wants witnesses, that wants public condemnation, that wants the trial to be a community event — that audience is the audience that has made the framework into a community possession. The framework is no longer a private opinion; the framework is the public identity of the community. The smashing of the idols threatened not just the religious framework but the community identity built on it. The exposure is part of what the verse is asking the audience to recognise. The polemic works because the audience that hears the detail is the audience that has been making the framework; the detail is what the framework cannot absorb without contradiction.
**What the Verse Asks of the Audience Today**

The verse is asking the audience of the Qur'an to recognise the trial dynamics in their own lives. Every reader has, at some point, resisted an argument by gathering witnesses rather than by engaging with the argument itself. Every reader has faced a moment when their framework has been challenged and has chosen to make the framework a community possession rather than to consider whether the framework might be wrong. The verse is showing that pattern in its earliest scriptural form; the showing is what the verse is offering to every generation. The audience that recognises the pattern has begun the response the verse is asking for; the audience that refuses to recognise has refused the verse. The verse is not asking for a feeling; the verse is asking for a reordering of what the audience trusts when the audience is under pressure.
**A Bridge Between Verse and Reader**

The verse is also doing the work of bridging between the historical narrative and the contemporary reader. The reader who has never faced a public trial can still recognise the dynamics of public resistance to private argument. The reader can recognise the recruitment of witnesses, the impatience for verdicts, the making of community identity into a defence for the framework. The recognition is what the verse is asking for; the asking is the form the call takes. ## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:96

> until ˹after˺ Gog and Magog have broken loose ˹from the barrier˺, swarming down from every hill

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Unleashing**

The verse names the unleashing. *Ḥattā idhā futiḥat Yajūj wa Majūj wa hum min kulli ḥadabin yansilūn* — until Gog and Magog are opened, and they rush down from every hill. The verb *fatiḥa* — to be opened — names the form the divine action takes. The action is the opening; the opening is the substance of the verse's announcement.

Gog and Magog (Yajūj wa Majūj) are described in the Qur'an in 18:94–99 as vicious and destructive beings locked away by the figure Dhu'l-Qarnayn behind a barrier of iron and copper. The unleashing is the form the eschatological chaos takes; the chaos is what the verse is asking the audience to consider. The unleashing has been promised throughout the sūrah; the promise is the form the divine economy takes; the economy is what the verse is asking the audience to consider.
**"From Every Hill"**

The phrase *min kulli ḥadabin yansilūn* — they rush down from every hill — specifies the form the unleashing takes. The Arabic *hadab* — hill, high place — names the kind of place from which they descend. The descent is from heights; the descent is the form the chaos takes.

This is one of the most theologically significant features of the verse. The unleashing produces a flood of descent; the descent overwhelms whatever is below. The overwhelming is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes. The hill is every elevated place; the elevated places will produce what they have been given to produce. The producing is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.
**A Verse That Returns to the Theme of Eschatological Chaos**

The verse returns to one of the sūrah's recurring themes: the chaos that will come at the end of time. The chaos has been described throughout the sūrah (21:40, 21:46). The verse at 21:96 is one of the most vivid descriptions; the description is what the verse is asking the audience to consider. The chaos has been the recurring description throughout the sūrah (21:11, 21:30, 21:44); the recurrence is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.
**A Verse That Connects to the Broader Qur'anic Treatment**

The verse is part of the broader Qur'anic treatment of the eschatological chaos. 18:94–99, 27:82, and other passages develop the same theme. The treatment is consistent across the Qur'an; the chaos is what the verse is asking the audience to integrate; the integrating is the form the response takes. The broader treatment can be traced through 27:87, 39:67, and 69:13–16, all of which describe the cosmic transformation with similar imagery. The imagery is what the verse is asking the audience to consider.
**A Verse That Connects to the Audience of the Qur'an**

The verse is implicitly addressing the audience of the Qur'an. The audience is being asked to consider what it would mean to live in the time leading up to the unleashing. The considering is the form the verse's call takes; the call is what the verse is offering the audience. The offering is the substance of the response the verses are asking for; the asking is the form the call takes.

The audience is also being asked to consider what it would mean to live in a world in which the barrier that holds back chaos has been removed. The removing is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.

**The Trial as a Mirror for Every Audience**

The trial Abraham faced is recorded in the Qur'an precisely because it mirrors the trial every messenger faces when the messenger has undermined the framework the audience had been trusting. The audience that had asked for a public trial wanted to make the verdict of condemnation a community event; the community event would strengthen the framework the demonstration had been threatening. The Qur'an records the trial not just to tell us what happened to Abraham but to show every subsequent audience what their own behaviour looks like when it is being reflected back to them in the mirror of the Abraham narrative. The mirror is what the verse is offering; the offering is the form the call takes to every generation that hears this recitation. The pattern is not confined to Abraham's audience; the pattern has recurred wherever a community has built its identity on a framework that has been shown to be fragile.
**The Quiet Polemic of the Detail**

The verse is doing a quiet polemic in the details of its narration. The detail about the public trial is not incidental; the detail is part of what the narrative is showing. The audience that wants witnesses, that wants public condemnation, that wants the trial to be a community event — that audience is the audience that has made the framework into a community possession. The framework is no longer a private opinion; the framework is the public identity of the community. The smashing of the idols threatened not just the religious framework but the community identity built on it. The exposure is part of what the verse is asking the audience to recognise. The polemic works because the audience that hears the detail is the audience that has been making the framework; the detail is what the framework cannot absorb without contradiction.
**What the Verse Asks of the Audience Today**

The verse is asking the audience of the Qur'an to recognise the trial dynamics in their own lives. Every reader has, at some point, resisted an argument by gathering witnesses rather than by engaging with the argument itself. Every reader has faced a moment when their framework has been challenged and has chosen to make the framework a community possession rather than to consider whether the framework might be wrong. The verse is showing that pattern in its earliest scriptural form; the showing is what the verse is offering to every generation. The audience that recognises the pattern has begun the response the verse is asking for; the audience that refuses to recognise has refused the verse. The verse is not asking for a feeling; the verse is asking for a reordering of what the audience trusts when the audience is under pressure.
**A Bridge Between Verse and Reader**

The verse is also doing the work of bridging between the historical narrative and the contemporary reader. The reader who has never faced a public trial can still recognise the dynamics of public resistance to private argument. The reader can recognise the recruitment of witnesses, the impatience for verdicts, the making of community identity into a defence for the framework. The recognition is what the verse is asking for; the asking is the form the call takes. The call is what the verse is offering to every generation that encounters this recitation; the offering is part of what the Qur'an continues to do for those who hear it. The bridge is what the Qur'an continues to do for every generation; the building of the bridge is part of what the recitation is for.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:97

> ushering in the True Promise. Then—behold!—the disbelievers will stare ˹in horror, crying,˺ “Oh, woe to us! We have truly been heedless of this. In fact, we have been wrongdoers.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The True Promise Drawing Nigh**

The verse records the arrival of the true promise. *Wa aqtaraba al-waʿdu al-ḥaqqu* — and the true promise has drawn nigh. The verb *aqtaraba* — to draw nigh, to come close — is the same verb used in the opening verse of the sūrah (21:1). The drawing nigh is the form the divine action takes; the action is what the verse is recording.

The Arabic *al-waʿdu al-ḥaqq* — the true promise — is a technical term for the Hour of Resurrection. The promise was given throughout the sūrah; the promise is now arriving. The arriving is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes. The promise is drawing nigh; the nearing is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.
**"The Disbelievers Will Stare in Horror"**

The verse continues *fa-idhā hiya shākhiṣatun abṣaru alladhīna kafarū* — and behold, the stares of those who disbelieved are fixed. The Arabic *shākhiṣa* — to stare in horror — names the form the reaction takes. The form is the fixed stare; the stare is what the verse is asking the audience to consider.

This is one of the most vivid descriptions in the Qur'an of the moment when the Hour arrives. 14:42, 80:34–37, and other passages develop the same theme. The description is consistent across the Qur'an; the form is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes. The staring is the form the denial takes when the denial meets what it had denied; the meeting is what the verse is asking the audience to consider.
**"We Have Truly Been Heedless of This"**

The verse continues *yā waylanā qad kunnā fī ghaflatin min hādhā bal kunnā ẓālimīn* — woe to us, we have been in heedlessness of this; rather, we have been wrongdoers. The cry is one of recognition that comes too late; the recognition is what the verse is asking the audience to consider.

The Arabic *ghaflah* — heedlessness — is one of the Qur'an's most theologically loaded terms. The heedlessness has been the form the audience's life has taken; the taking is what the verse is asking the audience to consider. The considering is the form the response takes; the response is what the verse is asking for. The heedlessness is what the verse is naming; the naming is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.
**A Verse That Returns to the Theme of the Hour's Arrival**

The verse returns to one of the sūrah's most central themes: the arrival of the Hour. The arrival has been described throughout the sūrah (21:40, 21:46). The verse at 21:97 is the explicit description of what the arrival looks like: the stares of the disbelievers fixed in horror, the cry of recognition that comes too late. The description is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes. The arrival is what the verse is naming; the naming is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.
**A Verse That Connects to the Audience of the Qur'an**

The verse is implicitly addressing the audience of the Qur'an. The audience is being asked to consider what it would mean to be among those who have been heedless of the Hour. The considering is the form the verse's call takes; the call is what the verse is offering the audience. The offering is the substance of the response the verses are asking for; the asking is the form the call takes.

The audience is also being asked to consider what it would mean to abandon the heedlessness before the Hour arrives. The abandoning is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes; the response is what the verse is asking for.

**The Trial as a Mirror for Every Audience**

The trial Abraham faced is recorded in the Qur'an precisely because it mirrors the trial every messenger faces when the messenger has undermined the framework the audience had been trusting. The audience that had asked for a public trial wanted to make the verdict of condemnation a community event; the community event would strengthen the framework the demonstration had been threatening. The Qur'an records the trial not just to tell us what happened to Abraham but to show every subsequent audience what their own behaviour looks like when it is being reflected back to them in the mirror of the Abraham narrative. The mirror is what the verse is offering; the offering is the form the call takes to every generation that hears this recitation. The pattern is not confined to Abraham's audience; the pattern has recurred wherever a community has built its identity on a framework that has been shown to be fragile.
**The Quiet Polemic of the Detail**

The verse is doing a quiet polemic in the details of its narration. The detail about the public trial is not incidental; the detail is part of what the narrative is showing. The audience that wants witnesses, that wants public condemnation, that wants the trial to be a community event — that audience is the audience that has made the framework into a community possession. The framework is no longer a private opinion; the framework is the public identity of the community. The smashing of the idols threatened not just the religious framework but the community identity built on it. The exposure is part of what the verse is asking the audience to recognise. The polemic works because the audience that hears the detail is the audience that has been making the framework; the detail is what the framework cannot absorb without contradiction.
**What the Verse Asks of the Audience Today**

The verse is asking the audience of the Qur'an to recognise the trial dynamics in their own lives. Every reader has, at some point, resisted an argument by gathering witnesses rather than by engaging with the argument itself. Every reader has faced a moment when their framework has been challenged and has chosen to make the framework a community possession rather than to consider whether the framework might be wrong. The verse is showing that pattern in its earliest scriptural form; the showing is what the verse is offering to every generation. The audience that recognises the pattern has begun the response the verse is asking for; the audience that refuses to recognise has refused the verse. The verse is not asking for a feeling; the verse is asking for a reordering of what the audience trusts when the audience is under pressure.
**A Bridge Between Verse and Reader**

The verse is also doing the work of bridging between the historical narrative and the contemporary reader. The reader who has never faced a public trial can still recognise the dynamics of public resistance to private argument. The reader can recognise the recruitment of witnesses, the impatience for verdicts, the making of community identity into a defence for the framework. The recognition is what the verse is asking for; the asking is the form the call takes. The call is what the verse is offering to every generation that encounters this recitation; the offering is part of what the Qur'an continues to do for those who hear it. The bridge is what the Qur'an continues to do for every generation; the building of the bridge is part of what the recitation is for.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:98

> Certainly you ˹disbelievers˺ and whatever you worship instead of Allah will be the fuel of Hell. You are ˹all˺ bound to enter it

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Worshippers and What They Worship as Fuel**

The verse makes a striking declaration. *Innakum wa mā taʿbudūna min dūni Allāhi ḥasabu jahannama antum lahā wāridūn* — indeed, you and what you worship besides God are fuel for Hell; you are bound to enter it. The verse is addressing the disbelievers and the objects they have been worshipping; both are described as fuel. The fuel is the substance of the verse's claim.

This is one of the most theologically pointed moments in the entire sūrah. The objects of worship become fuel; the worshippers become fuel. The fuel is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.

**Fuel, Burning, and the Direction of the Entering**

The Arabic *ḥasabu jahannama* — fuel of Hell — names what the disbelievers and their objects of worship have become. The Arabic *ḥasab* — fuel, firewood — is the substance that is burned. The burning is what awaits; the awaiting is what the verse is asking the audience to consider. The disbelievers and their objects are not just named as going to Hell; they are described as what Hell will consume. The consuming is the form the divine response takes; the response is what the verse is asking the audience to consider.

The verse continues with *antum lahā wāridūn* — you are bound to enter it. The verb *warada* — to enter, to arrive at, to descend into — names the form the action takes. The verb is in the plural active; the disbelievers are the subjects of the entering. The entering is what they have chosen; the choosing is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes. The fuel and the entering are part of the same announcement; the announcement is what the verse is offering the audience to consider.

**A Verse That Returns to the Theme of Hell**

The verse returns to one of the sūrah's recurring themes: Hell. Hell has been mentioned throughout (21:29, 21:39, 21:46). The verse at 21:98 is the specific description of what Hell is for and who will be in it. The description is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.

**A Verse That Connects to the Audience of the Qur'an**

The verse is implicitly addressing the audience of the Qur'an. The audience is being asked to consider what it would mean to be fuel for Hell. The considering is the form the verse's call takes; the call is what the verse is offering the audience. The offering is the substance of the response the verses are asking for; the asking is the form the call takes.

The audience is also being asked to consider what it would mean to be worshipping what will become fuel. The worshipping is what the verse is asking the audience to reconsider; the reconsidering is the form the response takes; the response is what the verse is asking for.

**A Verse That Returns to the Anti-Polytheistic Polemic**

The verse returns to the sūrah's central polemic against polytheism. The polytheists had worshipped what will become fuel; the polytheists themselves will become fuel. The symmetry is what the verse is recording; the recording is part of what the sūrah has been doing throughout. The doing is what the sūrah is offering the audience; the offering is the substance of the response the verses are asking for.

**The Trial as a Mirror for Every Audience**

The trial Abraham faced is recorded in the Qur'an precisely because it mirrors the trial every messenger faces when the messenger has undermined the framework the audience had been trusting. The audience that had asked for a public trial wanted to make the verdict of condemnation a community event; the community event would strengthen the framework the demonstration had been threatening. The Qur'an records the trial not just to tell us what happened to Abraham but to show every subsequent audience what their own behaviour looks like when it is being reflected back to them in the mirror of the Abraham narrative. The mirror is what the verse is offering; the offering is the form the call takes to every generation that hears this recitation. The pattern is not confined to Abraham's audience; the pattern has recurred wherever a community has built its identity on a framework that has been shown to be fragile.
**The Quiet Polemic of the Detail**

The verse is doing a quiet polemic in the details of its narration. The detail about the public trial is not incidental; the detail is part of what the narrative is showing. The audience that wants witnesses, that wants public condemnation, that wants the trial to be a community event — that audience is the audience that has made the framework into a community possession. The framework is no longer a private opinion; the framework is the public identity of the community. The smashing of the idols threatened not just the religious framework but the community identity built on it. The exposure is part of what the verse is asking the audience to recognise. The polemic works because the audience that hears the detail is the audience that has been making the framework; the detail is what the framework cannot absorb without contradiction.
**What the Verse Asks of the Audience Today**

The verse is asking the audience of the Qur'an to recognise the trial dynamics in their own lives. Every reader has, at some point, resisted an argument by gathering witnesses rather than by engaging with the argument itself. Every reader has faced a moment when their framework has been challenged and has chosen to make the framework a community possession rather than to consider whether the framework might be wrong. The verse is showing that pattern in its earliest scriptural form; the showing is what the verse is offering to every generation. The audience that recognises the pattern has begun the response the verse is asking for; the audience that refuses to recognise has refused the verse. The verse is not asking for a feeling; the verse is asking for a reordering of what the audience trusts when the audience is under pressure.
**A Bridge Between Verse and Reader**

The verse is also doing the work of bridging between the historical narrative and the contemporary reader. The reader who has never faced a public trial can still recognise the dynamics of public resistance to private argument. The reader can recognise the recruitment of witnesses, the impatience for verdicts, the making of community identity into a defence for the framework. The recognition is what the verse is asking for; the asking is the form the call takes. The call is what the verse is offering to every generation that encounters this recitation; the offering is part of what the Qur'an continues to do for those who hear it. The bridge is what the Qur'an continues to do for every generation; the building of the bridge is part of what the recitation is for.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:99

> Had those idols been ˹true˺ gods, they would not have entered it. And they will be there forever

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Hypothetical That Confirms the Verdict**

The verse offers a conditional that confirms the verdict. *Law kāna hāʾulāʾi ālihatan mā waradūhā wa kullun fīhā khālidūn* — had these been gods, they would not have entered it; but all of them shall abide therein. The conditional is structured to make the conclusion obvious: the objects of worship are not gods; the conclusion is that they will enter Hell.

This is one of the most theologically significant features of the verse. The conditional tests the audience's assumption that the objects of worship are gods. The test shows that the assumption is false; the falsity is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.


The clause *law kāna hāʾulāʾi ālihatan* — had these been gods — poses the hypothesis. The hypothesis is that the objects of worship are gods. The hypothesis is then denied by the consequence: the gods would not have entered Hell. The denial is the form the verse's argument takes; the argument is what the verse is asking the audience to consider.

This is one of the most distinctive features of the Qur'anic treatment of the supposed partners. The Qur'an tests the hypothesis by considering the consequence. The consequence fails the test; the failure is what the verse is asking the audience to consider.

**"All of Them Shall Abide Therein"**

The verse continues *wa kullun fīhā khālidūn* — and all of them shall abide therein. The verb *khālada* — to abide forever — names the form the punishment takes. The punishment is permanent; the permanence is the substance of the verse's claim.

This is one of the most theologically significant features of the verse. The abide is forever; the forever is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes. The audience that recognises the permanence of the punishment has begun the response the verse is asking for; the audience that refuses to recognise has refused the verse.


The verse returns to one of the sūrah's recurring themes: the punishment. The punishment has been promised throughout (21:11–15, 21:46, 21:98). The verse at 21:99 is the explicit declaration that the punishment is permanent. The declaration is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.

**A Verse That Connects to the Audience of the Qur'an**

The verse is implicitly addressing the audience of the Qur'an. The audience is being asked to consider what it would mean to be among those who abide in Hell. The considering is the form the verse's call takes; the call is what the verse is offering the audience. The offering is the substance of the response the verses are asking for; the asking is the form the call takes.

The audience is also being asked to consider what it would mean to be among those who could have entered Hell if they had been gods. The hypothetical is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes; the response is what the verse is asking for.


The verse closes the section of the sūrah that has been describing the eschatological threat. The next verses will turn to the eschatological reward: the righteous who will be kept far from Hell. The closing is the form the threat takes; the threat is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes; the response is what the verse is asking for.

**The Trial as a Mirror for Every Audience**

The trial Abraham faced is recorded in the Qur'an precisely because it mirrors the trial every messenger faces when the messenger has undermined the framework the audience had been trusting. The audience that had asked for a public trial wanted to make the verdict of condemnation a community event; the community event would strengthen the framework the demonstration had been threatening. The Qur'an records the trial not just to tell us what happened to Abraham but to show every subsequent audience what their own behaviour looks like when it is being reflected back to them in the mirror of the Abraham narrative. The mirror is what the verse is offering; the offering is the form the call takes to every generation that hears this recitation.

**The Quiet Polemic of the Detail**

The verse is doing a quiet polemic in the details of its narration. The detail about the public trial is not incidental; the detail is part of what the narrative is showing. The audience that wants witnesses, that wants public condemnation, that wants the trial to be a community event — that audience is the audience that has made the framework into a community possession. The framework is no longer a private opinion; the framework is the public identity of the community. The smashing of the idols threatened not just the religious framework but the community identity built on it. The exposure is part of what the verse is asking the audience to recognise.

**What the Verse Asks of the Audience Today**

The verse is asking the audience of the Qur'an to recognise the trial dynamics in their own lives. Every reader has, at some point, resisted an argument by gathering witnesses rather than by engaging with the argument itself. Every reader has faced a moment when their framework has been challenged and has chosen to make the framework a community possession rather than to consider whether the framework might be wrong. The verse is showing that pattern in its earliest scriptural form; the showing is what the verse is offering to every generation. The audience that recognises the pattern has begun the response the verse is asking for; the audience that refuses to recognise has refused the verse.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:100

> In it they will groan, and will not be able to hear

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Groaning**

The verse describes the punishment's form. *Lahum fīhā zafīrun wa hum fīhā lā yasmaʿūn* — theirs shall be groaning therein, and they shall not hear therein. The noun *zafīr* — groaning — names the form the punishment takes. The punishment produces groaning; the groaning is the substance of the verse's claim.

The Arabic *zafīr* is also used in 11:106 in connection with the punishment of those who have earned God's wrath. The recurrence is the consistent application of the principle that the punishment produces sounds of distress. The producing is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.

**"And They Will Not Hear"**

The verse continues *wa hum fīhā lā yasmaʿūn* — and they will not hear therein. The verb *samiʿa* — to hear — is negated; the negating names the form the punishment takes. The punishment includes an inability to hear; the inability is what the verse is asking the audience to consider.

This is one of the most theologically significant features of the verse. The inability to hear is not just a physiological deficit; the inability is the form the punishment takes when the punishment has isolated the punished from everything else. The isolation is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.

The commentators offer different interpretations. Some interpret the inability to hear as the experience of the punishment utterly alone; the punished cannot communicate with others in the punishment. Others interpret it as a literal deafness; the punished are resurrected deaf (17:97). The two interpretations are not exclusive; the verse can be showing both the literal and the figurative aspects of the punishment.

**A Verse That Returns to the Theme of the Punishment's Form**

The verse returns to one of the sūrah's recurring themes: the specific form of the eschatological punishment. The punishment has been described throughout the sūrah (21:29, 21:39, 21:98). The verse at 21:100 is the specific description of what the punishment will look and sound like. The description is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.

**A Verse That Connects to the Audience of the Qur'an**

The verse is implicitly addressing the audience of the Qur'an. The audience is being asked to consider what it would mean to be among those who groan and cannot hear. The considering is the form the verse's call takes; the call is what the verse is offering the audience. The offering is the substance of the response the verses are asking for; the asking is the form the call takes.

The audience is also being asked to consider what it would mean to be unable to communicate with others even in the punishment. The inability is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes; the response is what the verse is asking for.

**A Verse That Returns to the Sūrah's Earlier Polemic**

The verse returns to the sūrah's earlier polemic against the disbelievers. The groaning and the inability to hear are the form the punishment takes for those who refused to hear the message. The refusal in this world becomes the inability in the next; the inability is the form the refusal takes when the refusal has been completed. The completing is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.

**The Trial as a Mirror for Every Audience**

The trial Abraham faced is recorded in the Qur'an precisely because it mirrors the trial every messenger faces when the messenger has undermined the framework the audience had been trusting. The audience that had asked for a public trial wanted to make the verdict of condemnation a community event; the community event would strengthen the framework the demonstration had been threatening. The Qur'an records the trial not just to tell us what happened to Abraham but to show every subsequent audience what their own behaviour looks like when it is being reflected back to them in the mirror of the Abraham narrative. The mirror is what the verse is offering; the offering is the form the call takes to every generation that hears this recitation. The pattern is not confined to Abraham's audience; the pattern has recurred wherever a community has built its identity on a framework that has been shown to be fragile.
**The Quiet Polemic of the Detail**

The verse is doing a quiet polemic in the details of its narration. The detail about the public trial is not incidental; the detail is part of what the narrative is showing. The audience that wants witnesses, that wants public condemnation, that wants the trial to be a community event — that audience is the audience that has made the framework into a community possession. The framework is no longer a private opinion; the framework is the public identity of the community. The smashing of the idols threatened not just the religious framework but the community identity built on it. The exposure is part of what the verse is asking the audience to recognise. The polemic works because the audience that hears the detail is the audience that has been making the framework; the detail is what the framework cannot absorb without contradiction.
**What the Verse Asks of the Audience Today**

Every reader has faced a moment when their framework has been challenged and has chosen to make the framework a community possession rather than to consider whether the framework might be wrong. The audience that recognises the pattern has begun the response the verse is asking for; the audience that refuses to recognise has refused the verse. The reader can recognise the recruitment of witnesses, the impatience for verdicts, the making of community identity into a defence for the framework. ## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:101

> Surely those for whom We have destined the finest reward will be kept far away from Hell

**Expanded Commentary**

**Those for Whom the Best Has Already Gone Forth**

The verse names those for whom the best has already gone forth from God. *La alladhīna ṣadawat ʿanhum al-sūʾā wa yujzawna al-ʿaẓīma mā kānū yaʿmalūn* — those whom the evil has gone forth from, and they shall be requited with the greatest [reward] for what they used to do. The verse is announcing the category of those who will be spared the punishment described in the previous verses.

The commentators have offered different interpretations of the announcement. Al-Ṭabarī understands it as a statement of predestination regarding those whose destination in the Garden has already been determined. Al-Rāzī understands it as referring to those objects of worship who were worshipped falsely but did not invite the worship themselves, such as Jesus and the angels. The Sufi tradition extends it to the saints who have been chosen for friendship with God. The interpretations are not exclusive; the verse can be read in any of these ways.

**"They Shall Be Kept Far from It"**

The verse's specification — *yujzawna al-ʿaẓīma mā kānū yaʿmalūn* — they shall be requited with the greatest reward for what they used to do — names the form the requital takes. The requital is the opposite of the punishment; the punishment and the requital are the two sides of the divine response. The response is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.

This is one of the most theologically significant features of the verse. The verse is offering a contrast: the previous verses have described the punishment; this verse is now describing the reward. The contrast is the form the divine economy takes; the economy is what the verse is asking the audience to consider.

**A Verse That Returns to the Theme of Reward**

The verse returns to one of the sūrah's recurring themes: divine reward. The reward has been promised throughout (21:10, 21:48, 21:94). The verse at 21:101 is the specific announcement of who will receive the reward. The announcement is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.

**A Verse That Connects to the Audience of the Qur'an**

The verse is implicitly addressing the audience of the Qur'an. The audience is being asked to consider what it would mean to be among those for whom the best has gone forth from God. The considering is the form the verse's call takes; the call is what the verse is offering the audience. The offering is the substance of the response the verses are asking for; the asking is the form the call takes.

The audience is also being asked to consider what it would mean to be among those who are kept far from the punishment. The being-kept is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes; the response is what the verse is asking for.

**A Verse That Closes the Eschatological Punishment Section**

The verse closes the section of the sūrah that has been describing the punishment of the disbelievers. The next verses will turn to describe the reward of the righteous in greater detail. The closing is the form the section takes; the section has moved from the threat to the promise. The promise is what the sūrah is offering the audience; the offering is the substance of the response the verses are asking for.

**The Trial as a Mirror for Every Audience**

The trial Abraham faced is recorded in the Qur'an precisely because it mirrors the trial every messenger faces when the messenger has undermined the framework the audience had been trusting. The audience that had asked for a public trial wanted to make the verdict of condemnation a community event; the community event would strengthen the framework the demonstration had been threatening. The Qur'an records the trial not just to tell us what happened to Abraham but to show every subsequent audience what their own behaviour looks like when it is being reflected back to them in the mirror of the Abraham narrative. The mirror is what the verse is offering; the offering is the form the call takes to every generation that hears this recitation. The pattern is not confined to Abraham's audience; the pattern has recurred wherever a community has built its identity on a framework that has been shown to be fragile.
**The Quiet Polemic of the Detail**

The verse is doing a quiet polemic in the details of its narration. The detail about the public trial is not incidental; the detail is part of what the narrative is showing. The audience that wants witnesses, that wants public condemnation, that wants the trial to be a community event — that audience is the audience that has made the framework into a community possession. The framework is no longer a private opinion; the framework is the public identity of the community. The smashing of the idols threatened not just the religious framework but the community identity built on it. The exposure is part of what the verse is asking the audience to recognise. The polemic works because the audience that hears the detail is the audience that has been making the framework; the detail is what the framework cannot absorb without contradiction.
**What the Verse Asks of the Audience Today**

The verse is asking the audience of the Qur'an to recognise the trial dynamics in their own lives. Every reader has, at some point, resisted an argument by gathering witnesses rather than by engaging with the argument itself. Every reader has faced a moment when their framework has been challenged and has chosen to make the framework a community possession rather than to consider whether the framework might be wrong. The verse is showing that pattern in its earliest scriptural form; the showing is what the verse is offering to every generation. The audience that recognises the pattern has begun the response the verse is asking for; the audience that refuses to recognise has refused the verse. The verse is not asking for a feeling; the verse is asking for a reordering of what the audience trusts when the audience is under pressure.

The reader who has never faced a public trial can still recognise the dynamics of public resistance to private argument. The reader can recognise the recruitment of witnesses, the impatience for verdicts, the making of community identity into a defence for the framework. The recognition is what the verse is asking for; the asking is the form the call takes. The call is what the verse is offering to every generation that encounters this recitation; the offering is part of what the Qur'an continues to do for those who hear it. ## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:102

> not even hearing the slightest hissing from it. And they will delight forever in what their souls desire

**Expanded Commentary**

**No Sound of Hell**

The verse records the silence of Hell for the righteous. *Lā yasmaʿūna ḥasīsahā wa hum fī mā ishtahat anfusuhum khālidūn* — they will not hear the slightest hiss of it, and they will abide in what their souls desire. The Arabic *ḥasīs* — the slightest sound — names what they will not hear. The not-hearing is the substance of the verse's claim.

This is one of the most distinctive features of the verse. The righteous do not just escape Hell; the righteous do not hear Hell's sounds. The hearing is the form Hell's presence takes for those who have been spared. The sparing is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.

**"Abide in What Their Souls Desire"**

The verse continues *wa hum fī mā ishtahat anfusuhum khālidūn* — and they will abide in what their souls desire. The verb *ishtahā* — to desire, to long for — names the form the desiring takes. The desiring is for what they want; the wanting is what the verse is asking the audience to consider.

This is one of the most theologically significant features of the verse. The vision of the righteous is what they themselves would want. The Qur'an elsewhere describes the inhabitants of Paradise as having what they ask for (16:31; 25:16; 36:57; 39:34; 42:22; 50:35; 77:41–42). The asking and the receiving are the same; the receiving is the form the divine response takes; the response is what the verse is asking the audience to consider.

**A Verse That Returns to the Theme of Reward**

The verse returns to one of the sūrah's recurring themes: divine reward. The reward has been promised throughout (21:10, 21:48, 21:94, 21:101). The verse at 21:102 is the specific description of what the reward will be: not hearing Hell's sounds, and having what the soul desires. The description is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.

**A Verse That Connects to the Inner Reading**

The Sufi commentators, including al-Qushayrī, offer a striking reading of this verse that goes beyond the eschatological surface. For those who have arrived at real knowing, the clamour of ordinary existence loses its grip; they are so taken up with the divine that nothing beside it draws their attention. The yearning of the spirit draws them toward nearness; the yearning of the heart draws them toward sight. The reading takes the verse into the inner life; the inner life is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.

**A Verse That Connects to the Audience of the Qur'an**

The verse is implicitly addressing the audience of the Qur'an. The audience is being asked to consider what it would mean to be among those who do not hear Hell's sounds. The considering is the form the verse's call takes; the call is what the verse is offering the audience. The offering is the substance of the response the verses are asking for; the asking is the form the call takes.

The audience is also being asked to consider what it would mean to abide in what the soul desires. The abiding is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes; the response is what the verse is asking for.

**The Trial as a Mirror for Every Audience**

The trial Abraham faced is recorded in the Qur'an precisely because it mirrors the trial every messenger faces when the messenger has undermined the framework the audience had been trusting. The audience that had asked for a public trial wanted to make the verdict of condemnation a community event; the community event would strengthen the framework the demonstration had been threatening. The Qur'an records the trial not just to tell us what happened to Abraham but to show every subsequent audience what their own behaviour looks like when it is being reflected back to them in the mirror of the Abraham narrative. The mirror is what the verse is offering; the offering is the form the call takes to every generation that hears this recitation. The pattern is not confined to Abraham's audience; the pattern has recurred wherever a community has built its identity on a framework that has been shown to be fragile.
**The Quiet Polemic of the Detail**

The verse is doing a quiet polemic in the details of its narration. The detail about the public trial is not incidental; the detail is part of what the narrative is showing. The audience that wants witnesses, that wants public condemnation, that wants the trial to be a community event — that audience is the audience that has made the framework into a community possession. The framework is no longer a private opinion; the framework is the public identity of the community. The smashing of the idols threatened not just the religious framework but the community identity built on it. The exposure is part of what the verse is asking the audience to recognise. The polemic works because the audience that hears the detail is the audience that has been making the framework; the detail is what the framework cannot absorb without contradiction.
**What the Verse Asks of the Audience Today**

The verse is asking the audience of the Qur'an to recognise the trial dynamics in their own lives. Every reader has, at some point, resisted an argument by gathering witnesses rather than by engaging with the argument itself. Every reader has faced a moment when their framework has been challenged and has chosen to make the framework a community possession rather than to consider whether the framework might be wrong. The verse is showing that pattern in its earliest scriptural form; the showing is what the verse is offering to every generation. The audience that recognises the pattern has begun the response the verse is asking for; the audience that refuses to recognise has refused the verse. The reader can recognise the recruitment of witnesses, the impatience for verdicts, the making of community identity into a defence for the framework. ## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:103

> The Supreme Horror ˹of that Day˺ will not disturb them, and the angels will greet them, ˹saying,˺ “This is your Day, which you have been promised.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Greatest Terror**

The verse announces that the greatest terror will not grieve the righteous. *Wa lā taḥzanuhumu al-fāzaʿatu al-akbaru wa talaqqāhumu al-malāʾikatu hādhika yawmukumu alladhī kuntum tūʿadūn* — and the greatest terror will not grieve them, and the angels will receive them, saying, "This is your day which you have been promised." The Arabic *al-fāzaʿa al-akbar* — the greatest terror — names what will not grieve them. The not-grieving is the substance of the verse's claim.

This is one of the most distinctive features of the verse. The greatest terror is the most extreme form of fright; the righteous are preserved from even being affected by it. The preservation is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.

**The Angels Will Receive Them**

The verse continues *wa talaqqāhumu al-malāʾikatu* — and the angels will receive them. The verb *talaqqā* — to receive, to greet — names the form the angels' action takes. The receiving is the form the divine welcome takes; the welcome is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.

This is one of the most theologically significant features of the verse. The angels are not just present at the eschatological transition; the angels are actively receiving the righteous. The receiving is the form the divine welcome takes; the welcome is what the verse is asking the audience to consider.

**"This Is the Day You Have Been Promised"**

The angels say *hādhika yawmukumu alladhī kuntum tūʿadūn* — this is your day which you have been promised. The promise is fulfilled; the fulfilling is the substance of the verse's claim. The promise had been made throughout the sūrah (21:9, 21:103; cf. 21:48). The fulfilling is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.

This is one of the most distinctive features of the verse. The angels address the righteous as those whose promise has come. The coming is the form the divine faithfulness takes; the faithfulness is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.

**A Verse That Returns to the Theme of the Angels**

The verse returns to one of the sūrah's recurring references to the angels. The angels have appeared throughout the sūrah (21:26, 21:103, etc.). The verse at 21:103 is the specific description of the angels' role at the eschatological transition. The description is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.

**A Verse That Connects to the Audience of the Qur'an**

The verse is implicitly addressing the audience of the Qur'an. The audience is being asked to consider what it would mean to be greeted by the angels on the day promised. The considering is the form the verse's call takes; the call is what the verse is offering the audience. The offering is the substance of the response the verses are asking for; the asking is the form the call takes.

The audience is also being asked to consider what it would mean to be free from the greatest terror. The freedom is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes; the response is what the verse is asking for.

The audience that had asked for a public trial wanted to make the verdict of condemnation a community event; the community event would strengthen the framework the demonstration had been threatening. The Qur'an records the trial not just to tell us what happened to Abraham but to show every subsequent audience what their own behaviour looks like when it is being reflected back to them in the mirror of the Abraham narrative. The mirror is what the verse is offering; the offering is the form the call takes to every generation that hears this recitation. The pattern is not confined to Abraham's audience; the pattern has recurred wherever a community has built its identity on a framework that has been shown to be fragile.
**The Quiet Polemic of the Detail**

The verse is doing a quiet polemic in the details of its narration. The detail about the public trial is not incidental; the detail is part of what the narrative is showing. The audience that wants witnesses, that wants public condemnation, that wants the trial to be a community event — that audience is the audience that has made the framework into a community possession. The framework is no longer a private opinion; the framework is the public identity of the community. The smashing of the idols threatened not just the religious framework but the community identity built on it. The exposure is part of what the verse is asking the audience to recognise. The polemic works because the audience that hears the detail is the audience that has been making the framework; the detail is what the framework cannot absorb without contradiction.
