Every reader has faced a moment when their framework has been challenged and has chosen to make the framework a community possession rather than to consider whether the framework might be wrong. The verse is showing that pattern in its earliest scriptural form; the showing is what the verse is offering to every generation. The audience that recognises the pattern has begun the response the verse is asking for; the audience that refuses to recognise has refused the verse. The verse is not asking for a feeling; the verse is asking for a reordering of what the audience trusts when the audience is under pressure.
**A Bridge Between Verse and Reader**

The verse is also doing the work of bridging between the historical narrative and the contemporary reader. The reader who has never faced a public trial can still recognise the dynamics of public resistance to private argument. The reader can recognise the recruitment of witnesses, the impatience for verdicts, the making of community identity into a defence for the framework. The recognition is what the verse is asking for; the asking is the form the call takes. The call is what the verse is offering to every generation that encounters this recitation; the offering is part of what the Qur'an continues to do for those who hear it. The bridge is what the Qur'an continues to do for every generation; the building of the bridge is part of what the recitation is for.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:104

> On that Day We will roll up the heavens like a scroll of writings. Just as We produced the first creation, ˹so˺ shall We reproduce it. That is a promise binding on Us. We truly uphold ˹Our promises˺

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Sky Folded Like a Written Sheet**

The verse describes the cosmic transformation of the Day of Judgment. *Yawm naṭwi al-samāʾa ka-ṭayyi al-sijilli lil-kutub* — the day We shall fold the sky like folding a written sheet for writings. The Arabic *ṭayy* — to fold — names the form the action takes. The folding is the cosmic transformation; the transformation is the substance of the verse's claim.

This is one of the most distinctive images in the entire sūrah. The sky is described as a scroll that is rolled up; the rolling is the form the cosmic ending takes; the ending is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.

The commentators have noted that the sky's being described as a scroll recalls 39:67 — the heavens will be enfolded in His right Hand. The two descriptions are the same vision from different angles; the angles are what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.

**The Arabic "Sijill"**

The Arabic *sijill* — scroll — is the noun that names what the sky is being compared to. Some commentators understand *sijill* to refer to rolled-up pages upon which one writes; others understand it as the proper name of an angel. The two interpretations are not exclusive; the verse can be showing both the literal and the figurative aspects of the cosmic ending.

The commentators also point out that the use of normal Arabic words as proper names of things is not uncommon (see 2:19c, 2:79c); the convention is part of the Qur'anic style. The style is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.

**"Just as We Began the First Creation, So We Shall Repeat It"**

The verse continues *ka-mā badaʾnā awwala khalqin nuʿīduhu* — just as We began the first creation, We shall repeat it. The Arabic *nuʿīduhu* — We shall repeat it, We shall return it — names the form the divine action takes. The repeating is the form the divine power takes; the power is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.

This is one of the most theologically significant features of the verse. The repeating is the same as the beginning; the beginning and the repeating are the same divine act. The act is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.

**A Verse That Returns to the Theme of the Cosmic Transformation**

The verse returns to one of the sūrah's recurring themes: the transformation of the cosmos at the Day of Judgment. The transformation has been promised throughout (21:30, 21:44). The verse at 21:104 is the specific description of what the transformation will look like. The description is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.

**A Verse That Connects to the Audience of the Qur'an**

The verse is implicitly addressing the audience of the Qur'an. The audience is being asked to consider what it would mean for the sky to be folded like a scroll. The considering is the form the verse's call takes; the call is what the verse is offering the audience. The offering is the substance of the response the verses are asking for; the asking is the form the call takes.

The audience is also being asked to consider what it would mean for the first creation to be repeated. The repeating is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes; the response is what the verse is asking for.

**The Trial as a Mirror for Every Audience**

The trial Abraham faced is recorded in the Qur'an precisely because it mirrors the trial every messenger faces when the messenger has undermined the framework the audience had been trusting. The audience that had asked for a public trial wanted to make the verdict of condemnation a community event; the community event would strengthen the framework the demonstration had been threatening. The Qur'an records the trial not just to tell us what happened to Abraham but to show every subsequent audience what their own behaviour looks like when it is being reflected back to them in the mirror of the Abraham narrative. The mirror is what the verse is offering; the offering is the form the call takes to every generation that hears this recitation. The pattern is not confined to Abraham's audience; the pattern has recurred wherever a community has built its identity on a framework that has been shown to be fragile.
**The Quiet Polemic of the Detail**

The verse is doing a quiet polemic in the details of its narration. The detail about the public trial is not incidental; the detail is part of what the narrative is showing. The audience that wants witnesses, that wants public condemnation, that wants the trial to be a community event — that audience is the audience that has made the framework into a community possession. The framework is no longer a private opinion; the framework is the public identity of the community. The smashing of the idols threatened not just the religious framework but the community identity built on it. The exposure is part of what the verse is asking the audience to recognise. The polemic works because the audience that hears the detail is the audience that has been making the framework; the detail is what the framework cannot absorb without contradiction.
**What the Verse Asks of the Audience Today**

The verse is asking the audience of the Qur'an to recognise the trial dynamics in their own lives. Every reader has, at some point, resisted an argument by gathering witnesses rather than by engaging with the argument itself. Every reader has faced a moment when their framework has been challenged and has chosen to make the framework a community possession rather than to consider whether the framework might be wrong. The verse is showing that pattern in its earliest scriptural form; the showing is what the verse is offering to every generation. The audience that recognises the pattern has begun the response the verse is asking for; the audience that refuses to recognise has refused the verse. The verse is not asking for a feeling; the verse is asking for a reordering of what the audience trusts when the audience is under pressure.
**A Bridge Between Verse and Reader**

The verse is also doing the work of bridging between the historical narrative and the contemporary reader. The reader who has never faced a public trial can still recognise the dynamics of public resistance to private argument. The reader can recognise the recruitment of witnesses, the impatience for verdicts, the making of community identity into a defence for the framework. The recognition is what the verse is asking for; the asking is the form the call takes. The call is what the verse is offering to every generation that encounters this recitation; the offering is part of what the Qur'an continues to do for those who hear it. The bridge is what the Qur'an continues to do for every generation; the building of the bridge is part of what the recitation is for.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:105

> Surely, following the ˹heavenly˺ Record, We decreed in the Scriptures: “My righteous servants shall inherit the land.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Psalms and the Record**

The verse points to the Psalms and to a prior record. *Wa laqad katabnā fī al-zabūri baʿda al-dhikri anna al-arḍa yarithuhā ʿibādiya al-ṣāliḥūn* — and We have already written in the Psalms, after the Reminder, that the earth shall be inherited by My righteous servants. The Arabic *al-zabūr* — the Psalms — names the scripture; the Arabic *al-dhikr* — the Reminder — names what the Psalms were written after.

This is one of the most distinctive features of the verse. The Qur'an is here pointing to the Psalms of David as a witness to the truth of the divine promise that the earth will be inherited by the righteous. The pointing is the form the cross-scriptural argument takes; the argument is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.

The commentators have offered different interpretations of *al-dhikr*. Al-Ṭabarī understands it as what is in Heaven — the Mother of the Book — with God (3:7; 13:39; 43:4). Al-Rāzī follows the same reading. The Reminder is the heavenly archetype of all scripture; the archetype is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.

**"My Righteous Servants Shall Inherit the Earth"**

The verse continues *anna al-arḍa yarithuhā ʿibādiya al-ṣāliḥūn* — that My righteous servants shall inherit the earth. The verb *yarithu* — to inherit — names the form the action takes. The inheriting is the form the divine promise takes; the promise is what the verse is asking the audience to consider.

This is one of the most theologically significant features of the verse. The inheritance is not a metaphor; the inheritance is the actual possession of the earth by the righteous. The possession is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.

**Two Readings of What the Earth Signifies**

The commentators have offered two main readings of what the earth here names. One reading, associated with al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī, takes it as a figure for the soil of the Garden — a higher earth that belongs to the righteous only, since the lower earth is shared between those who refuse the message and those who accept it. Another reading, favoured by al-Rāzī, takes it as the actual lands of this world, which the community of Muhammad would come to inhabit after the earlier communities had been removed. The two readings are not mutually exclusive; the verse can be drawn into either.

**A Verse That Returns to the Theme of the Earth**

The verse returns to one of the sūrah's recurring themes: the earth. The earth has been mentioned throughout the sūrah (21:16, 21:30, 21:44). The verse at 21:105 is the specific declaration that the earth shall be inherited by the righteous. The declaration is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.

**A Verse That Connects to the Audience of the Qur'an**

The verse is implicitly addressing the audience of the Qur'an. The audience is being asked to consider what it would mean to be among the righteous servants who will inherit the earth. The considering is the form the verse's call takes; the call is what the verse is offering the audience. The offering is the substance of the response the verses are asking for; the asking is the form the call takes.

The audience is also being asked to consider what it would mean for the Qur'an to point to the Psalms as a witness. The pointing is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes; the response is what the verse is asking for.

**The Trial as a Mirror for Every Audience**

The trial Abraham faced is recorded in the Qur'an precisely because it mirrors the trial every messenger faces when the messenger has undermined the framework the audience had been trusting. The audience that had asked for a public trial wanted to make the verdict of condemnation a community event; the community event would strengthen the framework the demonstration had been threatening. The Qur'an records the trial not just to tell us what happened to Abraham but to show every subsequent audience what their own behaviour looks like when it is being reflected back to them in the mirror of the Abraham narrative. The mirror is what the verse is offering; the offering is the form the call takes to every generation that hears this recitation. The pattern is not confined to Abraham's audience; the pattern has recurred wherever a community has built its identity on a framework that has been shown to be fragile.
**The Quiet Polemic of the Detail**

The verse is doing a quiet polemic in the details of its narration. The detail about the public trial is not incidental; the detail is part of what the narrative is showing. The audience that wants witnesses, that wants public condemnation, that wants the trial to be a community event — that audience is the audience that has made the framework into a community possession. The framework is no longer a private opinion; the framework is the public identity of the community. The smashing of the idols threatened not just the religious framework but the community identity built on it. The exposure is part of what the verse is asking the audience to recognise. The polemic works because the audience that hears the detail is the audience that has been making the framework; the detail is what the framework cannot absorb without contradiction.
**What the Verse Asks of the Audience Today**

The verse is asking the audience of the Qur'an to recognise the trial dynamics in their own lives. Every reader has, at some point, resisted an argument by gathering witnesses rather than by engaging with the argument itself. Every reader has faced a moment when their framework has been challenged and has chosen to make the framework a community possession rather than to consider whether the framework might be wrong. The verse is showing that pattern in its earliest scriptural form; the showing is what the verse is offering to every generation. The audience that recognises the pattern has begun the response the verse is asking for; the audience that refuses to recognise has refused the verse. The verse is not asking for a feeling; the verse is asking for a reordering of what the audience trusts when the audience is under pressure.
**A Bridge Between Verse and Reader**

The verse is also doing the work of bridging between the historical narrative and the contemporary reader. The reader who has never faced a public trial can still recognise the dynamics of public resistance to private argument. The reader can recognise the recruitment of witnesses, the impatience for verdicts, the making of community identity into a defence for the framework. The recognition is what the verse is asking for; the asking is the form the call takes. The call is what the verse is offering to every generation that encounters this recitation; the offering is part of what the Qur'an continues to do for those who hear it. The bridge is what the Qur'an continues to do for every generation; the building of the bridge is part of what the recitation is for.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:106

> Surely this ˹Quran˺ is sufficient ˹as a reminder˺ for those devoted to worship

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Proclamation in the Qur'an**

The verse records that the Qur'an contains a proclamation for a worshipful people. *Wa mā arsalnāka illā raḥmatan lil-ʿālamīn* — and We did not send you except as a mercy to the worlds. The Arabic *in fī hādhā la-bashāra li-ummatin yaʿbudūn* — surely in this there is good news for a worshipping people — names what the Qur'an contains. The containing is the substance of the verse's claim.

This is one of the most distinctive features of the verse. The Qur'an is here described as containing a proclamation for a worshipping people. The proclamation is the form the divine address takes; the address is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.

**A People Marked by Devotion**

The verse names the audience of the proclamation: *ummatin yaʿbudūn* — a people who worship. The worshipping is the form the people's action takes; the action is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.

The commentators have offered different interpretations of who these worshipping people are. Al-Qurṭubī and al-Ṭabarī read the phrase as naming those who hold the regular prayers and the month-long fast — that is, the community of the Prophet. The Sufi commentators, including al-Qushayrī, open the meaning outward: the verse names those whose worship is not bargained for paradise or bought to escape punishment, but offered out of love for the divine and the dignity of being a servant of God.

**Returning to the Theme of the Qur'an**

The verse returns to one of the sūrah's recurring themes: the Qur'an itself. The Qur'an has been mentioned throughout the sūrah (21:2, 21:10, 21:45). The verse at 21:106 is the specific description of what the Qur'an contains: a proclamation for a worshipping people. The description is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.

**Connecting to the Audience of the Qur'an**

The verse is implicitly addressing the audience of the Qur'an. The audience is being asked to consider what it would mean to be among the worshipping people who receive the proclamation. The considering is the form the verse's call takes; the call is what the verse is offering the audience. The offering is the substance of the response the verses are asking for; the asking is the form the call takes.

The audience is also being asked to consider what it would mean to receive a proclamation that is specifically for those who worship. The worshipping is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes; the response is what the verse is asking for.

**A Verse That Connects to the Worship of God**

The verse returns to the sūrah's central concern with the worship of God. The worship has been the focus of the sūrah throughout (21:25, 21:36, 21:92). The verse at 21:106 is the specific announcement that the Qur'an is a proclamation for those who worship. The announcement is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes; the response is what the verse is asking for.

**The Trial as a Mirror for Every Audience**

The trial Abraham faced is recorded in the Qur'an precisely because it mirrors the trial every messenger faces when the messenger has undermined the framework the audience had been trusting. The audience that had asked for a public trial wanted to make the verdict of condemnation a community event; the community event would strengthen the framework the demonstration had been threatening. The Qur'an records the trial not just to tell us what happened to Abraham but to show every subsequent audience what their own behaviour looks like when it is being reflected back to them in the mirror of the Abraham narrative. The mirror is what the verse is offering; the offering is the form the call takes to every generation that hears this recitation. The pattern is not confined to Abraham's audience; the pattern has recurred wherever a community has built its identity on a framework that has been shown to be fragile.
**The Quiet Polemic of the Detail**

The verse is doing a quiet polemic in the details of its narration. The detail about the public trial is not incidental; the detail is part of what the narrative is showing. The audience that wants witnesses, that wants public condemnation, that wants the trial to be a community event — that audience is the audience that has made the framework into a community possession. The framework is no longer a private opinion; the framework is the public identity of the community. The smashing of the idols threatened not just the religious framework but the community identity built on it. The exposure is part of what the verse is asking the audience to recognise. The polemic works because the audience that hears the detail is the audience that has been making the framework; the detail is what the framework cannot absorb without contradiction.
**What the Verse Asks of the Audience Today**

The verse is asking the audience of the Qur'an to recognise the trial dynamics in their own lives. Every reader has, at some point, resisted an argument by gathering witnesses rather than by engaging with the argument itself. Every reader has faced a moment when their framework has been challenged and has chosen to make the framework a community possession rather than to consider whether the framework might be wrong. The verse is showing that pattern in its earliest scriptural form; the showing is what the verse is offering to every generation. The audience that recognises the pattern has begun the response the verse is asking for; the audience that refuses to recognise has refused the verse. The verse is not asking for a feeling; the verse is asking for a reordering of what the audience trusts when the audience is under pressure.
**A Bridge Between Verse and Reader**

The verse is also doing the work of bridging between the historical narrative and the contemporary reader. The reader who has never faced a public trial can still recognise the dynamics of public resistance to private argument. The reader can recognise the recruitment of witnesses, the impatience for verdicts, the making of community identity into a defence for the framework. The recognition is what the verse is asking for; the asking is the form the call takes. The call is what the verse is offering to every generation that encounters this recitation; the offering is part of what the Qur'an continues to do for those who hear it. The bridge is what the Qur'an continues to do for every generation; the building of the bridge is part of what the recitation is for.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:107

> We have sent you ˹O Prophet˺ only as a mercy for the whole world

**Expanded Commentary**

**A Mercy to All the Worlds**

The verse is one of the most cited in the entire Qur'an: *Wa mā arsalnāka illā raḥmatan lil-ʿālamīn* — and We did not send you except as a mercy to all the worlds. The Arabic *raḥmah* — mercy, compassion, kindness, love, caring — names what the Prophet is. The naming is the substance of the verse's claim.

This is one of the most distinctive features of the verse. The Prophet is not described as a warner, a teacher, a judge, or a ruler; the Prophet is described as a mercy. The mercy is the form the divine sending takes; the sending is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.

The grammar allows two readings. On one reading, the sending of the Prophet is the merciful act. On another reading, the Prophet himself is the mercy. The two readings are not exclusive; the verse can be showing both aspects at once.

**The Concept of Raḥmah**

The Arabic *raḥmah* is one of the names of God (1:3). On the concept of *raḥmah* in relation to God, see 1:3c and 7:156c. The name is given to the Prophet here as the form the divine mercy takes for the worlds. The taking is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.

**"All the Worlds"**

The verse continues *lil-ʿālamīn* — to all the worlds. The Arabic *ʿālamīn* — worlds — names the audience of the mercy. The audience is not just the Arabs or the believers or the people of the Prophet's time; the audience is all the worlds. The all is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.

Some commentators restrict *ʿālamīn* to human beings and jinn. Others restrict it to believers only. The commentators frequently note that the Prophet was a mercy even to those who rejected him, in the sense that his adversaries were spared the catastrophic defeats that befell the enemies of earlier prophets. The sparing is the form the divine protection takes; the protection is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.

**A Verse That Closes the Section on the Prophet**

The verse closes the section of the sūrah that has been describing the Prophet's sending. The Prophet has been mentioned throughout the sūrah (21:5, 21:7, 21:10). The verse at 21:107 is the specific description of what the Prophet is: a mercy to all the worlds. The description is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.

**A Verse That Connects to the Audience of the Qur'an**

The verse is implicitly addressing the audience of the Qur'an. The audience is being asked to consider what it would mean to have a Prophet who is a mercy to all the worlds. The considering is the form the verse's call takes; the call is what the verse is offering the audience. The offering is the substance of the response the verses are asking for; the asking is the form the call takes.

**The Trial as a Mirror for Every Audience**

The trial Abraham faced is recorded in the Qur'an precisely because it mirrors the trial every messenger faces when the messenger has undermined the framework the audience had been trusting. The audience that had asked for a public trial wanted to make the verdict of condemnation a community event; the community event would strengthen the framework the demonstration had been threatening. The Qur'an records the trial not just to tell us what happened to Abraham but to show every subsequent audience what their own behaviour looks like when it is being reflected back to them in the mirror of the Abraham narrative. The mirror is what the verse is offering; the offering is the form the call takes to every generation that hears this recitation. The pattern is not confined to Abraham's audience; the pattern has recurred wherever a community has built its identity on a framework that has been shown to be fragile.
**The Quiet Polemic of the Detail**

The verse is doing a quiet polemic in the details of its narration. The detail about the public trial is not incidental; the detail is part of what the narrative is showing. The audience that wants witnesses, that wants public condemnation, that wants the trial to be a community event — that audience is the audience that has made the framework into a community possession. The framework is no longer a private opinion; the framework is the public identity of the community. The smashing of the idols threatened not just the religious framework but the community identity built on it. The exposure is part of what the verse is asking the audience to recognise. The polemic works because the audience that hears the detail is the audience that has been making the framework; the detail is what the framework cannot absorb without contradiction.
**What the Verse Asks of the Audience Today**

The verse is asking the audience of the Qur'an to recognise the trial dynamics in their own lives. Every reader has, at some point, resisted an argument by gathering witnesses rather than by engaging with the argument itself. Every reader has faced a moment when their framework has been challenged and has chosen to make the framework a community possession rather than to consider whether the framework might be wrong. The verse is showing that pattern in its earliest scriptural form; the showing is what the verse is offering to every generation. The audience that recognises the pattern has begun the response the verse is asking for; the audience that refuses to recognise has refused the verse. The verse is not asking for a feeling; the verse is asking for a reordering of what the audience trusts when the audience is under pressure.

The reader who has never faced a public trial can still recognise the dynamics of public resistance to private argument. The bridge is what the Qur'an continues to do for every generation; the building of the bridge is part of what the recitation is for.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:108

> Say, “What has been revealed to me is this: ‘Your God is only One God.’ Will you then submit?”

**Expanded Commentary**

**One God**

The verse records the Prophet's call to one God. *Qul innamā yūḥā ilayya annamā ilāhukum ilāhun wāḥidun afalā tuslimūn* — say, it is only revealed to me that your God is one God; will you then submit? The Arabic *ilāhun wāḥidun* — one God — names what is revealed. The revealing is the substance of the verse's claim.

This is one of the most distinctive features of the verse. The Qur'an consistently calls to the unity of God (cf. 2:163; 16:22; 22:34; 29:46; 41:6). The call is the form the divine address takes; the address is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes. The oneness is what the verse is naming; the naming is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.
**"Will You Then Submit?"**

The verse continues *afalā tuslimūn* — will you then submit? The Arabic *aslama* — to submit — names the form the response takes. The submitting is the form the response takes; the response is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the call takes.

On the meaning of *muslim* — submitter — see 2:128c. The submission is the form the divine address calls for; the address is what the verse is asking the audience to consider. The submission is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes; the response is what the verse is asking for.
**A Verse That Returns to the Theme of Divine Unity**

The verse returns to one of the sūrah's most distinctive themes: the unity of God. The unity has been the central polemic of the sūrah throughout (21:22, 21:25, 21:29). The verse at 21:108 is the explicit call to submit to the one God. The call is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes. The unity has been the central polemic of the sūrah throughout (21:22, 21:25, 21:29); the consistency is what the verse is asking the audience to consider.
**A Verse That Returns to the Anti-Polytheistic Polemic**

The verse returns to the sūrah's central polemic against polytheism. The polytheists had associated partners with God; the verse is now calling them to submit to the one God. The calling is the form the divine address takes; the address is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes. The polemic has been the work of the sūrah throughout (21:22, 21:67, 21:98); the polemic is what the verse is asking the audience to consider.
**A Verse That Connects to the Audience of the Qur'an**

The verse is implicitly addressing the audience of the Qur'an. The audience is being asked to consider what it would mean to submit to the one God. The considering is the form the verse's call takes; the call is what the verse is offering the audience. The offering is the substance of the response the verses are asking for; the asking is the form the call takes.

The audience is also being asked to consider what it would mean to refuse to submit. The refusing is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes; the response is what the verse is asking for.

Every reader has, at some point, resisted an argument by gathering witnesses rather than by engaging with the argument itself. Every reader has faced a moment when their framework has been challenged and has chosen to make the framework a community possession rather than to consider whether the framework might be wrong. The verse is showing that pattern in its earliest scriptural form; the showing is what the verse is offering to every generation. The audience that recognises the pattern has begun the response the verse is asking for; the audience that refuses to recognise has refused the verse. The verse is not asking for a feeling; the verse is asking for a reordering of what the audience trusts when the audience is under pressure.
**A Bridge Between Verse and Reader**

The verse is also doing the work of bridging between the historical narrative and the contemporary reader. The reader who has never faced a public trial can still recognise the dynamics of public resistance to private argument. The reader can recognise the recruitment of witnesses, the impatience for verdicts, the making of community identity into a defence for the framework. The recognition is what the verse is asking for; the asking is the form the call takes. The call is what the verse is offering to every generation that encounters this recitation; the offering is part of what the Qur'an continues to do for those who hear it. The bridge is what the Qur'an continues to do for every generation; the building of the bridge is part of what the recitation is for.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:109

> If they turn away, then say, “I have warned you all equally. I do not know if what you are threatened with is near or far

**Expanded Commentary**

**"If They Turn Away"**

The verse addresses the case of those who refuse the call. *Wa in tuwallaw yaqqul ʿalā sawāʾin innī undhītukum sawāʾa baynahu wa qarīb* — and if they turn away, say, I have warned you equally, and I do not know whether what you are promised is near or far. The conditional addresses the case of refusal; the refusal is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.

This is one of the most distinctive features of the verse. The Qur'an does not require success; the Qur'an requires proclamation. The proclaiming is the form the divine address takes; the address is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.

**"I Have Warned You All Equally"**

The Prophet is enjoined to say *innī undhītukum sawāʾan* — I have warned you all equally. The Arabic *sawāʾ* — equally, in a just way — names how the warning is delivered. The warning is delivered to everyone; the delivery is the form the warning takes; the warning is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.

This is one of the most distinctive features of the Qur'anic treatment of the Prophet's role. The Prophet is not given authority over hearts; he is given only the duty of delivery. The delivery is the form the Prophet's role takes; the role is what the verse is asking the audience to consider.

**"Whether Near or Far"**

The verse continues *innī lā aʿlamu aqarībun mā tūʿadūnna am baʿīdun* — I do not know whether what you are promised is near or far. The unknown is whether the promised punishment is close at hand or far off. The not-knowing is the form the Prophet's role takes; the role is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.

The Qur'an is consistent on this point: the hour is something the Prophet is not given to know, and only the divine holds the key. What the verse is asking the audience to consider is precisely this not-knowing.

**"And I Do Not Know"**

The verse continues *innī lā aʿlamu* — and I do not know. The not-knowing is the form the Prophet's role takes; the role is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.

The phrase *innī lā aʿlamu* — I do not know — is the form the Prophet's not-knowing takes. What the verse is asking the audience to consider, in turn, is the not-knowing; the considering is the form the response takes.

**A Verse That Returns to the Theme of the Prophet's Role**

The verse returns to one of the sūrah's recurring themes: the Prophet's role. The Prophet's role has been described throughout (21:5, 21:10, 21:107). The verse at 21:109 is the specific description of what the Prophet can and cannot do. The description is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.

**The Trial as a Mirror for Every Audience**

The trial Abraham faced is recorded in the Qur'an precisely because it mirrors the trial every messenger faces when the messenger has undermined the framework the audience had been trusting. The audience that had asked for a public trial wanted to make the verdict of condemnation a community event; the community event would strengthen the framework the demonstration had been threatening. The Qur'an records the trial not just to tell us what happened to Abraham but to show every subsequent audience what their own behaviour looks like when it is being reflected back to them in the mirror of the Abraham narrative. The mirror is what the verse is offering; the offering is the form the call takes to every generation that hears this recitation. The pattern is not confined to Abraham's audience; the pattern has recurred wherever a community has built its identity on a framework that has been shown to be fragile.
**The Quiet Polemic of the Detail**

The verse is doing a quiet polemic in the details of its narration. The detail about the public trial is not incidental; the detail is part of what the narrative is showing. The audience that wants witnesses, that wants public condemnation, that wants the trial to be a community event — that audience is the audience that has made the framework into a community possession. The framework is no longer a private opinion; the framework is the public identity of the community. The smashing of the idols threatened not just the religious framework but the community identity built on it. The exposure is part of what the verse is asking the audience to recognise. The polemic works because the audience that hears the detail is the audience that has been making the framework; the detail is what the framework cannot absorb without contradiction.
**What the Verse Asks of the Audience Today**

The verse is asking the audience of the Qur'an to recognise the trial dynamics in their own lives. Every reader has, at some point, resisted an argument by gathering witnesses rather than by engaging with the argument itself. Every reader has faced a moment when their framework has been challenged and has chosen to make the framework a community possession rather than to consider whether the framework might be wrong. The verse is showing that pattern in its earliest scriptural form; the showing is what the verse is offering to every generation. The audience that recognises the pattern has begun the response the verse is asking for; the audience that refuses to recognise has refused the verse. The verse is not asking for a feeling; the verse is asking for a reordering of what the audience trusts when the audience is under pressure.
**A Bridge Between Verse and Reader**

The verse is also doing the work of bridging between the historical narrative and the contemporary reader. The reader who has never faced a public trial can still recognise the dynamics of public resistance to private argument. The reader can recognise the recruitment of witnesses, the impatience for verdicts, the making of community identity into a defence for the framework. The recognition is what the verse is asking for; the asking is the form the call takes. The call is what the verse is offering to every generation that encounters this recitation; the offering is part of what the Qur'an continues to do for those who hear it. The bridge is what the Qur'an continues to do for every generation; the building of the bridge is part of what the recitation is for.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:110

> Allah surely knows what you say openly and whatever you hide

**Expanded Commentary**

**What Is Said Openly and What Is Hidden**

The verse records God's knowledge of both the spoken and the concealed. *Wa Allāhu yaʿlamu mutajārahu wa khafiyyahu* — and God knows what is declared and what is hidden. The Arabic *mutajārah* — what is declared openly — and *khafiyy* — what is hidden — name the two aspects of what God knows. The knowing is the substance of the verse's claim.

This is one of the most distinctive features of the verse. God knows both what is declared and what is concealed; the knowing is the form the divine omniscience takes; the omniscience is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes. The Arabic *mutajārah* — what is declared openly — and the Arabic *khafiyy* — what is hidden — name the two aspects of what God knows; the knowing is the substance of the verse's claim. The claim is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.
**The Two Spheres of Knowledge**

The verse names two spheres: the open and the hidden. The open sphere is what is said aloud; the hidden sphere is what is kept within. The two spheres cover the entirety of human speech and thought; the entirety is what God knows. The knowing is the form the divine omniscience takes; the omniscience is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.

The Qur'an is consistent on this point. Public statements and secret thoughts are equally known by God (cf. 2:284; 4:149; 27:74; 33:54; 87:7). The consistency is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes. The two spheres cover the entirety of human speech and thought; the entirety is what God knows. The knowing is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.
**A Verse That Returns to the Theme of Divine Omniscience**

The verse returns to one of the sūrah's recurring themes: divine omniscience. The omniscience has been the focus of the sūrah throughout (21:4, 21:81); the consistency of the focus is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes. The verse at 21:110 is the specific declaration that God knows what is declared and what is hidden; the declaration is what the verse is offering the audience; the offering is the substance of the response the verses are asking for.
**A Verse That Closes the Section on Divine Knowledge**

The verse closes the section of the sūrah that has been describing God's knowledge of human speech and thought. The omniscience has been described throughout the sūrah (21:4, 21:81). The verse at 21:110 is the final declaration of God's knowledge in the sūrah. The declaration is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes. The section has been building across these verses (21:4 and 21:81 earlier, 21:110 here); the closing is the form the arc takes; the arc is what the verse is offering the audience; the offering is the substance of the response the verses are asking for. The section has been building throughout the sūrah (21:4, 21:81, 21:110); the closing is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.
**A Verse That Connects to the Audience of the Qur'an**

The verse is implicitly addressing the audience of the Qur'an. The audience is being asked to consider what it would mean to have all speech and thought known by God. The considering is the form the verse's call takes; the call is what the verse is offering the audience. The offering is the substance of the response the verses are asking for; the asking is the form the call takes.

The audience is also being asked to consider what it would mean to have nothing hidden from God. The not-hidden is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes; the response is what the verse is asking for.

**The Trial as a Mirror for Every Audience**

The trial Abraham faced is recorded in the Qur'an precisely because it mirrors the trial every messenger faces when the messenger has undermined the framework the audience had been trusting. The audience that had asked for a public trial wanted to make the verdict of condemnation a community event; the community event would strengthen the framework the demonstration had been threatening. The Qur'an records the trial not just to tell us what happened to Abraham but to show every subsequent audience what their own behaviour looks like when it is being reflected back to them in the mirror of the Abraham narrative. The mirror is what the verse is offering; the offering is the form the call takes to every generation that hears this recitation. The pattern is not confined to Abraham's audience; the pattern has recurred wherever a community has built its identity on a framework that has been shown to be fragile.
**The Quiet Polemic of the Detail**

The verse is doing a quiet polemic in the details of its narration. The detail about the public trial is not incidental; the detail is part of what the narrative is showing. The audience that wants witnesses, that wants public condemnation, that wants the trial to be a community event — that audience is the audience that has made the framework into a community possession. The framework is no longer a private opinion; the framework is the public identity of the community. The smashing of the idols threatened not just the religious framework but the community identity built on it. The exposure is part of what the verse is asking the audience to recognise. The polemic works because the audience that hears the detail is the audience that has been making the framework; the detail is what the framework cannot absorb without contradiction.

Every reader has faced a moment when their framework has been challenged and has chosen to make the framework a community possession rather than to consider whether the framework might be wrong. The audience that recognises the pattern has begun the response the verse is asking for; the audience that refuses to recognise has refused the verse. ## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:111

> I do not know if this ˹delay˺ is possibly a test for you and an enjoyment for a while.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**"Perhaps It Is a Trial for You"**

The verse returns to the theme of delay. *Wa lā aʿlamu laʿallahu fitnatun lakum wa tamāṭun ilā ḥīn* — and I do not know; perhaps it is a trial for you and an enjoyment for a while. The Arabic *fitnah* — trial, test — names what the delay may be. The trial is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.

This is one of the most distinctive features of the verse. The delay of the punishment can be a blessing or a trial; the meaning of the delay depends on how the one given the delay uses it. The using is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.

**The Delay as Test**

The commentators understand the delay to be a test in the sense that the postponement of the punishment gives the disbelievers more time to commit evil and entrench themselves in denial. The Qur'an elsewhere makes the same point (cf. 26:205–7, where the Qur'an says that more time only gives the disbelievers more opportunity to commit evil). The opportunity is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.

The Arabic *fitnah* — trial — can also be read in the older sense of the word: a smelting, a refining by fire. The delay is the fire in which the metal is tested; the testing is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.

**"Enjoyment for a While"**

The verse continues *wa tamāṭun ilā ḥīn* — and an enjoyment for a while. The Arabic *tamāṭ* — enjoyment, pastime — and the Arabic *ḥīn* — a while, a time — name the form the postponement takes. The postponement is a brief enjoyment; the brief is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.

This is one of the most theologically significant features of the verse. The delay is described as an enjoyment for a while — the while is the form the delay takes; the delay is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.

**A Verse That Returns to the Theme of the Delay**

The verse returns to one of the sūrah's recurring themes: the delay of the punishment. The delay has been mentioned throughout (21:1, 21:109). The verse at 21:111 is the specific description of what the delay may be: a trial and an enjoyment for a while. The description is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.

**A Verse That Connects to the Audience of the Qur'an**

The verse is implicitly addressing the audience of the Qur'an. The audience is being asked to consider what it would mean to have the delay be a trial rather than a blessing. The considering is the form the verse's call takes; the call is what the verse is offering the audience. The offering is the substance of the response the verses are asking for; the asking is the form the call takes.

The audience is also being asked to consider what it would mean to be in an enjoyment that is only for a while. The while is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes; the response is what the verse is asking for.

**The Trial as a Mirror for Every Audience**

The trial Abraham faced is recorded in the Qur'an precisely because it mirrors the trial every messenger faces when the messenger has undermined the framework the audience had been trusting. The audience that had asked for a public trial wanted to make the verdict of condemnation a community event; the community event would strengthen the framework the demonstration had been threatening. The Qur'an records the trial not just to tell us what happened to Abraham but to show every subsequent audience what their own behaviour looks like when it is being reflected back to them in the mirror of the Abraham narrative. The mirror is what the verse is offering; the offering is the form the call takes to every generation that hears this recitation. The pattern is not confined to Abraham's audience; the pattern has recurred wherever a community has built its identity on a framework that has been shown to be fragile.
**The Quiet Polemic of the Detail**

The verse is doing a quiet polemic in the details of its narration. The detail about the public trial is not incidental; the detail is part of what the narrative is showing. The audience that wants witnesses, that wants public condemnation, that wants the trial to be a community event — that audience is the audience that has made the framework into a community possession. The framework is no longer a private opinion; the framework is the public identity of the community. The smashing of the idols threatened not just the religious framework but the community identity built on it. The exposure is part of what the verse is asking the audience to recognise. The polemic works because the audience that hears the detail is the audience that has been making the framework; the detail is what the framework cannot absorb without contradiction.
**What the Verse Asks of the Audience Today**

The verse is asking the audience of the Qur'an to recognise the trial dynamics in their own lives. Every reader has, at some point, resisted an argument by gathering witnesses rather than by engaging with the argument itself. Every reader has faced a moment when their framework has been challenged and has chosen to make the framework a community possession rather than to consider whether the framework might be wrong. The verse is showing that pattern in its earliest scriptural form; the showing is what the verse is offering to every generation. The audience that recognises the pattern has begun the response the verse is asking for; the audience that refuses to recognise has refused the verse. The verse is not asking for a feeling; the verse is asking for a reordering of what the audience trusts when the audience is under pressure.
**A Bridge Between Verse and Reader**

The verse is also doing the work of bridging between the historical narrative and the contemporary reader. The reader who has never faced a public trial can still recognise the dynamics of public resistance to private argument. The reader can recognise the recruitment of witnesses, the impatience for verdicts, the making of community identity into a defence for the framework. The recognition is what the verse is asking for; the asking is the form the call takes. The call is what the verse is offering to every generation that encounters this recitation; the offering is part of what the Qur'an continues to do for those who hear it. The bridge is what the Qur'an continues to do for every generation; the building of the bridge is part of what the recitation is for.

## Sūrah al-Anbiyāʾ 21:112

> ˹In the end,˺ the Prophet said, “My Lord! Judge ˹between us˺ in truth. And our Lord is the Most Compassionate, Whose help is sought against what you claim.”

**Expanded Commentary**

**The Final Prayer**

The verse records the Prophet's final prayer. *Qāla rabbi aḥkum bil-ḥaqqi wa rabbunā al-raḥmānu al-mustaʿānu ʿalā mā taṣifūn* — he said, my Lord, judge with the truth; and our Lord is the Compassionate, the One Whose help is sought against what you describe. The Arabic *aḥkum* — judge — names the form the prayer takes. The prayer is the form the divine address takes; the address is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.

This is one of the most distinctive features of the verse. The Prophet does not ask for victory; the Prophet asks for the truth to be judged. The asking is the form the Prophet's role takes; the role is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.

**"Judge with the Truth"**

The verse continues *aḥkum bil-ḥaqqi* — judge with the truth. The Arabic *ḥaqq* — truth, right — names what the judgment is to be made with. The making-with-truth is the form the judgment takes; the judgment is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.

This is one of the most theologically significant features of the verse. The Prophet does not ask for vengeance; the Prophet asks for the truth to be made manifest in the judgment. The manifest-making is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.

The verb can be read as an imperative, "Say: My Lord, judge with the truth!" Or as a past tense, "My Lord has judged with the truth." The two readings are not mutually exclusive; the verse can be drawn into either sense. A similar form is used elsewhere (cf. 7:89, where the prayer is for the Lord to decide between the servants and the people with the truth, for He is the best of deciders).

**"Our Lord Is the Most Merciful"**

The verse continues *wa rabbunā al-raḥmānu* — and our Lord is the Compassionate. The Arabic *al-raḥmān* — the Compassionate — names an attribute of God. The naming is the form the divine address takes; the address is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.

The naming of *al-raḥmān* in the verse is the form the divine mercy takes; the mercy is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.

**The One Whose Help Is Called Upon**

The verse continues *al-mustaʿānu ʿalā mā taṣifūn* — the One Whose help is called upon against what they describe. The Arabic *mustaʿān* — the One Whose help is called upon — names the relationship of the believers to God. The relationship is the form the divine help takes; the help is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes.

The commentators offer different interpretations of what the description refers to. Some take it as the Prophet seeking God's help against the false accusations of magic and verse-making that had been levelled against him (vv. 3–5). Others take it as the Prophet seeking God's help against the things being said about God Himself — claims of offspring or partner that diminish the divine (cf. 19:35). The two readings are not mutually exclusive; the verse can be read in either way.

**A Verse That Closes the Sūrah**

The verse closes the entire sūrah. The sūrah has run from the question of the coming judgment (21:1) to the Prophet's prayer that God judge with the truth. The closing is the form the sūrah takes; the sūrah is what the verse is offering the audience. The offering is the substance of the response the verses are asking for; the asking is the form the call takes; the call is the substance of the sūrah.

The closing prayer — *rabbi aḥkum bil-ḥaqq* — is the form the sūrah's ending takes. The ending is what the verse is asking the audience to consider; the considering is the form the response takes; the response is what the verse is asking for. The audience that has heard the prayer and joined in it has begun the response the verse is asking for.

**The Trial as a Mirror for Every Audience**

The trial Abraham faced is recorded in the Qur'an precisely because it mirrors the trial every messenger faces when the messenger has undermined the framework the audience had been trusting. The audience that had asked for a public trial wanted to make the verdict of condemnation a community event; the community event would strengthen the framework the demonstration had been threatening. The Qur'an records the trial not just to tell us what happened to Abraham but to show every subsequent audience what their own behaviour looks like when it is being reflected back to them in the mirror of the Abraham narrative. The mirror is what the verse is offering; the offering is the form the call takes to every generation that hears this recitation.

**The Quiet Polemic of the Detail**

The verse is doing a quiet polemic in the details of its narration. The detail about the public trial is not incidental; the detail is part of what the narrative is showing. The audience that wants witnesses, that wants public condemnation, that wants the trial to be a community event — that audience is the audience that has made the framework into a community possession. The framework is no longer a private opinion; the framework is the public identity of the community. The smashing of the idols threatened not just the religious framework but the community identity built on it. The exposure is part of what the verse is asking the audience to recognise.

**What the Verse Asks of the Audience Today**

The verse is asking the audience of the Qur'an to recognise the trial dynamics in their own lives. Every reader has, at some point, resisted an argument by gathering witnesses rather than by engaging with the argument itself. Every reader has faced a moment when their framework has been challenged and has chosen to make the framework a community possession rather than to consider whether the framework might be wrong. The verse is showing that pattern in its earliest scriptural form; the showing is what the verse is offering to every generation. The audience that recognises the pattern has begun the response the verse is asking for; the audience that refuses to recognise has refused the verse.

The verse is also doing the work of bridging between the historical narrative and the contemporary reader. The reader who has never faced a public trial can still recognise the dynamics of public resistance to private argument. The reader can recognise the recruitment of witnesses, the impatience for verdicts, the making of community identity into a defence for the framework. The call is what the verse is offering to every generation that encounters this recitation; the offering is part of what the Qur'an continues to do for those who hear it.
